# Continual Learning: A Comprehensive Survey of Foundations, Methods, Theory, Applications, and Future Directions

## 1 Introduction and Foundations of Continual Learning

### 1.1 What is Continual Learning? Definitions and Core Concepts

## 1.1 Defining Continual Learning

Continual learning (CL), also referred to as lifelong learning, incremental learning, or sequential learning, is a machine learning paradigm in which a model acquires knowledge from a continuous, non-stationary stream of data over time, building upon previously learned experiences without losing what it has already mastered [1; 2]. This paradigm stands in stark contrast to the dominant tradition of batch learning, where a model is trained once on a fixed dataset drawn from a stationary distribution assumed to be independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.), and remains static thereafter. In real-world deployments, however, data rarely conforms to such idealized assumptions; instead, it arrives sequentially, often reflecting a shifting and evolving environment [3; 4].

### Core Terminology

To reason precisely about continual learning, it is essential to establish a clear vocabulary of foundational concepts:

- **Task**: A task is a well-defined learning problem, typically characterized by a dataset, a loss function, and an associated objective. Tasks may correspond to distinct classification problems, domains, or environments. In many formulations, tasks arrive in a sequence, one after another, and the learner is expected to perform well on all tasks encountered so far [2; 1].

- **Task Sequence**: A task sequence $\mathcal{T} = \{T_1, T_2, \ldots, T_n\}$ is an ordered collection of tasks presented to the learner over time. The ordering may be fixed or vary, and tasks may differ substantially in their data distributions, label spaces, or underlying generative processes [4; 5].

- **Experience**: Drawing inspiration from neuroscience and cognitive science, the term *experience* refers to a learning episode comprising a subset of data associated with a particular task or time step. An experience encapsulates the data distribution, supervision signal, and context available to the learner at a given point in the task sequence [2; 6].

- **Non-Stationarity**: A defining property of continual learning settings is that the data distribution $p_t(x, y)$ changes across time steps or tasks. This non-stationarity can manifest as covariate shift, label shift, concept drift, or the emergence of entirely new classes and domains [7; 8].

- **Knowledge Retention and Transfer**: Beyond merely learning new tasks, a continual learner is expected to retain previously acquired knowledge (avoiding *catastrophic forgetting*) and ideally leverage past experience to accelerate or improve learning on future tasks (i.e., *forward transfer*), while new learning may also refine representations useful for old tasks (i.e., *backward transfer*) [1; 9].

### Biological Inspiration

The notion of continual learning is deeply inspired by biological systems. Humans and animals effortlessly acquire new skills and knowledge throughout their lifetimes without catastrophically erasing prior competencies [1]. This biological capacity for lifelong learning motivates the central aspiration of the field: to endow artificial neural networks with the same graceful ability to accumulate, organize, and generalize knowledge over extended periods of time [10; 11]. As will be discussed in the following section, however, artificial neural networks conspicuously fail to replicate this biological property — a failure that manifests as catastrophic forgetting, the central challenge of the field.

### Distinction from Traditional Batch Learning

In classical supervised learning, it is assumed that all training data is available simultaneously and drawn i.i.d. from a fixed underlying distribution. Models are optimized in full knowledge of this static dataset, and their performance is evaluated on a held-out test set drawn from the same distribution. Continual learning fundamentally violates these assumptions: data arrives sequentially, previous data may no longer be accessible due to memory constraints or privacy requirements, and the distribution of incoming data can shift arbitrarily between tasks [3; 12]. This shift in assumptions necessitates entirely new algorithmic strategies, as standard gradient-based optimization on new data tends to overwrite previously learned representations — a phenomenon known as catastrophic forgetting that is examined in depth in Section 1.2 [2].

Formally, let a continual learning system receive a sequence of experiences $\{(D_1, \mathcal{L}_1), (D_2, \mathcal{L}_2), \ldots, (D_n, \mathcal{L}_n)\}$, where $D_t$ is the dataset and $\mathcal{L}_t$ is the loss function associated with task $T_t$. At each time step $t$, the learner has access only to $D_t$ (and potentially a small memory of past samples), and must update its parameters $\theta$ so as to minimize $\mathcal{L}_t$ while preserving performance on all previous tasks $T_1, \ldots, T_{t-1}$. This formulation captures the core challenge of continual learning: learning without forgetting, under strict constraints on data access and computational resources [12; 4].

The goal of continual learning, broadly stated, is therefore to develop learning systems that are simultaneously *plastic* — capable of efficiently incorporating new information — and *stable* — capable of retaining previously acquired knowledge — across an open-ended sequence of learning experiences [1; 13]. This dual requirement, and the inherent tension between these two properties, constitutes the foundation upon which the entire field is built. Understanding precisely why this tension is so difficult to resolve in practice requires a closer examination of the mechanisms underlying catastrophic forgetting, which we turn to next.

### 1.2 Catastrophic Forgetting: The Central Challenge

## 1.2 Catastrophic Forgetting: The Central Challenge

Catastrophic forgetting (CF) — sometimes termed catastrophic interference — is arguably the most fundamental obstacle confronting continual learning systems. As introduced in the preceding section, the core aspiration of continual learning is to develop systems that are simultaneously plastic and stable; catastrophic forgetting is precisely what happens when plasticity overwhelms stability. The phenomenon describes the abrupt and dramatic degradation of a neural network's performance on previously learned tasks when the network is subsequently trained on new tasks or data distributions. Unlike the gradual, selective forgetting observed in biological memory systems, which tend to retain important information while gracefully discarding peripheral details, artificial neural networks trained via gradient-based optimization can lose previously acquired knowledge almost entirely and almost instantaneously upon exposure to new training signals. This phenomenon was first identified in the context of connectionist models and has since been recognized as a pervasive, deep-seated challenge across virtually all neural network architectures and learning settings [14].

### Root Causes: Gradient-Based Optimization and Shared Parameterization

The origins of catastrophic forgetting lie in two intertwined properties of modern neural networks. First, gradient-based learning algorithms such as stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and its variants optimize network parameters to minimize the loss on the current batch of training data, with no explicit mechanism to preserve performance on data from prior tasks. When the training distribution shifts — as it does when a new task is introduced in a sequential learning scenario — the gradient updates are shaped entirely by the new data, and adjustments to shared parameters that were previously tuned for old tasks can rapidly overwrite the representations and decision boundaries those tasks relied upon [15]. Second, standard neural networks employ a shared parameter space across all tasks: the same weights that encode knowledge about task A are also modified during learning of task B. Because no structural separation is enforced between task-specific representations, updates beneficial for the new task frequently conflict with — and thereby destructively overwrite — the parameter configurations critical for prior tasks [16].

From a geometric perspective, each task defines a region of parameter space where its loss is acceptably low. These regions may overlap substantially when tasks are similar, but for dissimilar tasks they can occupy largely disjoint subspaces of the high-dimensional parameter landscape. Gradient descent during task B drives the parameters toward the loss basin of task B without regard for the loss basin of task A, and if these basins are far apart, the model exits the region of good performance for task A, yielding catastrophic forgetting [17]. The degree of overlap between task-specific loss minima, which is closely related to the functional and distributional similarity between tasks, therefore plays a central role in determining how severely forgetting manifests [18]. This geometric intuition also helps clarify the formal continual learning objective introduced in Section 1.1: the requirement to minimize $\mathcal{L}_t$ while preserving performance on all previous tasks $T_1, \ldots, T_{t-1}$ is precisely the requirement to find a single parameter configuration that resides simultaneously in the acceptable loss basins of all encountered tasks — a constraint that becomes increasingly difficult to satisfy as the number and diversity of tasks grows.

### Empirical Evidence Across Architectures and Settings

A substantial body of empirical research has documented catastrophic forgetting across a wide range of neural network architectures, task types, and training regimes. Early studies on feedforward networks established that sequential task learning leads to dramatic performance collapse on earlier tasks, and this finding has been replicated and extended to modern deep architectures including convolutional neural networks, recurrent networks, transformers, and graph neural networks [19; 20].

Investigations into the internal dynamics of forgetting reveal that it is not a monolithic phenomenon but rather a composite of several interacting processes. Representational analyses using techniques such as Centered Kernel Alignment and linear probing show that deeper layers of a network are disproportionately affected by catastrophic forgetting compared to earlier feature-extraction layers [18]. A detailed decomposition of the accuracy loss attributed to catastrophic forgetting identifies readout misalignment — the mismatch between shifted internal representations and fixed classification heads — as the dominant contributing factor, with a comparatively smaller fraction of information being irrecoverably lost from the representations themselves [21].

Studies examining individual training examples rather than task-level aggregates further illuminate the fine-grained structure of forgetting. By tracking whether specific examples transition from being classified correctly to incorrectly over the course of training, researchers have observed that certain examples are highly susceptible to forgetting while others are remarkably robust, and that this pattern of "forgettable" versus "unforgettable" examples generalizes across different network architectures [22]. This heterogeneity suggests that forgetting does not affect all knowledge uniformly but preferentially targets information encoded in less robust, more interference-prone regions of the parameter space.

The severity of catastrophic forgetting is also sensitive to network architecture choices. Empirical studies demonstrate that wider neural networks — those with greater numbers of units per layer — exhibit markedly less catastrophic forgetting than their narrower counterparts, a finding explained through the lenses of gradient orthogonality, representational sparsity, and proximity to the lazy training regime where parameter changes remain small relative to their initial values [23]. Similarly, the choice of activation function profoundly influences forgetting dynamics: activation functions that produce sparse representations and sparse gradients have been shown to reduce forgetting significantly across regression, classification, and reinforcement learning settings [24]. The role of training regimes — including learning rate schedules, batch size, and regularization strategies such as dropout — has also been documented as a critical determinant of the geometry of the loss landscape and consequently of the degree of catastrophic forgetting [25].

In the context of large language models (LLMs), catastrophic forgetting manifests during continual fine-tuning, with empirical studies showing that sequential instruction tuning across diverse NLP tasks leads to substantial degradation of previously acquired knowledge across domain understanding, reasoning, and reading comprehension [26]. Notably, the severity of forgetting in LLMs appears to scale with model size, and architectural choices such as decoder-only versus encoder-decoder designs influence the degree to which forgetting occurs.

### Theoretical Perspectives on Catastrophic Forgetting

Beyond empirical documentation, several theoretical frameworks have been developed to characterize and analyze catastrophic forgetting. One influential perspective uses the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) to formalize the relationship between task similarity and interference: the NTK overlap matrix between two tasks quantifies the degree to which gradient updates for one task affect the predictions of another, providing a principled measure of task alignment that predicts the magnitude of catastrophic forgetting [17]. Tasks with high NTK overlap experience more severe mutual interference, while orthogonal tasks — those whose gradient subspaces are largely non-overlapping — can be learned more sequentially without destructive interference. This theoretical insight underpins a family of gradient projection methods that mitigate forgetting by constraining updates to lie in the orthogonal complement of the gradient space of previous tasks.

Statistical mechanical analyses using teacher-student learning frameworks provide complementary theoretical insights, showing that the degree of forgetting depends jointly on the similarity of input distributions and the input-output relationships of successive tasks [27]. These analyses reveal an interesting asymmetry: low similarity between input distributions reduces forgetting, while high similarity between input-output relationships helps the network leverage previously learned mappings for new tasks. The analyses also predict the phenomenon of "overshoot," whereby a network that has undergone catastrophic forgetting on an earlier task may partially recover performance on that task after further training on the new task, a counter-intuitive prediction with implications for understanding knowledge dynamics in long task sequences.

It is also important to acknowledge that catastrophic forgetting coexists with a related but distinct challenge sometimes termed catastrophic remembering — the failure of a continually trained model to discriminate between data from different tasks due to interference in the opposite direction [28]. While most continual learning research focuses on preventing the forgetting of old knowledge, a comprehensive solution must also address the capacity of the network to maintain distinct, non-interfering representations for tasks with different statistical structures.

Taken together, the empirical and theoretical evidence establishes catastrophic forgetting as a deep and multifaceted challenge rooted in the fundamental mechanics of gradient-based learning in shared parameter spaces. Understanding its causes and manifestations across diverse settings is an essential prerequisite for developing principled and effective continual learning algorithms. Crucially, the analysis presented in this section also reveals why resolving catastrophic forgetting is inseparable from the broader question of how to balance stability and plasticity — the tension that will be examined in depth in the following section. Every mechanism that reduces forgetting does so, ultimately, by sacrificing some degree of plasticity; and every mechanism that enhances plasticity risks reintroducing forgetting. It is precisely this inescapable trade-off, and the search for principled ways to navigate it, that defines the stability-plasticity dilemma as the central organizing concept of the entire field.

### 1.3 The Stability-Plasticity Dilemma

## 1.3 The Stability-Plasticity Dilemma

At the heart of continual learning lies one of the most fundamental and enduring tensions in both biological and artificial learning systems: the stability-plasticity dilemma. Building directly on the phenomenon of catastrophic forgetting described in the preceding section, this dilemma captures the inherent conflict between two competing objectives that any learning system must balance. **Stability** refers to the capacity to preserve previously acquired knowledge and resist overwriting useful representations when exposed to new experiences. **Plasticity**, on the other hand, denotes the ability to rapidly incorporate new information, adapt to novel tasks, and update internal representations in response to changing environments. Achieving high performance on both dimensions simultaneously is extraordinarily difficult, and the failure to do so lies at the root of catastrophic forgetting in artificial neural networks [1; 29].

### Biological Roots of the Dilemma

The concept of the stability-plasticity dilemma was originally articulated in the context of neuroscience and cognitive psychology, where it was used to explain how biological brains manage to learn continuously across an organism's lifespan without systematically erasing prior knowledge [30]. Biological neural systems have evolved sophisticated mechanisms to navigate this tension. Synaptic consolidation processes selectively strengthen connections associated with important memories while allowing weaker, less-used pathways to remain malleable. Neuromodulatory systems, including dopaminergic and cholinergic circuits, dynamically regulate the degree of synaptic plasticity across brain regions and in response to environmental demands [31]. Memory consolidation during sleep, the complementary functions of the hippocampus and neocortex, and mechanisms of structural plasticity including neurogenesis all serve to balance the need for both stability and adaptability across timescales [1].

These observations from neuroscience have directly inspired artificial continual learning research. The observation that neurons can exhibit context-selective responses and that local availability variables can partially freeze plasticity for neurons relevant to prior tasks has been translated into computational models that emulate this behavior [32]. Similarly, proposals drawing from Drosophila learning systems that actively regulate forgetting via multiple learning modules have illustrated how biological intelligence coordinates stability and plasticity through modular, adaptive forgetting mechanisms rather than treating stability as an absolute objective [31].

### The Dilemma in Artificial Neural Networks

In artificial deep learning systems, the stability-plasticity dilemma manifests acutely and is inseparable from the gradient-based optimization and shared parameterization issues identified in the previous section as root causes of catastrophic forgetting. When a neural network is trained on a new task using stochastic gradient descent, parameter updates are driven exclusively by the gradient of the current task loss. Because neural network parameters are shared across tasks, these updates can substantially overwrite the parameter configurations that encoded prior task knowledge, leading to catastrophic forgetting [1]. Conversely, if the network is heavily regularized to prevent any parameter change, it loses the flexibility needed to absorb new information effectively—sacrificing plasticity for stability.

This tension is pervasive across virtually all learning paradigms and neural architectures. Highly plastic networks readily acquire new skills but forget previous ones rapidly; highly stable networks preserve prior knowledge but struggle to adapt to new distributions [29; 33]. In the online continual learning setting, where data arrives in a single-pass streaming fashion, this problem is especially pronounced because the network has limited opportunity to consolidate new information before the data distribution shifts again [33; 34]. As will become apparent in the subsequent section on problem settings, the precise manifestation of this dilemma varies considerably across task-incremental, class-incremental, domain-incremental, and online learning scenarios, each of which imposes distinct constraints on how stability and plasticity can be balanced.

Several empirical studies have demonstrated that the stability gap—a phenomenon whereby models experience significant temporary forgetting immediately upon encountering a new task before partially recovering performance—is a pervasive characteristic of many state-of-the-art continual learning methods, suggesting that the stability-plasticity balance is not merely a theoretical concern but a practical obstacle that affects real systems [35]. This gap was observed across experience replay, constraint-based replay, knowledge distillation, and parameter regularization methods alike, reinforcing that no single existing approach fully resolves the dilemma.

### Formalizing the Trade-off

The stability-plasticity dilemma can be understood through the lens of optimization. When learning task $t$ after having learned tasks $1, \ldots, t-1$, a continual learning algorithm must update model parameters $\theta$ to minimize the current task loss $\mathcal{L}_t(\theta)$ while simultaneously preserving $\theta \approx \theta^*_{1:t-1}$, the parameter configuration that performed well on prior tasks. These two objectives directly conflict: gradient steps that minimize $\mathcal{L}_t(\theta)$ generally move parameters away from $\theta^*_{1:t-1}$, and constraints that enforce proximity to $\theta^*_{1:t-1}$ impede the optimization of the new task. This fundamental tension cannot be eliminated without additional inductive structure, memory, or architectural design choices.

Theoretical analyses have formalized this observation. Memory lower bound results in the PAC framework establish that any continual learner must retain memory growing linearly with the number of tasks to avoid catastrophic forgetting, underscoring that perfect stability without memory overhead is computationally intractable [12]. This theoretical perspective implies that the stability-plasticity dilemma is not merely an artifact of specific algorithmic choices but is a fundamental property of sequential learning under bounded resources—a conclusion that complements the NTK-based and statistical mechanical analyses of catastrophic forgetting reviewed in the previous section.

### Measuring the Dilemma

Properly assessing how well a continual learning system balances stability and plasticity requires dedicated evaluation metrics. Standard accuracy measures computed only at the end of a task sequence are insufficient to capture the dynamic interplay between the two objectives throughout the learning process [36]. Recent work has proposed metrics that account for setup-induced forgetting—the inherent performance loss resulting from increasing task difficulty—and that disentangle stability and plasticity components of model behavior [36]. Continuous, per-iteration evaluation protocols that track worst-case performance rather than only final-task accuracy provide a more faithful picture of how systems navigate the stability-plasticity trade-off across the entire learning trajectory [35].

### Motivating a Generation of Solutions

The recognition of the stability-plasticity dilemma as the central challenge of continual learning has motivated every major family of approaches developed to address it. Regularization-based methods such as Elastic Weight Consolidation and Synaptic Intelligence seek stability by penalizing changes to parameters identified as important for prior tasks. Replay-based methods achieve a balance by periodically revisiting stored or generated exemplars of past experiences, effectively increasing plasticity on current tasks while rehearsing stability for prior ones. Architecture-based methods attempt to allocate separate or partially overlapping parameter subspaces to different tasks, allowing new tasks to be learned in plastic subspaces while old task representations remain frozen in stable ones [37]. Prompt-based and pre-trained model approaches leverage frozen feature representations from large pre-trained models to provide a stable foundation while adapting only a small number of task-specific parameters to maintain plasticity [38]. These methodological families are examined in depth in later sections of this survey, but their common motivation in addressing the stability-plasticity dilemma is worth emphasizing here.

Novel approaches have further sought to explicitly decouple the two objectives rather than treating them as a single unified optimization target. For instance, the PromptFusion framework introduces separate modules—a Stabilizer responsible for preventing forgetting and a Booster responsible for acquiring new knowledge—whose outputs are adaptively fused during inference [38]. Similarly, auxiliary network frameworks propose augmenting the primary stability-focused continual learner with a plasticity-promoting auxiliary network, with regularization interpolating between their objectives [39]. Weighted ensemble methods average the parameters of prior and current task models, leveraging Fisher information to selectively weigh each parameter's contribution to the ensemble and thus balance stability and plasticity at a per-parameter level [40].

Multi-mechanism bio-inspired paradigms have taken yet another approach, drawing from the observation that biological brains simultaneously employ neurogenesis, active forgetting, neuromodulation, metaplasticity, and experience rehearsal to achieve robust continual learning without catastrophic forgetting. Frameworks such as TriRE attempt to emulate this multi-process character by combining neuron retention, knowledge revision, and active neuron promotion in a unified learning paradigm [41].

Sparse distributed representations, originally motivated by the architecture of hippocampal and neocortical circuits, have also been proposed as a structural solution to the dilemma. By ensuring that different tasks activate largely non-overlapping subsets of neurons, sparse architectures naturally reduce the interference that underlies the stability-plasticity conflict [42; 43]. Architecture designs incorporating local learning rules, synaptic plasticity, and neuromodulatory signals have demonstrated the ability to support online continual learning without replay, using local error signals to update parameters in a manner that preserves prior representations without global parameter conflicts [44; 45].

### The Dilemma as an Organizing Principle

The stability-plasticity dilemma serves not merely as a description of a challenge but as an organizing principle for the entire field of continual learning. Every methodological decision—from the choice of regularizer to the design of memory buffers, from the selection of architecture to the specification of training objectives—can be analyzed through the lens of how it affects the stability-plasticity balance. This organizing role extends naturally to the taxonomy of problem settings discussed in the following section: the degree to which a particular scenario such as task-incremental or class-incremental learning stresses stability versus plasticity has direct implications for which algorithmic families are most appropriate and how evaluation protocols should be designed. Understanding this dilemma at a deep level, both theoretically and empirically, is therefore prerequisite for evaluating the strengths and limitations of existing approaches and for charting the course toward learning systems that can truly accumulate knowledge continuously without sacrificing either the lessons of the past or the ability to learn anew [46; 10].

In summary, the stability-plasticity dilemma is the foundational tension that defines continual learning as a field. Its origins in neuroscience, its formal characterization in computational frameworks, its empirical manifestation across diverse architectures and benchmarks, and its role in motivating the full spectrum of continual learning methods make it an indispensable concept for any comprehensive understanding of the challenges and opportunities in lifelong machine learning. With this dilemma firmly established, the following section turns to the diverse problem settings through which continual learning research has been operationalized, each of which instantiates the stability-plasticity tension in distinct and practically meaningful ways.

### 1.4 Continual Learning Scenarios and Problem Settings

## 1.4 Problem Settings and Evaluation Scenarios in Continual Learning

A critical step toward understanding continual learning research is recognizing that it encompasses a diverse family of problem settings, each imposing distinct constraints, assumptions, and levels of difficulty. As established in the preceding discussion of the stability-plasticity dilemma, the specific scenario under which a continual learner operates fundamentally shapes how the tension between stability and plasticity manifests, which algorithmic families are most appropriate, and how performance should be evaluated. The research community has converged on a set of canonical scenarios that differ primarily in what information is available at test time, how tasks are delineated, and how the label space evolves over time. Precisely characterizing these scenarios is essential, as the choice of evaluation setting profoundly influences which methods are appropriate and how results should be interpreted.

### Task-Incremental Learning (TIL)

In task-incremental learning, a model is trained on a sequence of distinct tasks, and crucially, the task identity is provided both at training and at inference time. This makes TIL the most tractable of the standard scenarios: because the task descriptor is always available, the learner can maintain separate output heads or decision boundaries per task and simply route each test sample to the appropriate head. The central challenge reduces primarily to preventing the shared representation from degrading as new tasks are introduced—a problem that closely mirrors the stability side of the stability-plasticity dilemma, since plasticity is largely decoupled from the interference problem through task-routing. While TIL serves as a useful testbed for studying representation drift and transfer, providing task identity at test time is an assumption that rarely holds in open-world deployments, limiting its ecological validity.

### Class-Incremental Learning (CIL)

Class-incremental learning removes the luxury of task identity at inference time. The model must classify test samples into any class encountered across all past and present tasks, with no indication of which task generated the sample. This scenario is substantially harder because the model must simultaneously solve a within-task discrimination problem and an across-task discrimination problem, placing acute pressure on both stability and plasticity simultaneously. As noted in [47], a strong CIL system requires both strong within-task prediction and effective out-of-distribution detection for each task, and the paper further establishes theoretical conditions under which CIL is learnable. The CIL setting has attracted the majority of attention in recent continual learning research; however, as argued in [48], an exclusive focus on this scenario may be limiting because class-incremental benchmarks artificially exacerbate catastrophic forgetting by design, suppressing the investigation of forward transfer and computational efficiency. In particular, because classes from earlier tasks never reappear in later experiences, CIL creates an extreme distribution shift that is not representative of many real-world data streams where previously seen concepts are naturally revisited.

### Domain-Incremental Learning (DIL)

In domain-incremental learning, the set of classes remains fixed across tasks, but the input distribution shifts from one experience to the next, reflecting changes in sensor conditions, imaging protocols, acquisition environments, or stylistic variations. Unlike CIL, no new output nodes are added; instead, the model must maintain accurate predictions across a fixed label set despite exposure to progressively different data distributions. The stability-plasticity tension here is primarily expressed as a challenge of domain adaptation under the constraint of no access to past data: the model must remain plastic enough to accommodate new domain statistics while stable enough to retain performance on prior domains. DIL arises naturally in applications such as autonomous driving—where a segmentation model is updated sequentially as it encounters new cities or weather conditions—medical imaging—where images from different clinical sites or acquisition protocols arrive over time—and natural language processing, where a sentiment classifier must generalize across evolving product domains. Methods for continual semantic segmentation under domain shift [49] and for aspect sentiment classification across product domains [50] illustrate how DIL appears in specialized sub-fields.

### Cross-Domain Incremental Learning

An emerging and more demanding variant, cross-domain incremental learning, combines elements of both CIL and DIL by requiring the model to handle tasks that originate from substantially different source domains or even different modalities. Unlike standard DIL where the shift is between closely related distributions, cross-domain settings involve tasks from heterogeneous domains such as natural images, medical scans, and satellite imagery appearing in the same task sequence. This setting poses the additional challenge of inter-domain data drift and feature misalignment, requiring methods that can align representations across domains while incrementally accumulating knowledge. The LifeLonger benchmark [51] explicitly introduces a cross-domain incremental learning scenario for disease classification, where models must handle datasets originating from different clinical institutions. The CDCL framework [52] directly addresses this challenge by combining inter- and intra-task cross-attention mechanisms with unsupervised domain adaptation to maintain alignment with features from previous tasks.

### Data-Incremental Learning

Data-incremental learning describes a setting in which the same underlying task is learned incrementally as more data becomes available over time, without any formal task boundary. There are no discrete task identifiers; rather, the distribution from which data is drawn may shift smoothly or abruptly, and the model must continuously incorporate new information. This scenario is closely related to online learning but differs in emphasizing the accumulation of a growing body of knowledge rather than moment-to-moment prediction accuracy. It arises in practice whenever labeled data is collected progressively, as in continuously growing clinical databases or evolving document corpora. From the perspective of the stability-plasticity dilemma, this setting is notable because the absence of explicit task boundaries means that the system must autonomously regulate its own balance between consolidation and adaptation.

### Online Continual Learning

Online continual learning represents a particularly demanding setting in which data arrives as a single continuous, non-stationary stream without explicit task boundaries, and each sample can be used only once for training before being discarded or moved to a small episodic buffer. As formalized in [7], the online setting requires models to be evaluated immediately upon seeing each new batch—before training on it—and subsequently assessed on historical data to measure retention, thereby coupling the learning efficiency and memory retention objectives into a single evaluation protocol. The work in [53] further refines this setting by proposing an anytime inference requirement, where the model must respond to queries at any point during the data stream, adding the constraint that useful representations must be maintained throughout training rather than only at the end. The single-pass constraint makes online continual learning especially challenging because the model has limited exposure to each sample and cannot revisit data, ruling out multi-epoch training strategies standard in offline settings. As noted in the previous section's discussion of the stability gap, this is precisely the regime in which the stability-plasticity dilemma is most acute, since the network has minimal opportunity to consolidate new information before the data distribution shifts again [33].

### Blurry and Task-Free Settings

Real-world data streams rarely exhibit sharp task transitions. The blurry task boundary setting relaxes the assumption that consecutive experiences contain entirely disjoint class sets, instead allowing classes to appear across multiple experiences with varying frequencies. This creates a more realistic but also more complex evaluation where the notion of a "task" is ill-defined. The i-Blurry benchmark [53] operationalizes this concept by mixing a small fraction of data from previous and future tasks into each experience, simulating natural co-occurrence of old and new concepts. Task-free or task-agnostic settings go further by removing all assumption of known task boundaries during training as well as inference, forcing methods to infer internal structure directly from the data stream. Domain-incremental variants of this setting, such as those explored in [54], propose automatic task-ID inference mechanisms that detect distribution shifts on-the-fly without relying on manual task boundary annotations.

### General Incremental Learning

Some recent works argue for a yet more general formulation that unifies class and domain shifts. In this setting, both the class distribution and the class-specific domain distribution change over time, introducing simultaneous inter-class and intra-class distribution shifts [55]. This general incremental learning paradigm captures real-world complexity more faithfully than any of the preceding scenarios but also demands methods capable of handling multiple sources of non-stationarity simultaneously, making it one of the most challenging problem settings in the literature. It is in this setting that the limitations of approaches optimized narrowly for stability or plasticity become most apparent, as the diversity of task types encountered across the learning lifetime requires a genuinely adaptive and cumulative knowledge-building process.

### Comparative Difficulty and Practical Considerations

The relative difficulty of these scenarios increases roughly in the order: TIL < DIL < CIL < blurry/task-free < cross-domain incremental < general incremental learning. However, practical difficulty also depends heavily on factors such as the number of tasks in the sequence, the degree of distribution shift between consecutive experiences, the size of available memory buffers, and whether computational budget is constrained. As highlighted in [56], most continual learning methods suffer from stability and underfitting issues, and basic experience replay, when properly tuned, remains a strong baseline even in challenging online class-incremental settings. The fragmentation of evaluation protocols across these diverse scenarios makes it difficult to compare methods fairly, underscoring the need for standardized benchmarks and scenario definitions that reflect real-world non-stationarity [57].

Understanding this taxonomy of scenarios is foundational to interpreting the continual learning literature, as algorithm design choices, theoretical analyses, and empirical evaluations are all deeply tied to the specific problem setting assumed. Moreover, as the following section discusses, a comprehensive view of continual learning must move beyond catastrophic forgetting prevention to consider how knowledge accumulated across these diverse settings can be actively transferred—both forward to benefit future tasks and backward to refine past ones—transforming continual learning from a defensive exercise into a genuinely cumulative and intelligent learning process.

### 1.5 Knowledge Transfer in Continual Learning

While catastrophic forgetting prevention has historically dominated the continual learning discourse, a broader and more ambitious objective concerns the active transfer of knowledge across tasks. The problem settings described in the previous section—ranging from task-incremental to general incremental learning—each implicitly assume that a well-functioning continual learner should not merely avoid forgetting old information but should actively leverage previously acquired knowledge to improve learning efficiency and generalization on both future and past tasks. This perspective frames continual learning as a cumulative knowledge-building process rather than a defensive preservation exercise, and it becomes especially relevant as we consider the practical deployment scenarios discussed in the following section, where real-world systems must remain adaptive and continuously improve over time.

**Forward Transfer** refers to the positive influence that knowledge learned from prior tasks exerts on the learning of subsequent, unseen tasks. A continual learner exhibiting strong forward transfer can learn new tasks more quickly and accurately by drawing upon representations, features, or inductive biases acquired during earlier experiences. Empirical evidence suggests that less forgetful representations tend to produce better forward transfer, indicating a strong correlation between retaining past information and improving learning efficiency on new tasks [58]. This insight challenges the notion that forgetting prevention and forward transfer are orthogonal objectives. Furthermore, it has been observed in sequential visual classification settings that forward facilitation—the accelerated learning of task $n+1$ having learned $n$ previous tasks—tends to grow with the number of previously mastered tasks, suggesting that broader domain exposure naturally cultivates transferable representations [59].

**Backward Transfer** refers to the influence that learning a new task has on the performance of previously learned tasks. While catastrophic forgetting represents negative backward transfer—where new learning degrades old performance—positive backward transfer occurs when acquiring new knowledge actually improves performance on prior tasks. Despite its potential significance, backward transfer has received considerably less attention in the continual learning literature [60]. Theoretical analyses have derived error bounds for both forward and backward transfer mechanisms, demonstrating that for a continual learner observing related tasks, both transfer directions can contribute to increasingly strong performance as more tasks are encountered [60]. Importantly, positive backward transfer has been demonstrated empirically as an achievable goal: methods such as CUBER achieve positive backward transfer on standard benchmarks without data replay by selectively modifying learned models based on task correlation analysis [61].

The pursuit of both transfer directions is not merely a desirable extension but a fundamental requirement for intelligent lifelong learning systems. Most existing continual learning methods focus exclusively on minimizing forgetting, often through strict constraints on parameter modification, which paradoxically limits the opportunity for beneficial backward transfer [61]. A more principled approach acknowledges that the learning of new tasks can and should update representations in ways that benefit past tasks when task correlations are positive. Methods such as SMART explicitly incorporate backward positive transfer constraints with theoretical guarantees on error bounds [62], and representation ensembling approaches have demonstrated both forward and backward transfer across diverse benchmarks by combining independently learned representations [63].

The distinction between similar and dissimilar tasks further complicates the transfer landscape. When tasks share structural features, knowledge transfer is more naturally facilitated; however, when tasks are dissimilar, aggressive knowledge transfer may cause negative forward transfer, whereby irrelevant old knowledge interferes with new task learning [64]. This challenge is directly relevant to the cross-domain incremental and general incremental learning scenarios described earlier, where tasks may originate from substantially different domains and thus offer highly variable degrees of structural overlap. Some methods have addressed this by proposing active forgetting mechanisms that selectively discard old knowledge conflicting with new learning, a principle inspired by biological neural systems [65; 31]. Theoretical studies using teacher-student frameworks have illuminated how task similarity at both feature and readout levels jointly determines the magnitude and direction of transfer and forgetting, revealing complex non-linear interactions between these factors [66].

Taken together, forward and backward transfer constitute the positive-outcome dimensions of continual learning that distinguish it from mere catastrophic forgetting avoidance. Building systems capable of accumulating knowledge synergistically across a task lifetime—analogous to how human learners build increasingly rich conceptual frameworks—requires that continual learning research move beyond forgetting metrics toward comprehensive evaluation of knowledge accumulation, transfer efficiency, and the conditions under which both forward and backward transfer can be reliably achieved [67; 46]. As the following section illustrates, these properties are not merely theoretically desirable but operationally necessary across the wide range of real-world application domains where continual learning systems are increasingly being deployed.

### 1.6 Motivation and Real-World Relevance

The practical motivation for continual learning emerges from a fundamental mismatch between how machine learning models are traditionally trained and how intelligent systems must operate in the real world. Conventional batch learning assumes that all training data is available simultaneously and follows an independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) assumption. However, real-world systems routinely encounter data that arrives sequentially, evolves over time, and exhibits non-stationary distributions. In such settings, retraining models from scratch each time new data arrives is often computationally prohibitive, logistically impractical, or constrained by data privacy regulations. These practical pressures directly motivate the knowledge transfer objectives discussed in the previous section—a continual learner that can accumulate and transfer knowledge across tasks is not merely theoretically appealing but operationally necessary across a wide range of deployment domains. At the same time, understanding these real-world demands helps contextualize the biological learning mechanisms explored in the following section, as nature has already solved many of the same challenges that applied continual learning seeks to address.

**Computational and Storage Constraints.** The cost of retraining large models from scratch on ever-growing datasets can be enormous. Research has demonstrated that under realistic computational budgets, traditional continual learning approaches consistently fail to outperform even simple baselines [68]. Similarly, real-time evaluation studies reveal that many existing continual learning methods are too computationally expensive for practical deployment, as they assume unlimited training time and fail when the data stream does not wait for model updates [69]. This underscores the pressing need for methods that are not only accurate but also computationally efficient and deployable under constrained resources.

**Data Privacy Constraints.** In many deployment scenarios, storing and revisiting past data is not merely inconvenient but legally or ethically impermissible. Healthcare applications, for instance, involve highly sensitive patient data that cannot be aggregated or retained across institutions without stringent privacy safeguards [70; 8]. Similarly, on-device personalization scenarios require models to learn from user data locally without transmitting sensitive information to central servers [71; 72]. Continual learning provides a principled framework for adapting to new data distributions while minimizing the need to store or reuse raw historical data.

**Computer Vision.** The computer vision domain offers perhaps the richest landscape of continual learning applications. Models deployed for image classification, object detection, and semantic segmentation in the real world must cope with changing visual environments, new object categories, and evolving domain characteristics [5]. Autonomous driving systems, for example, encounter new road conditions, weather patterns, and geographic contexts over time [73]. Benchmarks such as CORe50 capture the dynamic, object-centric nature of visual recognition tasks that benefit from continual adaptation [74]. Video understanding introduces additional temporal complexity, where models must adapt to new action categories or visual domains while retaining knowledge of previously learned ones [75]. These diverse vision challenges collectively illustrate that static, pre-trained models are insufficient for long-term deployment in evolving visual environments.

**Natural Language Processing.** Language models face an equally dynamic operational landscape. Large pre-trained language models must be continually updated to incorporate new domain knowledge, emerging vocabulary, and evolving world facts without losing their previously acquired linguistic competence. The deployment of vision-language models like CLIP in continual settings further highlights how zero-shot transfer capabilities can degrade when models are fine-tuned on sequential downstream tasks, necessitating specialized strategies to preserve general knowledge [76; 77]. Dialogue systems and task-oriented conversational agents similarly benefit from continual learning as they must accommodate new intents, domains, and user preferences over extended deployments.

**Robotics and Autonomous Systems.** Perhaps nowhere is the motivation for continual learning more compelling than in robotics. Autonomous agents operating in open-ended environments must continuously refine their perceptual and motor skills as they encounter new objects, tasks, and interaction contexts [4; 10]. Long-term deployment of robots in homes, factories, or public spaces requires models to learn from streams of sensory data without forgetting prior skills [78]. Human-robot interaction studies reinforce this point, demonstrating that users' trust and perceived competence of a robot significantly diminish when the robot forgets previously learned information across multiple interaction sessions [79]. Robotic benchmarks such as Continual World formalize this challenge by evaluating forward and backward transfer alongside catastrophic forgetting in realistic manipulation task sequences [9].

**Healthcare and Clinical Settings.** Clinical environments are characterized by continuously evolving patient populations, treatment protocols, medical devices, and disease patterns. Machine learning models trained on historical clinical data inevitably encounter covariate shift and distribution drift when deployed over extended periods. Studies on longitudinal ICU records demonstrate that while some continual learning methods can mitigate short-term forgetting, domain shift remains a profound challenge over long task sequences, with replay-based approaches showing the most stable long-term performance [8]. Investigations into class-incremental medical imaging reveal that continual learning enables a single model to sequentially learn tasks from diverse clinical specialties while maintaining performance comparable to naive retraining approaches, advancing the vision of general-purpose medical AI that can be shared across institutions [70].

**Embedded and Edge Systems.** The proliferation of intelligent edge devices—smartphones, wearables, IoT sensors, and embedded platforms—creates a distinct class of continual learning deployment scenarios where memory, computation, and energy budgets are severely constrained [80]. Applications including human activity recognition from body-worn sensors [81] and on-device personalization [71] require models that can learn incrementally from streaming data directly on resource-constrained hardware. Research has shown that it is both feasible and practical to run continual learning on-device within acceptable latency and energy budgets, enabling complete data privacy while maintaining model adaptability [80; 72].

**Smart Cities and Autonomous Vehicles.** Urban intelligence applications spanning traffic management, environmental monitoring, and autonomous driving further illustrate the breadth of real-world continual learning needs. Self-driving vehicles, unmanned aerial vehicles, and urban robots must continuously adapt their perception and decision-making capabilities as they encounter novel environments and evolving traffic conditions [73]. The non-stationary nature of urban data streams—ranging from seasonal environmental changes to the emergence of new vehicle types—makes static model deployment fundamentally inadequate and continual learning an operational necessity.

Taken together, these application domains establish a compelling case for continual learning as an essential paradigm for intelligent systems that must remain both adaptive and knowledgeable throughout their operational lifetimes. The convergence of computational constraints, privacy requirements, and the inherently sequential nature of real-world data makes the development of robust continual learning methods not merely an academic exercise but a practical imperative. Crucially, the demands imposed by these real-world scenarios—adapting rapidly to new tasks, transferring knowledge efficiently, and operating under tight resource budgets—closely mirror the challenges that biological systems have addressed through millions of years of evolution. As the following section explores, the neuroscientific mechanisms underlying lifelong learning in biological brains offer a rich source of inspiration for meeting precisely these practical demands.

### 1.7 Biological and Neuroscientific Inspirations

The human brain serves as a profound source of inspiration for continual learning research. Unlike artificial neural networks that suffer from catastrophic forgetting, biological systems manage to acquire, consolidate, and retrieve knowledge across an entire lifetime without significant interference between old and new memories. Understanding the neuroscientific mechanisms underlying this capability has directly shaped the design of many computational continual learning algorithms — and appreciating these biological foundations helps contextualize the practical motivations and algorithmic design choices discussed throughout this survey.

**Complementary Learning Systems (CLS) Theory**

One of the most influential neuroscientific frameworks in continual learning is the Complementary Learning Systems (CLS) theory, which posits that efficient lifelong learning in mammals emerges from the interplay between two distinct but complementary memory systems: the hippocampus, responsible for rapid instance-based encoding of specific experiences, and the neocortex, which gradually consolidates structured, generalizable knowledge [82; 83]. The hippocampus rapidly acquires new episodic memories, while the neocortex slowly integrates these experiences into a broader semantic knowledge base, preventing interference between old and new representations. This dual-system perspective has motivated many dual-memory architectures in machine learning [84; 85], where a fast-learning episodic memory interacts with a slow-learning semantic memory to achieve effective knowledge accumulation and retention.

**Synaptic Consolidation**

Another cornerstone concept from neuroscience is synaptic consolidation, the biological process by which synaptic connections are selectively strengthened or stabilized to preserve important memories. In biological neural circuits, synapses that are repeatedly activated during learning become progressively more resistant to modification, effectively protecting acquired knowledge from being overwritten by new experiences [1]. This principle has directly inspired regularization-based continual learning methods, which estimate the importance of neural network parameters and penalize changes to those deemed critical for previously learned tasks [86]. The biological notion of metaplasticity — where the history of synaptic activity influences future plasticity — has similarly motivated algorithms that track parameter usage statistics over time to guide selective weight freezing [41; 87].

**Memory Replay During Sleep**

Sleep plays a critical role in memory consolidation in biological systems. During sleep, the brain is thought to replay and consolidate the experiences acquired during wakefulness, reinforcing important memories and integrating new information with existing knowledge structures [88]. Specifically, different sleep stages serve distinct functions: non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep is associated with veridical replaying of recent experiences to strengthen memory traces, while rapid eye movement (REM) sleep supports a more generative, associative form of memory consolidation that facilitates creative and abstract generalization [89]. Furthermore, synaptic downscaling during sleep is hypothesized to tune signal-to-noise ratios in neural circuits by weakening less important connections, thereby preventing memory saturation and improving overall network efficiency [88]. These sleep mechanisms have inspired computational analogues including experience replay [6], generative replay [84], and weight consolidation strategies [89; 90] that simulate offline memory processing to improve retention of past tasks.

**Structural Plasticity and Neurogenesis**

Beyond synaptic changes, biological brains also undergo structural plasticity — the growth, pruning, and rewiring of synaptic connections and even the formation of new neurons (neurogenesis) in response to learning demands [1]. This structural adaptation allows the brain to dynamically allocate capacity to new learning while protecting existing memory traces. In artificial systems, structural plasticity has motivated dynamic architecture methods that expand network capacity when encountering new tasks [41], as well as pruning-based approaches that selectively remove redundant parameters to free up capacity for future learning [90].

**Neuromodulatory Processes**

Neuromodulation refers to the process by which neurotransmitters such as dopamine, acetylcholine, and norepinephrine modulate the activity and plasticity of neural circuits in a context-dependent manner [31]. These neuromodulatory signals effectively gate learning by selectively enabling or suppressing plasticity across different brain regions depending on the behavioral context and task demands. This biological mechanism has inspired neuromodulation-based continual learning architectures [91; 44] that learn to dynamically control the plasticity of network weights based on context signals, as well as meta-learning approaches that meta-learn activation-gating functions to enable selective parameter updates [92]. Adaptive Resonance Theory, which integrates uncertainty-based neuromodulatory mechanisms, represents another biologically grounded framework that supports stable yet plastic learning without catastrophic forgetting [93].

**Sparse and Non-Overlapping Neural Representations**

Neuroscientific studies suggest that the brain represents distinct memories using sparse, non-overlapping patterns of neural activity, particularly in the hippocampus [43]. This pattern separation mechanism reduces interference between memories stored in overlapping neural circuits. Computational models inspired by this principle enforce activation sparsity in neural networks to minimize overlap between representations of different tasks, thereby reducing catastrophic forgetting [85; 94]. Saliency-guided and associative memory retrieval mechanisms that emulate how the brain selectively encodes and reconstructs memories from partial cues further leverage these insights [95].

**Schematic Memory and Context-Dependent Gating**

Beyond episodic and semantic memory, the brain also organizes knowledge into schemas — structured frameworks that guide the assimilation of new information into existing knowledge structures. Schematic memory allows for efficient encoding and forgetting of redundant details while retaining abstract, generalizable patterns [62]. Context-dependent gating, where neural circuits selectively activate different subsets of neurons depending on the current task context, provides another powerful mechanism for reducing interference between tasks [32; 96]. These biological principles have been translated into computational frameworks that combine selective neuron activation with context-dependent modulation to achieve effective task separation and knowledge retention.

**Integrating Multiple Biological Mechanisms**

Perhaps most importantly, biological continual learning does not rely on a single mechanism but emerges from the coordinated operation of multiple neurophysiological processes simultaneously. Inspired by this multi-mechanism perspective, recent computational frameworks have proposed paradigms that synergistically combine synaptic consolidation, experience replay, structural plasticity, neuromodulation, and context-dependent gating [41; 86; 31]. Such integrated approaches have consistently demonstrated superior performance over methods that rely on a single strategy in isolation, underscoring the value of deeply engaging with neuroscientific principles in the design of continual learning algorithms.

Overall, neuroscience has served as a rich and productive source of inspiration for continual learning research. From CLS theory and synaptic consolidation to sleep-inspired replay, structural plasticity, neuromodulation, and sparse coding, biological mechanisms offer a diverse toolkit of principles that have guided the development of increasingly effective and robust artificial continual learning systems [1; 10; 46]. These biological insights not only help explain why existing continual learning approaches work when they do, but also point toward principled directions for future research. As our understanding of biological learning deepens, the prospect of translating further neuroscientific insights into practical algorithmic advances remains one of the most promising frontiers in the field — a theme that will recur throughout the algorithmic discussions in subsequent sections.

### 1.8 Relationships to Adjacent Machine Learning Paradigms

Continual learning does not exist in isolation within the machine learning landscape; rather, it intersects and contrasts with several adjacent paradigms in important ways. Understanding these relationships is essential for appreciating both the unique challenges that continual learning addresses and the opportunities that arise from drawing on insights developed in neighboring fields. Just as the neuroscientific foundations discussed in the previous section illuminate the biological principles motivating continual learning, situating the field within the broader machine learning ecosystem clarifies its computational identity and helps delineate the solution space that subsequent sections will explore in depth.

**Transfer Learning.** Transfer learning focuses on leveraging knowledge acquired from a source domain or task to improve learning on a target domain or task, typically in a one-shot or few-shot transfer scenario. Continual learning shares the goal of knowledge reuse across tasks but differs fundamentally in that it requires the model to perform well on *all* tasks encountered throughout the learning sequence, not merely the most recent one. While transfer learning is generally concerned with a single directional knowledge transfer from source to target, continual learning demands bidirectional knowledge flow: forward transfer (where past knowledge accelerates new learning) and backward transfer (where new knowledge can even improve performance on prior tasks). Thus, transfer learning can be viewed as a special, limited case of the broader continual learning problem.

**Multi-Task Learning.** Multi-task learning (MTL) trains a model simultaneously on all tasks, with access to data from every task at once. This joint training approach naturally avoids catastrophic forgetting since all tasks are optimized concurrently. In contrast, continual learning imposes the constraint that tasks arrive sequentially and that data from previous tasks is typically unavailable or severely restricted during later training phases. Interestingly, MTL is frequently employed as a performance upper bound in continual learning evaluations, and while this assumption is empirically reasonable in many settings, it is not universally valid [97]. In some continual learning scenarios — particularly in self-supervised settings — continual learners can surpass their MTL counterparts, especially when certain tasks act adversarially within a joint training framework [97]. This nuanced relationship highlights that the sequential learning constraint is not simply a handicap but can occasionally yield advantages.

**Meta-Learning.** Meta-learning, or "learning to learn," aims to equip models with the capacity to rapidly adapt to new tasks by learning across a distribution of tasks at a meta-level. Its connection to continual learning is deep and bidirectional. On one hand, meta-learning techniques can be leveraged to endow continual learners with better initialization or optimization strategies that reduce interference between tasks [98; 99; 100]. On the other hand, the continual learning problem can itself be recast as a sequence modeling problem amenable to meta-training across multiple continual learning episodes [101]. The distinction lies in scope and assumption: meta-learning typically assumes access to a distribution of tasks during meta-training, whereas continual learning assumes tasks arrive one at a time with no such global distribution available upfront. The rich synergies between these fields have spurred the development of meta-continual learning frameworks that combine the strengths of both paradigms [102].

**Online Learning.** Online learning is concerned with models that update incrementally from a stream of data points, optimizing performance on each incoming example before moving to the next. Continual learning shares this sequential, non-i.i.d. data stream assumption but additionally emphasizes knowledge retention across distinct tasks or distributions — a concern that classical online learning theory does not directly address. Online continual learning represents a synthesis of both paradigms, requiring models to learn from a single-pass non-stationary data stream without explicit task boundaries while simultaneously retaining knowledge of previously observed distributions [7; 103]. The critical difference is that online learning typically focuses on regret minimization over a single evolving distribution, whereas continual learning explicitly measures stability (forgetting prevention) alongside plasticity (new task learning) — precisely the stability-plasticity tension that the neuroscientific and methodological discussions throughout this survey seek to address.

**Curriculum Learning.** Curriculum learning organizes the presentation of training examples or tasks in a structured order, typically progressing from simpler to more complex instances to improve convergence and generalization. While continual learning does not prescribe a particular task ordering, task order has been shown to influence the degree of catastrophic forgetting and forward transfer substantially. Research has explored whether continual learning models can exploit structured curricula across tasks, finding that most current strategies show limited ability to leverage task ordering [57]. This highlights curriculum learning as a promising complement to continual learning, as systematically designed task sequences could mitigate interference and facilitate more efficient knowledge accumulation.

**Federated Learning.** Federated learning addresses the challenge of training models across distributed clients who cannot share raw data due to privacy constraints, instead communicating model updates or gradients. While federated learning primarily targets collaborative training under data heterogeneity and privacy, it does not inherently address the temporal non-stationarity of data within each client. Federated continual learning emerges at the intersection of these paradigms, where each client independently experiences a sequential stream of tasks and must learn continually while collaborating without raw data sharing [104; 105; 106]. This setting introduces additional challenges including heterogeneous task distributions across clients, communication cost constraints, and the need to balance local continual adaptation with global knowledge consolidation.

In summary, continual learning occupies a unique niche in the machine learning landscape that is defined by the combination of sequential task exposure, the unavailability of past data, and the simultaneous requirement for stability and plasticity. While it shares motivations and techniques with transfer learning, multi-task learning, meta-learning, online learning, curriculum learning, and federated learning, none of these adjacent paradigms alone captures the full scope of the continual learning challenge. Recognizing these overlaps and distinctions is crucial for researchers seeking to import insights across fields and for practitioners designing systems that must adapt intelligently to non-stationary real-world environments [3; 4]. With this broader landscape in view, we now turn to a high-level orientation of the major methodological families — from regularization and replay to architecture-based and prompt-based approaches — that constitute the core toolkit of the field and will be systematically examined in the sections that follow.

### 1.9 Overview of Major Methodological Families

The continual learning literature has converged around several principal methodological families, each addressing catastrophic forgetting through a distinct lens. Having situated continual learning within the broader machine learning landscape — distinguishing it from transfer learning, multi-task learning, meta-learning, and related paradigms — we now turn to a high-level orientation of the major methodological families that constitute the field's core toolkit. Understanding these families before examining their theoretical underpinnings and empirical trade-offs in detail is essential, as they collectively define the solution space that subsequent sections of this survey will systematically explore.

**Regularization-Based Methods.** These approaches protect previously acquired knowledge by imposing penalty terms on parameter changes deemed important for past tasks. Rather than storing raw data, they estimate parameter importance and constrain optimization to avoid overwriting critical weights. Representative techniques include Elastic Weight Consolidation, Synaptic Intelligence, Memory Aware Synapses, and variational Bayesian extensions [107; 108; 109]. While computationally lightweight, regularization methods can struggle in class-incremental scenarios with long task sequences, where accumulated constraints become increasingly restrictive [110; 111].

**Replay-Based Methods.** Replay methods maintain a memory buffer of representative past samples and interleave them with new training data to rehearse prior knowledge. Strategies for populating and sampling this buffer range from reservoir sampling to sophisticated gradient coreset selection [112], maximally interfered retrieval [113], and loss-aware reservoir sampling [109]. Extensions such as compressed activation replay further reduce memory overhead while maintaining regularization quality [114]. Replay-based methods consistently rank among the most effective approaches across benchmarks, though they incur storage costs and raise data-privacy concerns [115; 116].

**Generative Replay Methods.** Instead of storing raw examples, generative replay trains a generative model — typically a GAN or VAE — alongside the task model and synthesizes pseudo-data representing past distributions for rehearsal [6]. Variants include generative feature replay and negative replay, where generated samples serve as antagonists to sharpen discrimination of new classes [117]. While appealing for privacy-sensitive settings — directly addressing one of the key limitations of standard replay methods noted above — generative replay faces scalability challenges when tasks involve high-dimensional data or long sequences [118].

**Architecture-Based and Parameter Isolation Methods.** These methods allocate distinct or partially overlapping network capacity to different tasks, directly preventing interference by construction. Approaches include fixed-architecture sparse subnetwork selection, dynamic network expansion, pruning and quantization for capacity management, and Bayesian structure adaptation [119; 120; 121]. By assigning task-specific parameters, these methods can achieve zero forgetting at the cost of growing model size or reduced parameter sharing [122]. This represents a fundamentally different trade-off point in the stability-plasticity space compared to regularization and replay methods.

**Optimization-Based Methods.** Optimization-based approaches modify the learning objective or the gradient update procedure to reduce task interference, operating at the level of the learning dynamics themselves rather than through explicit memory or architectural constraints. Representative techniques include gradient projection into orthogonal subspaces, gradient episodic memory, and bilevel meta-learning formulations that learn to learn without forgetting [123]. These methods can complement other families and have been shown to support both forward and backward knowledge transfer [124], echoing the knowledge transfer objectives discussed earlier in this section.

**Prompt-Based and Pre-Trained Model Methods.** With the rise of large foundation models, a growing family of methods leverages frozen or partially frozen pre-trained representations for continual learning, inserting small learnable prompts or adapters to steer inference for each task [125; 126]. Because the backbone parameters are largely preserved, catastrophic forgetting is naturally mitigated, and competitive performance can be achieved with minimal memory overhead. This family has grown rapidly in prominence as pre-trained models have become central to modern AI systems.

**Hybrid and Bio-Inspired Methods.** Many state-of-the-art systems combine multiple strategies to exploit their complementary strengths — for example, pairing synaptic regularization with experience replay, or coupling dynamic architecture expansion with prototype-based memory [127; 44]. Bio-inspired mechanisms such as neuromodulation, local learning rules, and complementary learning systems theory also motivate hybrid designs that more closely mirror the flexibility of biological memory [44], connecting back to the neuroscientific motivations outlined earlier in this survey.

Together, these families span a rich design space characterized by trade-offs among memory overhead, computational cost, scalability, and forgetting mitigation. Their diversity reflects the multifaceted nature of the continual learning challenge itself: no single approach universally dominates, and the choice of method depends critically on the scenario type, resource constraints, and application requirements reviewed throughout this section. The following sections provide a detailed taxonomy and comparative analysis of each family, equipping the reader with a structured understanding of the continual learning method landscape [5; 122].

### 1.10 Survey Scope, Structure, and Roadmap

This survey provides a comprehensive and systematic treatment of continual learning, encompassing its theoretical foundations, methodological families, evaluation frameworks, domain-specific applications, and emerging research directions. The scope is deliberately broad, reflecting the multidisciplinary nature of the field and its growing importance across artificial intelligence research and deployment contexts.

**Scope of Coverage.** The survey covers the full spectrum of continual learning research, organized along several principal dimensions. From a methodological standpoint, it addresses all major families of approaches: regularization-based methods, replay-based and generative replay strategies, architecture-based and parameter isolation techniques, optimization-based methods, prompt-based and pre-trained model approaches, and hybrid bio-inspired paradigms — each of which has been previewed in the preceding discussion of methodological families. On the theoretical side, the survey encompasses computational complexity analyses, information-theoretic perspectives, generalization bounds, and formal studies of knowledge transfer and forgetting. The evaluation dimension covers metrics, benchmarks, protocols, and software frameworks that enable rigorous and reproducible assessment of continual learning algorithms. Application domains surveyed include computer vision, natural language processing, multimodal systems, reinforcement learning, robotics, healthcare, speech recognition, and smart city systems. Finally, the survey addresses advanced and emerging paradigms including unsupervised, semi-supervised, online, federated, and cross-domain continual learning, as well as the integration of continual learning with meta-learning, neural architecture search, and foundation models.

**Organizational Logic and Roadmap.** The survey is structured to guide readers from foundational concepts through increasingly specialized topics in a logically progressive manner.
