The present section, Section 1, establishes the conceptual foundations: defining continual learning and its core terminology, characterizing the central challenge of catastrophic forgetting and the stability-plasticity dilemma, presenting the taxonomy of learning scenarios, discussing knowledge transfer objectives, motivating the practical relevance of the field, reviewing biological and neuroscientific inspirations, situating continual learning within the broader machine learning landscape, and previewing the major methodological families that collectively define the solution space explored throughout this survey.

Section 2 presents a comprehensive taxonomy of continual learning methods, systematically covering each major family from regularization-based approaches through prompt-based methods and concluding with a comparative analysis of their strengths, limitations, and trade-offs to assist practitioners in selecting appropriate methods for specific deployment contexts.

Section 3 addresses theoretical perspectives and evaluation frameworks, covering computational complexity results, information-theoretic and neural tangent kernel analyses, generalization bounds, evaluation metrics, standard and emerging benchmarks, scenario definitions as used in evaluation protocols, and software frameworks that standardize experimentation and promote reproducibility.

Sections 4, 5, and 6 shift focus to domain-specific applications. Section 4 surveys continual learning in computer vision, covering image classification, object detection, semantic segmentation, video understanding, 3D point cloud processing, and multiple object tracking. Section 5 examines continual learning in natural language processing and multimodal systems, addressing text classification, continual pre-training, instruction tuning, knowledge updating in large language models, question answering, dialogue systems, and parameter-efficient adaptation strategies. Section 6 explores continual learning in reinforcement learning, robotics, and specialized domains including healthcare, speech recognition, human activity recognition, and smart city applications, with dedicated attention to privacy, safety, and ethical considerations.

Section 7 surveys advanced topics and emerging paradigms, covering unsupervised and self-supervised continual learning, semi-supervised settings, online continual learning, federated continual learning, distribution shift and out-of-distribution challenges, cross-domain continual learning, integration with meta-learning and neural architecture search, and biologically inspired neuromodulatory approaches, reflecting the rapid expansion of the field into previously underexplored territories.

Section 8 concludes the survey by synthesizing open challenges and future research directions, addressing scalability to long task sequences, standardized evaluation, model calibration and uncertainty, on-device deployment, personalization, the gap between artificial and biological systems, ethical considerations, and the integration of continual learning with foundation models and emerging AI paradigms.

This organizational structure reflects a deliberate progression from fundamentals to methodology, from theory to practice, and from established methods to open frontiers, providing a coherent and accessible reference for researchers and practitioners at all levels of familiarity with continual learning. The breadth of coverage is motivated by the recognition that advancing the field requires not only algorithmic innovation but also rigorous theoretical understanding, realistic evaluation infrastructure, and sensitivity to the practical demands of real-world deployment across diverse application domains. Together, the sections that follow build upon the conceptual and methodological foundations laid in this opening section, offering a structured and thorough examination of the continual learning landscape as it stands today.

## 2 Taxonomy of Continual Learning Methods

### 2.1 Overview and Taxonomy of Continual Learning Methods

The field of continual learning has given rise to a diverse ecosystem of algorithmic approaches, each motivated by distinct perspectives on how to balance the stability-plasticity dilemma—the fundamental tension between retaining previously acquired knowledge and remaining sufficiently flexible to accommodate new information [1; 2]. At a high level, these approaches can be organized into several principal families: regularization-based methods, replay-based methods, generative replay methods, architecture-based and parameter isolation methods, optimization-based methods, prompt-based and pre-trained model methods, and hybrid or bio-inspired methods [5; 6].

**Regularization-based methods** address catastrophic forgetting by introducing auxiliary loss terms that penalize changes to parameters deemed important for previously learned tasks. The underlying principle is to selectively protect critical weights while allowing less important ones to adapt freely, drawing theoretical motivation from Bayesian posterior approximation and second-order Taylor expansion of the loss landscape [128; 119]. As the following subsection details, this family encompasses a rich set of techniques ranging from Fisher information-based penalties to variational Bayesian formulations and explainability-guided parameter protection.

**Replay-based methods** maintain a memory buffer of past experiences—either raw data samples or compressed representations—and intermittently revisit these stored examples during training on new tasks [129; 112; 130]. The core intuition is that interleaving old and new training signals prevents the gradient updates for new tasks from catastrophically overwriting representations useful for previous ones. Buffer management strategies such as reservoir sampling, coreset selection, and prototype-guided memory replay represent important design dimensions within this family [131].

**Generative replay methods** replace stored data with synthetic samples produced by generative models, offering a way to rehearse past distributions without retaining raw data [132; 133]. This approach is particularly relevant in privacy-sensitive scenarios where direct data storage is prohibited.

**Architecture-based and parameter isolation methods** allocate dedicated or partially overlapping network capacity to different tasks, ensuring that updates for new tasks do not overwrite the representations essential for previous ones [119; 42]. These methods range from fixed subnetwork masking to dynamic network expansion and modular architectures that grow with the task sequence.

**Optimization-based methods** tackle forgetting by modifying the learning objective or gradient update rules so that parameter updates for new tasks remain compatible with the constraints imposed by prior tasks [134]. Gradient projection techniques, for instance, constrain weight updates to lie in subspaces orthogonal to those critical for old tasks, thereby reducing task interference.

**Prompt-based and pre-trained model methods** represent an emerging paradigm that leverages the rich representations encoded in large foundation models, applying lightweight prompt or adapter modules to steer the frozen or minimally fine-tuned backbone toward new tasks [135; 136; 126]. Because the backbone parameters are largely preserved, catastrophic forgetting is substantially reduced by design.

**Hybrid and bio-inspired methods** combine principles from multiple families, often drawing explicit inspiration from neuroscientific theories such as complementary learning systems, synaptic consolidation, and neuromodulatory gating [1; 92; 11]. These approaches typically yield more robust continual learners by exploiting synergies between different forgetting-mitigation mechanisms.

Each family embodies a different stance on the stability-plasticity trade-off. Regularization methods lean toward stability by constraining parameter drift; replay and generative methods restore plasticity-stability balance through data-level diversity; architecture methods achieve stability through isolation at the cost of model scalability; optimization and prompt methods seek efficient trade-offs via targeted gradient control and representation reuse. Understanding these contrasting philosophies is essential for selecting appropriate algorithms in practice [3; 4]. The following subsections provide detailed treatments of each methodological family, beginning with regularization-based methods and progressing through replay, architecture, optimization, and prompt-based approaches, examining representative algorithms, theoretical foundations, and empirical performance across standard benchmarks.

### 2.2 Regularization-Based Methods

## 2.2 Regularization-Based Methods

Regularization-based continual learning methods address catastrophic forgetting by introducing additional terms into the training objective that penalize changes to parameters deemed important for previously learned tasks. As introduced in the preceding overview, these approaches encode past knowledge directly within the network's parameter constraints rather than storing raw data from prior tasks, making them memory-efficient and privacy-preserving alternatives to the replay-based strategies discussed in the following section.

## Core Principle: Weight Importance and Parameter Protection

The foundational intuition shared by most regularization-based methods is straightforward: not all parameters contribute equally to the performance of a previously learned task. By estimating the importance of each parameter and applying stronger resistance to changes in highly important parameters, the network can accommodate new task learning while minimizing interference with prior knowledge. This principle connects naturally to the stability-plasticity dilemma [14], as the regularization strength directly controls the trade-off between retaining old information (stability) and adapting to new data (plasticity)—the central tension identified in the previous section's overview of continual learning families.

## Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC)

Among the most influential regularization-based approaches is Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC), which draws on Bayesian principles to estimate parameter importance via the diagonal of the Fisher Information Matrix (FIM). After completing training on a task, EWC computes the Fisher information for each parameter as a proxy for its contribution to the posterior distribution over model parameters. When learning subsequent tasks, a quadratic penalty term proportional to the Fisher information is added to the loss function, constraining updates to parameters critical for previous tasks. This construction has a direct theoretical motivation through a second-order Taylor expansion of the log-posterior: the Fisher information approximates the curvature of the loss landscape around the previous task's optimal parameters, and the EWC penalty effectively keeps the new solution within a low-loss region for prior tasks [17; 137].

The EWC regularizer for task $t$ takes the form:
$$\mathcal{L}(\theta) = \mathcal{L}_t(\theta) + \sum_{i} \frac{\lambda}{2} F_i (\theta_i - \theta_{t-1,i}^*)^2$$
where $F_i$ is the Fisher information for parameter $i$, $\theta_{t-1,i}^*$ is the optimal parameter after the previous task, and $\lambda$ controls the regularization strength. Empirical investigations confirm that EWC effectively reduces forgetting across multiple benchmark datasets [19], though it can struggle when applied over long task sequences due to the accumulating overhead of storing per-task Fisher estimates.

## Synaptic Intelligence (SI)

Synaptic Intelligence extends the EWC framework by computing parameter importance in an online fashion during the training trajectory, rather than as a post-hoc computation at task boundaries. Drawing biological inspiration from the complex biochemical cascades at synaptic junctions that operate across multiple timescales [138], SI accumulates importance scores as a running sum of the product of the parameter gradient and the parameter update throughout training. This online computation allows SI to capture the cumulative contribution of each parameter to the reduction of the training loss, yielding importance estimates that reflect the entire learning trajectory rather than only the final checkpoint. The resulting method incurs lower computational overhead per task than EWC and is naturally suited to online learning scenarios.

## Memory Aware Synapses (MAS)

Memory Aware Synapses (MAS) offers an alternative importance estimation strategy that does not rely on labeled data or the Fisher information. Instead, MAS estimates parameter importance based on the sensitivity of the output function to parameter perturbations, computed using the squared $\ell_2$ norm of the output gradients. This formulation is particularly appealing in unsupervised or unlabeled settings, and the importance estimates can be updated incrementally as new data arrives, supporting continual adaptation. Comparisons across methods reveal that the choice of importance metric has a significant impact on the degree of forgetting and knowledge transfer, and that no single metric universally dominates [19].

## Incremental Moment Matching (IMM)

Incremental Moment Matching (IMM) adopts a distinct perspective by framing continual learning as the problem of combining posterior distributions from multiple tasks [139]. Rather than penalizing deviations from a single previous solution, IMM incrementally matches the moments (mean and optionally variance) of the posterior parameter distributions inferred for each task. IMM is complemented by transfer learning techniques including weight transfer, L2-norm constraints, and dropout-based regularization on old parameters, ensuring that the merged posterior remains in a region of parameter space that performs well on all observed tasks. Empirical evaluations on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and other benchmarks demonstrate competitive performance, particularly in task-incremental settings.

## Bayesian and Variational Frameworks

Regularization-based continual learning finds its most principled formulation within Bayesian frameworks, where the goal is to maintain a posterior distribution over model parameters that faithfully reflects all observed tasks. Variational Continual Learning (VCL) instantiates this idea by using variational inference to maintain an approximate posterior that is updated sequentially as each task arrives. The posterior from the previous task serves as the prior for the next, and the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the new approximate posterior and the old one acts as a natural regularization term preventing catastrophic forgetting. This framework generalizes EWC when the approximate posterior is constrained to a diagonal Gaussian, and provides a coherent probabilistic interpretation of the weight-importance family of methods. Online Variational Bayes extensions further support task-agnostic continual learning where task boundaries are not explicitly provided during training [140], representing an important step toward more realistic deployment scenarios.

## Theoretical Connections and Loss Landscape Perspectives

A unifying perspective across regularization-based methods is their relationship to the geometry of the loss landscape. Methods that penalize large parameter changes effectively constrain learning to regions near the previous task's optimum, and the nature of this constraint is determined by the importance measure used. Research on the geometry of local minima reveals that flat minima, characterized by broad loss basins, are more robust to distributional shift and sequential task learning, since perturbations to parameters within a flat basin leave the loss approximately unchanged [25; 141]. Approaches such as C&F (Create and Find Flatness) explicitly exploit this insight by shaping the loss landscape during training to create flat regions, then using the flatness degree as an importance signal for subsequent regularization [142]. The NTK overlap matrix provides a complementary theoretical lens, characterizing catastrophic forgetting in terms of task alignment in the neural tangent kernel feature space and explaining why projected gradient methods can mitigate forgetting by operating in subspaces of low task interference [17]. These theoretical insights connect naturally to the optimization-based methods discussed in later sections, which similarly exploit loss landscape geometry but through direct gradient manipulation rather than parameter penalties.

## Regularization through Explainability

An unconventional extension of the regularization paradigm leverages explainability tools to identify and protect task-relevant parameters. The training-by-explaining approach employs Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) to identify parameters most relevant to previously learned tasks, and constrains updates to these parameters during subsequent training [143]. This approach offers an interpretable view of parameter importance that is grounded in the network's input-output behavior rather than statistical curvature, and empirical results demonstrate competitive performance with lower memory requirements than some replay-based baselines.

## Structural Analysis of Where Forgetting Occurs

Empirical studies of representational change under sequential training provide important guidance for where regularization should be applied most strongly. Investigations using representational similarity analysis consistently find that deeper layers undergo the greatest representational change during sequential task learning and are the primary locus of catastrophic forgetting [18; 21]. The latter study decomposes forgetting into three components—readout misalignment, representational geometry distortion, and irrecoverable information loss—finding that the dominant contributor is the misalignment between shifting internal representations and fixed readout layers. These findings suggest that layer-specific regularization strategies, with stronger protection applied to deeper layers or to the readout alignment, may offer more efficient protection than uniform parameter regularization.

## Strengths and Limitations

Regularization-based methods offer several practical advantages: they require no additional memory for storing past data, they are architecturally agnostic and can in principle be applied to any gradient-trained model, and they introduce modest computational overhead at training time. For short task sequences with moderate task dissimilarity, methods like EWC and SI effectively reduce forgetting while maintaining plasticity [19].

However, regularization-based approaches face well-documented limitations. First, they generally require access to task identity information to compute importance estimates or to apply task-specific penalties, which is restrictive in task-agnostic settings. Second, as the number of tasks grows, the regularization constraints accumulate and the available parameter space for learning new tasks shrinks, leading to diminishing plasticity in very long task sequences [14]. Third, the diagonal Fisher approximation used in EWC ignores parameter correlations, and the quality of the importance estimate degrades with this approximation; full-matrix Fisher computation is intractable for modern large-scale networks. Fourth, the choice of regularization strength $\lambda$ introduces a critical hyperparameter whose optimal value is task-dependent and difficult to set without access to previous task data [25]. Finally, on its own, regularization without any replay mechanism cannot provide guarantees against forgetting when tasks are highly similar or when the model capacity is insufficient, as the optimal solutions for different tasks may fundamentally conflict in the parameter space [137].

Despite these limitations, regularization-based methods remain a foundational and widely-used family within continual learning, frequently serving as components within hybrid approaches that combine parameter protection with replay or architecture-based mechanisms. These shortcomings—particularly the challenges of long task sequences and the absence of direct data-level supervision—motivate the replay-based methods examined in the following section, which address forgetting by maintaining explicit memory of past experiences rather than encoding it solely through parameter constraints.

### 2.3 Replay-Based Methods

## 2.3 Replay-Based Methods

Replay-based continual learning methods address catastrophic forgetting by explicitly revisiting stored or synthesized representations of past experiences during training on new tasks. The core intuition mirrors biological memory consolidation mechanisms: by interleaving old experiences with new ones, the network is prevented from overwriting previously learned representations. In contrast to the regularization-based methods discussed in the previous section, which encode past knowledge implicitly through parameter constraints, replay-based methods maintain a more direct connection to historical data, making them among the most empirically effective approaches to continual learning. Their various instantiations differ primarily in how memories are selected, stored, managed, and utilized during the learning process—distinctions that have significant implications for both performance and practical feasibility.

### Memory Selection and Buffer Management

A fundamental design choice in replay-based methods concerns which past examples to retain in a finite memory buffer. **Reservoir sampling** provides a statistically principled approach, maintaining a uniform random sample of all observed data regardless of the order in which it arrives, thereby ensuring that each past example has an equal probability of being represented in the buffer. Beyond uniform sampling, more sophisticated strategies have been proposed to maximize the utility of limited buffer capacity. **Loss-aware sampling** prioritizes examples that are most likely to be forgotten, selecting samples for which the model exhibits high loss, thereby concentrating memory on the most vulnerable knowledge. **Gradient coreset selection** approaches, such as those underlying gradient episodic memory frameworks, choose a subset of stored examples whose gradient signals best summarize the full past data distribution, ensuring that replaying a small coreset approximates the effect of replaying the entire historical dataset. **Maximally Interfered Retrieval** targets examples that are most susceptible to interference from current gradient updates, retrieving samples for which the ongoing learning process is most likely to cause forgetting.

### Experience Replay Strategies and Meta-Learning Augmentation

Standard experience replay simply adds buffered examples to the current training batch, effectively treating the combined data as a joint training signal. More advanced variants augment this with meta-learning principles. **Meta-Experience Replay (MER)** frames the replay objective within a meta-learning optimization, seeking model parameters that simultaneously minimize loss on current data and on replayed past experiences, encouraging updates that do not interfere with prior knowledge [100]. This bilevel perspective encourages the model to find parameter regions that generalize well across all encountered tasks. Online continual learning frameworks have further developed meta-learning augmented replay, combining episodic memory with meta-update strategies and knowledge distillation to maintain both stability and plasticity under streaming data conditions [34].

### Memory Management: Compressed Activation Replay and Associative Storage

Storing raw input samples is memory-intensive, motivating techniques that store more compact representations of past experiences. **Compressed activation replay** retains intermediate feature activations rather than raw inputs, dramatically reducing storage requirements while preserving sufficient information for knowledge consolidation. **Associative memory storage** draws inspiration from Hopfield networks and sparse distributed memory, encoding past experiences in associative structures that allow efficient retrieval based on partial cues [42]. Such biologically inspired memory systems offer advantages in terms of generalization from stored patterns and resistance to interference between memories. These compressed and associative storage strategies can be seen as a conceptual bridge toward generative replay, where the memory of past experiences is encoded not in raw samples but in a learned model of the data distribution.

### Practical Considerations: Buffer Size and Data Augmentation

The effectiveness of replay-based methods is strongly influenced by practical hyperparameter choices. Buffer size represents a fundamental trade-off: larger buffers provide better coverage of past data distributions but impose greater memory overhead, while smaller buffers risk under-representing earlier tasks. Empirical studies have demonstrated that replay methods achieve strong performance even with relatively small buffers, particularly when combined with intelligent sampling strategies. Data augmentation applied to buffered examples can further enrich the effective diversity of replayed memories, improving generalization and reducing overfitting to the specific stored samples.

Replay-based methods have demonstrated strong empirical performance across a wide range of continual learning benchmarks [29]. Their primary limitation lies in the privacy and storage constraints associated with retaining raw data samples—a concern that is particularly acute in privacy-sensitive domains such as healthcare and federated learning, and that has directly motivated the development of generative replay approaches discussed in the following section. Additionally, as task sequences grow longer, the challenge of maintaining a representative buffer becomes more acute, necessitating increasingly sophisticated memory management policies. Despite these limitations, the combination of replay with other strategies such as regularization and architecture adaptation has yielded some of the most competitive continual learning systems to date [41], underscoring the central role of experience replay in the continual learning toolkit. The following section examines how generative models can serve as an alternative memory substrate, synthesizing pseudo-data from past tasks to replicate the benefits of replay without the need to store raw examples.

### 2.4 Generative Replay Methods

## 2.4 Generative Replay Methods

Generative replay methods represent a biologically inspired family of continual learning approaches that address catastrophic forgetting by training generative models to synthesize pseudo-data from past tasks, which is then interleaved with new task data during training. As discussed in the previous section, exemplar-based replay methods directly store raw data samples in a memory buffer to revisit past experiences; generative replay methods instead leverage the compressive power of generative models—most notably Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) and Variational Autoencoders (VAEs)—to implicitly encode the distributions of prior tasks, thereby reducing memory overhead while maintaining access to a synthetic approximation of historical data. This distinction makes generative replay particularly appealing in privacy-sensitive domains such as healthcare and federated learning, where retaining raw exemplars is infeasible. At the same time, the structural and algorithmic questions raised by generative replay—particularly around how model capacity is allocated and partitioned across tasks—anticipate the architecture-based methods discussed in the following section.

### Core Paradigms: Conditional and Marginal Replay

Generative replay methods can be broadly categorized into **marginal replay** and **conditional replay** paradigms. In marginal replay, the generative model learns to sample from the unconditional data distribution of past tasks, producing synthetic inputs that are subsequently classified by the task model. In conditional replay, the generative model is conditioned on task identity or class labels, allowing it to generate more task-specific and class-discriminative pseudo-samples. Conditional approaches generally yield higher fidelity synthetic data, as the generative model can focus its capacity on specific regions of the data manifold relevant to individual tasks or classes.

### GAN and VAE-Based Sequential Generation

The integration of GANs and VAEs into the continual learning framework introduces unique challenges, as both the generator and the task-solving model must be updated sequentially without forgetting. In the prototypical generative replay architecture, a generator is trained alongside a solver: when a new task arrives, the generator produces synthetic replays of past tasks by sampling from its learned distribution, and these synthetic samples are combined with new task data to jointly train both the solver and an updated generator. This cycle of generation and joint training allows knowledge of past tasks to propagate forward through the task sequence.

VAE-based approaches offer advantages in terms of training stability and the ability to perform structured interpolation in the latent space, while GANs can produce sharper and more realistic images. However, both paradigms suffer from well-known training instabilities—mode collapse in GANs and posterior collapse in VAEs—which are exacerbated in the continual learning setting because the generator itself is subject to forgetting. As the generator is updated on new task data, it may lose its ability to accurately reproduce past task distributions, leading to a compounding degradation of replay quality over long task sequences.

### Replay-Through-Feedback Architectures

A particularly notable architectural variant is the **replay-through-feedback** approach, in which a generative model is embedded within the primary network and replay occurs via feedback connections rather than through an external memory buffer. Inspired by the brain's ability to reconstruct past experiences through internal generative processes, these architectures allow top-down feedback signals to reconstruct lower-level representations of past inputs, which are then used to regularize learning on new tasks. This tightly coupled design eliminates the need for a separate generative model and can reduce the total parameter overhead, though it introduces complexity in the training dynamics. Notably, the integration of generative capacity directly into the network architecture foreshadows the structural partitioning strategies characteristic of the architecture-based methods examined in the following section.

### Generative Feature Replay

An important variant that has emerged to address the scalability limitations of pixel-level generative replay is **generative feature replay**, where the generative model operates in a latent feature space rather than in the raw input space. By generating compressed feature representations of past tasks instead of full high-dimensional inputs, feature replay methods substantially reduce the complexity of the generative modeling problem, making it more tractable for complex datasets. The generated feature vectors are fed to the upper layers of the task model, enabling knowledge distillation from past tasks without requiring the generator to produce photorealistic images. This approach has shown promise in scaling generative replay beyond simple datasets such as MNIST to more challenging benchmarks, and it shares a conceptual affinity with the compressed activation replay strategies introduced in the previous section.

### Challenges in Scaling to Complex Datasets and Long Task Sequences

Despite their conceptual elegance, generative replay methods face significant practical challenges when applied to complex, high-dimensional datasets and long task sequences. The quality of generated pseudo-data degrades progressively as the number of tasks grows, because the generator must capture an increasingly complex mixture of past distributions within a fixed model capacity. This phenomenon—sometimes termed **generative forgetting**—means that replay samples from early tasks become increasingly inaccurate over time, undermining the anti-forgetting objective. Furthermore, training high-quality generative models for datasets such as ImageNet or medical imaging collections requires substantial computational resources, making generative replay computationally expensive compared to exemplar-based replay methods.

The challenge of jointly preventing forgetting in both the generator and the solver—a dual forgetting problem—remains an open research issue. Some works have explored conditioning the generator on task embeddings or using separate generator heads for different tasks to mitigate inter-task interference in the generative model itself, a strategy that parallels the parameter isolation principles central to architecture-based methods. Others have combined generative replay with regularization or architectural constraints to stabilize the generator across the task sequence. The integration of modern high-fidelity generative models, such as diffusion models, into the generative replay framework is an active and promising direction that may partially address the quality and scalability concerns of GAN- and VAE-based approaches [144].

### Relationship to Other Replay Paradigms

Generative replay occupies a middle ground between purely memory-free regularization methods and exemplar-based replay approaches. Unlike regularization methods such as Elastic Weight Consolidation, generative replay does not require explicit importance weighting of parameters and can, in principle, provide more flexible protection against forgetting across diverse task types. Compared to exemplar-based replay, generative replay avoids storing raw data, which is particularly advantageous in privacy-sensitive applications such as healthcare and federated learning settings. However, the quality guarantee of generative replay is inherently probabilistic and depends on the fidelity of the generative model, whereas exemplar replay provides exact replicas of past data points. In this sense, generative replay trades the storage determinism of exemplar-based methods for a learned, compressive representation of the past—a trade-off that connects naturally to the capacity management concerns addressed by the architecture-based and parameter isolation methods discussed in the following section.

In summary, generative replay methods offer a compelling and biologically plausible approach to continual learning, but their practical deployment remains constrained by the difficulty of training stable, high-quality generative models in a sequential setting and by the compounding degradation of replay fidelity over long task sequences. Addressing these limitations through advances in generative modeling, latent space compression, and hybrid generative-exemplar strategies constitutes an important direction for future research in this area. The structural questions raised by generative replay—particularly how network capacity should be organized and allocated across tasks—lead naturally to the architecture-based and parameter isolation methods examined next.

### 2.5 Architecture-Based and Parameter Isolation Methods

## 2.5 Architecture-Based and Parameter Isolation Methods

Architecture-based and parameter isolation methods address catastrophic forgetting by allocating distinct or partially overlapping network capacity to different tasks, ensuring that learning new tasks does not overwrite previously acquired knowledge. In contrast to the generative replay approaches discussed in the previous section—which combat forgetting by synthesizing pseudo-data from past task distributions—and unlike the optimization-based methods examined in the following section—which constrain gradient update directions—architecture-based approaches structurally partition or expand the network itself to maintain task-specific representations. This structural perspective offers strong, often provable guarantees against interference, at the cost of additional considerations around scalability and task identity management.

### Fixed-Architecture Sparse Subnetwork Methods

A prominent class of architecture-based approaches operates within a fixed network capacity by identifying and isolating sparse subnetworks for each task. The core intuition is that a large neural network contains sufficient redundant capacity to accommodate multiple tasks if each task utilizes only a subset of the parameters. Methods in this family typically employ masking or gating mechanisms to discover task-specific subnetworks, such that parameters critical for past tasks are frozen while the remaining free parameters are available for new task learning. This strategy effectively prevents interference between tasks by ensuring that gradient updates for a new task do not alter the weights constituting previously learned subnetworks [145]. Beyond purely preventing forgetting, soft-masking variants allow the new task to selectively access and reuse previously learned features, enabling forward knowledge transfer even across dissimilar tasks [145].

Investigations into how weight sharing decisions within pruning-based continual learning affect knowledge transfer have shown that task complexity and similarity crucially determine which prior subnetwork weights should be shared with new tasks [146]. Sharing all weights indiscriminately can yield sub-optimal performance, whereas informed sharing guided by task relationships leads to measurable improvements in forward knowledge transfer [146].

### Dynamic and Expandable Architectures

While fixed-capacity methods constrain all tasks to share a single network, dynamic architecture methods grow the network as new tasks arrive, allocating fresh parameters when existing capacity is insufficient. This expansion strategy provides a natural solution to the stability-plasticity dilemma: previously learned parameters can be frozen for stability, while newly introduced parameters provide plasticity for the incoming task. To prevent unbounded growth, complementary mechanisms such as parameter pruning and compression are incorporated [147]. For instance, methods that maintain all previously learned feature extractors while applying feature extractor pruning ensure that earlier learned knowledge is preserved with reduced forgetting, particularly mitigating the common observation that knowledge from earlier tasks in a long sequence suffers disproportionately compared to recently learned tasks [147].

### Modular Architectures and Task-Specific Modulation

Modular architectures decompose the network into reusable functional modules that can be composed differently for each task, providing a more flexible form of parameter isolation. Task-specific modulation parameters offer a complementary approach: rather than structurally isolating entire subnetworks, these methods introduce lightweight, task-specific parameters that modulate the activations or weights of a shared backbone. Because only the modulation parameters are updated during new task learning, the shared backbone remains unchanged, achieving zero forgetting of prior tasks by construction [148]. This approach maximizes new task performance while providing formal guarantees of no forgetting, a strong constraint that most regularization-based methods can only approximate.

### Bayesian Structure Adaptation

Bayesian approaches to architecture-based continual learning model uncertainty over both parameters and network structures, enabling principled decisions about which parameters or subnetworks should be active for each task. By maintaining posterior distributions over network masks or connection strengths, these methods can infer task-specific sparse structures in a probabilistic manner, naturally trading off between reusing shared knowledge and isolating task-specific representations. Active forgetting mechanisms inspired by biological synaptic expansion and convergence processes represent a related direction: new parameters are dynamically introduced for each task and then selectively merged or pruned based on their relevance, formally mimicking the synaptic expansion-convergence process observed in biological neural circuits [65]. Importantly, such methods can actively discard knowledge that negatively interferes with new task learning, turning active forgetting into a beneficial mechanism rather than a pathology [65].

### Neuromodulation and Context-Dependent Gating

Neuromodulation-inspired architectures introduce context-dependent gating mechanisms that selectively activate different subsets of network units depending on the current task. A prominent example is the A Neuromodulated Meta-Learning Algorithm (ANML), where a dedicated neuromodulatory network learns to gate the forward and backward passes of a prediction network, effectively learning which parts of the network should be plastic for each task context [92]. This meta-learned gating enables selective plasticity at scale, demonstrating state-of-the-art continual learning performance on long sequences of tasks. More broadly, context-dependent gating is a core neurophysiological principle exploited by multi-mechanism continual learning paradigms that combine parameter isolation with experience rehearsal and active neuron promotion, significantly reducing task interference [41]. This intersection with rehearsal-based thinking anticipates the optimization-based hybrid strategies discussed in the following section, where gradient constraints and episodic memory are jointly leveraged to manage task interference.

### Balancing Capacity, Efficiency, and Transfer

A fundamental challenge for architecture-based methods is managing the trade-off between model capacity, computational efficiency, and knowledge transfer. Fixed-architecture methods are memory-efficient but face capacity saturation as task sequences grow long, while dynamic methods avoid saturation at the cost of increasing model size. Pruning and quantization techniques help control model growth in dynamic approaches but introduce additional complexity in implementation. Crucially, the degree of parameter sharing across tasks directly governs the extent of knowledge transfer: complete isolation maximizes stability at the expense of transfer, while partial sharing enables positive transfer but risks forgetting [146; 145]. Understanding task similarity and complexity is therefore essential for making informed weight-sharing decisions that optimize both forgetting prevention and knowledge reuse [146].

Overall, architecture-based and parameter isolation methods represent a principled and structurally motivated family of continual learning approaches. Their core strength lies in providing strong guarantees against catastrophic forgetting through structural separation of task knowledge, while their main limitations include scalability constraints as task sequences lengthen and the need for task identity information to route computations through task-specific subnetworks. These structural limitations motivate the shift toward optimization-based methods, which seek to achieve comparable interference prevention through gradient-level constraints rather than explicit architectural partitioning. Advances combining dynamic expansion, Bayesian structure learning, neuromodulation, and modular composition continue to push this family toward more scalable and transfer-capable continual learning systems [1; 41].

### 2.6 Optimization-Based Methods

## 2.6 Optimization-Based Methods

Optimization-based continual learning methods address catastrophic forgetting not by storing past data or modifying network architecture, but by carefully designing the learning objective or constraining the parameter update rules themselves. These approaches operate at the level of gradients and optimization landscapes, seeking to ensure that new task learning does not destructively interfere with previously acquired knowledge. Unlike the architecture-based and parameter isolation methods examined in Section 2.5—which structurally partition or expand the network to prevent cross-task interference—optimization-based methods achieve comparable interference prevention through gradient-level constraints rather than explicit architectural modifications. Similarly, they differ from the prompt-based and pre-trained model methods discussed in Section 2.7, which sidestep forgetting by anchoring learning in stable pre-trained representations; instead, optimization-based methods actively manage the trajectory of parameter updates throughout the entire learning process. By imposing geometric or directional constraints on how model parameters evolve, these approaches offer an elegant and often memory-efficient complement to replay, regularization, and architectural paradigms.

### Gradient Projection and Orthogonal Subspace Learning

A central idea in optimization-based continual learning is the notion of gradient projection, wherein parameter updates for new tasks are constrained to lie in a subspace that minimally disturbs the representations or outputs associated with prior tasks. The theoretical motivation is straightforward: if the gradient of a new task's loss is projected onto the orthogonal complement of the subspace spanned by gradients from previous tasks, updates can proceed without overwriting prior knowledge. This family of methods ensures that task-specific gradient directions remain approximately orthogonal, thereby reducing cross-task interference.

Such gradient orthogonality constraints naturally connect to the broader idea of orthogonal subspace learning, where the parameter space is partitioned into task-relevant and task-irrelevant subspaces. In this respect, gradient projection methods share conceptual ground with the sparse subnetwork approaches discussed in Section 2.5, but achieve parameter space partitioning implicitly through update constraints rather than explicit masking. Critically, these approaches do not require storing large episodic memory buffers, making them attractive for memory-constrained deployment scenarios. However, the identification of relevant subspaces typically requires storing or approximating the gradient covariance structure from previous tasks, which introduces its own computational and memory overhead.

### Gradient Episodic Memory and Gradient Alignment

Another prominent branch of optimization-based methods centers on gradient episodic memory (GEM) and its variants. In GEM-type approaches, a small episodic memory buffer is maintained containing representative examples from past tasks, but rather than directly replaying these samples for training—as in the rehearsal-based methods reviewed in earlier sections—they are used to enforce inequality constraints on the current parameter update. Specifically, the update direction is modified so that the loss on stored episodic examples does not increase, geometrically ensuring non-negative backward transfer.

Extensions of GEM have focused on averaging gradient constraints across tasks (A-GEM) to reduce computational complexity, and on maximally aligning gradients across tasks to promote positive forward transfer alongside forgetting prevention. These gradient alignment strategies are particularly relevant for settings where task boundaries are known and a modest memory buffer is available, and they connect naturally to meta-learning objectives that seek to identify initialization points from which fast adaptation to new tasks is possible while preserving generalization on old ones. Importantly, the dual use of episodic memory—both for direct rehearsal and for gradient constraint enforcement—illustrates how optimization-based and replay-based strategies can be seamlessly integrated, anticipating the hybrid approaches discussed in Section 2.8.

### Bilevel Optimization Frameworks

Bilevel optimization provides a principled mathematical framework for continual learning by decomposing the learning problem into an inner optimization (adapting to new tasks) and an outer optimization (preserving performance on past tasks or optimizing for transfer). This hierarchical formulation allows the learner to explicitly balance the competing objectives of plasticity and stability within a unified training procedure, addressing at an algorithmic level the same stability-plasticity dilemma that architectural methods address through structural design.

Bilevel approaches are closely related to meta-learning formulations such as Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML), which optimize for an initialization that enables rapid fine-tuning to new tasks with minimal gradient steps. When extended to sequential task settings, these meta-learning objectives can be interpreted as learning to learn without forgetting: the model is trained to remain in a region of parameter space from which all observed tasks are efficiently recoverable. This connection between bilevel optimization and continual learning has inspired several practical algorithms that alternate between task-specific adaptation and global consolidation updates.

### Meta-Learning Approaches for Continual Learning

Meta-learning and continual learning share a deep conceptual relationship, as both paradigms are concerned with efficiently acquiring and retaining knowledge across a distribution of tasks. In the context of optimization-based continual learning, meta-learning approaches explicitly optimize for performance across multiple tasks simultaneously, often treating the entire task sequence as a meta-training distribution. The resulting models are endowed with inductive biases that facilitate rapid adaptation to new tasks without catastrophic interference with prior ones.

Systems such as Meta-Experience Replay combine gradient-based meta-learning with episodic memory replay, using stored past experiences not only for direct rehearsal but also to construct meta-learning updates that improve both forward and backward transfer [4]. Hardware-aware meta-continual learning systems such as LifeLearner extend these ideas to resource-constrained embedded platforms, combining meta-learning with rehearsal strategies and compression techniques to achieve near-optimal continual learning performance under strict memory and energy budgets [72]. These meta-learning formulations also share structural parallels with the neuromodulation-inspired gating mechanisms discussed in Section 2.5, where a higher-level controller learns to modulate the learning dynamics of a lower-level network—both perspectives reflecting a broader principle of learning how to learn efficiently in sequential settings.

### Strengths, Limitations, and Connections to Other Methods

Optimization-based methods offer several appealing properties. By constraining gradient updates rather than storing raw data, they can achieve strong forgetting prevention with relatively modest memory footprints compared to replay-heavy approaches. Gradient projection methods are theoretically grounded in the geometry of the loss landscape and provide interpretable mechanisms for understanding why forgetting occurs and how it can be mitigated.

Nevertheless, these methods face important practical challenges. Accurately estimating the relevant gradient subspaces from past tasks requires maintaining gradient statistics or episodic memory, both of which scale with the number of tasks. For long task sequences, the cumulative subspace constraints can become overly restrictive, limiting plasticity for genuinely novel tasks. Furthermore, bilevel and meta-learning formulations typically involve computationally expensive nested optimization loops, which may be prohibitive for resource-constrained deployments [68].

Optimization-based methods also interact richly with other continual learning paradigms. They can be naturally combined with regularization-based objectives, where gradient constraints complement parameter importance penalties, or with replay buffers, where stored samples serve dual roles in direct rehearsal and gradient constraint enforcement. Looking ahead to Section 2.7, the emergence of large pre-trained models introduces a further dimension: when the backbone is already stable due to rich pre-training, optimization-based gradient constraints become most valuable for managing the fine-tuning of task-specific components, suggesting that the two paradigms are not competing but complementary. As continual learning research matures, hybrid approaches that integrate optimization-based gradient management with architectural flexibility and large pre-trained representations are likely to yield increasingly capable lifelong learning systems.

### 2.7 Prompt-Based and Pre-trained Model Methods

## 2.7 Prompt-Based and Pre-trained Model Methods

A relatively recent and rapidly growing paradigm in continual learning leverages large pre-trained foundation models—such as vision transformers, BERT-style language models, and multimodal models—as strong initializations that inherently reduce the severity of catastrophic forgetting. The core intuition is straightforward: when a model has been pre-trained on vast and diverse data, its learned representations are already rich, general, and broadly transferable, meaning that incremental fine-tuning on new tasks causes comparatively little disruption to previously acquired knowledge. This stands in contrast to learning from randomly initialized networks, where the entire representational space must be shaped from scratch for each task, creating strong competition among tasks for the same parameters. Unlike the optimization-based methods discussed in Section 2.6, which constrain gradient updates to preserve prior knowledge, prompt-based and pre-trained model methods address forgetting more directly by anchoring learning in representations that are already stable and broadly informative before the continual learning phase begins.

### Frozen and Partially Frozen Pre-trained Representations

One of the most direct strategies is to freeze the backbone of a pre-trained model entirely and learn only lightweight task-specific components on top of the fixed representations. Because the shared representational space remains static, there is no mechanism by which new task learning can overwrite features useful to old tasks—catastrophic forgetting is essentially eliminated for the frozen layers. This approach is closely related to parameter isolation methods (Section 2.5), but the key distinction is that the frozen parameters were never trained on any of the continual learning tasks; rather, they provide a universal feature extractor that benefits all tasks simultaneously. In this sense, the approach can be viewed as a natural complement to optimization-based gradient management: rather than carefully steering updates away from previously learned subspaces, the pre-trained backbone sidesteps the problem by providing a fixed, task-agnostic representational foundation.

The effectiveness of frozen pre-trained representations has been explored and validated across multiple empirical studies. Research examining how pre-training affects continual learning performance raises important questions about the degree to which strong initialization genuinely constitutes continual learning progress versus simply reducing the difficulty of the underlying problem [13]. Nevertheless, the practical benefits are undeniable: initializing from a powerful pre-trained model consistently reduces forgetting and improves forward transfer across task sequences.

### Prompt Tuning and Prefix Tuning for Incremental Learning

Rather than fine-tuning all or even a subset of model parameters, prompt-based methods introduce a small set of learnable vectors—termed prompts or prefixes—that are prepended to or injected into the input or intermediate representations of a frozen pre-trained model. These prompts steer the model's behavior for a specific task without modifying the underlying weights, preserving all previously learned knowledge encoded in the pre-trained backbone.

In the continual learning context, each new task can be associated with its own dedicated prompt or a set of shared prompts that are incrementally updated. Because the pre-trained weights are untouched, learning new task-specific prompts does not interfere with the model's ability to perform old tasks when their corresponding prompts are retrieved. This creates a natural parameter isolation mechanism at a much lower memory cost than traditional architecture-based methods, since prompts typically consist of only a few hundred to a few thousand parameters compared to millions in the full backbone.

Prefix tuning extends this idea by inserting learned prefix vectors at every transformer layer, providing more expressive task conditioning throughout the full depth of the network while still keeping the backbone frozen. The combination of frozen backbone and lightweight prompt learning thus achieves an attractive balance: high plasticity for new tasks through prompt adaptation, and near-perfect stability for old tasks through backbone preservation. This balance mirrors, at an architectural level, the stability-plasticity trade-off that optimization-based methods such as gradient projection and bilevel optimization seek to achieve through careful parameter update constraints.

### Advantages of Pre-trained Model Initialization

The advantages of pre-trained model-based continual learning are multifaceted. First, the rich semantic representations learned during large-scale pre-training provide strong inductive biases that generalize well across diverse downstream tasks, reducing the amount of task-specific data needed for competent performance. Second, because the pre-trained representations capture general visual or linguistic structure, the features required by different continual tasks tend to overlap substantially in the pre-trained feature space, facilitating positive forward transfer. Third, the reduced parameter count of prompt-based adaptation dramatically lowers memory and computational overhead compared to rehearsal-based or architecture-expansion methods.

Neuroscience-inspired perspectives also support this approach: the stable backbone can be seen as analogous to the slow neocortical learning system in Complementary Learning Systems (CLS) theory, which gradually acquires structured world knowledge, while the fast-adapting prompts play the role of the hippocampal system that rapidly encodes task-specific information [82; 83]. This biological parallel, which will be revisited in greater depth in the context of hybrid and bio-inspired methods in Section 2.8, suggests that the effectiveness of pre-trained models in continual learning is not coincidental but reflects a fundamental principle about how stable general knowledge and flexible task-specific adaptation can coexist.

### Challenges and Limitations

Despite their promise, prompt-based and pre-trained model methods face several important challenges. First, their effectiveness is contingent on the quality and breadth of pre-training: if the pre-trained model has not encountered data distributions similar to the target continual learning tasks, the frozen representations may be insufficient, and fine-tuning a larger portion of the network becomes necessary, reintroducing forgetting risk. Second, the design of prompt retrieval mechanisms—determining which prompt or combination of prompts to use at inference time when task identities are unknown—is non-trivial in class-incremental or domain-incremental settings where task boundaries are absent.

Third, as the number of tasks grows, naively storing one prompt per task leads to linear growth in the number of prompt parameters, undermining the computational efficiency argument. Methods that share prompts across tasks or learn compact prompt pools can address this but introduce additional complexity in managing interference between shared prompt components. Fourth, there is an ongoing debate about whether pre-trained model methods truly solve continual learning or simply sidestep it by leveraging massive prior knowledge that the model accumulated before the continual learning phase began [13]. This distinction matters for understanding the degree to which these methods generalize to settings where large pre-trained models are unavailable or where the target domain is far from any pre-training distribution.

Finally, prompt-based methods are most naturally suited to transformer-based architectures and may require significant adaptation to apply to convolutional networks, recurrent models, or other architectures that lack the attention mechanisms prompts naturally interface with. Ongoing research continues to explore how to extend the core ideas of lightweight task conditioning and frozen backbone preservation to a broader range of model families and continual learning scenarios.

In summary, prompt-based and pre-trained model methods represent a powerful and increasingly popular family of continual learning approaches that exploit the stability provided by rich pre-trained representations, achieving strong performance on incremental learning benchmarks with minimal memory overhead and parameter growth. Their rise reflects a broader trend in machine learning toward leveraging foundation models as universal priors. Importantly, these methods do not exist in isolation: as the following section discusses, hybrid and bio-inspired approaches frequently draw on the same complementary-systems intuitions that underlie prompt-based designs, integrating multiple mechanisms—including memory replay, architectural modulation, and neuromodulatory gating—to address the failure modes that pre-trained model methods alone may leave unresolved. The continued development of prompt-based and pre-trained model methods, particularly in concert with such hybrid strategies, will likely play a central role in bridging the gap between controlled continual learning research and practical real-world deployment.

### 2.8 Hybrid and Bio-Inspired Methods

## 2.8 Hybrid and Bio-Inspired Methods

Having examined prompt-based and pre-trained model methods in Section 2.7 — which address catastrophic forgetting by anchoring learning in stable, broadly informative representations — we now turn to a complementary and increasingly influential paradigm: hybrid and bio-inspired approaches. These methods occupy an important niche in the methodological landscape, drawing on insights from neuroscience, cognitive science, and multiple complementary algorithmic principles to construct more robust and capable continual learners. Rather than relying on a single mechanism such as regularization or replay alone, these methods integrate multiple strategies, often motivated by the observation that biological learning systems themselves employ a rich ensemble of complementary mechanisms to achieve lifelong knowledge acquisition without catastrophic forgetting. As we will see, many of the intuitions underlying prompt-based designs — particularly the Complementary Learning Systems analogy between frozen backbones and slow neocortical learning — resurface here in more explicit and architecturally diverse forms.

### Synergistic Combinations of Synaptic Consolidation and Experience Replay

One prominent hybrid paradigm combines synaptic consolidation mechanisms with experience replay, exploiting the complementary strengths of both approaches. Regularization-based methods such as Elastic Weight Consolidation protect important parameters but struggle when task sequences grow long and consolidation errors accumulate. Replay-based methods mitigate forgetting by revisiting stored exemplars but require memory buffers. Combining both principles allows the system to benefit from parameter-level protection while also rehearsing critical past experiences, yielding synergistic gains in both stability and plasticity. Methods such as Meta-Experience Replay [98] explicitly integrate experience replay with meta-learning optimization, demonstrating that jointly optimizing for transfer and interference minimization across multiple mechanisms leads to consistently stronger performance than any single-strategy baseline across diverse benchmarks. These combinations directly address the failure modes identified in the comparative analysis of individual method families: where regularization alone succumbs to accumulating constraint rigidity and replay alone is bottlenecked by buffer constraints, their integration provides mutual compensation.

### Multi-Mechanism Paradigms and Adversarial Hybrid Architectures

Several hybrid methods propose disjoint or jointly learned representations that separate shared task-invariant features from task-specific components. For instance, the Adversarial Continual Learning framework [127] learns a disjoint representation for task-invariant and task-specific features, combining architecture growth to prevent forgetting of task-specific skills with experience replay to preserve shared skills. This hybrid design demonstrates superiority over purely architecture-based or purely memory-based approaches on class-incremental benchmarks, underscoring that multi-mechanism frameworks can address complementary failure modes simultaneously.

Adaptive weight sharing frameworks such as CLAW (Continual Learning with Adaptive Weights) [149] extend this philosophy into a probabilistic modeling framework using variational inference to dynamically determine which portions of the network to share across tasks. By adaptively identifying shared and task-specific components in a data-driven manner, CLAW avoids the brittleness of rigidly specified sharing strategies, achieving state-of-the-art results on multiple benchmarks through a principled Bayesian hybridization of architecture adaptation and regularization.

### Mixture-of-Experts and Hierarchical Gating Mechanisms

Bio-inspired gating mechanisms offer another route to hybrid continual learning. The Mixture-of-Variational-Experts (MoVE) framework [150; 151] introduces a novel neural network layer that allocates information processing through distinct paths governed by a learned gating policy, drawing inspiration from the modular organization of biological neural circuits. By combining variational inference with a hierarchical information-theoretic optimality principle, this approach creates a diverse ensemble of specialized sub-networks, each learning to handle specific task types, while a shared gating policy governs inter-path routing. The resulting architecture operates in a task-agnostic setting — requiring no explicit task identity at inference — and is applicable across supervised learning, reinforcement learning, and generative modeling, demonstrating the broad versatility of principled hybrid methods. Notably, this task-agnostic property addresses one of the key limitations of architecture-based and prompt-based methods identified in the preceding sections, both of which often require task identity at inference time.

### Sparse Distributed Memory and Bio-Inspired Local Learning Rules

Biological neural systems employ sparse and distributed representations that inherently support continual learning by reducing representational overlap between memories. Computational realizations of Sparse Distributed Memory (SDM) have been shown to translate directly into architectural components that mitigate catastrophic forgetting [42]. Connecting SDM principles to the Transformer model via a modified Multi-Layer Perceptron architecture, this approach demonstrates that biologically motivated sparsity mechanisms — including threshold-based activation functions and activity-dependent routing — enable strong continual learning without requiring any experience replay or explicit task boundary information. Notably, each component derived from biological principles is shown to be necessary for the continual learning capability, reinforcing the importance of faithfully incorporating neuroscientific insights rather than superficially borrowing terminology.

Self-supervised and contrastive learning objectives have also been integrated into hybrid continual learning pipelines as biologically plausible representation stabilization mechanisms. Methods such as contrastive continual learning [152] combine feature propagation with contrastive representation alignment, bridging domain shifts across task transitions by ensuring current and previous representation spaces remain mutually consistent. The explicit alignment of embedding spaces using supervised contrastive losses, combined with architectural feature propagation mechanisms, exemplifies how hybrid methods can leverage multiple complementary signals to handle both task-level and class-level distributional shifts — challenges that regularization-only or replay-only approaches address only partially.

### Complementary Learning Systems and Multi-Memory Architectures

The Complementary Learning Systems (CLS) theory from cognitive neuroscience posits that the brain employs two interacting memory systems: a fast-learning hippocampal system that rapidly encodes new episodic experiences and a slow-learning neocortical system that gradually consolidates structured knowledge. As introduced in Section 2.7 in the context of prompt-based methods — where the frozen pre-trained backbone serves as a functional analogue of the slow neocortical system and lightweight prompts play the role of rapid hippocampal encoding — this framework takes on richer architectural expression in hybrid methods. Triple memory network architectures instantiate working memory, episodic memory, and semantic memory components within a single continual learning system, coordinating rapid episodic encoding, memory consolidation replay, and long-term semantic knowledge storage through coordinated inter-system interactions that closely mirror the neuroscientific mechanisms underlying human continual learning.

The meta-consolidation framework MERLIN [100] represents another bio-inspired hybrid approach, modeling neural network weights for each task as samples from an incrementally learned and consolidated meta-distribution. Operating in the challenging online continual learning setting where each data point is observed only once, MERLIN combines meta-learning with Bayesian consolidation to maintain a dynamic prior over task-specific weights, demonstrating consistent improvements over multiple baselines including state-of-the-art methods.

### Bayesian Hybrid Methods and Natural Gradient Approaches

Several bio-inspired hybrid methods ground their design in Bayesian inference, drawing parallels to how biological synapses encode uncertainty and regulate plasticity based on prior experience. Bayesian structure adaptation methods [119] learn sparse task-specific subnetwork structures within shared weight matrices, enabling inter-task knowledge transfer through the overlap of learned subsets while protecting task-specific parameters from interference. This Bayesian hybrid of regularization and architecture-based isolation subsumes the strengths of both approaches within a single principled framework, directly addressing the capacity and scalability limitations that pure architecture-based methods face when task sequences grow long. Similarly, facilitating Bayesian continual learning through natural gradients and Stein gradients [128] demonstrates that combining posterior-based parameter consolidation with coreset-based data distribution preservation yields complementary benefits, with natural gradients improving parameter-level knowledge retention and Stein gradients improving the diversity and representativeness of retained data points.

### Neuromodulation and Context-Dependent Gating

Neuromodulatory processes in the brain — such as the modulation of synaptic plasticity by dopamine, acetylcholine, and norepinephrine — provide context-dependent signals that regulate learning rates and memory consolidation at the cellular level. Computational analogues of these mechanisms have been incorporated into hybrid continual learning architectures through context-dependent gating networks, adaptive learning rate modulators, and neuromodulatory feedback connections. The Self-Attention Meta-Learner (SAM) [99] operationalizes a related principle by learning a meta-level attention mechanism that selects which prior knowledge representations are relevant for each new task, allowing task-specific branches to be constructed on top of selectively gated prior knowledge. This design reflects the biological principle that not all previously acquired knowledge is equally relevant to new learning episodes, and that intelligent selective activation of prior representations reduces catastrophic interference. In this respect, neuromodulatory gating can be understood as a dynamic, learned analogue to the fixed prompt retrieval mechanisms discussed in Section 2.7, achieving context-sensitive knowledge activation without requiring predefined task identifiers.

### Discussion: The Case for Hybrid and Bio-Inspired Integration

The accumulating evidence from hybrid and bio-inspired continual learning methods strongly suggests that no single mechanism is sufficient to fully address the stability-plasticity dilemma across the diversity of real-world continual learning scenarios. As the comparative analysis in the following section will elaborate, pure regularization methods struggle with long task sequences due to accumulating approximation errors; pure replay methods face memory and privacy constraints; pure architectural isolation methods scale poorly in model size; and pure optimization methods can be sensitive to task ordering and gradient alignment. By integrating multiple complementary mechanisms — consolidation, replay, architecture adaptation, sparsity, gating, and meta-learning — hybrid methods can address the failure modes of each individual component, yielding systems that are simultaneously more stable, more plastic, and more computationally efficient.

This multi-mechanism philosophy closely mirrors the redundancy and complementarity observed in biological neural circuits, where multiple overlapping memory systems, plasticity rules, and neuromodulatory processes collectively achieve robust lifelong learning. The connection to prompt-based and pre-trained model methods is particularly instructive: while Section 2.7 showed that stable pre-trained representations can dramatically reduce the burden placed on any single continual learning mechanism, hybrid bio-inspired methods demonstrate that even when such representations are unavailable or insufficient, carefully orchestrated combinations of complementary algorithmic principles can recover comparable robustness through architectural and computational means. Future advances in hybrid and bio-inspired continual learning will likely require deeper collaboration between computational neuroscience and machine learning, translating increasingly precise mechanistic insights from biological systems into scalable algorithmic components that can operate effectively in complex, open-ended real-world environments.

### 2.9 Comparative Analysis, Strengths, and Limitations

Having surveyed the principal families of continual learning methods across the preceding subsections — from regularization and replay to architecture-based isolation, optimization strategies, prompt-based approaches, and hybrid bio-inspired frameworks — we now synthesize their relative strengths and limitations across several key dimensions: memory overhead, computational cost, scalability to long task sequences, applicability across learning scenarios, reliance on task-boundary information, and overall empirical effectiveness. This comparative analysis is intended to guide practitioners in selecting appropriate strategies for specific deployment contexts, and to illuminate the broader structural relationships between these methodological families.

**Regularization-Based Methods**

Regularization-based approaches, including Elastic Weight Consolidation, Synaptic Intelligence, and Memory Aware Synapses, offer the appealing property of requiring no additional data storage beyond the model parameters themselves, making them highly memory-efficient [122]. By penalizing changes to parameters deemed important for previous tasks, these methods preserve prior knowledge without explicit rehearsal. However, their effectiveness degrades as the number of tasks grows, because the cumulative regularization constraints become increasingly rigid, leaving limited plasticity for new learning [115]. Furthermore, importance estimates computed over a finite parameter space must be tracked and accumulated across all tasks, leading to non-trivial computational overhead at scale. In class-incremental scenarios, regularization alone often proves insufficient, particularly when task sequences are long and class distributions shift substantially [153]. Empirical evaluations have repeatedly shown that simple baselines such as L2 regularization can surprisingly approach the performance of more sophisticated regularization variants [110], casting doubt on the marginal gains of complex importance-weighting schemes in many practical settings.

**Replay-Based Methods**

Experience replay and its variants occupy a prominent position in continual learning owing to their strong empirical performance and conceptual simplicity. By storing and revisiting a small episodic memory of past samples, replay methods directly counteract catastrophic forgetting through rehearsal [6]. Theoretical analysis confirms that optimal continual learning — defined as never forgetting — requires memory growing linearly with the number of tasks, providing a principled justification for replay [115]. Practical implementations such as reservoir sampling, gradient coreset selection, and maximally interfered retrieval differ in their buffer management policies, with gradient-based selection strategies such as GCR demonstrating consistent gains over random sampling [112; 113]. The principal limitation of replay is the memory buffer itself: performance degrades noticeably when buffer size is tightly constrained, and privacy-sensitive domains may prohibit storing raw samples entirely [154]. Practical recommendations suggest that replay benefits substantially from data augmentation and careful weighting policies, enabling competitive performance even with small buffers [116]. Replay methods generally perform well across task-incremental, class-incremental, and domain-incremental scenarios, and many do not require explicit task-boundary information during inference, lending them broad applicability.

**Generative Replay Methods**

Generative replay addresses the privacy and storage limitations of episodic replay by training generative models to synthesize pseudo-samples for past tasks rather than retaining raw data. This approach is particularly attractive under strict memory constraints where storing real samples is infeasible [118]. However, generative replay introduces a substantial additional computational burden from training and maintaining high-quality generative models alongside the primary classifier.
