The quality of synthesized samples critically determines performance: on low-dimensional benchmarks, generative replay is effective, but scaling to complex high-resolution datasets such as ImageNet-scale data remains challenging [117]. An interesting recent finding demonstrates that even when synthesized images do not improve classification of old classes, they can serve effectively as negative examples to sharpen decision boundaries for new classes [117], suggesting that the role of generative replay extends beyond simple knowledge consolidation. The scalability of generative replay to very long task sequences is further hampered by the compounding approximation errors in the generative model as it is itself retrained continually — a limitation that motivates the hybrid combinations discussed in the preceding subsection.

**Architecture-Based and Parameter Isolation Methods**

Architecture-based methods, including sparse subnetwork allocation, dynamic network expansion, and pruning-and-quantization approaches, achieve near-zero forgetting for previously learned tasks by dedicating distinct or non-overlapping parameter subsets to each task [121; 120]. This makes them theoretically attractive for scenarios requiring strict knowledge preservation. The primary limitation is model capacity: as the number of tasks grows, a fixed-capacity network eventually becomes saturated, forcing parameter sharing or necessitating model expansion. Dynamic expansion methods address this but at the cost of growing memory and inference time. Bayesian structure adaptation further augments this family by learning probabilistic masks that support inter-task transfer through overlapping sparse subsets [119]. Parameter isolation methods typically require task identity at inference time, which restricts their applicability to task-incremental scenarios unless additional mechanisms are provided for task inference. Combining pruning with quantization, as in Ada-QPacknet, mitigates capacity constraints by compressing task-specific subnetworks [120], but introduces further complexity in the training pipeline.

**Optimization-Based Methods**

Optimization-based methods, including gradient projection into orthogonal subspaces and gradient episodic memory, modify the learning dynamics directly to minimize interference between tasks. Approaches such as learning in low-rank orthogonal subspaces have demonstrated strong results that match or exceed experience replay baselines on standard benchmarks without requiring explicit task boundaries [123]. The computational overhead of projecting gradients or solving constrained optimization problems can be significant, particularly for large networks with many parameters. Like regularization methods, purely optimization-based approaches do not require additional storage of past data, but their effectiveness across very long task sequences depends critically on the dimensionality of the parameter space and whether sufficient orthogonal directions remain available for new task learning.

**Prompt-Based and Pre-trained Model Methods**

Prompt-based and pre-trained model approaches have emerged as a highly competitive family by leveraging the rich representations encoded in large foundation models. By keeping the pre-trained backbone frozen or minimally modified and learning lightweight task-specific prompts, these methods benefit from strong initialization that inherently reduces catastrophic forgetting [125; 126]. The main limitation is their dependence on the availability and quality of a suitable pre-trained model: under self-supervised pre-training — more realistic in practice — prompt-based strategies can fall short of their potential [125]. Additionally, these methods are most naturally suited to task-incremental or class-incremental scenarios where the pre-trained representation provides adequate coverage of the task distribution; their applicability to highly specialized or out-of-distribution domains may be limited.

**Hybrid and Bio-Inspired Methods**

As discussed in the preceding subsection, hybrid methods that combine regularization, replay, and architectural elements generally achieve superior performance by compensating for the individual weaknesses of each component. For instance, combining Bayesian sparsity priors with full experience replay mitigates cross-task parameter interference while maintaining a compact model [109]. Bio-inspired architectures incorporating synaptic plasticity and neuromodulation offer promising alternatives to gradient-based methods, particularly under strict memory constraints, demonstrating competitive performance on standard benchmarks [44]. The multi-mechanism philosophy underlying these hybrid approaches closely mirrors the complementarity observed in biological neural circuits, where overlapping memory systems and neuromodulatory processes collectively achieve robust lifelong learning. The trade-off is increased system complexity, which complicates hyperparameter tuning and reproducibility.

**Summary of Key Trade-offs**

Table 2.1 conceptually summarizes the comparative landscape across all surveyed families. Regularization methods excel in memory efficiency but struggle with long sequences and class-incremental settings. Replay methods achieve strong empirical performance across scenarios but are constrained by buffer size and privacy concerns. Generative replay removes the need for raw sample storage but suffers from scalability and quality degradation in complex domains. Architecture-based methods provide strong forgetting prevention but face capacity limits and often require task identity at test time. Optimization-based methods offer theoretically grounded interference reduction without additional data but at higher computational cost. Prompt-based methods provide state-of-the-art performance with minimal additional parameters when a suitable pre-trained model is available. Hybrid approaches frequently achieve the best overall performance at the cost of system complexity.

A unifying theoretical perspective reveals that despite their diverse origins, many of these methods share common mathematical structures, as they can be understood as instances of a general optimization objective that balances current task performance against knowledge consolidation [107]. This observation underscores the complementarity of existing approaches and motivates the development of hybrid strategies that synergistically combine their respective strengths. Empirical evaluations further emphasize that simple strong baselines — such as naive rehearsal with standard regularization — can match many sophisticated methods [110], highlighting the importance of rigorous benchmarking when assessing the genuine contribution of novel continual learning algorithms. Practitioners are advised to consider the specific constraints of their deployment setting — including available memory budget, privacy requirements, task-boundary availability, and desired scalability — when selecting among these families, recognizing that no single approach dominates across all dimensions.

## 3 Theoretical Perspectives and Evaluation Frameworks

### 3.1 Computational Complexity of Continual Learning

## 3.1 Computational Complexity and Memory Lower Bounds

Understanding the fundamental computational limits of continual learning is essential for grounding algorithmic design in rigorous theoretical principles. A central question is whether it is possible to build efficient continual learners without incurring prohibitive memory or computational costs, and theoretical analyses have revealed sobering hardness results that constrain what any algorithm can achieve. These complexity-theoretic results form a natural starting point for the theoretical study of continual learning, complementing the information-theoretic and kernel-based analyses that follow in subsequent sections.

### Memory Lower Bounds

One of the most significant theoretical contributions to continual learning complexity theory concerns the memory requirements of any viable continual learner. Formal complexity-theoretic analysis within the PAC framework has established that any continual learner — even one permitted to perform improper learning — requires memory that grows **linearly** with the number of tasks $k$ [12]. This result is derived through novel applications of communication complexity arguments and carries profound implications: it strongly suggests that the problem of continual learning, in its most general form, is inherently intractable without memory that scales with the task sequence length. Crucially, this lower bound applies regardless of whether the learner is proper or improper, meaning that no clever hypothesis class reformulation can circumvent the fundamental memory requirement.

Interestingly, the same analysis demonstrates that if logarithmically many passes over learning tasks are permitted, an algorithm based on the multiplicative weights update method can achieve memory requirements that scale more favorably, but only under the relaxation of improper learning [12]. This result reveals a fundamental trade-off: reducing memory overhead necessitates either allowing multiple passes over data or accepting improperness in the learned hypotheses.

### Intractability of Forgetting-Free Learning

Beyond memory bounds, theoretical work has examined the intractability of avoiding catastrophic forgetting entirely without access to perfect memory. Analyses of gradient-based optimization reveal that the shared parameterization inherent in neural networks creates what may be termed *distal interference* — a phenomenon where training on a subset of the input domain induces non-local parameter changes that affect predictions on entirely different subsets of the domain [155]. This analysis demonstrates that uniformly trainable models free from distal interference must be **exponentially large**, establishing a fundamental complexity barrier for model-only approaches to continual learning. The implication is stark: avoiding catastrophic forgetting through architectural constraints alone, without augmenting training data or modifying the learning algorithm, requires exponential model size and is therefore computationally infeasible in practice [155].

### Complexity in Reinforcement Learning Settings

Computational hardness extends into the reinforcement learning domain as well. Maintaining optimal value functions under non-stationary environments compounds the standard difficulties of continual learning, as the agent must simultaneously manage changing reward structures, shifting environment dynamics, and the forgetting of previously acquired skills. These additional layers of non-stationarity render the reinforcement learning variant of continual learning particularly challenging from a computational perspective [9].

### Implications for Algorithm Design

These theoretical results carry several important practical implications for the design of continual learning algorithms:

1. **Memory is unavoidable**: The linear memory lower bound [12] directly motivates replay-based methods, which maintain experience buffers to store representative past data [129; 112]. Rather than treating memory as an optional enhancement, theory establishes it as a necessary component of any scalable continual learner.

2. **Model-only solutions are fundamentally limited**: The exponential size requirement for interference-free models [155] explains why regularization-based methods, which constrain parameter updates without external memory, inevitably face degradation on long task sequences. This motivates hybrid approaches that combine regularization with replay or architectural expansion.

3. **Approximation is necessary**: Since exact continual learning is computationally intractable in general, practical algorithms must embrace approximate solutions. This perspective motivates the design of methods that trade off optimality for computational efficiency, such as approximate gradient projection methods and lossy memory compression schemes.

4. **Task boundary information matters**: The complexity of continual learning is further modulated by whether task boundary information is available. Without explicit task signals, the learner must simultaneously perform task inference and knowledge retention, compounding the computational burden [81].

Together, these theoretical results provide a rigorous foundation for understanding why continual learning is fundamentally difficult and why practical methods must make principled trade-offs among memory, computation, and approximation quality. This foundation also sets the stage for the more fine-grained analyses presented in the following section, where information-theoretic frameworks and Neural Tangent Kernel perspectives offer complementary tools for characterizing precisely how and where forgetting occurs within the architecture of a neural network.

### 3.2 Information-Theoretic Analyses and Neural Tangent Kernel Perspectives

## 3.2 Information-Theoretic Analyses and Neural Tangent Kernel Perspectives

Building upon the computational complexity and memory lower bounds established in Section 3.1, which demonstrate the fundamental intractability of forgetting-free learning and the necessity of memory-augmenting mechanisms, this section examines two complementary theoretical frameworks that go further by characterizing precisely *how* and *where* forgetting occurs within neural networks: information-theoretic analyses and Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) perspectives. Together, these approaches move beyond the coarse hardness results of complexity theory to provide principled mathematical tools for understanding the internal dynamics of catastrophic forgetting, thereby laying the groundwork for the generalization bounds and unified frameworks examined in Section 3.3.

### Information-Theoretic Frameworks

Information-theoretic analyses offer a probabilistic lens through which the flow of task-relevant information across network layers can be studied. A key insight from this perspective is that forgetting is not uniformly distributed across a network's layers. Empirical and theoretical investigations have shown that deeper layers are disproportionately responsible for catastrophic forgetting, as they encode the most task-specific, high-level representations that are most susceptible to overwriting during sequential training [18]. This finding has important implications: stabilizing the deeper representational layers of a neural network is both necessary and sufficient to substantially reduce forgetting, a principle validated by studying how mitigation methods act selectively on those layers.

From a representational standpoint, catastrophic forgetting can be decomposed into distinct processes: a misalignment between hidden representations and readout layers, partial distortion of representational geometry, and a smaller component involving the irreversible loss of task-relevant information [21]. This decomposition reveals that a substantial fraction of apparent forgetting is attributable to representational drift and readout misalignment rather than complete erasure of encoded knowledge, suggesting that information-theoretically, representations are more robust than task-performance metrics alone suggest. This insight also provides a more fine-grained complement to the complexity-theoretic result that any continual learner requires linearly growing memory [12]: even when memory is constrained, much of the underlying representational structure may survive, and the challenge is as much one of readout alignment as of raw information preservation.

The concept of representation forgetting—measured through the degradation of optimal linear probe performance on previously learned tasks—provides a more nuanced tool than raw accuracy for probing how continual learning algorithms alter internal representations [156]. Crucially, this framework reveals that models trained without explicit forgetting controls often maintain surprisingly stable representations in longer task sequences, a finding inconsistent with the magnitude of forgetting indicated by performance metrics alone. This dissociation between representational stability and task performance highlights the importance of readout alignment as a separate information-preservation concern, one that cannot be addressed by memory bounds or architectural constraints alone.

Information-theoretic perspectives also help explain why certain training regimes and architectural choices yield less forgetting. For example, wider neural networks with sparser activations and gradient flows have been shown to experience reduced catastrophic forgetting, which can be understood through the lens of preserving task-relevant information in distributed, low-interference representations [23]. The sparsity of both activations and gradients—properties that effectively reduce the overlap of information representations across tasks—emerges as a theoretically grounded mechanism for information preservation, and one that connects naturally to the NTK perspective examined in the following subsection.

### Neural Tangent Kernel Perspectives

The Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) framework provides a complementary, analytically tractable approach to characterizing catastrophic forgetting by studying the functional similarity and interference structure between tasks. In this framework, the NTK of a neural network describes the linearized behavior of the network during gradient descent training, and the overlap between the NTKs of two tasks directly quantifies the degree to which learning one task will interfere with performance on the other.

Formally, the NTK overlap matrix between two tasks captures the inner products of their respective gradient features in parameter space. High overlap between the NTK of a new task and that of a previously learned task predicts severe catastrophic forgetting, as gradient updates for the new task will necessarily modify parameters that are critical for the old task [17]. This formalization transforms the intuitive notion of "task similarity causes forgetting" into a precise, measurable quantity grounded in the geometry of the loss landscape, thereby providing a more rigorous underpinning for the complexity-theoretic notion of distal interference introduced in Section 3.1.

Building on this framework, it has been demonstrated that tasks with highly aligned NTK structures suffer the most from mutual interference, while tasks with largely orthogonal NTK representations can be learned sequentially with minimal forgetting [17]. This analysis also provides a theoretical foundation for gradient projection methods: by projecting gradient updates into the subspace orthogonal to the gradients of previous tasks—effectively exploiting the structure of the NTK—algorithms such as Orthogonal Gradient Descent (OGD) can mitigate forgetting in a principled manner. A variant leveraging Principal Component Analysis to identify the most informative directions within the NTK structure has been proposed to make such projection methods more data-efficient and practically effective. As will be discussed in Section 3.3, these gradient projection insights connect directly to the null space analyses that form the basis of generalization bounds for continual learning.

The NTK perspective thus unifies several empirical observations about catastrophic forgetting: the dependence of forgetting on task relationships, the effectiveness of gradient orthogonalization methods, and the role of network width in reducing task interference. Wider networks, in the NTK regime, operate closer to the kernel limit where gradient updates become increasingly orthogonal across tasks, offering a theoretical explanation consistent with the information-theoretic findings on sparse, low-interference representations discussed above [23].

### Layer-wise Information Preservation and Knowledge Transfer

Combining information-theoretic and NTK perspectives yields a richer understanding of layer-wise dynamics in continual learning. The observation that forgetting is concentrated in deeper, more task-specific layers aligns with NTK analyses showing that the NTK overlap is dominated by the contributions of later network layers, which encode the most discriminative and task-specific features. Methods that enforce stability on these layers—either through regularization, distillation, or architectural isolation—can therefore be understood as targeting the layer-wise information bottlenecks that are most critical for knowledge retention. This layer-wise view also clarifies why the model-only solutions shown to be computationally infeasible in Section 3.1 fail in practice: without external memory or architectural expansion, stabilizing the task-specific deeper layers necessarily reduces plasticity for new task learning.

Furthermore, information-theoretic analyses of sequential training reveal a nuanced interplay between forward and backward knowledge transfer. When tasks share structural similarities, the shared information encoded in early layers provides a common substrate for positive forward transfer, while the task-specific information in deeper layers requires explicit protection to prevent backward interference [18]. This layered view of information flow provides guidance for designing continual learning algorithms that selectively stabilize the most forgetting-prone components of a network while allowing sufficient plasticity in shared lower-level representations, a balance whose theoretical characterization will be formalized through the generalization bounds and stability-plasticity frameworks examined in Section 3.3.

Taken together, information-theoretic and NTK frameworks provide complementary and mutually reinforcing theoretical foundations for understanding catastrophic forgetting. They offer quantitative tools for predicting forgetting from first principles, for explaining the efficacy of existing mitigation strategies, and for guiding the design of new algorithms that preserve task-relevant information across long sequences of learning experiences. By characterizing precisely where and how forgetting manifests within the architecture of a neural network, these frameworks set the stage for the more encompassing theoretical treatments of generalization bounds, stability-plasticity trade-offs, and knowledge transfer dynamics that follow in the next section.

### 3.3 Generalization Bounds and Theoretical Frameworks for Continual Learning

## 3.3 Generalization Bounds and Theoretical Frameworks for Continual Learning

Building upon the information-theoretic and NTK perspectives established in the previous section, which characterize forgetting in terms of representational dynamics and gradient interference geometry, this section examines the complementary body of work that seeks to establish rigorous theoretical guarantees for continual learning algorithms. Together, these frameworks form a comprehensive theoretical foundation for understanding the fundamental capabilities and limitations of sequential learners, drawing from PAC learning theory, information theory, and optimization theory.

### PAC-Learning Analyses and Memory Bounds

One of the most foundational theoretical results in continual learning concerns the necessary memory requirements for any algorithm that hopes to avoid catastrophic forgetting. A landmark complexity-theoretic study [12] establishes, through novel uses of communication complexity arguments, that any continual learner—even an improper one—requires memory that grows linearly with the number of tasks $k$. This result strongly suggests the fundamental intractability of continual learning without sufficient memory, as no algorithm can circumvent this lower bound while maintaining performance across all previously seen tasks. Importantly, the same work demonstrates that when logarithmically many passes over the learning tasks are permitted, an algorithm based on the multiplicative weights update framework achieves memory requirements that scale favorably, though this comes at the cost of requiring improper learning. These results carry profound implications for practical algorithm design: they formally justify the necessity of replay buffers, parameter isolation, or other memory-augmenting mechanisms in real-world continual learning systems.

### Generalization Error Bounds and Task Similarity

Beyond memory lower bounds, theoretical frameworks have been developed to characterize how generalization error evolves across a sequence of tasks and how it is influenced by the structural relationships between tasks. A key insight emerging from these analyses is that both task similarity and task ordering jointly determine the degree of forgetting and the quality of forward transfer. Specifically, when consecutive tasks share substantial representational structure, gradient updates for new tasks are less likely to destructively interfere with parameters encoding prior knowledge, leading to reduced forgetting and potentially improved generalization on both old and new tasks—a finding that directly complements the NTK overlap analyses discussed in Section 3.2.

Analyses grounded in the study of stability and plasticity trade-offs formalize these intuitions [29]. Theoretical investigations of forgetting under overparameterized linear models have proven particularly tractable, revealing how the geometry of task gradients and the curvature of the loss landscape together determine the magnitude of interference between sequentially learned tasks [157]. In the linear setting, it can be shown that projecting gradient updates onto the null space of previous task feature representations eliminates interference entirely, while the dimensionality of this null space determines the available plasticity for learning new tasks [158; 159]. These theoretical insights have directly inspired practical algorithms that seek to maximize the null space dimension available for new task learning, establishing a principled bridge between theory and method design, and providing formal underpinning for the gradient projection methods analyzed through the NTK lens in Section 3.2.

Error bounds for forward and backward knowledge transfer have also received theoretical attention. Forward transfer bounds characterize how much learning a prior task reduces the sample complexity of a subsequent related task, while backward transfer bounds quantify the conditions under which revisiting or replaying earlier tasks can improve performance on them. The existence of such bounds provides theoretical justification for the widely observed empirical phenomenon that related task sequences lead to better overall continual learning performance than unrelated or adversarially ordered sequences. These formal characterizations of transfer will serve as the foundation for the more detailed treatment of knowledge transfer dynamics in Section 3.4.

### Unified Frameworks Bridging Theory and Practice

Several unified theoretical frameworks have been proposed to provide a more complete account of continual learning behavior. The notion of an Ideal Continual Learner serves as a theoretical reference point against which practical algorithms can be evaluated, characterizing the performance achievable by a system with perfect memory and unlimited computation [1]. Such idealized analyses clarify that practical algorithms must navigate fundamental trade-offs between memory efficiency, computational cost, and generalization performance, and that no single method can simultaneously optimize all three dimensions.

Theoretical perspectives inspired by the stability-plasticity dilemma also illuminate the interplay between regularization strength and generalization [160; 37]. Regularization-based methods can be analyzed through the lens of Bayesian inference, where the posterior over model parameters after observing a task sequence provides a principled foundation for deriving forgetting bounds. In this Bayesian framework, the Fisher information matrix plays a central role as an approximation to the local curvature of the posterior, and regularization methods that penalize deviations from prior task optima can be seen as enforcing an approximate posterior update [29]. This Bayesian view connects naturally to the information-theoretic characterizations of representational stability discussed in Section 3.2, where the preservation of task-relevant information in deeper layers can be understood as maintaining fidelity to the posterior over task-specific representations.

From an optimization perspective, theoretical analyses of gradient-based continual learning reveal that the stability gap—a phenomenon wherein performance on old tasks temporarily degrades sharply before recovering during the learning of new tasks—arises from the greedy nature of gradient descent and the non-stationary nature of the effective loss landscape [35]. Such analyses underscore the importance of evaluating continual learning algorithms not only at task boundaries but throughout the learning process, motivating the development of per-iteration evaluation protocols.

### Spurious Features and Distribution Shift

Theoretical investigations have also examined how spurious correlations in sequential data streams contribute to generalization degradation in continual learners. When models learn features that are correlated with labels in early tasks but not in later ones, the resulting feature representations become miscalibrated under distribution shift, leading to degraded performance even in the absence of explicit parameter overwriting. This line of theoretical inquiry connects continual learning to the broader literature on domain generalization and out-of-distribution robustness, suggesting that building continual learners that are robust to spurious feature selection requires not only forgetting prevention mechanisms but also principled feature learning objectives [1]. This concern for spurious feature dynamics extends naturally into Section 3.4, which examines how such features interact with the broader mechanisms of knowledge transfer and task correlation to determine whether forward and backward transfer are positive or detrimental.

Collectively, these theoretical frameworks—spanning PAC-learning memory bounds, generalization error analyses, unified stability-plasticity treatments, and spurious feature dynamics—provide a rigorous foundation for understanding the fundamental challenges of continual learning, quantifying the costs of forgetting and the benefits of transfer, and guiding the design of algorithms with provable properties. However, gaps remain between the idealized settings amenable to theoretical analysis and the complex, high-dimensional non-stationary environments encountered in practice, motivating continued theoretical work on more realistic models of continual learning. The following section builds directly on these foundations by examining the formal conditions governing knowledge transfer and the cumulative error dynamics that arise in long task sequences.

### 3.4 Formal Study of Knowledge Transfer and Forgetting

## 3.4 Knowledge Transfer, Cumulative Error, and Learnability in Continual Learning

Building directly on the generalization bounds and unified theoretical frameworks established in Section 3.3, this section examines the formal conditions governing knowledge transfer between tasks, the cumulative error dynamics that arise in long task sequences, and the fundamental learnability properties of continual learning problems. While Section 3.3 addressed the broad question of what performance guarantees are achievable by continual learning algorithms, the focus here narrows to the mechanistic question of when and why knowledge is transferred productively between tasks—or destructively interferes with previously acquired competencies. These theoretical foundations will in turn provide the conceptual grounding for the evaluation metrics discussed in Section 3.5, particularly the formal definitions of forward and backward transfer that underpin the empirical metrics used to assess algorithm behavior.

### Forward and Backward Transfer: Definitions and Conditions

Forward transfer refers to the positive influence that knowledge accumulated from past tasks exerts on the learning of future tasks, while backward transfer describes the effect that learning new tasks has on the performance of previously learned ones. In ideal continual learning systems, both forms of transfer should be positive, contributing to a compounding accumulation of knowledge. Theoretical analyses have sought to characterize the conditions under which such transfer occurs, examining how task similarity, parameter sharing structure, and gradient alignment jointly determine whether a model update helps or harms prior task performance.

Gradient projection methods provide one of the clearest analytical frameworks for studying backward transfer. By projecting gradient updates for new tasks onto subspaces that are approximately orthogonal to the gradients of old tasks, these methods seek to ensure that new learning does not disrupt previously acquired knowledge. Theoretical analyses of gradient projection reveal that positive backward transfer can occur when the gradient directions of new tasks are aligned with directions that also reduce the loss on old tasks—a condition closely related to the notion of task relatedness in the feature space. The degree of alignment, and therefore the feasibility of simultaneous improvement across tasks, depends critically on the intrinsic dimensionality of the gradient spaces associated with each task and the degree of overlap between these spaces. This analysis extends the NTK-based perspective on gradient interference introduced in Section 3.2, now providing conditions not merely for avoiding forgetting but for achieving actively beneficial backward transfer.

### Generalization and Cumulative Error

A central concern in theoretical treatments of continual learning is bounding the cumulative error that accrues as a model learns an increasing number of tasks. As the task sequence grows longer, error can compound through two mechanisms: forgetting of old tasks and impaired learning of new ones due to interference. Theoretical frameworks drawing from statistical learning theory establish bounds on this cumulative error as a function of task similarity, the capacity of the shared model, and the length of the task sequence. These results generally show that without any form of knowledge consolidation or selective transfer, the cumulative error grows with the number of tasks, underscoring the necessity of principled methods for managing task interference—and providing formal support for the memory lower bounds established in Section 3.3.

The role of weight sharing is particularly important in determining optimal strategies for managing cumulative error. Theoretical analyses of weight sharing decisions reveal a fundamental trade-off: sharing parameters across tasks reduces model complexity and can promote positive transfer when tasks are similar, but increases the risk of destructive interference when tasks are dissimilar. This motivates architecture-based and parameter isolation approaches [161] that seek to allocate shared and task-specific capacity in a principled manner. More recent theoretical work has explored how the geometry of the loss landscape—particularly the flatness of local minima—affects the degree of forgetting, with flatter minima corresponding to solutions that are more robust to the parameter updates induced by subsequent task learning [162]. These geometric considerations complement the null space analyses of Section 3.3, connecting loss landscape curvature to the broader question of how cumulative error can be controlled across long task sequences.

### Task Correlation, Complexity, and Optimal Sharing

The relationship between task correlation and optimal weight sharing is a recurrent theme in the theoretical continual learning literature. When tasks share underlying generative structure—for instance, when they are drawn from related domains or require similar feature representations—sharing parameters can yield positive transfer and reduce the total amount of data required for learning. Conversely, when tasks are drawn from dissimilar distributions, forced sharing may degrade performance on all tasks. Theoretical studies have formalized this intuition by analyzing how the inter-task correlation matrix, often estimated via the Fisher information or the Neural Tangent Kernel, determines the expected degree of forgetting and transfer. These analyses provide guidance for regularization-based methods such as Elastic Weight Consolidation, which use the Fisher information to identify parameters that are important for old tasks and should therefore be protected from large updates—connecting this line of work to the Bayesian regularization perspective discussed in Section 3.3.

Theoretical treatments of task complexity also inform understanding of forgetting dynamics. More complex tasks, which require a larger fraction of the model's representational capacity, tend to produce stronger interference with other tasks when parameters are shared. This suggests that the sequencing and relative complexity of tasks in the curriculum can substantially affect forgetting rates, an observation that has motivated empirical studies of curriculum design in continual learning [57]. Formal analyses of how task ordering interacts with forgetting and transfer remain an active area of research, with some results indicating that presenting tasks in order of increasing complexity can reduce cumulative forgetting by allowing the model to build progressively richer representations.

### Spurious Feature Selection and Performance Degradation

An important and relatively underexplored theoretical dimension of continual learning concerns the role of spurious features—correlations present in the training data of individual tasks that do not reflect stable underlying structure—in contributing to performance degradation over task sequences. When a model is trained on a new task whose spurious correlations conflict with those of previous tasks, the resulting parameter updates can selectively reinforce features that are unreliable for old tasks, leading to degraded performance even in the absence of direct parameter overwriting. Theoretical analyses of this phenomenon draw on tools from statistical learning theory and causal inference, characterizing conditions under which spurious feature selection is more or less likely to occur as a function of task distribution overlap and the degree of regularization applied during training. This perspective extends the discussion of spurious features introduced in Section 3.3 by providing a more mechanistic account of how such features interact with transfer dynamics across task sequences.

The stability-plasticity dilemma [162] is reflected formally in these analyses: excessive regularization to preserve old features may prevent the model from learning new stable features, while insufficient regularization allows the overwriting of stable features by spurious ones from new tasks. Bridging these theoretical insights with practical algorithm design remains a key challenge, and recent work has begun to explore how bounds on spurious feature selection can be used to design regularizers that are both theoretically motivated and practically effective. Understanding the formal conditions under which knowledge transfer is positive versus negative, and how these conditions can be systematically promoted or avoided, continues to be one of the most important open theoretical problems in continual learning.

### Theoretical Insights from Learnability Analysis

Beyond error bounds and gradient analyses, theoretical treatments of learnability in continual learning address the fundamental question of whether and when a continual learning problem is solvable in principle. Results in this area demonstrate that class-incremental learning is learnable under specific conditions related to the separability of task distributions and the availability of sufficient model capacity [47]. These findings establish that strong within-task prediction and out-of-distribution detection are jointly necessary conditions for effective class-incremental continual learning, providing a theoretical foundation for hybrid algorithmic designs that combine discriminative and generative components. These learnability conditions complement the PAC-learning memory bounds of Section 3.3, together delineating both the structural requirements and the computational resources necessary for principled continual learning.

Collectively, these theoretical perspectives—spanning gradient projection analyses, cumulative error bounds, weight sharing trade-offs, spurious feature dynamics, and learnability results—form a growing formal foundation for understanding knowledge transfer and forgetting in continual learning. While many results remain asymptotic or idealized, they provide valuable conceptual tools for understanding the behavior of practical algorithms and for identifying the conditions under which those algorithms are expected to succeed or fail. Crucially, the formal definitions of forward and backward transfer developed in this section map directly onto the evaluation metrics discussed in Section 3.5, where the theoretical conditions for positive transfer are assessed empirically through metrics such as BWT, FWT, and knowledge transfer efficiency, providing a principled bridge between theory and experimental evaluation.

### 3.5 Evaluation Metrics for Continual Learning

## 3.5 Evaluation Metrics for Continual Learning

Rigorous evaluation is fundamental to measuring the progress of continual learning algorithms and understanding their behavior across diverse task sequences. The theoretical foundations of knowledge transfer and forgetting—including gradient alignment conditions, cumulative error bounds, and learnability analyses discussed in the preceding section—ultimately require empirical validation, and the quality of that validation depends critically on the metrics used to assess algorithm behavior. Over the years, the community has converged on a set of standard metrics, while also recognizing their limitations and proposing more nuanced alternatives that better reflect the multidimensional objectives of continual learning. These metrics, in turn, directly inform the design of the benchmarks and evaluation protocols discussed in the following section.

### Core Evaluation Metrics

The most widely used evaluation metric in continual learning is **Average Accuracy (AA)**, computed as the mean classification accuracy across all tasks after the model has completed training on the entire task sequence. While straightforward and intuitive, average accuracy alone is insufficient to characterize the richness of continual learning behavior, as it collapses the competing objectives of stability and plasticity into a single scalar and does not reveal how performance on individual tasks evolves over time.

To capture the temporal dynamics of learning, the community has adopted complementary metrics. The **Forgetting Measure (FM)** quantifies how much performance on previously learned tasks degrades over the course of learning new tasks, typically computed as the average difference between the maximum accuracy achieved on each task during training and the accuracy on that task at the end of the entire learning process [1]. A high forgetting measure indicates that the model suffers severely from catastrophic forgetting, whereas a low forgetting measure signals that stability has been maintained.

**Backward Transfer (BWT)** is a closely related metric that measures the influence of learning new tasks on the performance of previously learned tasks [1; 60]. Mathematically, BWT is often defined as the average change in test accuracy on all preceding tasks after their training has concluded. Negative BWT corresponds to forgetting, while positive BWT—sometimes termed backward knowledge transfer—indicates that learning later tasks actually improves performance on earlier ones [61; 163]. Achieving positive BWT is a more ambitious goal that goes beyond merely preventing forgetting, and several works have explicitly targeted this objective [61; 62; 63]. This connection between positive BWT and the gradient alignment conditions analyzed theoretically in the previous section is direct: backward transfer becomes positive precisely when the conditions identified by gradient projection analyses—alignment between new task gradients and directions that reduce old task losses—are satisfied in practice.

**Forward Transfer (FWT)** measures how well knowledge accumulated from previously learned tasks facilitates learning on new, yet-to-be-encountered tasks [1; 60]. Positive forward transfer implies that the representations or parameters shaped by earlier tasks accelerate or improve learning of future tasks, analogous to the acceleration of new task learning observed in human learners with growing domain experience [59]. The relationship between forgetting prevention and forward transfer has been a subject of debate; some studies argue that less forgetful representations yield better forward transfer because they produce more diverse and discriminative feature spaces [58]. Together, BWT and FWT provide a more complete picture of the dual objectives of continual learning—knowledge retention and knowledge accumulation—and their joint optimization is central to building truly capable lifelong learning systems [60; 63].

### Limitations of Standard Metrics

Despite their widespread adoption, the standard metrics face several important limitations. First, average accuracy, forgetting measure, backward transfer, and forward transfer are typically computed at fixed evaluation checkpoints after each task, which may not adequately capture the dynamics of online learning settings where models process data in a single pass without explicit task boundaries [1]. This limitation becomes especially salient in the online and semi-supervised evaluation protocols discussed in the following section, where temporal granularity of measurement is essential.

Second, these metrics generally assume that all tasks are equally difficult and equally sized. In practice, tasks vary substantially in complexity, data volume, and class distribution, meaning that naive averaging can be misleading. A model that performs very well on easy tasks but catastrophically forgets difficult ones may appear to achieve high average accuracy despite poor behavior on the most challenging parts of the sequence [46]. The theoretical treatment of task complexity and its interaction with forgetting dynamics, reviewed in the previous section, underscores why equal weighting of tasks in metric aggregation can obscure meaningful behavioral differences.

Third, the standard metrics do not fully disentangle the stability-plasticity trade-off. Average accuracy aggregates the effects of stability (retention of old task performance) and plasticity (learning of new tasks) into a single number, making it impossible to determine whether a high-accuracy model achieves its score by being highly plastic, highly stable, or some balanced combination of both. This limitation is particularly problematic when comparing methods that make different trade-offs between these two objectives [1; 46].

Fourth, some metrics are sensitive to what has been termed **setup-induced forgetting**: the forgetting that occurs not due to catastrophic interference but simply because earlier tasks were evaluated under more restricted model capacity or less favorable initialization conditions at the time of testing. This conflation between algorithmic forgetting and evaluation-protocol artifacts can lead to misleading comparisons between methods.

Fifth, the standard metrics do not adequately account for the efficiency of learning, such as the number of gradient steps or memory operations required to achieve a given level of performance. A method that achieves high accuracy but at enormous computational cost may be impractical despite strong metric scores [1]. This efficiency dimension is directly addressed by the computationally budgeted evaluation frameworks discussed in the following section.

### Newer Metric Proposals

Recognizing these shortcomings, the community has proposed a variety of supplementary and alternative metrics aimed at providing more nuanced evaluation. Some proposals introduce **intransigence measures**, which quantify a model's inability to learn new tasks effectively due to overly rigid regularization—a complementary failure mode to forgetting that standard metrics tend to ignore. This failure mode is the empirical counterpart to the theoretical observation that excessive regularization to preserve old features can prevent acquisition of new stable features, as discussed in the context of spurious feature dynamics in the previous section.

Other proposals advocate for **unified evaluation frameworks** that simultaneously report metrics capturing stability, plasticity, and generalizability across diverse evaluation scenarios. These frameworks assess not only how well a model retains old tasks and learns new ones, but also how well it generalizes to out-of-distribution data encountered after training [46]. This tripartite evaluation is closely related to the objectives of building AI systems capable of information retrospection, information prospection, and information transfer, as articulated in frameworks for human-level continual intelligence [46].

Metrics that measure **knowledge transfer efficiency** have also been proposed, capturing how much the model benefits from prior tasks relative to a randomly initialized baseline trained only on the new task [60; 63]. Such metrics are particularly important for evaluating the lifelong learning hypothesis that a continual learner should become increasingly competent as the task sequence grows longer [59; 9]. These efficiency-oriented metrics bridge the gap between the theoretical bounds on cumulative error and forward transfer established in the previous section and the practical performance differences observed in benchmark evaluations.

In reinforcement learning and robotics settings, specialized metrics such as **transfer efficiency** and **adaptation speed** have been proposed alongside forgetting measures to capture the unique evaluation demands of non-stationary environment interaction [9]. These metrics acknowledge that in RL settings, what matters is not just final task accuracy but also the rate at which an agent adapts to environmental changes and the degree to which prior experience accelerates this adaptation. The design of domain-specific benchmarks such as Continual World, discussed in the following section, is intimately tied to the development of these specialized metrics.

### Discussion

The diversity of proposed metrics reflects the multidimensional nature of continual learning performance. No single metric is sufficient to fully characterize a continual learning system; instead, reporting a complementary suite—including average accuracy, backward transfer, forward transfer, forgetting measure, and ideally efficiency-related metrics—provides a more complete picture [1; 60]. This multi-metric perspective is essential for connecting the theoretical analyses of knowledge transfer and forgetting, which characterize algorithm behavior in terms of gradient alignment, loss landscape geometry, and cumulative error bounds, to the empirical comparisons that rely on benchmark evaluations. As the field matures, the development of standardized, community-wide evaluation protocols that adopt such comprehensive metric suites remains an important open challenge, with significant implications for fair and reproducible comparison of continual learning algorithms across the diverse benchmarks and task sequences surveyed in the following section.

### 3.6 Benchmarks and Evaluation Protocols

## 3.6 Benchmarks and Evaluation Protocols

Benchmarks and evaluation protocols form the empirical backbone of continual learning research, and their design choices profoundly influence which algorithms appear effective and how progress is measured across the field. The metrics discussed in the preceding section—average accuracy, backward transfer, forward transfer, and forgetting measure—derive their meaning only within the context of well-designed benchmarks that specify the task sequence, distributional shifts, and evaluation conditions. Conversely, the scenario types examined in the following section—task-incremental, domain-incremental, and class-incremental learning—are instantiated concretely through the benchmark datasets and evaluation protocols surveyed here. Understanding the landscape of available benchmarks is therefore essential for interpreting experimental results and situating algorithmic progress within a coherent empirical framework.

**Standard Benchmarks.** The earliest and most widely adopted benchmarks in continual learning are built from well-known image recognition datasets. Split-MNIST divides the MNIST digit classification dataset into sequential binary classification tasks, while Permuted-MNIST creates a sequence of tasks by applying distinct fixed pixel permutations to the original images, producing statistically distinct but structurally similar distributions. Split-CIFAR variants partition CIFAR-10 or CIFAR-100 into subsets of classes presented sequentially, introducing greater visual complexity than MNIST-derived benchmarks. These datasets have enabled rapid prototyping and comparison of continual learning methods across regularization-based, replay-based, and architecture-based families. CORe50, a dedicated benchmark for object recognition in continuous learning settings, provides a more realistic evaluation scenario featuring 50 common household objects captured across 11 different sessions with varying backgrounds and lighting conditions [74]. CORe50 supports multiple continual learning scenarios including class-incremental, domain-incremental, and the challenging new-instances-and-classes setting, making it particularly valuable for assessing real-world robustness. While these standard benchmarks have been instrumental in establishing a common empirical foundation, their relatively small scale and limited distributional diversity have increasingly been recognized as insufficient for capturing the complexity of realistic deployment scenarios.

**Large-Scale and Domain-Diverse Benchmarks.** As the field has matured, researchers have recognized that small-scale benchmarks fail to capture the complexity of realistic deployment scenarios. ImageNet-scale evaluations, including Split-ImageNet and the Multi-domain Task Incremental Learning (MTIL) benchmark, stress-test continual learning methods across tasks drawn from substantially different visual domains rather than class-separated subsets of a single dataset [76]. Large-scale benchmarks such as ImageNet2K and Continual Google Landmarks V2 (CGLM) have been introduced to evaluate continual learning under data-incremental, class-incremental, and time-incremental settings with thousands of GPU-hours of computation, revealing that most existing methods fail to outperform simple uniform memory sampling baselines under realistic computational constraints [68]. Similarly, the CLOC benchmark containing 39 million time-stamped geolocation images enables real-time continual learning evaluation where the data stream does not pause during training, exposing fundamental limitations of methods designed without computational constraints in mind [69]. These large-scale evaluations directly operationalize the efficiency-oriented metrics discussed in the preceding section, demonstrating that computational budget is an indispensable dimension of benchmark design.

**Curriculum and Multi-Dataset Benchmarks.** A significant limitation of early benchmarks is their implicit assumption that all tasks have equal complexity and that task ordering is arbitrary. To address this, curriculum-structured benchmarks have been introduced that present tasks in orders of increasing or decreasing difficulty, evaluating whether continual learning algorithms can exploit structured task sequences [57]. These benchmarks combine multiple heterogeneous datasets spanning six image collections with varying levels of complexity and quality, providing a more rigorous evaluation of generalization and forgetting under realistic curriculum conditions. The Stream-51 and related multi-domain benchmarks extend this philosophy by incorporating tasks from 71 diverse image classification datasets spanning heterogeneous domains and difficulties, demonstrating that most continual learning approaches fail to maintain performance across long task sequences [164]. Such curriculum-structured and multi-dataset protocols also provide richer ground for measuring forward transfer, since the structured ordering of tasks creates conditions where prior knowledge may systematically accelerate or impede learning of subsequent tasks.

**Robotics and Reinforcement Learning Benchmarks.** Beyond visual recognition, domain-specific benchmarks have been developed to evaluate continual learning in embodied settings. Continual World provides a robotic manipulation benchmark built on top of Meta-World, consisting of ten realistic and meaningfully diverse robotic tasks designed to evaluate forward transfer, backward transfer, and catastrophic forgetting in continual reinforcement learning agents [9]. Unlike classification benchmarks, Continual World explicitly measures the tension between preventing forgetting and achieving positive forward transfer, highlighting unique algorithmic challenges in the reinforcement learning setting that are absent from supervised continual learning evaluations. The specialized metrics of transfer efficiency and adaptation speed discussed in the preceding section find their primary empirical instantiation in benchmarks of this kind, underscoring the tight coupling between metric design and benchmark construction.

**Online and Semi-Supervised Evaluation Protocols.** Standard offline evaluation protocols, where models are evaluated only after completing training on each task, fail to capture the temporal dynamics of real-world learning. Online continual learning benchmarks address this by requiring models to make predictions before observing each incoming data point, then immediately updating on it as a single continuous stream without explicit task boundaries [7]. A novel class-incremental blurry task configuration further relaxes the assumption of sharp task boundaries, introducing a more practical setting where task transitions are gradual and inference queries may arrive at any moment during training [53]. In the semi-supervised domain, benchmarks incorporating sparse label rates with constrained computational budgets on datasets such as CLOC and ImageNet10K reveal that existing continual learning methods are ill-equipped for the combined challenge of label scarcity and computational restriction [165]. These online and semi-supervised protocols are particularly relevant for assessing the limitations of standard metrics identified in the preceding section, since temporal granularity of measurement and computational cost become first-class evaluation concerns rather than afterthoughts.

**Sensor-Based and Sequential Data Benchmarks.** Continual learning evaluation has also expanded to non-visual sequential data domains. Benchmarks based on human activity recognition using wearable sensor data test task-free, data-incremental continual learning frameworks that must handle naturally streaming sensor signals without explicit task boundaries [81]. New benchmarks for recurrent neural networks on sequential NLP and time-series data have been proposed to address the fragmented evaluation landscape for continual learning with sequential architectures, revealing that sequence length and scenario specification critically affect forgetting dynamics [166]. These non-visual benchmarks are important for establishing the generality of continual learning algorithms beyond image classification, and they raise distinct challenges for the scenario definitions discussed in the following section, where assumptions about task structure and boundary availability may not transfer straightforwardly from the visual domain.

**Benchmark Design Considerations.** A recurring theme across the continual learning literature is that benchmark design choices critically shape which methods appear competitive. Key design dimensions include the number of tasks in the sequence, task size and class distribution, the presence or absence of task boundary information, the type of distributional shift (class-incremental versus domain-incremental versus data-incremental), computational and memory budgets, whether evaluation is online or offline, and the diversity of domains represented. Benchmarks that impose no computational constraints tend to favor methods with expensive rehearsal mechanisms, while compute-constrained evaluations expose that simpler baselines often dominate [68]. Similarly, the choice between task-incremental and class-incremental scenarios dramatically affects relative method rankings, since class-incremental settings without task identity information at test time are substantially harder and favor different algorithmic families—a distinction elaborated in detail in the following section. Prompt-based methods leveraging pre-trained vision transformers have introduced new benchmark standards including Split ImageNet-R to better assess rehearsal-free continual learning with strong pre-trained representations [167]. The development of flexible data loading frameworks such as Continuum has been instrumental in standardizing scenario construction and reducing implementation errors that previously made benchmark comparisons unreliable [168]. Together, these considerations underscore the need for community-wide standardization of evaluation protocols that reflect the computational, distributional, and temporal realities of real-world continual learning deployment—a need whose methodological implications for scenario selection are taken up directly in the following section.

### 3.7 Continual Learning Scenarios in Evaluation Protocols

## 3.7 Continual Learning Scenarios in Evaluation Protocols

Building upon the benchmarks and evaluation frameworks discussed in the preceding sections, the choice of evaluation scenario represents one of the most consequential design decisions in continual learning research. It determines which algorithmic families are applicable, how difficulty is calibrated, and what assumptions are made about the information available to the learner at training and inference time. A careful understanding of the major scenarios and their implications is therefore essential for interpreting experimental results and ensuring fair algorithmic comparison—and forms a natural complement to the software infrastructure and benchmark datasets described above.

### Major Scenario Types

The continual learning literature has converged on three canonical scenario types, each corresponding to a distinct set of assumptions about task identity information and output space structure. In **task-incremental learning (Task-IL)**, the model is provided with explicit task identity at both training and inference time, enabling it to select task-specific output heads. This scenario is generally the least challenging, as task identity resolves most of the ambiguity in prediction. In **domain-incremental learning (Domain-IL)**, tasks share the same output space but differ in their input distributions; task identity is available during training but not inference, requiring the model to handle domain shift without task labels at test time. In **class-incremental learning (Class-IL)**, neither the task identity nor the boundaries between classes are provided at inference, and the model must discriminate across all classes encountered over the entire learning sequence. Class-IL is widely recognized as the most difficult scenario and serves as the primary test-bed for evaluating whether a method truly overcomes catastrophic forgetting [1; 46].

### Impact of Scenario Choice on Algorithmic Families

The scenario fundamentally shapes which methodological families are competitive. Regularization-based methods such as Elastic Weight Consolidation and Synaptic Intelligence perform reasonably in Task-IL and Domain-IL settings but frequently struggle in Class-IL, where the absence of task identity at inference imposes strict demands on discriminative power across all seen categories [1]. Architecture-based and parameter isolation approaches, which maintain task-specific subnetworks or heads, are natively suited to Task-IL but require special adaptations for Class-IL where task identity is unavailable at test time [41]. Replay-based methods tend to be the most broadly applicable across scenarios, as rehearsing past experiences provides the model with sufficient information to maintain discriminative boundaries regardless of whether task labels are provided [82; 86].

Hybrid approaches that combine multiple mechanisms often exhibit stronger robustness across all three canonical scenarios. For example, methods inspired by complementary learning systems theory and synaptic consolidation [86; 83] have demonstrated competitive performance in general continual learning settings that do not presuppose scenario-specific privileges such as task boundary access. This observation aligns closely with the motivation behind comprehensive benchmark suites discussed previously, which deliberately span multiple scenario types to avoid rewarding methods that are narrowly optimized for a single setting.

### Online Versus Offline Evaluation Settings

Beyond the three canonical scenarios, a further axis of variation concerns whether the evaluation protocol assumes an **online** or **offline** (multi-epoch) learning regime. In offline evaluation, the model may perform multiple passes over the data from each task before moving to the next, enabling more thorough optimization. In online evaluation, each data point is seen at most once, placing much more stringent demands on sample efficiency and the ability to extract information rapidly from a single pass [169]. Online settings are more demanding and arguably more ecologically valid, as they reflect the conditions under which deployed systems must operate in non-stationary environments. Several recent works have explicitly adopted online evaluation protocols to stress-test methods and to ensure that improvements cannot be attributed simply to additional training time [100; 82]. This distinction is particularly relevant given the large-scale streaming benchmarks discussed earlier, such as CLOC and real-time continual learning evaluations, which impose precisely these online constraints.

### Task Boundary Availability and Its Methodological Implications

The availability of **task boundary information** is another critical axis that shapes evaluation methodology. Many classical continual learning methods assume that explicit, sharp boundaries between tasks are observable, enabling the model to reset optimizers, update importance weights, or trigger memory consolidation procedures at well-defined transition points. However, this assumption is frequently unrealistic in real-world deployments where data distributions shift gradually and continuously without clearly demarcated episodes.

Methods designed for **general continual learning** relax the task boundary assumption entirely, requiring that the algorithm function without oracle knowledge of when one task ends and another begins, and without task identity labels at inference time [82; 86]. This general setting is substantially harder than task-boundary-aware protocols, and methods that perform well in it must rely on more flexible internal mechanisms such as self-regulated memory consolidation or adaptive parameter regularization [85; 109].

Bio-inspired approaches that use local learning rules or activity-based regularization also explicitly target task-free operation, avoiding the requirement for discrete task signals altogether [43; 44]. The absence of task boundary information complicates not only the algorithm design but also evaluation, since standard metrics that compute per-task accuracy require ground-truth task assignments to attribute test samples to their corresponding tasks—a challenge that the software frameworks discussed in the following section must also accommodate.

### Blurry and Probabilistic Task Boundaries

An emerging extension of standard evaluation protocols involves **blurry task boundaries**, where transitions between data distributions are gradual rather than abrupt. In this setting, data from different tasks or domains can co-occur during a transition period, producing a more realistic non-stationary stream. Evaluation under blurry boundaries has been advocated as a way to move beyond artificial benchmark constructs toward more faithful representations of real-world data streams [94; 169]. Methods that perform well under sharp boundaries may degrade when boundaries are blurred if they rely on precise change-point detection or task-triggered consolidation procedures. This consideration directly motivates the class-incremental blurry task configuration highlighted in prior benchmark discussions, underscoring the tight connection between scenario design choices and benchmark construction.

### Synthesis

In sum, the choice of continual learning scenario—along the axes of task identity availability, class-incremental difficulty, online versus offline learning, and task boundary sharpness—constitutes a critical design decision that profoundly affects which methods appear competitive and what conclusions can be drawn from experimental comparisons. A rigorous evaluation methodology should explicitly specify the scenario type and boundary assumptions, and ideally assess methods across multiple scenarios to characterize their robustness and generality [107; 46; 13]. This imperative for multi-scenario assessment reinforces the value of flexible software frameworks such as Continuum and Avalanche, discussed in the following section, which are specifically designed to support standardized scenario construction and reproducible cross-scenario comparison. The field would benefit from greater standardization of scenario definitions and clearer reporting of the exact evaluation protocol used, enabling more reliable cross-paper comparison and accelerating progress toward truly general continual learning systems.

### 3.8 Software Frameworks for Continual Learning Research

The rapid growth of continual learning research has necessitated the development of dedicated software frameworks that standardize experimental protocols, reduce implementation overhead, and promote reproducibility across the community. Without such infrastructure, comparing methods across papers becomes unreliable due to inconsistencies in data preprocessing, evaluation metrics, and scenario definitions—a fragmentation problem that has long hindered objective progress assessment in the field. As established in the preceding discussion of evaluation scenarios, the diversity of task identity assumptions, boundary conditions, and learning regimes makes it especially important that software tooling support consistent and transparent experimental construction.

**Continuum** is one prominent example of a flexible and modular data management framework designed specifically for continual learning [168]. Its core contribution lies in providing carefully engineered data loaders that simulate a wide variety of non-i.i.d. scenarios, including task-incremental, class-incremental, and domain-incremental settings. The framework emphasizes reproducibility by standardizing data ordering, preprocessing pipelines, and test set construction—factors whose subtle mishandling can significantly distort algorithm comparisons. Continuum is designed to be easily extensible, allowing researchers to define novel continual learning scenarios for specific application needs without rebuilding data infrastructure from scratch [168]. By separating scenario management from model implementation, it enables researchers to focus their efforts on algorithmic innovation rather than engineering bookkeeping. Crucially, this separation also makes it straightforward to evaluate the same method across multiple scenario types—precisely the kind of cross-scenario assessment advocated in the preceding section as essential for characterizing algorithmic robustness and generality.

**Avalanche** represents a more comprehensive end-to-end continual learning library that integrates data loading, model training, evaluation metrics, and logging into a unified pipeline. Avalanche supports a broad range of continual learning scenarios and provides implementations of many established baseline methods, enabling fair side-by-side comparisons. Its plugin-based architecture allows users to extend the framework with new strategies, benchmarks, and metrics, making it suitable for both research prototyping and systematic empirical evaluation. The framework's design philosophy emphasizes modularity and reusability, which facilitates the kind of rigorous and reproducible evaluation that the field increasingly demands [57].

**Sequoia** takes a different but complementary approach by framing the continual learning landscape as a unified taxonomy of settings, ranging from standard supervised learning and multi-task learning through to continual and reinforcement learning. By making the relationships between these settings explicit and hierarchical, Sequoia allows methods to be evaluated across multiple points on the continual learning spectrum within a single framework. This taxonomy-based design promotes generalization of algorithms and highlights which components of a method are specific to certain assumptions, such as known task boundaries or access to task identities at test time—distinctions that, as discussed in the preceding section, carry profound implications for algorithmic applicability and experimental fairness.

**BeGin** extends the standardization effort to graph-based continual learning, addressing a domain that standard vision-oriented frameworks do not adequately support. Graph continual learning poses unique challenges due to the non-Euclidean structure of data and the difficulty of defining meaningful task splits on graph datasets. BeGin provides benchmarks and evaluation protocols tailored to node-level, edge-level, and graph-level continual learning tasks, filling an important gap in the software ecosystem.

Collectively, these frameworks address a critical need identified across the continual learning literature: the absence of standardized evaluation makes it difficult to objectively assess strategies and determine genuine progress [57] [4]. By offering consistent implementations of benchmarks, metrics such as average accuracy, backward transfer, and forward transfer, and support for diverse continual learning scenarios, these tools lower the barrier to entry for new researchers while enabling experienced practitioners to conduct fair and comprehensive comparisons. Their value is further amplified by the scenario diversity discussed in the preceding section: only with shared, well-defined infrastructure can the community reliably determine whether performance gains reflect genuine algorithmic advances or merely favorable scenario assumptions. Future standardization efforts will benefit from expanding these frameworks to cover emerging settings such as online continual learning, federated continual learning, and continual reinforcement learning, further bridging the gap between controlled academic benchmarks and the demands of real-world deployment.

## 4 Continual Learning in Computer Vision

### 4.1 Overview and Taxonomy of Continual Learning in Computer Vision

## 4.1 Overview and Taxonomy of Continual Learning in Computer Vision

Computer vision represents one of the most active and consequential application domains for continual learning research. Visual data introduces a distinct set of challenges that go beyond those encountered in simpler tabular or low-dimensional settings. Images and video frames are inherently high-dimensional, exhibiting rich spatial structure, hierarchical compositional patterns, and temporal dynamics that must be preserved and leveraged throughout sequential learning. When a model incrementally learns new visual concepts, categories, or domains, the shared convolutional or transformer-based feature representations are particularly susceptible to catastrophic forgetting, as subtle distributional shifts in visual statistics can rapidly overwrite previously encoded spatial and semantic features [2; 5].

The scale and diversity of real-world visual environments further compound these difficulties. Unlike controlled laboratory benchmarks, practical computer vision systems must contend with varying lighting conditions, viewpoints, occlusions, sensor modalities, and temporal evolution in scene content. These factors collectively drive non-stationarity in the data distribution, motivating the need for robust continual learning strategies specifically tailored to visual recognition problems [7].

### Taxonomy of Continual Learning Settings in Computer Vision

To systematically organize the landscape of continual learning in computer vision, it is useful to consider both the type of visual task and the learning scenario in which incremental learning occurs. The intersection of these two dimensions gives rise to a rich taxonomy that structures the remainder of this section.

**By Learning Scenario.** Following the general continual learning literature, computer vision methods can be categorized according to whether task identity is provided at test time (task-incremental learning), whether the model must discriminate across all classes encountered so far without task labels (class-incremental learning), or whether the input domain shifts while the output space remains fixed (domain-incremental learning) [2; 51]. More recently, cross-domain incremental learning has emerged as a variant explicitly addressing scenarios where tasks originate from fundamentally different visual domains, adding further complexity due to large inter-domain feature misalignment [51]. As discussed in detail in Section 4.2, the class-incremental setting is widely regarded as the most demanding, since models must distinguish between all previously and newly encountered classes without access to task identity at inference time.

**By Visual Task Type.** The nature of the visual recognition problem fundamentally shapes the continual learning challenges encountered:

- *Image Classification* is the most extensively studied setting, where models must sequentially learn to categorize images into an expanding or shifting set of classes. Benchmarks such as Split-CIFAR, CORe50, and Split-ImageNet have served as standard evaluation grounds [5; 11].
