The breadth of methods developed for this setting — spanning regularization, replay, architecture isolation, and prompt-based adaptation — makes it the natural starting point for surveying continual learning in computer vision, and it is treated in depth in the following subsection.

- *Object Detection* introduces additional complexity beyond classification, as models must localize and identify multiple objects simultaneously. Incremental learning of new object categories is particularly challenging due to the background shift problem and missing annotations for previously learned classes.

- *Semantic and Panoptic Segmentation* extends the challenges of detection to dense pixel-level prediction, where the background class undergoes systematic semantic drift as new classes are introduced, severely disrupting the learning of stable visual boundaries [170].

- *Video Understanding* presents unique temporal challenges, as models must maintain consistency across frames while adapting to evolving activity distributions. The sequential nature of video data introduces additional forgetting pathways through temporal dependencies.

- *3D Point Cloud Processing* addresses sparse and irregular geometric data from sensors such as LiDAR, requiring continual learning methods that account for uneven spatial sampling and geometric transformations in addition to standard distributional shift.

**By Data Availability and Stream Structure.** A further dimension concerns whether the model operates in an offline setting where all data from a task is available simultaneously, or an online setting where data arrives as a single continuous stream without explicit task boundaries [7]. The online setting is particularly challenging as it requires both forgetting prevention and efficient single-pass learning.

### Connecting the Taxonomy to Subsequent Subsections

This multi-dimensional taxonomy reflects the breadth and heterogeneity of continual learning research in computer vision. Methods developed for one corner of this taxonomy — for instance, replay-based approaches for class-incremental image classification — do not always transfer seamlessly to other settings such as online domain-incremental segmentation, highlighting the importance of clearly situating methods within this framework [170; 5]. The general principles of regularization, replay, architecture isolation, and prompt-based adaptation that underpin the broader continual learning literature manifest in distinct forms across each visual task type, adapted to address their unique representational and distributional demands. The following subsections survey methods and benchmarks along each of these task-specific axes in turn, beginning with image classification — the most mature and extensively benchmarked domain — before progressing to object detection, segmentation, video understanding, and beyond.

### 4.2 Continual Learning for Image Classification

## 4.2 Continual Learning for Image Classification

Image classification represents the most extensively studied application domain in continual learning research, serving as the primary testbed for evaluating new algorithms and theoretical frameworks. As established in the taxonomy of Section 4.1, this setting spans task-incremental, class-incremental, and domain-incremental scenarios, with the class-incremental variant widely regarded as the most demanding due to the absence of task identity at inference time. The core challenge is enabling a model to sequentially learn to classify images from new categories or domains without degrading performance on previously learned ones — a challenge fundamentally rooted in catastrophic forgetting [15]. The maturity of this domain makes it the natural foundation for the survey of continual learning in computer vision: the four principal families of solutions — regularization, replay, architecture isolation, and prompt-based adaptation — are most fully developed here before being carried forward, in adapted forms, into the object detection and segmentation settings examined in subsequent subsections.

### Benchmarks and Evaluation Protocols

Several standard benchmarks have become canonical in the continual image classification literature. **Split-MNIST** and **Permuted-MNIST** divide the MNIST digit recognition task into sequential sub-problems, either by splitting classes or by applying random pixel permutations, providing controlled low-complexity environments for initial method validation. **Split-CIFAR-10** and **Split-CIFAR-100** extend this paradigm to more challenging visual recognition problems by partitioning the CIFAR datasets into disjoint class subsets presented sequentially. **Split-ImageNet** pushes evaluation to large-scale, high-resolution visual recognition, revealing scalability challenges that smaller benchmarks obscure [19]. More realistic temporal benchmarks such as **CORe50**, which captures object recognition from continuous egocentric video streams, and **CLEAR**, which encodes natural distribution shift over time derived from real-world internet imagery, have been introduced to better reflect non-stationary conditions in practical deployments — conditions that become even more pronounced in the video understanding and autonomous driving benchmarks discussed in later subsections.

Benchmark design choices critically influence algorithm rankings. Studies have shown that performance comparisons across methods are highly sensitive to dataset choice and the specific continual learning scenario — task-incremental, class-incremental, or domain-incremental — employed in evaluation [19]. The class-incremental setting is widely recognized as the most challenging for image classification, as models must distinguish between classes from all tasks without access to task identity at inference time [171].

### Regularization-Based Approaches

Regularization methods protect previously learned visual representations by constraining parameter updates during new task training. Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC) and its variants anchor important parameters using importance weights estimated from Fisher information, a strategy empirically validated on sequential image classification tasks. Incremental Moment Matching (IMM) [139] extends this principle by merging task-specific posterior distributions over network parameters, finding that combining weight transfer and moment-based merging achieves competitive performance on MNIST and CIFAR-style benchmarks. The Hard Attention to the Task (HAT) mechanism [16] learns binary attention masks per task through gradient descent, preserving task-specific parameters and demonstrating up to 80% reduction in catastrophic forgetting rates on standard image classification benchmarks. More recent methods such as the **Create and Find Flatness (C&F)** framework [142] address forgetting by proactively constructing flat loss landscape regions during current task learning, which reduces interference when adapting to future tasks while preserving past knowledge.

Work analyzing the geometry of the loss landscape has revealed that wider minima correspond to greater forgetting robustness, and that training regimes — including learning rate scheduling, batch size selection, and dropout — play an equally critical role as algorithmic choices in determining the degree of forgetting [25]. Pre-trained models have been shown to inherently produce wider loss minima, which partially explains their empirical advantage in continual image classification settings [141]. These geometric insights carry direct implications for the distillation-based methods that dominate continual object detection, where preserving the loss landscape structure of previously learned detection heads is equally critical.

### Replay-Based Approaches

Experience replay methods maintain a memory buffer of previously seen images and interleave them during new task training. The fundamental empirical insight driving this line of work is that rehearsing small representative subsets of past data can substantially mitigate forgetting without full data retention [129]. Gradient-based approaches such as Gradient Episodic Memory (GEM) and its successor A-GEM enforce inequality constraints on gradients to prevent performance degradation on previous tasks, with A-GEM combined with soft constraints [172] offering improved trade-offs between stability and plasticity at lower computational cost. An alternative to storing raw images is **generative replay**, in which a generative model is trained alongside the classifier and used to synthesize pseudo-exemplars of past classes, avoiding the need to retain original training data.

Studies of example-level forgetting dynamics within single-task training [22] have informed smarter buffer population strategies, demonstrating that certain examples are disproportionately prone to transitioning from correctly to incorrectly classified during training, suggesting their prioritization in replay buffers. Generating entirely synthetic data optimized to prevent catastrophic forgetting through meta-gradient optimization [173] has also been explored on Split-MNIST, yielding promising results without requiring storage of original training examples.

Research investigating **representation forgetting** — changes in the model's internal representations measured via optimal linear probing performance rather than task accuracy — has offered a more nuanced view of replay efficacy [156]. These studies demonstrate that representations trained without explicit forgetting control often undergo smaller representation-level forgetting than task performance metrics alone suggest, and that simple supervised contrastive learning with prototype-based classification provides a competitive replay-free baseline on longer task sequences. The core replay principles developed here — intelligent buffer management, annotation-aware example selection, and generative synthesis — are subsequently adapted in the continual object detection literature to accommodate the additional complexity of bounding box annotations and multi-object scenes.

### Architecture-Based and Parameter Isolation Approaches

Architecture-based methods allocate separate or partially shared parameter subsets to different tasks, inherently bounding interference between tasks. The HAT mechanism [16] exemplifies this strategy, using hard binary masks to isolate task-relevant parameters while still enabling controlled sharing for knowledge transfer. **Elephant activation functions** [24] constitute a novel architectural innovation targeting forgetting at its source through activation functions that produce simultaneously sparse representations and sparse gradients. Experimental results confirm that replacing standard activation functions with elephant activations significantly reduces catastrophic forgetting across class-incremental and task-incremental image classification settings, even in single-pass learning without replay or task boundary information.

The role of network width in continual learning has received dedicated empirical and theoretical study [23], revealing that wider networks exhibit substantially reduced forgetting due to increased gradient orthogonality between tasks, sparser task-specific representations, and tendency toward lazy training regimes where parameter changes are smaller. These findings have direct practical implications for architecture selection in continual image classification pipelines, and anticipate the gating and parameter isolation mechanisms that are adapted for continual object detection in Section 4.3.

Theoretical analysis of parameter isolation methods [137] provides formal guarantees on catastrophic forgetting through the geometry of loss landscapes, offering a principled foundation for architecture-based designs. Additionally, lightweight adaptive weight modulation approaches [148] achieve zero forgetting guarantees by learning task-specific modulation parameters while keeping backbone weights frozen after initial training, showing strong performance on sequential image classification without any rehearsal.

### Prompt-Based and Pre-trained Model Approaches

The availability of powerful pre-trained vision models — including ResNets trained on large-scale datasets and Vision Transformers — has fundamentally changed the continual image classification landscape. Pre-training substantially reduces forgetting by providing rich, transferable representations initialized in wide, flat loss landscape regions [141]. Prompt-based continual learning methods leverage frozen or lightly adapted pre-trained representations by learning small sets of input-space or latent-space prompts per task, enabling knowledge accumulation with minimal parameter overhead and significantly reduced interference between tasks. The modular nature of these approaches — isolating task-specific adaptation in lightweight components while preserving a shared backbone — foreshadows the memory-augmented transformer architectures that prove effective in continual object detection, where transformer-based detectors similarly benefit from dedicated memory tokens encoding prior task knowledge.

Analytical studies decomposing the causes of accuracy degradation in continual learning [21] have found that readout layer misalignment — rather than irreversible loss of representational information — accounts for the largest component of performance decline. This insight motivates prompt-based and adapter-based approaches that keep backbone representations stable while adapting only lightweight task-specific components.

### Representation Analysis and Theoretical Insights

Understanding how forgetting manifests in the internal representations of neural networks has guided algorithm design for image classification. Analysis using representational similarity techniques shows that deeper network layers undergo disproportionate forgetting, and that the degree of forgetting is maximized for task sequences with intermediate visual similarity [18]. Theoretical frameworks based on the **Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) overlap matrix** [17] formalize task similarity and show that forgetting increases as tasks become more aligned, providing a principled basis for understanding when and why forgetting is severe in visual classification sequences.

Studies examining knowledge accumulation in long sequential image classification settings [174] challenge the conventional view that catastrophic forgetting universally and severely degrades performance, showing that with data re-occurrence and extended task sequences, SGD-trained networks can exhibit net knowledge accumulation even without explicit continual learning mechanisms.

### Representation Quality and Class-Incremental Learning

Investigations into the essential components of effective class-incremental learning [171] identify poor representation quality — quantified via dark knowledge and secondary class information encoded by the model — as a key driver of performance degradation, distinct from parameter interference. These studies demonstrate that balancing intra-task and inter-task learning objectives with appropriate regularization substantially improves class-incremental results on CIFAR-100 and ImageNet, providing a simpler yet highly effective approach compared to more complex architectural solutions.

The **Centroids Matching** approach [175] proposes preserving the geometry of the learned embedding space across task transitions by matching class centroids, achieving competitive results on multiple image classification benchmarks while operating with a small memory footprint. The principle of anchoring feature space geometry through prototype-level constraints introduced here is directly extended by prototype-guided approaches in continual object detection, where class-specific feature prototypes serve an analogous role in constraining representational drift of the detection backbone.

### Competitions and Community Progress

The **CVPR CLVision Challenge** has played a pivotal role in accelerating progress in continual image classification, benchmarking competing methods on realistic task sequences derived from CORe50 and similar datasets under standardized evaluation protocols. These competitions have catalyzed the development of hybrid methods combining replay, regularization, and pre-training, and have highlighted the importance of realistic evaluation settings — including domain shift, varying task lengths, and class imbalance — that differ substantially from idealized academic benchmarks. The multi-benchmark philosophy promoted by these competitions is echoed in the evaluation designs of continual object detection and segmentation, where diverse benchmark suites spanning PASCAL VOC, MS-COCO, and domain-specific datasets are employed to reveal generalization gaps that no single benchmark can expose.

Collectively, the continual image classification literature has matured significantly from early empirical explorations of catastrophic forgetting [15] to sophisticated frameworks integrating representation learning theory, architecture design, memory management, and pre-trained model adaptation. The four methodological families surveyed here — regularization, replay, architecture isolation, and prompt-based adaptation — each find adapted counterparts in the continual object detection methods examined in the following subsection, where the additional demands of localization, multi-label prediction, and annotation sparsity require their careful re-engineering. Remaining challenges include scaling to very long task sequences with long-tail class distributions, maintaining calibrated uncertainty estimates under sequential training, and deploying efficient continual classifiers on resource-constrained hardware without sacrificing accuracy.

### 4.3 Continual Learning for Object Detection

## 4.3 Continual Learning for Object Detection

Applying continual learning to object detection introduces a set of challenges that extend well beyond those encountered in image classification — the domain examined in the preceding subsection. Unlike classification, which assigns a single label to an entire image, object detection requires simultaneously localizing and categorizing multiple objects within a scene, making the problem significantly more complex and the effects of catastrophic forgetting both broader and harder to diagnose. Three core difficulties arise in continual object detection settings. First, the **background relegation** problem occurs because object categories not yet introduced in the current training phase are treated as background, causing the model to progressively suppress its responses to previously learned classes as new ones are introduced — a challenge conceptually related to, yet distinct from, the background shift problem that will be seen to dominate continual semantic segmentation in the following subsection. Second, **missing annotations** for previously learned classes compound this issue: when new task data is collected, bounding box annotations are typically provided only for newly introduced categories, leaving the model without supervisory signals to retain prior knowledge. Third, the **complexity of bounding box regression** means that both classification heads and localization branches must be preserved across sequential training phases, requiring careful architectural and optimization design that goes beyond what pure classification continual learning methods demand.

### Knowledge Distillation-Based Approaches

Knowledge distillation has emerged as one of the primary strategies for addressing catastrophic forgetting in continual object detection. The central idea is to use the predictions of an older model as soft targets that guide the training of the updated model, thereby preserving the feature representations and detection outputs associated with previously learned classes. Distillation can be applied at the level of feature maps, region proposal networks, bounding box regression outputs, or final classification logits. Multi-scale pooling distillation schemes have been proposed to ensure that spatial context at different resolutions is preserved across task transitions, which is particularly important for detecting objects of varying sizes. These methods have been evaluated on standard benchmarks including PASCAL VOC and MS-COCO, where they demonstrate substantial improvements over naive sequential fine-tuning baselines.

### Replay-Augmented Detection Frameworks

Replay-based methods, which revisit stored or synthesized examples from previous tasks, have also been adapted for object detection. Storing and replaying full detection images is memory-intensive, and managing annotations for bounding boxes adds further complexity compared to the classification replay strategies discussed in the previous subsection. Compressed rehearsal strategies that store compact representations of past scenes, alongside their associated bounding box annotations, help reduce memory overhead while maintaining sufficient fidelity for replay training. Smart data selection policies, analogous to coreset selection in classification, have been explored to prioritize the most informative past examples for replay, ensuring that the buffer covers diverse scene contexts and object configurations. These replay-augmented frameworks help mitigate both the background relegation problem and missing annotation issues by providing explicit positive training signals for old categories.

### Prototype-Guided and Gating Mechanisms

Prototype-guided approaches maintain class-specific feature prototypes derived from previously seen object instances. During training on new tasks, these prototypes serve as anchors that constrain feature drift, ensuring that the detection backbone does not undergo representational shifts that would degrade recognition of previously learned categories. Gating mechanisms can be integrated into the detection network architecture to selectively activate task-relevant feature channels, reducing interference between old and new class representations. Such mechanisms draw inspiration from the parameter isolation principles introduced in the image classification literature and are particularly effective in multi-task incremental detection settings where domain or category boundaries are well defined.

### Memory-Based Transformer Detectors

The advent of transformer-based object detectors such as DETR and its variants has opened new avenues for continual detection. Transformer architectures, with their attention-based feature aggregation and set-prediction formulations, offer natural interfaces for integrating memory modules that store compressed representations of past task knowledge. Memory-based transformer detectors maintain dedicated memory tokens or key-value stores that encode prior task information, which can be queried during inference to supplement current task representations. These architectures have shown promise in class-incremental detection scenarios on large-scale benchmarks including MS-COCO, where the diversity and density of object categories make forgetting particularly severe. The modular attention-driven structure of transformers also anticipates the adaptive distillation strategies that prove effective in continual semantic segmentation, discussed in the following subsection.

### Evaluation Benchmarks

The evaluation of continual object detection methods has relied on a range of benchmarks of varying scale and realism. **PASCAL VOC** and **MS-COCO** are the most widely used, typically split into sequential subsets of object categories to simulate class-incremental learning. More recently, the **CLAD** (Continual Learning for Autonomous Driving) benchmark has been introduced to evaluate continual detection under realistic domain shifts encountered in autonomous driving scenarios, combining both class-incremental and domain-incremental challenges. The **EgoObjects** benchmark, derived from egocentric video footage, introduces additional challenges arising from viewpoint diversity, object occlusion, and fine-grained category distinctions that are characteristic of real-world deployment environments. Together, these benchmarks span a spectrum of complexity that enables comprehensive evaluation of continual detection algorithms across diverse operational conditions — a breadth of evaluation that mirrors the multi-benchmark philosophy adopted for continual segmentation.

### Summary

Continual learning for object detection occupies an intermediate level of complexity between continual image classification and continual semantic segmentation: it inherits the class-incremental challenges of classification while introducing the dense, multi-output prediction demands that become even more pronounced in segmentation. Knowledge distillation, replay augmentation, prototype-guided gating, and memory-augmented transformer architectures represent the principal families of solutions that have been developed to address these challenges. Progress on benchmarks such as PASCAL VOC, MS-COCO, CLAD, and EgoObjects highlights both the advances achieved and the considerable gaps that remain before continual detection systems can be reliably deployed in real-world applications. Many of the core techniques developed in this domain — particularly distillation at multiple spatial scales and intelligent replay under annotation constraints — carry forward directly into the continual segmentation methods examined next.

### 4.4 Continual Semantic and Panoptic Segmentation

## 4.4 Continual Semantic and Panoptic Segmentation

Continual semantic segmentation presents unique and formidable challenges that distinguish it from both class-incremental image classification and the object detection settings examined in the preceding subsection. While object detection must contend with background relegation and missing bounding box annotations across task transitions, semantic segmentation intensifies these difficulties through its requirement for dense, pixel-level prediction across the entire image. The most critical challenge is the **background shift problem**: in standard segmentation datasets, the background class implicitly subsumes all categories not yet introduced in the current task. As new classes are incrementally added, pixels previously labeled as background may now belong to newly learned foreground categories, creating severe label conflicts between old and new training stages. This phenomenon amplifies catastrophic forgetting beyond what is observed in either classification or detection settings, since the model must simultaneously redefine its notion of background while consolidating knowledge of previously seen classes.

### Core Challenges in Continual Segmentation

The pixel-wise nature of segmentation tasks exacerbates the stability-plasticity dilemma. Unlike classification, where a single label pertains to an entire image, or detection, where forgetting is confined to bounding box regression and classification heads, segmentation requires dense prediction at every spatial location, meaning that forgetting can manifest in subtle and spatially inconsistent patterns. Furthermore, the absence of annotations for old classes in new training batches means that naive fine-tuning readily overwrites the decision boundaries learned in prior stages. This is compounded in panoptic segmentation, which must simultaneously handle both "stuff" (amorphous background regions) and "things" (countable object instances), introducing further complexity in maintaining consistent semantic boundaries across incremental updates — a demand that extends well beyond what even continual object detection methods are required to address.

### Representative Methods and Techniques

**Multi-Scale Pooling and Distillation Schemes.** Early efforts to tackle continual semantic segmentation adapted knowledge distillation principles from classification to the dense prediction domain — echoing the distillation strategies already seen in continual object detection, but adapted to the requirements of pixel-level supervision. Knowledge distillation-based approaches transfer soft logits or intermediate feature maps from an old model to constrain the updates of a new model, effectively serving as a form of implicit replay of the model's prior predictions. Multi-scale pooling distillation schemes extend this idea by aligning feature representations at multiple levels of spatial granularity, ensuring that both coarse semantic structure and fine-grained local details are preserved across incremental steps. These methods are evaluated predominantly on Pascal VOC and ADE20K benchmarks, where the number of incrementally added categories ranges from single-class additions to large multi-step protocols.

**Pseudo-Labeling Strategies.** Pseudo-labeling represents a powerful complement to distillation. Since annotations for previously learned classes are unavailable during new task training, one can generate pseudo ground-truth labels for old classes by querying the frozen prior model. These pseudo-labels are then used to supervise the current model on old-class regions, effectively approximating the missing supervision. Careful thresholding and uncertainty-aware selection of pseudo-labels are necessary to prevent the propagation of erroneous predictions, particularly at class boundaries where the old model may be less confident. Pseudo-labeling has proven especially beneficial in incremental protocols with large numbers of steps, where distillation alone tends to degrade because the teacher model itself undergoes compounding approximation errors.

**Contrastive Attention Approaches.** More recent continual segmentation methods leverage contrastive learning to maintain discriminative feature spaces across tasks. Contrastive attention mechanisms enforce that features extracted from the same semantic class remain cohesive in embedding space, while features from different classes are pushed apart, even when the network is being updated on new categories. This contrastive regularization, applied in conjunction with self-attention modules, helps stabilize the internal representations of previously learned classes without requiring access to old training data. Such approaches have demonstrated competitive performance on Cityscapes and ADE20K, particularly in settings that simulate real-world autonomous driving scenarios where both the category set and domain conditions evolve over time [49].

**Replay-Based Smart Data Selection.** Replay-based methods for segmentation face additional memory challenges compared to both classification and detection replay strategies discussed in the preceding subsections. Storing full-resolution segmentation images with dense pixel-level annotations is significantly more costly than storing classification images with single labels or detection images with sparse bounding box annotations. Smart data selection strategies therefore prioritize which images and which annotated regions to retain in the replay buffer, optimizing for coverage of learned class distributions while respecting strict memory budgets. Some approaches select replay samples based on class frequency, feature diversity, or geometric coverage, ensuring that the replayed data provides a representative summary of prior knowledge. Domain-incremental segmentation, as explored in autonomous driving contexts [176; 49], requires that the model generalize not only to new semantic categories but also to new visual domains such as different cities or weather conditions, further motivating intelligent curation of replay sets.

**Transformer-Based Adaptive Distillation.** With the adoption of transformer architectures for segmentation — including models based on vision transformers and masked attention — continual learning methods have evolved to exploit the modular and attention-driven nature of these networks. As anticipated by the memory-based transformer detectors discussed in the preceding subsection, transformer-based adaptive distillation selectively transfers knowledge through attention maps and query-key-value interactions, allowing fine-grained control over which spatial regions and semantic concepts receive stronger distillation constraints. This is particularly relevant for panoptic segmentation, where the distinction between instance-level and semantic-level representations must be preserved independently across incremental updates. Prompt-based adaptation of large pre-trained segmentation models has also emerged as a promising direction, leveraging the stability of frozen backbone representations to minimize forgetting while using lightweight prompt modules to encode task-specific knowledge — a strategy that will recur in the video understanding settings examined in the following subsection.

### Evaluation Benchmarks and Protocols

The dominant benchmarks for continual semantic segmentation are **Pascal VOC 2012**, **ADE20K**, and **Cityscapes**. Pascal VOC is widely used due to its manageable size and well-defined incremental protocols, ranging from disjoint splits (e.g., 15+1, 10+10, 15+5 class divisions) to overlapping and sequential settings. ADE20K, with its 150 semantic categories and significantly higher visual complexity, provides a more challenging testbed that better approximates real-world diversity. Cityscapes is the standard benchmark for domain-incremental segmentation in autonomous driving, simulating scenarios where models must generalize to new urban environments while retaining performance on previously encountered cities [176; 49]. This multi-benchmark evaluation philosophy mirrors the approach adopted in continual object detection, where benchmarks spanning PASCAL VOC, MS-COCO, CLAD, and EgoObjects collectively characterize performance across diverse operational conditions.

Medical imaging datasets also play an increasingly prominent role as benchmarks for continual segmentation, given the clinical demand for models that can be updated with new imaging modalities or anatomical structures without retraining from scratch. In medical settings, the privacy constraints on patient data make replay-based approaches less straightforward, motivating the development of strong distillation and regularization alternatives that operate without retaining raw images.

### Domain-Incremental Segmentation

Beyond class-incremental protocols, domain-incremental continual segmentation addresses the scenario where the set of semantic categories remains fixed but the visual domain shifts — for example, transitioning from one geographic region to another in urban driving, or from one hospital's imaging protocol to another's in clinical settings. Methods addressing this setting must handle distribution shift in both input and feature spaces without the anchor of class-boundary annotations to guide adaptation [49; 176]. Combining style transfer techniques with distillation frameworks has proven effective in this regime, enabling models to generalize their incrementally acquired task knowledge across all encountered domains while remaining robust to catastrophic forgetting [49]. This concern for domain variability under sequential updates anticipates the challenges of temporal domain shift that characterize continual learning for video understanding, examined in the following subsection.

### Summary

Continual semantic and panoptic segmentation constitutes one of the most challenging testbeds for continual learning methods, combining the difficulties of dense prediction, implicit background redefinition, and limited annotation availability across sequential steps — difficulties that collectively exceed those encountered in continual object detection while directly foreshadowing the temporal consistency demands of continual video understanding. The field has advanced substantially through the synergistic application of distillation, pseudo-labeling, contrastive learning, and intelligent replay, with transformer-based architectures opening new avenues for modular and prompt-driven adaptation. Comprehensive evaluation across Pascal VOC, ADE20K, Cityscapes, and medical imaging benchmarks has been essential in characterizing method strengths and weaknesses, and future progress will likely depend on tighter integration of domain adaptation principles with incremental learning frameworks to address the full complexity of real-world non-stationary visual environments.

### 4.5 Continual Learning for Video Understanding

## 4.5 Continual Learning for Video Understanding

Continual learning in video domains presents a distinct set of challenges that go beyond those encountered in static image-based tasks such as the semantic and panoptic segmentation methods discussed in the preceding section. While segmentation already demands dense, pixel-level prediction under incremental learning conditions, video data introduces an additional temporal dimension that requires models to maintain consistency across frames while simultaneously adapting to new categories of actions or objects. The high dimensionality of video sequences, coupled with the computational cost of processing temporal information, makes memory management and replay particularly demanding. Unlike static images, video frames are highly correlated, and naïve frame-level memory selection strategies risk storing redundant information that poorly represents the diversity of past experiences.

### Video Action Recognition

Video action recognition under continual learning settings requires models to incrementally learn new action categories from streaming video data while avoiding catastrophic forgetting of previously learned actions. The temporal structure of video data necessitates specialized approaches for feature extraction and knowledge consolidation. Prompt-based methods have emerged as a promising direction in this space, leveraging the representational power of large pre-trained video transformers while introducing task-specific prompts to guide incremental adaptation. Such approaches align naturally with the broader trend of exploiting pre-trained foundation models for continual learning, as the frozen backbone reduces plasticity-stability conflicts by preserving generic spatiotemporal representations across tasks.

A key challenge in continual action recognition is maintaining temporal consistency in learned representations. When a model sequentially trains on new action categories, the internal representations encoding temporal dynamics can shift, leading to degraded recognition of previously learned actions that rely on motion-sensitive features. Temporal consistency regularization has been proposed as a mechanism to preserve the integrity of temporal feature representations across task transitions, penalizing drift in the spatiotemporal embeddings that underpin action discrimination. This challenge is analogous to, yet distinct from, the background shift problem in continual segmentation: whereas segmentation must contend with spatially inconsistent label redefinition, video action recognition must contend with temporally inconsistent feature drift.

Benchmarks such as ActivityNet and vCLIMB have been introduced to provide standardized evaluation of continual learning algorithms in video action recognition settings. These benchmarks capture the diversity of real-world action categories and enable systematic measurement of forgetting and forward transfer across incrementally introduced action classes.

### Video Object Segmentation

Continual learning for video object segmentation (VOS) presents additional challenges rooted in the need to track and segment objects across temporal sequences while learning new object categories over time. This setting extends the difficulties of continual semantic segmentation—where pixel-level annotation scarcity and background shift are already formidable—into the temporal domain, where inter-frame dependencies and object motion further complicate incremental adaptation. Frame-level memory selection becomes critical: an effective continual learner must identify which frames carry the most informative and diverse content for replay without exhausting memory budgets. Unlike image segmentation, where individual images can be independently stored, video data requires strategies that account for inter-frame redundancy and temporal coherence when constructing episodic memory buffers.

The CLVOS23 benchmark has been developed specifically to evaluate catastrophic forgetting in continual video object segmentation scenarios, providing a structured testbed for assessing how well models retain segmentation capabilities for previously encountered object categories as new ones are introduced sequentially. Methods evaluated on such benchmarks must balance the competing demands of preserving segmentation accuracy on old object classes against achieving high performance on newly introduced ones.

Distillation-based approaches, which transfer knowledge from a frozen teacher model representing prior task competencies to a student model being trained on new tasks, have shown utility in video segmentation continual learning by constraining the evolution of temporal feature representations. This mirrors the role of distillation in continual semantic segmentation, where soft logit transfer and feature-level alignment are used to prevent forgetting of previously learned categories. Similarly, replay-based frameworks adapted for video settings must address the challenge of selecting maximally informative and temporally diverse video clips or frames rather than isolated images, echoing the smart data selection strategies developed for image segmentation replay.

### Unique Challenges and Future Directions

Several unique challenges distinguish continual learning for video understanding from its image-based counterpart. First, the computational burden of processing video sequences is substantially higher, making memory-efficient replay and lightweight adaptation mechanisms especially important. Second, temporal consistency across task boundaries is a non-trivial requirement: as models learn new categories, the temporal feature representations underlying previously learned categories can degrade in ways not captured by frame-level evaluation metrics alone. Third, the annotation cost for video data is significantly higher than for images, motivating the development of semi-supervised and self-supervised continual learning strategies tailored to video domains. These difficulties are compounded by the domain variability that characterizes real-world video streams, where changes in scene appearance, camera motion, and lighting conditions can interact with task-incremental updates in complex ways.

The intersection of continual learning with video understanding remains a relatively underexplored frontier compared to image classification and segmentation, with significant opportunities for the development of novel benchmarks, memory management strategies, and temporally-aware regularization methods. The broader continual learning literature on replay, regularization, and prompt-based adaptation [1] provides foundational principles that require careful adaptation to accommodate the temporal structure inherent in video data. As continual learning methods increasingly target 3D and spatiotemporal data modalities—including the 3D point cloud settings discussed in the following section—the lessons learned from video understanding will play an important role in shaping domain-specific adaptations of general continual learning frameworks.

### 4.6 Continual Learning for 3D Point Cloud Processing

## 4.6 Continual Learning for 3D Point Cloud Understanding

Continual learning for 3D point cloud data presents a distinct set of challenges compared to standard 2D image-based continual learning, including the video-based settings discussed in the preceding section. While video understanding demands temporal consistency across frames and efficient management of high-dimensional sequential data, 3D point cloud learning introduces an additional layer of complexity rooted in the geometric structure of data itself. Point clouds are inherently sparse, unstructured, and unevenly distributed across 3D space, making it difficult to apply standard replay or regularization techniques directly. Unlike image pixels, which reside on regular grids, point cloud data exhibits high geometric variability across instances and scanning conditions, leading to complex distribution shifts when new classes or environments are introduced incrementally.

Key tasks in this domain include 3D shape classification, LiDAR-based semantic segmentation, and 3D object shape reconstruction. For 3D shape classification, class-incremental learning frameworks must contend with high intra-class geometric variability and the challenge of distinguishing semantically similar 3D shapes as the number of categories grows. Benchmarks such as ModelNet have been used to evaluate continual learning methods in this setting, where coarse-to-fine class-incremental approaches progressively refine shape representations at multiple levels of geometric abstraction.

LiDAR semantic segmentation presents even greater challenges, as sequential scans from autonomous driving environments such as SemanticKITTI contain millions of points with long-tail class distributions and significant spatial non-stationarity. Methods that adopt compressed rehearsal strategies — storing compact representations of past point cloud experiences rather than raw scans — have been explored to reduce memory overhead while maintaining knowledge of previously encountered scene categories. Geometric equivariance distillation approaches seek to preserve learned equivariant feature representations across tasks, ensuring that rotationally and translationally consistent features acquired from prior tasks are not disrupted by subsequent training. This use of distillation to constrain feature evolution mirrors strategies employed in continual video object segmentation and image-based segmentation, where soft logit transfer and feature-level alignment prevent forgetting of previously learned categories, yet here the constraints must additionally respect the geometric symmetries inherent to 3D representations.

The sparse and uneven nature of 3D point cloud distributions also complicates generative replay, as generating realistic and geometrically coherent pseudo-point-clouds is considerably harder than generating 2D images. This motivates the use of feature-space replay rather than raw data replay for this modality, echoing the broader trend across continual vision tasks of shifting replay strategies away from raw pixel storage toward more compact and informative representational summaries.

Overall, continual learning in 3D point cloud processing is an emerging research area that benefits from domain-specific adaptations of general continual learning strategies. The unique geometric properties of point cloud data demand that forgetting-prevention mechanisms account for structural and spatial consistency, and that rehearsal or distillation strategies are designed with awareness of the sparse, irregular nature of 3D representations. Work in this area connects naturally to broader continual learning efforts in computer vision [5] and to real-world deployment scenarios in autonomous systems and robotics [73], where 3D perception is a critical capability. These connections to autonomous driving perception are further explored in the following section, which examines continual learning across a diverse range of vision applications including multiple object tracking, deepfake detection, and autonomous driving, where 3D understanding constitutes a key component of robust and adaptive perception systems.

### 4.7 Continual Learning for Multiple Object Tracking and Other Vision Tasks

Beyond classification, detection, and segmentation, continual learning has been extended to a broader set of computer vision tasks that present unique challenges for sequential knowledge acquisition and retention. These tasks span modalities and application contexts that differ substantially from standard image classification, and include multiple object tracking (MOT), deepfake detection, and autonomous driving perception. Each of these settings introduces distinct non-stationarities that standard batch learning paradigms cannot adequately address, and each connects naturally to the broader challenge of deploying continual vision systems in dynamic, real-world environments.

**Multiple Object Tracking.** Multiple object tracking requires a system to simultaneously localize and maintain identities of potentially numerous objects across video frames. As tracking environments evolve—new object categories appear, visual domains shift, or tracker appearance models become outdated—maintaining consistent tracking performance without retraining from scratch becomes critical. Continual learning approaches for MOT have explored contrastive learning principles to update appearance-based re-identification modules without catastrophic forgetting of previously observed object appearances. The core challenge lies in the fact that appearance models must remain discriminative for previously encountered identities while rapidly adapting to novel ones, making the stability-plasticity dilemma particularly acute. Methods inspired by replay-based strategies and representation distillation have been applied to prevent appearance embedding spaces from drifting as new object domains are encountered sequentially.

**Deepfake Detection.** The rapid proliferation of generative deepfake techniques poses a continual learning challenge: detection models trained on known manipulation methods frequently fail on novel forgery techniques that emerge after deployment. Continual representation distillation methods address this challenge by preserving discriminative features learned from previously encountered fake media types while adapting to detect new manipulation artifacts. The domain of deepfake detection exemplifies a real-world scenario where task boundaries are ill-defined and novel forgery distributions continuously emerge, necessitating methods that can integrate new knowledge without catastrophically overwriting previously acquired forgery detectors. Strategies combining knowledge distillation with selective replay of representative fake and real samples have shown promise in maintaining detection performance across evolving generative model families.

**Autonomous Driving Perception.** Autonomous driving represents a particularly demanding application domain for continual learning, where perception systems must handle both class-incremental challenges (new object categories such as construction vehicles or cyclists) and domain-incremental challenges (new geographic locations, weather conditions, or sensor configurations). The CLAD benchmark was specifically designed to simulate realistic autonomous driving scenarios incorporating both class-incremental and domain-incremental challenges, providing a controlled evaluation environment that reflects the temporal evolution of real-world driving data. Such benchmarks highlight the inadequacy of standard evaluation protocols that assume uniform task sizes and clearly delineated boundaries, motivating more realistic evaluation methodologies—a concern that connects directly to the broader discussion of benchmark design and evaluation protocols addressed in the following section. Continual learning methods applied in this context must contend with high-stakes safety requirements, where forgetting a previously learned object category in a novel driving environment could have severe consequences.

**Common Themes and Challenges.** Across these diverse vision tasks, several common themes emerge. First, the non-stationarity of real-world visual environments means that purely batch-trained models rapidly become obsolete. Second, task boundaries are often ambiguous or absent, as in the case of continuously evolving deepfake techniques or gradually shifting driving domains. Third, the computational and memory constraints imposed by real-time applications such as autonomous driving require continual learning methods to be efficient as well as accurate. Taken together, these challenges underscore the need for rigorous and realistic evaluation frameworks capable of capturing the full complexity of sequential visual learning in practice. The interplay between continual learning and adjacent paradigms such as self-supervised representation learning and knowledge distillation appears particularly promising for these applications, as strong pre-trained representations can serve as stable anchors during sequential updates, reducing the severity of catastrophic forgetting while preserving the flexibility to adapt to new visual experiences [1; 10].

### 4.8 Benchmarks, Evaluation Protocols, and Datasets for Continual Vision Learning

Rigorous evaluation of continual learning algorithms in computer vision depends critically on the quality and diversity of benchmarks, datasets, and evaluation protocols employed. As highlighted in the preceding discussion of diverse vision tasks—from multiple object tracking to autonomous driving perception—the complexity and non-stationarity of real-world visual environments demand evaluation frameworks that go well beyond simple controlled settings. Over the years, the community has developed a wide spectrum of benchmarks ranging from simple, controlled academic datasets to more realistic and complex scenarios designed to reflect the challenges of real-world visual learning.

**Standard and Foundational Benchmarks**

The earliest and most widely adopted benchmarks in continual vision learning include Split-MNIST, Permuted-MNIST, Split-CIFAR-10, and Split-CIFAR-100. These benchmarks partition standard image classification datasets into sequential subsets or apply permutations to create artificial task boundaries, enabling controlled evaluation of catastrophic forgetting and knowledge retention. While these datasets offer reproducibility and computational accessibility, they are generally acknowledged to underrepresent the complexity encountered in real-world scenarios. The fragmentation of evaluation protocols across the continual learning literature—with different papers adopting incompatible benchmarks, metrics, and data splits—has long been recognized as a barrier to objective progress assessment [57].

**Heterogeneous and Curriculum-Structured Benchmarks**

Recognizing the limitations of homogeneous benchmarks, researchers have proposed evaluation protocols that involve multiple heterogeneous datasets of varying complexity. For instance, benchmarks that combine tasks from six different image datasets with tasks ordered according to a curriculum—presenting tasks in increasing or decreasing order of complexity—have been introduced to assess whether continual learners can exploit task structure [57]. Such benchmarks reveal that popular continual learning strategies yield sub-par performance and exhibit limited ability to leverage curriculum ordering, highlighting the need for more robust evaluation frameworks closer to real-world scenarios. This concern is directly echoed by domain-specific benchmarks such as CLAD, introduced in the context of autonomous driving, which similarly exposes the inadequacy of assuming uniform task sizes and clearly delineated boundaries.

**Object-Centric and Large-Scale Benchmarks**

For more realistic evaluation, benchmarks such as CORe50 provide object-centric sequences captured across different temporal sessions and environmental conditions, simulating the kind of domain diversity and temporal evolution encountered in practical deployment. Split-ImageNet further scales the difficulty by drawing from a large-scale object recognition dataset, testing the scalability of continual learning methods. Benchmarks like CLEAR introduce naturally evolving data distributions with temporal image streams, enabling evaluation under genuine non-stationarity rather than artificially constructed task boundaries.

**Evaluation Metrics in Continual Vision Learning**

Standard evaluation metrics applied in visual recognition settings include average accuracy computed over all tasks at the end of training, backward transfer (BWT) measuring the influence of new task learning on previously learned tasks, forward transfer (FWT) capturing the benefit of prior learning on future tasks, and the forgetting measure quantifying the degree of performance degradation on earlier tasks [4; 9]. These metrics are typically reported together to provide a comprehensive picture of the stability-plasticity trade-off exhibited by each method. However, limitations remain: many metrics do not disentangle whether forgetting arises from the learning algorithm itself or from the benchmark structure, and they may fail to capture the increasing difficulty of later tasks in long sequences.

**Online and Streaming Evaluation Protocols**

Beyond offline evaluation, streaming evaluation protocols assess models as they encounter a continuous non-stationary data stream without explicit task boundaries, requiring both high online learning efficiency and strong information retention. This paradigm is especially relevant for the real-world applications discussed previously—such as autonomous driving and deepfake detection—where task boundaries are often absent or ill-defined. Online continual learning benchmarks based on large-scale visual datasets with natural distribution shifts have been developed to evaluate performance under this more challenging and realistic regime [7]. In this setting, each incoming mini-batch is first used for evaluation before training, ensuring a fair assessment of the model's generalization capability at every point in time.

**Domain Diversity and Realistic Temporal Evolution**

A key insight emerging from recent benchmark development is that domain diversity and realistic temporal evolution are essential components of meaningful evaluation. Benchmarks that incorporate multiple domains—spanning natural images, medical imaging, autonomous driving scenes, and synthetic data—provide a more faithful representation of the distribution shifts continual vision systems encounter in deployment. Evaluation protocols that simulate gradual concept drift rather than abrupt task switches more closely reflect real-world dynamics and reveal distinct failure modes not captured by standard split-dataset benchmarks [7].

**Software Frameworks Supporting Reproducible Evaluation**

The development of standardized software frameworks has played an important role in enabling fair and reproducible comparison of continual vision learning methods. Frameworks such as Continuum provide flexible data loaders and scenario management tools that support a wide range of continual learning scenarios, reducing implementation errors and ensuring consistent preprocessing and evaluation [168]. These tools facilitate systematic benchmarking by allowing researchers to directly compare methods on identical data streams with consistent evaluation protocols.

**Summary**

In summary, the landscape of benchmarks and evaluation protocols for continual vision learning has evolved considerably, moving from simple split-dataset protocols toward heterogeneous, curriculum-structured, and streaming benchmarks that better capture the demands of real-world deployment [57; 7]. This evolution mirrors the broader trajectory of continual learning research itself, which has progressively shifted its focus from controlled laboratory settings toward the complex, open-ended visual environments discussed throughout this survey. Nevertheless, significant challenges remain in achieving community-wide standardization, particularly regarding the choice of metrics, evaluation scenarios, and the degree of realism required to meaningfully assess practical continual vision systems.

## 5 Continual Learning in Natural Language Processing and Multimodal Systems

### 5.1 Continual Learning for Text Classification and Sequence Labeling

## 5.1 Continual Learning for Text Classification and Sequence Labeling

Text classification and sequence labeling represent foundational tasks in natural language processing, and applying continual learning to these settings introduces a distinct set of challenges compared to computer vision domains. Unlike image data, textual inputs exhibit complex semantic structures, vocabulary shifts, and nuanced distributional changes over time, making catastrophic forgetting particularly problematic when models encounter new topics, domains, or labeling schemas sequentially. This section examines the core challenges, dominant methodological families, and emerging directions in continual NLP, laying the groundwork for the discussion of continual pre-training and domain-adaptive pre-training of large language models that follows in Section 5.2.

### Challenges in NLP Continual Learning

A primary challenge in continual text classification is **semantic drift**, whereby the statistical properties of language evolve across tasks or domains. When a model trained on sentiment analysis of product reviews is subsequently fine-tuned on news categorization, the learned representations for shared vocabulary items can shift substantially, causing rapid degradation of performance on earlier tasks. This phenomenon is exacerbated by the high-dimensional, sparse nature of text representations and the reliance on contextual embeddings that encode global semantic relationships. Similarly, in sequence labeling tasks such as named entity recognition or part-of-speech tagging, the label space itself may expand incrementally as new entity types or syntactic categories are introduced, posing class-incremental challenges analogous to those observed in visual recognition [5].

**Domain shift** further compounds these difficulties, as models deployed across different textual domains—such as biomedical literature, social media, and legal documents—encounter vocabulary distributions that diverge markedly from those seen during earlier training phases. The non-stationary nature of streaming text data means that a continual learner must simultaneously retain semantic knowledge from prior domains while plastically adapting its representations to accommodate incoming data [2]. These challenges are not merely quantitative extensions of those encountered in vision-based continual learning; they reflect qualitative differences in how knowledge is structured and shared across linguistic tasks, motivating NLP-specific adaptations of general continual learning principles.

### Regularization-Based Approaches

Regularization-based methods have been among the earliest and most straightforward approaches adapted from computer vision to NLP continual learning. Techniques such as Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC), which penalizes changes to parameters deemed important for previous tasks through Fisher information estimates, have been applied to text classification pipelines to reduce forgetting across sequential task arrivals [1]. The appeal of regularization methods in NLP lies in their architectural transparency—they impose no structural changes on the underlying language model, making them directly applicable to standard transformer-based encoders. However, these methods face scalability limitations when applied to large pre-trained language models: computing and storing Fisher information matrices or equivalent importance weights for billions of parameters is computationally prohibitive, and approximations that operate on subsets of parameters may provide insufficient protection against forgetting in deep transformer architectures.

Bayesian regularization variants, including approaches that maintain posterior distributions over network weights and use these as priors for subsequent tasks, offer a principled probabilistic framework for knowledge retention [128]. In text classification settings, such methods can model uncertainty in learned representations, enabling the model to distinguish between regions of parameter space that are well-constrained by prior data and those with high uncertainty where plasticity can be safely permitted. These scalability and expressiveness concerns with purely regularization-based methods have motivated the adoption of complementary strategies, including experience replay and architecture-based approaches, which are discussed in the following subsections.

### Replay-Based Strategies in NLP

Experience replay, which maintains a buffer of exemplars from previous tasks and interleaves them with new training data, has been extensively studied as a practical remedy for catastrophic forgetting in sequential text classification [129]. Adapting replay to NLP requires careful consideration of how exemplars are selected and stored, as raw text sequences can be significantly more memory-intensive than fixed-dimensional feature vectors. Reservoir sampling provides a computationally efficient mechanism for maintaining a representative buffer of past examples under a fixed memory budget [6], while more sophisticated selection strategies prioritize examples that are most likely to be forgotten or that maximally represent the decision boundaries of past tasks.

Prototype-based replay methods offer a memory-efficient alternative by storing compact class-representative summaries rather than individual training instances [131]. In text classification, such prototypes can be constructed as averaged contextual embeddings for each class, enabling the model to rehearse the essential structure of previous task distributions without storing raw text. Variational prototype approaches extend this idea by representing class distributions probabilistically, allowing for sampling-based replay that generates diverse pseudo-examples for rehearsal [133].

Gradient-based coreset selection methods, which choose replay exemplars that approximate the gradient of the full historical dataset with respect to current model parameters, provide theoretically motivated selection criteria that can be adapted to NLP tasks [112]. By ensuring that the selected exemplars produce gradient signals representative of all past data, such methods offer stronger guarantees against catastrophic forgetting than purely random or heuristic selection strategies. The memory and computational demands of replay strategies, however, motivate exploration of parameter-efficient alternatives that reduce reliance on stored data.

### The Role of Pre-Trained Language Models

The emergence of large pre-trained language models such as BERT and its successors has fundamentally altered the landscape of continual learning for NLP. These models, pre-trained on massive corpora using self-supervised objectives, encode rich general-purpose linguistic knowledge that provides a strong initialization point for sequential task learning. Empirical studies have consistently demonstrated that fine-tuning pre-trained transformers on new NLP tasks results in substantially less catastrophic forgetting compared to training smaller models from scratch, as the extensive pre-training establishes broadly useful representations that are not easily overwritten by task-specific fine-tuning on limited data [13]. The pre-trained representations serve as a stable semantic substrate that resists complete overwriting, effectively providing an implicit regularization effect.

This observation has motivated investigation into how much of the forgetting resistance in pre-trained models is attributable to the scale and diversity of pre-training versus the specific architectural choices of transformers. Evidence suggests that the representational richness of pre-trained embeddings reduces the gradient interference between tasks, as learned features are more likely to be broadly useful across different textual domains rather than narrowly specialized [177]. However, pre-trained models are not immune to catastrophic forgetting, particularly when fine-tuned extensively on datasets with distributions that diverge sharply from pre-training corpora or when sequential tasks involve conflicting labeling conventions. This limitation motivates the parameter-efficient tuning strategies described next, as well as the broader investigation of continual pre-training pipelines addressed in Section 5.2.

### Parameter-Efficient Tuning Methods

Given the prohibitive cost of fully fine-tuning large pre-trained language models for each new task in a continual learning pipeline, parameter-efficient tuning methods have emerged as a practically important family of approaches. These methods freeze the majority of pre-trained parameters and introduce small task-specific modules—such as adapters, low-rank decompositions, or prefix vectors—that absorb task-specific knowledge while preserving the shared pre-trained backbone. By limiting the parameters modified during continual fine-tuning, these approaches inherently reduce the scope for catastrophic forgetting: the frozen backbone retains general linguistic knowledge while the lightweight task-specific components capture task-particular information.

Prompt-based tuning methods represent a particularly compelling approach for continual text classification, enabling models to learn task-relevant behavior through small sets of learnable prompt tokens prepended to the input sequence without modifying the pre-trained weights [135]. The prompt pool mechanism, which maintains a collection of learnable prompts indexed by input features, allows the model to dynamically retrieve and compose task-relevant prompts at inference time, effectively routing different inputs through different learned behaviors without task identity labels. Convolutional prompt creation mechanisms further enhance this approach by maintaining layer-wise shared embeddings that enable both layer-specific learning and knowledge transfer across tasks, with large language models used to generate semantic task descriptions for similarity-based prompt retrieval [136].

The use of intermediate layer representations extends the utility of pre-trained models beyond their final embeddings, with methods that leverage second-order statistics from multiple transformer layers providing richer and more invariant features that better support continual class- and domain-incremental text classification [126]. These parameter-efficient strategies are closely related to the domain-adaptive pre-training methods discussed in Section 5.2, where similar principles of selective parameter updates are applied at the pre-training stage rather than the fine-tuning stage.

### Sequence Labeling Considerations

Continual learning for sequence labeling tasks introduces additional complexity compared to document-level classification, as predictions must be made at the token level and label sequences exhibit strong local dependencies captured by conditional random field layers or autoregressive decoding. The class-incremental scenario in named entity recognition, where new entity types are introduced sequentially, must contend with the background class shift problem analogous to that observed in continual semantic segmentation: tokens belonging to new entity classes were previously annotated as non-entity background, creating conflicting supervision signals that accelerate forgetting of previously learned entity boundaries.

Methods addressing this challenge in NLP sequence labeling have drawn on distillation-based approaches that preserve the output distributions of previous model versions on new data, supplementing standard cross-entropy training with soft targets that encode prior task knowledge. Pseudo-labeling strategies, which generate silver annotations for previous entity types on new task data, help mitigate the background shift problem by providing explicit supervision for previously learned classes even in the absence of gold annotations [5]. These distillation and pseudo-labeling techniques share conceptual foundations with the knowledge integration methods employed in domain-adaptive pre-training, forming a coherent methodological thread that runs through both supervised and self-supervised continual NLP settings.

### Online and Task-Free Settings

The most challenging deployment scenario for continual text classification involves online learning from a continuous stream of data without explicit task boundaries or identifiers. In such settings, models must detect distribution shifts, adapt to new topics or domains, and retain prior knowledge simultaneously, all without access to explicit signals demarcating when new tasks begin [7]. Task-free data-incremental learning frameworks that process streaming text without requiring task boundary annotations represent an important direction, leveraging prototype adaptation and contrastive objectives to maintain inter-class discrimination across evolving data distributions [81]. The streaming setting also connects naturally to the question of continual pre-training on evolving corpora, where domain boundaries are similarly ill-defined and models must adapt gracefully to gradual distribution shifts over time.

In summary, continual learning for text classification and sequence labeling involves navigating the interplay between semantic drift, domain shift, and catastrophic forgetting within the framework of increasingly capable pre-trained language models. The field has progressed from simple regularization and replay adaptations toward sophisticated parameter-efficient and prompt-based paradigms that leverage the inherent stability of large pre-trained representations, while open challenges remain in scaling these methods to long task sequences, handling task-free streaming settings, and ensuring equitable performance across diverse linguistic domains. The insights developed in these supervised and structured prediction settings directly inform the challenges of continual pre-training and domain-adaptive pre-training examined in the following section.

### 5.2 Continual Pre-Training and Domain-Adaptive Pre-Training of Language Models

## 5.2 Continual Pre-Training and Domain-Adaptive Pre-Training of Language Models

Building upon the supervised and structured prediction settings examined in Section 5.1, continual pre-training of large language models (LLMs) on evolving domain corpora represents a critical challenge at the intersection of self-supervised learning and continual learning. Unlike supervised fine-tuning, where task-specific labels drive adaptation, pre-training objectives such as masked language modeling and next-token prediction operate over unlabeled text corpora, providing a natural form of regularization that can inherently mitigate catastrophic forgetting. The self-supervised nature of these objectives encourages the model to retain broadly useful linguistic representations while adapting to new domain-specific vocabulary, syntax, and knowledge, offering an implicit stability mechanism that is absent in purely supervised sequential learning pipelines—an important contrast with the text classification and sequence labeling settings discussed previously.

A key observation motivating this line of research is that generic pre-training on large corpora appears to ease forgetting by leading models toward wider loss basins in the parameter space [141]. This geometric perspective suggests that pre-trained weight initializations occupy flatter regions of the loss landscape compared to randomly initialized models, making them inherently more resistant to parameter perturbations induced by learning on new domain corpora. Empirical studies across diverse text and image classification benchmarks have confirmed that pre-trained models exhibit substantially reduced catastrophic forgetting relative to randomly initialized counterparts when fine-tuned on sequential tasks [141], reinforcing the foundational argument that the quality and breadth of initial pre-training directly influences continual learning robustness. This observation complements the findings discussed in Section 5.1 regarding the implicit regularization effect of large pre-trained language models in supervised fine-tuning scenarios.

Domain-adaptive pre-training (DAPT) extends the standard pre-training paradigm by continuing the pre-training process on domain-specific unlabeled corpora, enabling models to specialize their internal representations to target domains such as biomedical text, scientific literature, legal documents, or financial data. A central challenge in DAPT is preventing the model from forgetting general linguistic knowledge acquired during initial pre-training while absorbing new domain-specific patterns. Soft-masking mechanisms have been proposed as a principled approach to address this tension: by identifying parameters critical to general knowledge and applying differential learning rates or gradient constraints based on parameter importance, these methods enable selective plasticity that updates domain-relevant parameters while preserving those encoding foundational language understanding. These selective update strategies share conceptual grounding with the parameter-efficient tuning methods reviewed in Section 5.1, where similar principles of restricting which parameters are modified underpin adapter-based and prompt-based continual fine-tuning approaches.

Knowledge integration techniques offer complementary strategies by explicitly combining representations or knowledge structures from the pre-trained model and the domain-adapted version. These approaches draw conceptual parallels to regularization-based continual learning methods such as Elastic Weight Consolidation and Synaptic Intelligence, adapting the core principle of penalizing deviation from important prior parameters to the pre-training context. The Fisher information matrix and related second-order approximations of parameter importance, well-established in the broader continual learning literature, have been explored in the language model pre-training context to identify which parameters encode the most critical general knowledge and should therefore be most strongly protected during domain adaptation. As noted in Section 5.1, computing exact Fisher information matrices remains computationally prohibitive at billion-parameter scale, and this challenge is equally acute—if not more so—in the continual pre-training setting.

An important consideration in continual domain-adaptive pre-training is the sequential nature of domain exposure: models may be required to adapt to a series of new domains over time, each introducing its own distribution shift, without access to data from previously seen domains. This setting closely mirrors the task-incremental continual learning scenario and introduces the risk of cumulative forgetting across multiple domain shifts. Methods that maintain episodic memory buffers storing representative samples from prior domain corpora, or that employ generative mechanisms to synthesize pseudo-data approximating past distributions, provide practical solutions but introduce their own challenges related to memory capacity and sample fidelity. These concerns echo those encountered with replay-based strategies in supervised NLP settings, where memory constraints and sample representativeness similarly limit practical applicability.
