The difficulty of scaling these approaches to the massive parameter counts and data volumes characteristic of modern LLMs remains an active research concern.

Cross-domain knowledge transfer is another central benefit enabled by continual pre-training strategies. When a model has been pre-trained on a broad general corpus and subsequently adapted to specialized domains, forward transfer allows domain-specific knowledge to benefit downstream tasks in related areas even when those tasks have not been explicitly encountered. This positive transfer effect is facilitated by the compositional and hierarchical nature of linguistic representations learned during general pre-training, which provide reusable feature abstractions applicable across diverse specialized contexts. Understanding the conditions under which continual domain-adaptive pre-training yields positive versus negative transfer across domain sequences constitutes an important open question, with task similarity, domain overlap, and corpus size all playing significant roles.

The interplay between continual pre-training and instruction tuning introduces additional complexity that serves as a natural bridge to the instruction tuning challenges examined in Section 5.3: models that undergo domain-adaptive pre-training must subsequently be instruction-tuned for task-following behavior, and the ordering and interaction of these training phases can substantially affect both domain knowledge retention and instruction-following capability [26]. Empirical investigations have shown that catastrophic forgetting during continual fine-tuning of LLMs is widespread across model scales ranging from one billion to seven billion parameters, with forgetting severity tending to increase with model size, underscoring the importance of carefully managing the pre-training and adaptation pipeline to preserve the broad capabilities developed during initial pre-training [26]. This finding directly motivates the dedicated treatment of continual instruction tuning that follows.

Overall, continual pre-training and domain-adaptive pre-training represent a practically significant frontier in continual learning for NLP, offering pathways to maintain and expand the capabilities of large language models as new domain knowledge becomes available over time. The methodological principles developed here—selective parameter updating, importance-weighted regularization, and memory-augmented replay—form a coherent thread running from the supervised continual learning methods of Section 5.1 through to the instruction tuning paradigm of Section 5.3. The field continues to develop more principled methods for balancing general and domain-specific knowledge, reducing computational costs associated with large-scale continual pre-training, and ensuring that adapted models retain the broad linguistic competencies that make them valuable across diverse downstream applications.

### 5.3 Continual Instruction Tuning for Large Language Models

## 5.3 Continual Instruction Tuning for Large Language Models

Building upon the foundations established by continual pre-training and domain-adaptive pre-training, the emergence of instruction tuning as a dominant paradigm for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human intent has introduced a new and particularly challenging dimension to continual learning research. While continual pre-training focuses on preserving general linguistic representations across evolving domain corpora, continual instruction tuning must additionally safeguard the model's acquired capacity to follow natural language instructions—a capability that sits at a higher level of abstraction and is uniquely vulnerable to sequential fine-tuning. When LLMs are sequentially fine-tuned on diverse instruction-following datasets spanning different NLP tasks and domains, they exhibit pronounced catastrophic forgetting, whereby performance on previously mastered instruction templates degrades sharply as new task-specific instructions are absorbed. This phenomenon is distinct from conventional continual learning in that it involves not merely forgetting discriminative feature representations, but rather degrading the model's generalized capacity to follow natural language instructions—a capability painstakingly acquired during large-scale instruction pre-training.

The interplay between catastrophic forgetting and instruction alignment degradation is a central concern in this setting. Unlike standard task-incremental classification, instruction-tuned LLMs must maintain broad generalization across heterogeneous task formats, including summarization, commonsense reasoning, question answering, and dialogue, while continuously acquiring new instruction-following competencies. Sequential fine-tuning without appropriate safeguards tends to erode both task-specific knowledge and the meta-level instruction comprehension that constitutes the core value of instruction-tuned models. The stability-plasticity dilemma [29; 1] manifests here with particular severity because the distributional diversity of instruction datasets is substantially greater than that encountered in typical image classification benchmarks. This challenge is compounded by the empirical finding noted in the preceding section that catastrophic forgetting during continual fine-tuning is widespread across model scales ranging from one billion to seven billion parameters, with forgetting severity tending to increase with model size [26], making the management of instruction alignment a concern that grows more pressing as models scale.

To systematically evaluate and compare methods in this emerging paradigm, the Continual Instruction Tuning Benchmark (CITB) and related evaluation frameworks have been proposed. These benchmarks sequence multiple NLP datasets as distinct instruction-following tasks and measure both forward and backward transfer alongside forgetting metrics, providing a principled basis for comparing diverse algorithmic approaches. Evaluation dimensions typically encompass average accuracy across all seen tasks, backward transfer capturing performance recovery or degradation on past tasks, and forward transfer measuring how instruction learning on one task aids performance on future tasks.

A range of methodological families have been explored for continual instruction tuning. **Data replay** approaches maintain a buffer of exemplars from prior instruction datasets and interleave them with current task data during fine-tuning, a strategy directly analogous to experience replay in standard continual learning [29]. While conceptually straightforward, replay in the instruction tuning context demands careful curation to ensure that replayed examples faithfully represent the diversity of prior instruction formats rather than merely their surface statistics.

**Model expansion** strategies address forgetting by augmenting the LLM architecture with task-specific modules or adapter layers for each new instruction domain, thereby avoiding direct overwriting of shared parameters. Such approaches draw inspiration from parameter isolation principles [1] and have been adapted to the LLM setting through lightweight adapter insertion, mixture-of-experts layers, and low-rank decomposition modules that can be selectively activated based on task identity or inferred from instruction context.

**Regularization-based methods** penalize updates to parameters deemed important for prior instruction tasks, building on the foundational intuition of weight importance estimation [1]. In the LLM context, computing exact Fisher information matrices is computationally prohibitive given the scale of modern transformer architectures, motivating approximate importance estimation schemes and structured sparsity constraints that operate efficiently at billion-parameter scale. This challenge mirrors the difficulties encountered in continual domain-adaptive pre-training, where second-order importance measures must similarly be approximated to remain tractable at scale.

**Prompt-based approaches** represent a particularly natural fit for continual instruction tuning, since prompts and prefix tokens can encode task-specific context without modifying the underlying model weights. By maintaining a pool of learnable prompt vectors associated with different instruction domains and routing inputs to the most relevant prompts, these methods achieve a degree of parameter isolation while preserving the full expressive capacity of the frozen backbone [38]. The frozen pre-trained backbone provides a stable representational foundation that inherently mitigates catastrophic forgetting of general language understanding, while prompt parameters absorb task-specific instruction knowledge.

A critical finding emerging from empirical studies in this area is that naïve sequential fine-tuning of instruction-tuned LLMs on new task collections causes disproportionate degradation of instruction-following alignment relative to the improvements gained on new tasks. This asymmetry underscores the importance of treating instruction alignment as a first-class continual learning objective rather than a mere byproduct of task accuracy. This concern connects naturally to the broader challenge of continual knowledge learning addressed in the following section: just as instruction-following capability must be preserved across sequential fine-tuning episodes, the factual knowledge embedded within model parameters must similarly be managed to prevent both inadvertent forgetting of valid information and inappropriate retention of outdated facts. Future work in this area will benefit from deeper integration of alignment-aware evaluation protocols with principled continual learning methodology, and from leveraging advances in parameter-efficient adaptation to enable scalable, privacy-preserving continual instruction tuning across evolving real-world task ecosystems.

### 5.4 Continual Knowledge Learning and Updating in Language Models

## 5.4 Continual Knowledge Learning and Updating in Language Models

Building upon the continual instruction tuning challenges examined in Section 5.3, where preserving instruction-following alignment across sequential fine-tuning episodes emerged as a critical concern, we now turn to a closely related but distinct problem: keeping the factual knowledge encoded within LLM parameters current and accurate over time. While continual instruction tuning focuses on maintaining the meta-level capability to follow natural language instructions, continual knowledge learning targets the fine-grained factual associations that constitute the substantive content of what a model knows. Large language models encode vast amounts of world knowledge within their parameters during pre-training; however, the world is inherently dynamic—facts change, new entities emerge, and outdated information becomes misleading over time. This creates a fundamental challenge distinct from standard continual learning scenarios, requiring targeted management of parametric knowledge rather than merely preserving broad distributional representations.

### Problem Formulation

The problem of **Continual Knowledge Learning (CKL)** specifically addresses how language models can acquire new factual knowledge, update outdated facts, and retain previously valid information in a sequential manner. Unlike conventional continual learning, which primarily focuses on task performance across shifting data distributions, CKL demands fine-grained control over individual factual associations stored within model weights. A related formulation, **Online Continual Knowledge Learning**, further constrains the setting by assuming that knowledge updates arrive in a streaming, single-pass fashion, mirroring real-world deployment conditions where models must adapt to continuously evolving information without the luxury of offline re-training.

These formulations highlight three distinct knowledge operations that a capable continual learner must support: (1) **acquisition** of entirely new facts not previously encountered, (2) **updating** of outdated or superseded facts with correct replacements, and (3) **retention** of still-valid knowledge that should not be perturbed. Standard continual learning objectives, which typically minimize task-specific losses over sequential distributions, are not naturally aligned with these fine-grained requirements, motivating dedicated benchmarks and evaluation frameworks. Notably, the retention objective echoes the concern for preserving instruction-following alignment raised in Section 5.3, but now applies at the level of individual factual associations rather than task-level behavioral competencies.

### Benchmarks and Evaluation Metrics

Evaluating continual knowledge learning requires benchmarks that can precisely measure what a model knows, when it learned it, and whether that knowledge remains accurate after subsequent updates. Effective benchmarks for CKL typically involve temporally structured knowledge corpora—such as news streams, encyclopedia snapshots across time, or domain-specific knowledge bases with known update timestamps—enabling controlled experiments on knowledge acquisition and decay.

Evaluation metrics in this space go beyond standard accuracy measures to include: **knowledge acquisition rate**, measuring how effectively a model incorporates new facts; **retention rate**, quantifying how much previously valid knowledge is preserved after updates; **update accuracy**, assessing whether outdated facts are correctly replaced by their successors; and **backward and forward knowledge transfer**, measuring whether learning new facts helps or harms related existing knowledge. These metrics collectively capture the multi-dimensional nature of knowledge management in dynamic language models, and distinguish CKL evaluation from standard NLP benchmarks that assess general language understanding at a single point in time. This multi-dimensional evaluation philosophy mirrors the benchmark design principles seen in the Continual Instruction Tuning Benchmark discussed in Section 5.3, where average accuracy, backward transfer, and forward transfer were similarly combined to capture the full scope of continual learning dynamics.

The challenge of realistic benchmark construction is compounded by the difficulty of clearly demarcating "knowledge" from "language skill" within LLM representations. A model may correctly answer a factual question by leveraging superficial linguistic patterns rather than genuine parametric knowledge storage, complicating the interpretation of benchmark results. Recent benchmark designs attempt to mitigate this by including counterfactual probes and paraphrase variants that test whether knowledge is robustly encoded rather than pattern-matched. These design considerations carry forward into the question answering and information retrieval benchmarks discussed in the following section, where analogous difficulties in standardizing evaluation protocols remain an open challenge.

### Why Standard Continual Learning Methods Fall Short

Standard continual learning methods developed for task-incremental or class-incremental settings exhibit several fundamental inadequacies when applied to the CKL problem.

**Regularization-based methods**, such as Elastic Weight Consolidation, protect important parameters from modification by penalizing deviations from previous parameter values. While effective at preserving task performance, these methods inherently resist the targeted, local modifications required for fact updating—the very parameters encoding a specific outdated fact must be selectively modified while preserving surrounding knowledge, a requirement that conflicts with broad parameter protection strategies [178]. This tension between protection and selective modification is more acute in CKL than in continual instruction tuning, where regularization can more coarsely protect instruction-following parameters without conflicting with the need for fine-grained factual updates.

**Replay-based methods** require storing exemplars of past knowledge to rehearse during future updates. In the knowledge domain, this translates to retaining large volumes of factual text, which quickly becomes intractable as the knowledge base grows. Moreover, replaying outdated facts could actively reinforce incorrect information, a hazard unique to the knowledge updating scenario. The replay paradigm also struggles with the open-ended, unstructured nature of factual knowledge, which does not decompose cleanly into discrete task boundaries suitable for buffer management.

**Architecture-based methods** that isolate parameters per task cannot scale to the granularity required for individual fact management, and the very notion of "task" becomes ill-defined when knowledge updates span overlapping semantic domains without clear boundaries.

**Continual pre-training approaches** [179] that continue self-supervised training on updated corpora can partially address knowledge acquisition but suffer from indiscriminate modification of model parameters, leading to unintended forgetting of valid existing knowledge. The self-supervised objective does not distinguish between facts that should be updated and those that should be retained, making it difficult to achieve the precision required by CKL.

Furthermore, large language models introduce unique scaling challenges for knowledge updating. The sheer number of parameters in modern LLMs means that even small learning rates applied during continual fine-tuning can produce broad interference effects, overwriting extensive regions of factual knowledge stored across distributed representations. The entanglement of factual knowledge with linguistic competencies within shared transformer representations makes surgical updates particularly difficult without inadvertently degrading general language capabilities—a concern that directly parallels the instruction alignment degradation observed during continual instruction tuning in Section 5.3.

### Connections to Adjacent Challenges

Continual knowledge learning intersects closely with the emerging area of **model editing**, which seeks to perform targeted, minimal interventions on LLM parameters to update specific facts. While model editing methods offer more surgical precision than full fine-tuning, they face scalability limitations when applied to large volumes of sequential knowledge updates and may introduce unintended side effects on semantically related knowledge.

The CKL problem also connects to **continual domain-adaptive pre-training** [179], where models are sequentially adapted to evolving domain corpora. However, domain adaptation targets distributional alignment rather than precise factual accuracy, and the two objectives may not be simultaneously optimizable with standard training recipes.

Addressing CKL effectively will likely require hybrid approaches that combine fine-grained parameter identification mechanisms—analogous to weight importance measures in regularization-based continual learning—with selective update strategies that can target specific factual associations while leaving surrounding knowledge intact. Progress in this area is essential for deploying LLMs in dynamic real-world settings where the cost of factual staleness is high, such as medical information systems, legal databases, and news-aware dialogue agents. As we will see in the following section, this challenge of maintaining accurate and current knowledge extends naturally into question answering and information retrieval systems, where both parametric and non-parametric knowledge stores must be jointly managed under continual learning constraints.

### 5.5 Continual Learning for Question Answering and Information Retrieval

## 5.5 Continual Learning for Question Answering and Information Retrieval

Building on the challenges of continual knowledge learning in language models discussed in the previous section, question answering (QA) and neural information retrieval (IR) present a closely related but distinct set of continual learning challenges. While Section 5.4 focused on updating parametric knowledge stored within model weights, QA and IR systems must additionally maintain coherent representations of evolving knowledge bases, handle topic shifts across query distributions, and preserve retrieval accuracy over dynamically growing document collections. Unlike image or text classification tasks, these systems integrate both parametric knowledge (encoded in model weights) and non-parametric knowledge (stored in retrieval indices or document stores), making the preservation of both types simultaneously difficult. When models are fine-tuned sequentially on new topics or domains, embedding space drift can cause previously learned query-document alignments to degrade, resulting in retrieval performance collapse on earlier topics—a manifestation of catastrophic forgetting specific to the retrieval context.

A central challenge in continual QA is that questions and their supporting evidence span diverse domains, and sequential training on new question types can cause the model to overwrite the semantic associations built during earlier training phases. As new documents or topics are introduced, retrieval indices may need to be updated, and the embedding model generating document representations must remain compatible with previously indexed content. This coupling between the embedding model and the retrieval index introduces a structural dependency that has no direct analogue in the classification or knowledge updating settings considered previously.

Several strategies drawn from the broader continual learning literature have been adapted to address these challenges. Replay-based approaches, in which representative question-answer pairs or document embeddings from previous tasks are stored and interleaved with new training data, offer a straightforward mechanism for mitigating forgetting in retrieval systems. This aligns with the broader experience replay paradigm [129], which has demonstrated effectiveness in maintaining performance across sequential tasks. However, storing raw text or full document representations can be memory-intensive, motivating the use of compressed or prototype-based memory representations.

Parameter-efficient tuning methods, such as prompt-based adaptation and low-rank decomposition, offer an alternative pathway for continual QA. By keeping large pre-trained language model backbones frozen and learning lightweight task-specific parameters, these approaches leverage the stability of pre-trained representations to reduce forgetting while enabling specialization to new QA domains [136]. The frozen backbone serves as a stable semantic anchor, ensuring that embedding spaces do not drift drastically when adapting to new retrieval tasks—a strategy that, as we will see in the following section, proves equally valuable for continual dialogue systems.

Meta-learning provides another promising direction, where models are trained to rapidly adapt to new question types or retrieval domains with minimal data, while retaining the ability to answer queries from prior domains. Approaches such as ANML [92] and self-attention meta-learners [99] demonstrate the utility of learning an adaptive prior that constrains updates during sequential learning, which can be directly applied to question answering pipelines where new topics must be incorporated without degrading performance on established ones.

Knowledge transfer also plays an important role in continual QA. Forward transfer—where representations learned from prior question domains facilitate faster learning on new ones—can be systematically encouraged through shared encoder architectures and task-similarity-aware training schedules [60; 61]. Backward transfer, though less studied in the QA context, offers the potential to improve performance on previously encountered question types as new, structurally related topics are learned.

In the neural IR setting, continual learning must address the challenge of maintaining a consistent embedding space as new documents are indexed. Incremental indexing strategies combined with continual fine-tuning of dense retrievers must balance two competing objectives: adapting the retrieval model to new document distributions while ensuring that previously indexed embeddings remain compatible with current query encodings. Distillation-based approaches, where a teacher model representing prior knowledge guides the student model during adaptation to new content, offer a principled mechanism for this alignment. This connects to broader knowledge distillation strategies widely employed across continual learning [1].

Despite these advances, significant open challenges remain. Evaluation protocols for continual QA and IR are not yet standardized, and most benchmarks focus on static snapshots rather than dynamically evolving document collections—a limitation that echoes the benchmark construction difficulties noted for continual knowledge learning in the previous section. Additionally, the interplay between parametric and non-parametric knowledge in retrieval-augmented systems introduces complexity not fully captured by existing continual learning frameworks, pointing toward the need for specialized theoretical analyses and benchmarks tailored to the QA and IR settings. These structural complexities also carry over into the dialogue systems domain, where multi-turn context dependencies and mixed-domain interactions pose analogous challenges for continual learning, as explored in the following section.

### 5.6 Continual Learning for Dialogue Systems and Task-Oriented Dialogue

## 5.6 Continual Learning for Dialogue Systems and Task-Oriented Dialogue

Building on the structural complexities of continual learning for question answering and information retrieval explored in the previous section, dialogue systems introduce an additional layer of challenge: unlike retrieval systems that primarily manage knowledge access, dialogue models must maintain coherent conversational behavior across multi-turn interactions, evolving user preferences, and expanding domain coverage. Dialogue systems represent a particularly demanding application domain for continual learning, as they must seamlessly acquire new conversational skills, adapt to new domains and intents, and handle evolving user preferences—all without forgetting previously mastered conversational behaviors. The deployment lifecycle of real-world dialogue systems inherently involves sequential updates: new domains are added, new intents are discovered, and user interaction patterns shift over time, making catastrophic forgetting a critical obstacle to maintaining robust conversational performance.

**Task-Oriented Dialogue and Domain Expansion.** Task-oriented dialogue (TOD) systems, which help users accomplish specific goals such as booking restaurants or querying schedules, face the challenge of continual domain expansion. As new service domains are introduced, these systems must learn new ontologies, slot types, and dialogue acts while retaining the ability to handle previously supported domains. The domain expansion problem is compounded by the fact that dialogue state tracking—a core component of TOD systems—requires maintaining consistent representations of conversational context across all supported domains. Naive sequential fine-tuning inevitably causes the model to forget slot filling and intent recognition capabilities acquired for earlier domains, degrading overall system reliability. This form of catastrophic forgetting in the dialogue context closely parallels the embedding space drift observed in continual neural IR, but manifests through degraded conversational coherence rather than retrieval performance collapse.

**Continual Prompt Tuning for Dialogue.** Prompt-based continual learning methods have emerged as particularly promising for dialogue systems, as they can leverage the rich conversational knowledge encoded in pre-trained language models while minimizing the risk of catastrophic forgetting. By freezing the backbone model and learning lightweight task-specific prompts sequentially, these approaches allow dialogue systems to accumulate domain-specific conversational patterns without interfering with general language understanding capabilities [167; 180]. As noted in the context of continual QA systems, frozen pre-trained backbones serve as stable semantic anchors that prevent drastic representation drift—a property that proves equally valuable here, where the number of conversational domains can grow substantially over time and full model fine-tuning for each new domain would be computationally prohibitive.

**Dynamic Architecture Expansion.** Dynamic architecture methods offer another avenue for continual dialogue learning, where new network capacity is allocated as new conversational domains or tasks are introduced. These approaches can accommodate the structural diversity of different dialogue domains by dedicating distinct architectural components—such as domain-specific adapters or modular networks—to each domain, while shared components capture cross-domain conversational patterns. Such strategies help balance the competing demands of domain specialization and cross-domain knowledge reuse, which is critical for dialogue systems where many conversational skills are transferable across domains. This modularity foreshadows the broader use of dynamic transformer architectures and Mixture-of-Experts adapter approaches that will be discussed in the context of multimodal continual learning in the following section.

**Knowledge Transfer and Data Augmentation.** Effective continual learning for dialogue systems requires not only preventing forgetting but also facilitating forward transfer, enabling knowledge from previously learned domains to accelerate learning in new ones. Knowledge transfer mechanisms such as distillation-based approaches allow the model to preserve soft predictions from earlier dialogue models, maintaining nuanced conversational capabilities while adapting to new domains. Data augmentation strategies, including the synthesis of plausible dialogue turns for previously seen domains, can supplement replay-based methods in dialogue settings where storing complete conversation histories may be privacy-sensitive.

**Intent Drift and Open-Domain Dialogue.** Beyond task-oriented settings, open-domain dialogue generation faces the challenge of intent drift, where the distribution of user intents and conversational styles evolves continuously. Continual learning for open-domain dialogue generation must handle not only new conversational topics but also subtle shifts in language use, cultural references, and conversational norms. These challenges are exacerbated by the absence of clear task boundaries in open-domain settings, making it difficult to apply standard continual learning protocols that assume discrete task transitions [7]. This absence of well-defined task boundaries is a recurring theme across the NLP application domains covered in this chapter, and it becomes even more pronounced in multimodal settings where visual and linguistic data streams may evolve at different rates.

**Unique Challenges in Dialogue Continual Learning.** Several challenges are unique or particularly acute in dialogue continual learning. First, dialogue systems involve multi-turn context dependencies, meaning that forgetting earlier conversational skills can cascade across turns and substantially degrade coherence. Second, evaluation of dialogue systems is inherently more complex than classification tasks, requiring assessment of response quality, contextual consistency, and domain accuracy simultaneously. Third, the interaction between continual learning and dialogue safety is non-trivial: a system that forgets prior safety constraints when learning new domains poses significant deployment risks. Finally, the need to handle mixed-domain dialogues—where a single conversation spans multiple domains—places additional demands on continual dialogue models to maintain concurrent access to knowledge from all learned domains. These structural complexities, particularly the need to manage multiple co-existing representational spaces without interference, carry forward naturally into the multimodal setting explored in the following section.

Addressing these challenges requires dialogue-specific adaptations of general continual learning principles, integrating prompt-based methods, architectural expansion, knowledge distillation, and domain-aware memory management into cohesive frameworks suited to the dynamic nature of conversational AI systems. As we will see in Section 5.7, many of these same principles—parameter isolation, distillation-based stabilization, and prompt-based adaptation—resurface as core strategies when continual learning is extended to vision-language models, where cross-modal alignment introduces yet another dimension of representational complexity to preserve.

### 5.7 Continual Learning for Multimodal and Vision-Language Models

## 5.7 Continual Learning for Multimodal and Vision-Language Models

Building upon the dialogue system challenges discussed in the previous section, continual learning for multimodal and vision-language models introduces an additional layer of complexity: models must simultaneously preserve cross-modal alignment between visual and linguistic representations while adapting to new tasks or domains. Models such as CLIP and large multimodal language models (MLLMs) represent a frontier where continual learning faces uniquely compounded challenges. When fine-tuned sequentially, they are susceptible to cross-modal alignment drift, wherein the learned correspondences between vision and language encoders degrade as the model adapts to new data distributions. This phenomenon is particularly severe because forgetting in one modality can cascade into degraded performance in the other, even when neither modality encoder individually exhibits substantial forgetting. These concerns directly motivate the more specialized treatment of continual instruction tuning for large multimodal models that follows in Section 5.8.

**Challenges in Multimodal Continual Learning**

The core difficulty in continually training multimodal models stems from their architectural complexity and the interdependency between modalities. Standard continual learning strategies developed for unimodal classifiers often transfer poorly to vision-language settings. Regularization-based methods, for instance, must account for cross-modal parameter interactions, where importance estimates computed for one modality may fail to protect representations critical for cross-modal reasoning. Similarly, replay-based methods must ensure that stored samples adequately represent both visual and linguistic aspects of prior experiences, complicating buffer design.

Zero-shot transfer preservation is another critical concern unique to large pre-trained vision-language models. These models are prized for their strong zero-shot generalization capabilities, which are often undermined by sequential fine-tuning. A key open challenge is therefore how to continually adapt such models to specialized downstream task sequences while preserving their broad zero-shot transfer abilities. This requires methods that can selectively update task-relevant parameters without disturbing the general-purpose multimodal representations learned during large-scale pre-training.

**Benchmarks for Multimodal Continual Learning**

The community has developed dedicated benchmarks to assess continual learning in multimodal and vision-language settings. CLiMB (Continual Learning in Multimodal Benchmarks) evaluates models on sequences of vision-language tasks, measuring not only task accuracy but also forgetting and forward transfer across heterogeneous multimodal scenarios. CoIN (Continual Instruction tuning) benchmarks address the specific challenge of sequentially fine-tuning instruction-following multimodal models, examining how instruction alignment and multimodal reasoning capabilities evolve over task sequences. These benchmarks reveal that off-the-shelf continual learning methods originally designed for image classification can fail substantially in multimodal settings due to the higher-dimensional and cross-modal nature of the forgetting dynamics—a finding that equally motivates the dedicated evaluation protocols discussed in Section 5.8 for large multimodal models under continual instruction tuning.

**Methods for Multimodal Continual Learning**

Several methodological families have emerged to address continual learning in vision-language models. Dynamic transformer architectures adapt the network structure in response to new tasks by allocating new parameters or modules while freezing those associated with previously learned tasks. This parameter isolation strategy mitigates cross-modal interference but must be carefully managed to prevent unbounded model growth across long task sequences.

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) adapter approaches offer a promising middle ground between full fine-tuning and strict parameter isolation. By routing inputs through task-specific expert modules while sharing a common backbone, MoE adapters enable efficient knowledge specialization without full parameter duplication. This approach is particularly attractive for multimodal models because it can maintain modality-specific and cross-modal knowledge in separate experts, reducing the risk of alignment drift when adapting to new domains. As will be seen in Section 5.8, this idea is further developed through Mixture-of-Experts LoRA formulations specifically tailored to multimodal instruction tuning.

Prompt-based approaches have gained significant traction for continual learning in large pre-trained models, and their extension to multimodal settings is a natural development. Prompt tuning and prefix tuning methods condition the frozen pre-trained model on learned task-specific prompts, leaving the underlying representations largely intact. In multimodal contexts, prompts can be designed jointly for visual and textual inputs, enabling coordinated cross-modal adaptation without disturbing the pre-trained alignment. Methods such as progressive prompts and shared attention frameworks demonstrate that small, learnable prompt vectors can steer multimodal models toward new tasks with minimal forgetting of prior ones, an approach well-suited to the continual instruction tuning setting.

Knowledge distillation remains a widely employed strategy to preserve prior knowledge during sequential multimodal fine-tuning. Feature-level distillation from a frozen copy of the model constrains the representation drift in both visual and language encoders, while output-level distillation preserves prior task predictions. Distillation is often combined with parameter-efficient tuning methods to achieve a balance between stability and plasticity within the multimodal representation space.

Zero-shot transfer preservation strategies specifically aim to maintain the broad generalization capability of models like CLIP during sequential fine-tuning. One approach constrains updates such that the cosine similarity between visual and textual embeddings of held-out anchor concepts does not degrade beyond a threshold. Another direction leverages the structure of the pre-trained embedding space as a regularizer, penalizing fine-tuning trajectories that significantly alter the geometry of cross-modal alignments.

**Biological and Architectural Inspirations**

Drawing from biologically inspired continual learning research, several multimodal methods incorporate mechanisms analogous to complementary learning systems, where fast-learning task-specific modules interact with a slow-learning shared backbone [82; 83]. In multimodal contexts, this translates to architectures where task-specific adapters or prompt modules serve as fast learners, rapidly encoding new cross-modal associations, while the shared pre-trained backbone acts as the slow learner preserving general multimodal representations across the continuum of tasks.

Hybrid approaches that combine regularization, replay, and architecture-based strategies have shown strong empirical results on multimodal benchmarks. For example, combining a small replay buffer of multimodal samples with lightweight adapter-based parameter isolation can simultaneously address cross-modal alignment drift and catastrophic forgetting of prior task-specific knowledge. Such hybrid paradigms echo the broader trend in continual learning of combining multiple mechanisms to achieve robustness that no single method can provide in isolation [86; 41].

**Open Challenges and Future Directions**

Despite recent progress, continual learning for multimodal and vision-language models remains an open problem. Key challenges include scaling to extremely long sequences of diverse multimodal tasks, handling cross-modal distribution shifts where visual and linguistic data evolve at different rates, and preserving zero-shot generalization while enabling meaningful task-specific adaptation. Evaluation methodologies must also evolve to capture the unique forgetting dynamics in cross-modal systems, going beyond scalar accuracy metrics to assess the integrity of learned cross-modal alignments throughout a task sequence. These challenges become even more pronounced in the specific context of continual instruction tuning for large multimodal models, which we examine in detail in the following section. Future work is likely to explore tighter integration of continual learning with the training objectives and architectures of foundation multimodal models, developing methods that are grounded in both the computational structure of these systems and the neuroscientific principles that inspire robust biological continual learning.

### 5.8 Continual Instruction Tuning for Large Multimodal Models

## 5.8 Continual Instruction Tuning for Large Multimodal Models

Building directly upon the multimodal continual learning challenges outlined in Section 5.7, continual instruction tuning for large multimodal models (LMMs) represents a particularly demanding instantiation of these problems. The rapid development of LMMs that jointly process vision and language inputs has introduced a new frontier where continual learning must simultaneously preserve alignment across visual and linguistic modalities while sequentially absorbing new vision-language tasks, making catastrophic forgetting an especially acute concern. As will become clear, the parameter-efficient strategies that emerge from this setting share deep connections with the broader parameter-efficient continual learning approaches for large language models discussed in Section 5.9.

When large multimodal language models are sequentially fine-tuned on diverse vision-language instruction datasets, they exhibit severe forgetting of previously learned capabilities. This phenomenon is compounded by the complexity of cross-modal alignment, where gradual drift in either the visual encoder or the language decoder can disrupt the carefully learned correspondences established during initial instruction tuning — echoing the cross-modal alignment drift problem introduced in Section 5.7, but now amplified by the instruction-following objective. Beyond forgetting of old knowledge, sequential multimodal instruction tuning also risks negative forward transfer, where learning earlier tasks degrades performance on subsequently introduced tasks due to conflicting representational demands.

To address these challenges, several methodological directions have been explored. Task-similarity-informed regularization approaches compute inter-task similarity scores to selectively apply stronger consolidation constraints to parameter subsets most relevant to previously learned vision-language alignments, thereby reducing forgetting without uniformly penalizing all weight updates. Such strategies naturally extend regularization-based methods — analogous in spirit to approaches developed for unimodal settings — into the multimodal domain, though the presence of dual encoders and cross-attention mechanisms introduces additional complexity beyond what standard regularization methods were designed to handle.

Mixture-of-Experts LoRA (MoELoRA) methods have emerged as a particularly promising direction, directly extending the Mixture-of-Experts adapter concept introduced in Section 5.7 into the low-rank adaptation regime. By maintaining a pool of low-rank adaptation modules organized as a mixture of experts, these approaches enable different subsets of parameters to specialize for different vision-language instruction tasks. The gating mechanism dynamically routes inputs to the most relevant expert adapters, reducing inter-task interference while maintaining a compact parameter footprint. This design draws conceptual parallels to mixture-of-experts strategies explored in continual reinforcement learning and classification settings [150], though MoELoRA specifically optimizes for parameter-efficient sequential adaptation under multimodal instruction-following objectives. Notably, the low-rank adaptation principle underlying MoELoRA also serves as a cornerstone of the parameter-efficient continual learning methods for unimodal LLMs discussed in the following section.

Prompt gradient projection methods offer another avenue for mitigating catastrophic forgetting in multimodal instruction tuning. By projecting gradient updates for new instruction-tuning tasks into subspaces orthogonal to those occupied by gradients from previously learned tasks, these approaches prevent destructive interference in shared parameters. This optimization-based perspective is closely related to gradient projection methods originally developed for continual learning in classification settings and adapted here to the specific challenges of multimodal prompting and instruction alignment. As will be seen in Section 5.9, orthogonality-based interference prevention emerges as a recurring principle across both multimodal and unimodal parameter-efficient continual learning.

Model expansion strategies represent a complementary approach, dynamically adding new parameters or adapter modules when new multimodal instruction domains are encountered, thus preserving previously learned capabilities in frozen components while allocating fresh capacity for new tasks. Such dynamic architecture growth strategies [127] balance the stability-plasticity trade-off by architectural means rather than purely through optimization constraints, paralleling the adapter-based expansion approaches discussed in Section 5.7 for broader multimodal continual learning.

Evaluation of these methods relies on benchmarks specifically designed to measure catastrophic forgetting in sequential vision-language instruction tuning scenarios — including the CoIN benchmark introduced in Section 5.7 — which assess both backward transfer, quantifying how much previously learned multimodal instruction-following capabilities degrade, and forward transfer, measuring whether earlier instruction-tuning phases benefit or hinder learning of subsequent tasks. Findings from such evaluations consistently reveal that naive sequential fine-tuning of large multimodal models produces dramatic performance drops on earlier instruction sets, underscoring the necessity of dedicated continual learning strategies.

Collectively, these developments highlight that continual instruction tuning for large multimodal models is a distinct and demanding problem that cannot be adequately addressed by simply porting methods from unimodal continual learning. The interplay between cross-modal alignment drift, instruction-following degradation, and the scale of modern multimodal architectures demands specialized algorithmic solutions that jointly consider efficiency, forgetting prevention, and positive knowledge transfer across heterogeneous vision-language task sequences. These efficiency-centered insights carry forward naturally into Section 5.9, where the focus shifts to parameter-efficient continual learning for unimodal large language models — a setting that shares the same fundamental tension between adaptation and forgetting but operates under distinct architectural and linguistic constraints.

### 5.9 Parameter-Efficient Continual Learning for Large Language Models

## 5.9 Parameter-Efficient Continual Learning for Large Language Models

Parameter-efficient continual learning (PECL) has emerged as a critical research direction for adapting large language models (LLMs) to sequential tasks without incurring the prohibitive costs of full fine-tuning or maintaining extensive replay buffers. As LLMs grow in scale, the challenge of catastrophic forgetting becomes entangled with computational and memory constraints, motivating the development of lightweight adaptation strategies that preserve prior knowledge while enabling acquisition of new capabilities. The methods explored in this area share a common philosophy with the multimodal instruction tuning strategies discussed in the preceding section — namely, leveraging parameter isolation and efficient adaptation to navigate the stability-plasticity trade-off — but focus specifically on unimodal language settings and the broader ecosystem of NLP tasks.

**Prompt-Based and Prefix Tuning Approaches**

Prompt-based methods have gained significant traction as parameter-efficient solutions for continual learning in LLMs. These approaches freeze the majority of pre-trained model parameters and instead learn small sets of soft tokens or prefix vectors that guide model behavior for specific tasks. The key advantage is that task-specific knowledge can be isolated within prompt parameters, substantially reducing interference between tasks. Progressive prompts represent one prominent instantiation, where prompts are grown incrementally as new tasks arrive, with earlier prompts frozen to prevent forgetting of prior task knowledge. This architecture allows the model to accumulate task-specific adaptations while sharing the powerful representational capacity of the frozen backbone. As noted in research on prompt-based continual learning [125], current prompt strategies may fall short of their full potential under self-supervised pre-training paradigms, since task-specific knowledge must be encoded into instructed representations via prompt parameters and subsequently predicted by uninstructed representations at test time. To address this limitation, hierarchical decomposition approaches explicitly optimize within-task prediction, task-identity inference, and task-adaptive prediction as distinct components, yielding substantial performance improvements across benchmarks such as Split CIFAR-100 and Split ImageNet-R [125].

**Shared Attention and Adapter-Based Frameworks**

Shared attention mechanisms and adapter modules offer complementary strategies for parameter-efficient continual learning. Adapter-based approaches insert small trainable modules between frozen transformer layers, enabling task-specific adaptations without modifying core model weights. This parameter isolation property directly mitigates catastrophic forgetting by ensuring that updates for new tasks do not overwrite representations critical for prior tasks. Shared attention frameworks explore the extent to which attention patterns can be shared across tasks, learning a minimal set of additional parameters that modulate attention computations in a task-aware manner. These methods balance the competing demands of knowledge sharing — which enables forward transfer — and parameter isolation — which prevents backward interference. The modular nature of adapter-based expansion allows the model capacity to grow gracefully as new tasks are encountered, though this introduces a trade-off between memory overhead for storing task-specific adapters and the computational efficiency gains from parameter sharing.

**Low-Rank Adaptation and Task Arithmetic**

Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) has emerged as a highly efficient approach for fine-tuning LLMs by decomposing weight updates into products of low-rank matrices, dramatically reducing the number of trainable parameters. When combined with task arithmetic principles — where task-specific adaptations are represented as vectors in weight space that can be composed, added, or subtracted — LoRA provides a flexible framework for continual learning. Task vectors learned via LoRA for individual tasks can potentially be combined to recover multi-task performance, offering a form of backward transfer that does not require access to previous task data. However, the composition of task vectors is not always interference-free, particularly when tasks induce conflicting gradient directions in the low-rank subspace. Research on continual learning in low-rank orthogonal subspaces [123] has demonstrated that enforcing orthogonality constraints between task-specific subspaces can substantially reduce interference, providing a principled mechanism for combining low-rank adaptation with continual learning objectives. This orthogonality principle is conceptually related to the gradient projection methods discussed in the context of multimodal instruction tuning, reflecting a shared underlying insight that preventing destructive interference in parameter space is fundamental to effective continual learning.

**Prompt Pooling and Dynamic Selection**

Prompt pooling strategies address the challenge of task-identity inference by maintaining a shared pool of learnable prompt components that are dynamically selected or combined based on input characteristics. Rather than assigning a fixed prompt to each task, pooling approaches learn a dictionary of prompt components and a selection mechanism that retrieves the most relevant components for a given input. This enables soft sharing of prompt knowledge across related tasks while maintaining sufficient task specificity to prevent forgetting. The selection mechanism can be trained jointly with the prompt components or learned separately using clustering or attention-based matching. Prompt pooling is particularly attractive in class-incremental settings where task identity is unavailable at inference time, as the selection mechanism can implicitly perform task inference based on input features.

**Trade-offs and Practical Considerations**

The trade-offs among different parameter-efficient continual learning approaches are multifaceted. Methods that achieve higher parameter efficiency — such as LoRA with very low rank or prompt tuning with few tokens — may sacrifice learning capacity, particularly for tasks that are highly dissimilar from the pre-training distribution. Conversely, adapter-based expansion strategies that grow model capacity with each task maintain strong per-task performance but accumulate memory overhead linearly with the number of tasks, undermining one of the key advantages of parameter efficiency. Memory considerations extend beyond model parameters to include the storage of task-specific adaptation modules, which may become prohibitive for long task sequences. Computational efficiency during inference is another critical dimension: methods that require task-identity information to select appropriate adapters or prompts incur additional inference overhead compared to methods with shared computation paths.

Empirical evaluations across diverse NLP benchmarks reveal that the relative performance of PECL methods depends strongly on the degree of task heterogeneity, the scale of the pre-trained model, and the evaluation scenario. In class-incremental settings without task identity, prompt pooling and hierarchical prompt decomposition methods tend to outperform simple prompt expansion approaches. The strength of the pre-trained initialization also plays a substantial role: stronger pre-trained representations reduce the need for task-specific adaptations, thereby reducing forgetting and improving forward transfer [13]. As LLMs continue to scale, parameter-efficient continual learning methods that exploit the rich representational capacity of frozen pre-trained models while adding minimal task-specific overhead represent one of the most promising directions for practical deployment of continual learning in NLP systems. These considerations carry forward naturally into the multilingual setting explored in the following section, where the additional complexity of cross-lingual alignment and typological diversity further constrains the design of efficient and forgetting-resistant adaptation strategies.

### 5.10 Continual Learning in Multilingual and Cross-Lingual NLP

## 5.10 Continual Learning in Multilingual and Cross-Lingual NLP

Multilingual and cross-lingual settings introduce a distinct and particularly challenging dimension to continual learning in NLP, extending the parameter-efficient adaptation challenges discussed in the preceding section into a regime where linguistic diversity, typological variation, and cross-lingual alignment structures impose additional constraints on the stability-plasticity trade-off. Massively multilingual pre-trained models such as mBERT and XLM-R encode representations across dozens or hundreds of languages within a shared parameter space, making them especially susceptible to catastrophic forgetting when sequentially fine-tuned or extended to new languages, dialects, or cross-lingual tasks. The fundamental challenge arises because updating shared multilingual representations to accommodate new linguistic knowledge can overwrite or distort previously acquired language-specific and cross-lingual alignment structures — structures that are precisely what the parameter-efficient methods of the preceding section aim to preserve.

A central concern in this setting is the dynamics of forgetting across languages of varying typological similarity. When a multilingual model is continually adapted to a new language, the degree of forgetting experienced by previously learned languages depends strongly on their linguistic proximity to the newly introduced language. Languages sharing morphological structures, syntactic patterns, or lexical overlap with the new language tend to experience less forgetting, as the model can exploit shared representations. In contrast, typologically distant languages suffer more pronounced degradation, suggesting that language similarity is a critical factor governing the stability-plasticity trade-off in multilingual continual learning.

Learning rate scheduling has emerged as a deceptively simple yet important mitigation strategy in this context. Aggressive learning rates during sequential language adaptation cause rapid parameter drift that disproportionately harms low-resource and typologically isolated languages already underrepresented in the original pre-training data. Gradual warm-up schedules, layer-wise learning rate decay, and discriminative fine-tuning strategies that apply smaller updates to lower layers — where more universal cross-lingual features are encoded — help preserve multilingual generalization while accommodating new linguistic knowledge.

Beyond learning rate strategies, researchers have explored regularization-based approaches adapted for the multilingual setting. Elastic Weight Consolidation and its variants can be applied to protect parameters identified as critical for previously learned languages, although computing language-specific Fisher information matrices for each of potentially hundreds of languages introduces significant computational overhead. Parameter-efficient adaptation methods, including adapter modules and LoRA-based approaches inserted per language or per task, offer a more scalable alternative by isolating language-specific or task-specific updates while keeping the shared backbone frozen or minimally perturbed, thereby preserving cross-lingual transfer capabilities. This connection to the adapter and LoRA strategies surveyed in the preceding section highlights the unifying role that parameter isolation plays across both monolingual and multilingual continual learning scenarios.

The challenge of extending multilingual models to entirely new languages — particularly low-resource languages or dialects with limited training data — is especially acute. In these scenarios, the model must achieve positive forward transfer from related languages while avoiding negative backward transfer to existing languages. Vocabulary expansion for new scripts or orthographic systems compounds the difficulty, as embedding layer modifications can destabilize representations throughout the network. Specialized strategies such as vocabulary-constrained continual learning and script-aware initialization of new token embeddings have been proposed to address these issues.

Cross-lingual task transfer in the continual learning regime also raises questions about how task-specific knowledge learned in one language generalizes to the same task in other languages encountered later. Ideally, a continually trained multilingual model should exhibit zero-shot cross-lingual transfer for new task-language combinations without explicit retraining, but sequential task-language ordering can impair this capability if task-specific representations become too narrowly tuned to the languages present during fine-tuning. Replay-based methods that maintain small buffers of multilingual exemplars and periodically rehearse diverse language-task combinations have shown promise in preserving broad cross-lingual generalization.

Evaluation of continual learning in multilingual settings requires metrics that go beyond average accuracy across tasks to assess per-language forgetting rates, cross-lingual transfer efficiency, and the model's ability to maintain zero-shot performance on unseen language-task combinations. These requirements — for fine-grained, multi-dimensional evaluation that captures both forgetting and transfer dynamics — connect directly to the broader evaluation challenges surveyed in the following section, where the design of rigorous benchmarks, standardized protocols, and comprehensive metrics emerges as a critical concern for the entire NLP continual learning community. The construction of appropriate benchmarks that cover diverse language families, scripts, and resource levels remains an active area of development. Overall, multilingual continual learning represents a microcosm of the broader challenges in the field — balancing stability and plasticity — but amplified by the additional complexity of linguistic diversity, typological variation, and the critical importance of preserving cross-lingual alignment structures built during large-scale pre-training.

### 5.11 Benchmarks, Evaluation Protocols, and Metrics for NLP Continual Learning

## 5.11 Benchmarks, Evaluation Protocols, and Metrics for NLP Continual Learning

Building on the domain-specific and multilingual challenges surveyed in preceding subsections, rigorous evaluation of continual learning methods in natural language processing and multimodal settings demands carefully designed benchmarks, standardized protocols, and comprehensive metrics that go beyond simple accuracy measurement. Unlike computer vision benchmarks, NLP continual learning evaluation must account for the semantic richness, compositional structure, and diverse task formats inherent to language data, making the design of fair and informative evaluation frameworks particularly challenging. The difficulties identified in multilingual and cross-lingual settings—such as per-language forgetting, cross-lingual transfer efficiency, and the need for diverse language family coverage—further underscore why evaluation frameworks must be both broad and fine-grained.

### Benchmarks Across NLP Task Categories

The NLP continual learning community has developed benchmarks spanning multiple task categories. For **text classification**, datasets such as Amazon product review corpora, Yelp sentiment datasets, and domain-shifted news classification collections have been organized into sequential task streams to evaluate how models retain discriminative knowledge while adapting to new textual domains. For **question answering**, benchmarks incorporate sequential streams of reading comprehension datasets such as SQuAD, TriviaQA, and Natural Questions, where models must maintain retrieval and comprehension accuracy across diverse topics and question types as new knowledge is incorporated.

For **dialogue systems**, benchmarks simulating sequential domain expansion in task-oriented dialogue have been proposed, wherein models must learn new conversational intents and slot types without forgetting previously mastered dialogue capabilities. For **instruction tuning**, the Continual Instruction Tuning Benchmark (CITB) has emerged as a structured evaluation platform that sequences diverse NLP instruction-following tasks to measure how large language models degrade when fine-tuned incrementally on heterogeneous instructions. For **knowledge updating**, specialized benchmarks measure a model's capacity to integrate new factual information while retaining previously acquired world knowledge, evaluating both the accuracy of updated facts and the preservation of unrelated prior knowledge.

Multimodal continual learning benchmarks such as CLiMB and CoIN extend these principles to vision-language settings, sequencing tasks that require joint understanding of visual and textual inputs and measuring cross-modal forgetting alongside unimodal retention. These multimodal benchmarks are particularly relevant given the growing deployment of large vision-language models in real-world applications where both linguistic and perceptual knowledge must be preserved across sequential updates.

### Evaluation Protocols

Evaluation protocols in NLP continual learning typically follow either an **offline** protocol, where model performance is assessed after all tasks in the sequence have been learned, or an **online** protocol, where performance is evaluated after each new task is introduced. The offline protocol enables measurement of final accumulated knowledge, while the online protocol captures the dynamics of forgetting and adaptation throughout the learning trajectory.

A critical design choice in evaluation protocols concerns **task boundary information**: some protocols assume explicit task identity is available at test time, while others simulate more realistic deployment conditions where task identity is unknown, forcing models to perform task inference alongside knowledge retention. The choice between these settings substantially affects the relative difficulty of benchmarks and the suitability of different algorithmic families. This distinction is especially consequential in NLP settings such as open-domain question answering or multilingual inference, where task identity at deployment time is rarely explicitly signaled.

Standardized protocols also specify whether models are evaluated under **task-incremental**, **class-incremental**, or **domain-incremental** scenarios, each imposing distinct constraints on how knowledge must be organized and retrieved. In class-incremental NLP settings, for instance, models must distinguish between classes from all previously encountered tasks using a single unified classifier, which is especially challenging for language models that tend to overwrite representations when fine-tuned on new label sets. Each of these scenarios reflects a different real-world deployment condition, and the choice of scenario substantially shapes which continual learning strategies are most effective.

### Evaluation Metrics and Dimensions

Comprehensive evaluation of NLP continual learners requires metrics along multiple dimensions:

**Stability** measures how well a model retains performance on previously learned tasks after training on subsequent ones. The standard metric is **Backward Transfer (BWT)**, computed as the average change in performance on task $i$ after all subsequent tasks have been learned, with negative values indicating forgetting.

**Plasticity** quantifies how effectively a model acquires new task knowledge. This is typically measured through **Forward Transfer (FWT)**, which captures the influence that learning task $i$ has on subsequent performance on task $j > i$, with positive values indicating beneficial transfer of prior knowledge.

**Average Accuracy (AA)** across all tasks at the end of training provides an overall performance summary but conflates stability and plasticity effects without disentangling their individual contributions.

**Forgetting Measure (FM)** explicitly quantifies the average maximum degradation experienced across previously learned tasks, providing a direct measure of catastrophic forgetting severity.

Beyond these standard metrics, recent work has highlighted important limitations of existing measurement approaches. Several studies have noted that standard metrics do not account for the increasing difficulty of classification tasks as more classes are introduced, leading to what has been termed **setup-induced forgetting**—performance degradation attributable to task difficulty growth rather than genuine forgetting of previously acquired knowledge [36]. To address this, newer metric proposals normalize performance against the inherent difficulty of the expanded classification problem, providing more informative assessments of the true stability-plasticity trade-off achieved by continual learners [36].

Additionally, **Generalizability** has been proposed as a third evaluation dimension complementing stability and plasticity, capturing a model's ability to transfer knowledge to unseen tasks or domains beyond those encountered during training [181]. This three-dimensional evaluation framework—measuring stability, plasticity, and generalizability simultaneously—enables a more nuanced understanding of a model's continual learning profile and highlights trade-offs that binary metrics obscure. Empirical work has demonstrated that this unified metric is more sensitive to the impact of task ordering and knowledge distillation choices, making it particularly informative for practical deployment scenarios [181].

**Knowledge Retention Rate** metrics specific to factual knowledge updating evaluate the proportion of previously learned facts that remain accurately retrievable after new information is integrated, complementing task-level accuracy metrics with fine-grained assessments of semantic memory integrity. Such metrics are directly relevant to the knowledge-updating challenges discussed in prior subsections, where factual drift and temporal inconsistency represent distinct failure modes beyond standard task forgetting.

### Challenges and Future Directions

Despite progress in benchmark and metric design, significant challenges remain. Many existing NLP continual learning benchmarks employ small numbers of tasks with relatively uniform sizes, which do not reflect real-world scenarios involving long task sequences, heterogeneous task difficulty distributions, and highly imbalanced data volumes across tasks [57]. Furthermore, benchmark design choices—including task ordering, class distribution, and task boundary definition—critically affect algorithm comparison outcomes, making cross-paper comparisons unreliable when evaluation protocols differ.

There is also a pressing need for benchmarks that incorporate **naturally occurring distribution shifts** rather than artificially constructed task boundaries, better reflecting how language and knowledge evolve continuously in real deployments. This need is particularly acute for multilingual settings, where typological diversity, script variation, and resource imbalance across languages require evaluation protocols that go well beyond standard task-incremental streams to assess per-language forgetting rates, zero-shot cross-lingual transfer, and the preservation of cross-lingual alignment structures. The fragmentation of evaluation protocols across the continual NLP literature underscores the need for community-wide standardization efforts, unified software frameworks, and agreed-upon metrics that jointly assess stability, plasticity, forward transfer, backward transfer, and knowledge retention across diverse NLP and multimodal tasks.

## 6 Continual Learning in Reinforcement Learning, Robotics, and Specialized Domains

### 6.1 Foundations of Continual Reinforcement Learning

## 6.1 Foundations of Continual Reinforcement Learning

Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) represents the intersection of two fundamentally challenging paradigms: reinforcement learning (RL), where agents learn through interaction with an environment via reward signals, and continual learning, where knowledge must be accumulated sequentially without catastrophic forgetting of prior skills. In standard RL, an agent operates within a Markov Decision Process (MDP) defined by a tuple $(\mathcal{S}, \mathcal{A}, \mathcal{T}, \mathcal{R}, \gamma)$, comprising a state space $\mathcal{S}$, action space $\mathcal{A}$, transition dynamics $\mathcal{T}$, reward function $\mathcal{R}$, and discount factor $\gamma$. Continual RL generalizes this framework to a sequence of MDPs or a single non-stationary MDP, where the transition dynamics $\mathcal{T}_t$ and reward function $\mathcal{R}_t$ may change over time, rendering the environment fundamentally non-stationary. Understanding the precise nature of this non-stationarity is a prerequisite for developing effective detection and adaptation strategies, which we examine in detail in Section 6.2.

### Taxonomy of Non-Stationarity

Non-stationarity in CRL environments can be systematically categorized along several axes. First, **task-level non-stationarity** occurs when an agent faces a discrete sequence of distinct tasks, each defined by a different reward function or environmental context, analogous to the task-incremental scenario in supervised continual learning. Second, **gradual environmental drift** describes scenarios where transition dynamics or reward landscapes change smoothly and continuously over time, without clear task boundaries. Third, **abrupt context switches** characterize environments where the MDP parameters change sharply at specific time points, often without explicit signaling to the agent. Fourth, **partial observability and latent non-stationarity** arise when the sources of change are not directly observable by the agent, necessitating the inference of hidden environment states or context variables to maintain effective policies.

This taxonomic perspective is closely aligned with the broader continual learning literature [4], which distinguishes between scenarios based on the nature and timing of distributional shifts. In the RL setting, however, non-stationarity is compounded by the fact that the data distribution itself depends on the agent's own policy, creating a fundamentally different and more complex learning dynamic than in supervised continual learning. Each category of non-stationarity in this taxonomy correspondingly demands different detection mechanisms and adaptation strategies, motivating the statistical and latent-variable approaches discussed in the following section.

### Unique Challenges of Continual Reinforcement Learning

Combining RL with continual learning introduces a set of challenges that go beyond those encountered in supervised settings. In supervised continual learning, the primary concern is catastrophic forgetting of previously learned input-output mappings when training on new data distributions [2]. In CRL, forgetting manifests in the degradation of previously learned policies and value functions, but the consequences are further amplified by several RL-specific factors.

**Reward non-stationarity** poses a critical challenge: when the reward function changes, the agent must not only learn a new policy but also revise its internal representations of value, potentially overwriting knowledge encoded in value function approximators that was useful for prior tasks. Unlike supervised learning, where labels directly supervise model outputs, reward signals in RL are delayed, sparse, and shaped by the agent's behavior, making disentangling useful prior knowledge from outdated value estimates considerably harder.

**Environment dynamics shift** introduces another layer of complexity. When transition dynamics $\mathcal{T}$ change, a policy that was previously optimal may become suboptimal or even dangerous in safety-critical applications. Agents must therefore detect changes in dynamics, adapt their models of the world, and update their policies accordingly, all while retaining skills and representations that remain useful across the transition [10].

**Exploration and non-stationarity interaction** further complicates CRL. In stationary MDPs, exploration strategies can be designed with the knowledge that the environment is fixed. In non-stationary environments, the agent must simultaneously explore to detect changes, exploit current knowledge to maintain performance, and avoid forgetting prior skills—a three-way tension that has no direct analogue in supervised continual learning [9]. This tension is precisely what reactive exploration strategies, introduced in Section 6.2, are designed to resolve.

**Temporal credit assignment under non-stationarity** is also substantially harder in CRL than in standard RL. When both the policy and the environment are changing simultaneously, attributing long-term outcomes to specific past actions becomes extremely difficult, as the mapping from actions to future rewards is itself non-stationary.

### Computational Complexity Considerations

The difficulty of continual reinforcement learning is not merely empirical but has deep theoretical roots. The memory complexity results established for supervised continual learning [12] provide important lower bounds: any continual learner that aims to maintain performance across $k$ tasks requires memory growing at least linearly with $k$. In the RL setting, where each "task" corresponds to a different MDP or environmental context, these bounds imply that agents cannot maintain optimal value functions across all encountered MDPs without proportionally growing memory resources. These theoretical constraints directly motivate the adaptive memory mechanisms discussed in Section 6.2, which seek to manage finite memory budgets intelligently under non-stationarity.

Furthermore, maintaining optimal value functions under changing environments requires solving a sequence of potentially conflicting optimization problems. Each optimal value function $V^*(s)$ is defined with respect to a specific MDP; when the MDP changes, the optimal value function changes accordingly. Attempting to maintain all prior value functions within a fixed-capacity neural network approximator faces the same representational interference that drives catastrophic forgetting in supervised settings [2], but with the additional complication that value function targets are themselves non-stationary during RL training even within a single task.

The non-stationarity of bootstrapped targets in temporal-difference learning—a core algorithmic principle underlying methods like Q-learning and actor-critic algorithms—means that the effective distribution of training data for the value function approximator is constantly changing, even before environmental non-stationarity is introduced.
