This creates a particularly challenging optimization landscape where standard convergence guarantees of RL algorithms may no longer apply, and the interaction between algorithmic non-stationarity and environmental non-stationarity can lead to compounding instability.

### Relationship to Supervised Continual Learning Foundations

While CRL shares the fundamental challenge of catastrophic forgetting with supervised continual learning [1], the unique structure of the RL problem motivates specialized algorithmic approaches. Replay-based methods, which have proven effective in supervised settings [129], must be adapted to account for the off-policy nature of RL algorithms and the fact that stored transitions may become stale under changing dynamics—a concern that directly informs the adaptive memory management strategies examined in Section 6.2. Regularization-based methods face the challenge that the importance of parameters for maintaining good policies is harder to estimate than for supervised loss functions.

The benchmark community has responded to these challenges by developing specialized evaluation frameworks for CRL [9], which assess not only catastrophic forgetting but also forward transfer, backward transfer, and adaptation speed—metrics that capture the full scope of challenges facing continual reinforcement learning agents in realistic deployment scenarios. These foundational considerations collectively underscore the necessity of principled algorithmic and theoretical frameworks that address the distinctive difficulties of learning in non-stationary, reward-driven environments over extended time horizons, and they set the stage for the detection and adaptation methodologies presented in the sections that follow.

### 6.2 Non-Stationarity Detection and Adaptation Strategies

## 6.2 Non-Stationarity Detection and Adaptation Strategies

A fundamental challenge in continual reinforcement learning, arising directly from the non-stationary MDP formulation introduced in Section 6.1, is equipping agents with the ability to detect and respond appropriately to changes in their environment. Non-stationarity in reinforcement learning manifests in diverse forms, including shifts in reward functions, transition dynamics, observation distributions, and task objectives—spanning the full taxonomy of abrupt context switches, gradual environmental drift, and latent non-stationarity described previously. Effective adaptation requires agents to first recognize that a meaningful change has occurred and then selectively update their policies while preserving knowledge that remains relevant. The strategies examined in this section directly address the three-way tension between detection, adaptation, and retention that was identified in Section 6.1 as one of the defining challenges distinguishing continual RL from its supervised counterpart.

### Statistical Change-Point Detection

Statistical change-point detection methods form a cornerstone of non-stationarity detection in sequential decision-making environments. These approaches monitor statistical properties of incoming data streams—such as reward distributions, state transition frequencies, or prediction errors—and trigger adaptation mechanisms when detected deviations exceed predefined thresholds. Classical techniques including cumulative sum (CUSUM) algorithms and sequential probability ratio tests have been adapted to reinforcement learning contexts, where they can signal task boundaries or environment shifts without requiring explicit external labels. The identification of change points allows agents to reset or adjust their learning rates, reinitialize exploration policies, or switch between stored behavioral policies suited to recurring environment configurations. This detection capability is particularly critical for handling the abrupt context switches and gradual environmental drift identified in the taxonomy of Section 6.1, as different detection sensitivities are required for each category of non-stationarity.

### Reactive Exploration Strategies

Beyond passive detection, reactive exploration strategies enable agents to actively probe their environments following detected shifts, directly resolving the three-way tension between exploration, exploitation, and forgetting identified in Section 6.1. When a change is identified, agents may temporarily increase their exploration rate to gather informative samples about the new environmental dynamics, leveraging epistemic uncertainty measures to guide this exploratory behavior efficiently. Such strategies draw on the insight that the stability-plasticity trade-off inherent in continual learning must be dynamically balanced: excessive stability leads to failure when genuine change occurs, while excessive plasticity results in catastrophic forgetting of still-valid knowledge [15]. Reactive exploration thus serves as a bridge between detection and adaptation, ensuring that the agent collects sufficient experience to support accurate policy updates without discarding valuable historical knowledge prematurely. This reactive mechanism also lays the groundwork for the more proactive epistemic uncertainty-driven exploration strategies discussed in the following section, which extend these principles to the broader challenge of lifelong policy optimization.

### Latent Variable Models for Environment Representation

Latent variable models offer a principled framework for representing the underlying factors driving non-stationarity in reinforcement learning environments, providing a natural tool for addressing the partial observability and latent non-stationarity category identified in Section 6.1. By maintaining compact latent representations that capture the current context or environment configuration, these models enable agents to infer whether observed changes reflect genuine distributional shifts or merely stochastic variability. Variational autoencoder-based approaches and recurrent latent variable models have been explored in this context, allowing agents to encode context vectors that modulate policy behavior and learning dynamics. These latent representations can also support multi-task generalization, with agents learning to recognize familiar environment configurations and reuse previously acquired policies, thereby reducing the sample complexity of adaptation. This approach connects closely to task inference mechanisms relevant to the broader continual learning literature, where identifying the current task or context is essential for appropriate memory retrieval and knowledge application [140]. The context vectors produced by these models further serve as natural inputs to the adaptive memory mechanisms described in the following subsection, enabling principled decisions about which stored experience remains relevant under the inferred current environment configuration.

### Adaptive Memory Mechanisms

Adaptive memory mechanisms address a central tension in non-stationary reinforcement learning that was foreshadowed in Section 6.1: historical experience may be highly informative for future performance but may also become obsolete or misleading as the environment evolves. The theoretical memory bounds established for supervised continual learning carry over to the RL setting, motivating intelligent management of finite memory budgets rather than indiscriminate retention of all past experience. Experience replay buffers, widely used in deep reinforcement learning to improve sample efficiency, require careful management under non-stationarity to avoid reinforcing outdated behavioral patterns [129]. Strategies for adaptive memory management include importance-weighted sample retention, where transitions from recently changed environment configurations are prioritized, and principled forgetting mechanisms that decay the influence of samples from distant past configurations. Reservoir sampling provides a simple baseline for maintaining diverse historical experience, while more sophisticated approaches selectively retain samples based on their relevance to the current policy gradient or temporal recency.

### Balancing Retention and Forgetting of Outdated Information

A recurring theme across these strategies is the need to distinguish between knowledge that remains valid across environment changes and knowledge that has been superseded by new dynamics—a challenge that is more acute in CRL than in supervised settings due to the non-stationarity of bootstrapped value function targets discussed in Section 6.1. Methods inspired by biological memory consolidation [138] model synaptic complexity at multiple timescales, allowing slow-changing components of the environment to be encoded in stable long-term representations while fast-changing aspects are captured by more plastic short-term components. Similarly, neuromodulatory mechanisms [182] have been proposed to dynamically regulate the learning rate of individual parameters based on their estimated relevance to stable versus volatile aspects of the environment, providing a biologically inspired solution to the challenge of selectively updating knowledge in response to non-stationarity.

Together, these methodological families—statistical detection, reactive exploration, latent variable modeling, and adaptive memory management—constitute the toolkit available to continual reinforcement learning researchers for building agents capable of detecting, responding to, and recovering from the diverse forms of non-stationarity encountered in realistic sequential decision-making environments. These mechanisms collectively set the stage for the exploration strategy design and policy optimization approaches examined in the following section, which build upon this detection-and-adaptation foundation to address the sustained, long-horizon learning challenges that define the lifelong reinforcement learning problem.

### 6.3 Exploration Strategies and Policy Optimization in Lifelong RL

Exploration strategies occupy a central role in lifelong and continual reinforcement learning, where agents must continuously discover useful behaviors across shifting task distributions while avoiding the twin pitfalls of excessive forgetting and insufficient adaptation. Building on the detection and adaptation mechanisms discussed in the previous section, this subsection examines how exploration strategy design and policy optimization interact to shape an agent's capacity for sustained learning across non-stationary sequential decision-making environments. Unlike standard reinforcement learning settings that assume stationary environments, lifelong RL requires agents to balance exploiting previously acquired policies with exploring novel state-action spaces that may emerge as the environment dynamics or reward functions evolve over time. This dual demand places unique pressure on both the exploration mechanism and the underlying policy optimization algorithm.

Within policy-gradient families, methods such as proximal policy optimization and actor-critic architectures offer natural mechanisms for smooth policy updates that can be regulated to prevent catastrophic forgetting of earlier behavioral competencies. However, vanilla policy-gradient approaches tend to be sensitive to distributional shift, as the on-policy nature of gradient estimation means that outdated experience is quickly discarded. Q-learning families, including deep Q-networks and their variants, offer off-policy alternatives that can in principle retain and replay diverse experience from non-stationary streams, though they face their own challenges around value function instability when reward landscapes change abruptly. Comparative analyses of these families in continual domain shifts suggest that neither paradigm uniformly dominates; rather, the optimal choice depends critically on the degree of task similarity, the frequency of distributional transitions, and the availability of task-boundary signals.

Automatic curriculum learning has emerged as a promising strategy for structuring the sequential presentation of tasks so that earlier, simpler experiences scaffold the acquisition of later, more complex behaviors. By adaptively selecting which tasks or environments to present based on agent competence, curriculum-based approaches can accelerate knowledge accumulation and reduce interference between tasks, effectively serving as a form of guided exploration that steers the agent toward productive regions of the state-action space. This principle connects to broader ideas of developmental learning discussed in the continual learning literature [10], where the progressive acquisition of skills mirrors developmental trajectories observed in biological organisms.

Epistemic uncertainty-driven exploration provides a complementary mechanism for guiding agents in non-stationary environments. By maintaining calibrated estimates of uncertainty over value functions or policy parameters, agents can direct exploratory behavior toward states where their knowledge is most unreliable, potentially coinciding with regions where environmental dynamics have shifted. This perspective extends naturally from the reactive exploration strategies described previously, where detected changes trigger targeted information gathering. Bayesian approaches and ensemble methods have been explored in this context, enabling agents to distinguish between aleatoric uncertainty arising from irreducible stochasticity and epistemic uncertainty attributable to insufficient experience with recently changed dynamics. Such uncertainty quantification supports more principled decisions about when to explore versus exploit and when to trigger adaptation of stored policies.

A particularly challenging and practically important setting involves task-agnostic online learning, where agents must adapt to non-stationary dynamics without being provided with explicit task-boundary signals, pre-training phases, or task identity information during deployment. In such settings, the agent must simultaneously detect distributional shifts, decide which prior knowledge to retain or update, and continue improving its performance, all from a single streaming experience. Methods addressing this regime often combine change-point detection with adaptive memory mechanisms, enabling the agent to automatically identify when the environment has changed sufficiently to warrant significant policy revision. The absence of pre-training assumptions makes these approaches broadly applicable to real-world scenarios where task sequences are unknown in advance and may be irregular in length and content, aligning with the broader motivation for continual learning articulated throughout the literature [1].

The interplay between exploration strategy and policy optimization is further complicated in multi-task continual RL by the phenomenon of task interference, wherein gradient updates for newly encountered tasks can destructively overwrite policy representations useful for earlier tasks. Gradient projection and subspace orthogonality techniques, inspired by their successful application in supervised continual learning [158], have begun to be adapted for reinforcement learning settings to ensure that policy gradient updates for new tasks do not catastrophically disrupt previously learned value functions or behavioral primitives. By constraining policy updates to lie within subspaces that are approximately orthogonal to the gradient directions of prior tasks, such methods aim to preserve behavioral stability while maintaining sufficient plasticity for new task acquisition. This gradient-level approach to mitigating interference connects directly to the architectural and memory-based strategies examined in the following section, which address the same stability-plasticity challenge through structural modifications to the agent's representational capacity.

Together, these advances in exploration strategy design and policy optimization represent a growing toolkit for lifelong RL practitioners, though significant challenges remain in scaling these methods to long task sequences with heterogeneous dynamics, developing theoretically grounded guarantees on exploration efficiency under non-stationarity, and establishing evaluation protocols that fairly assess both knowledge retention and adaptive capability across diverse continual reinforcement learning benchmarks.

### 6.4 Architecture and Memory Approaches for Continual RL

## Architectural and Memory-Based Approaches in Continual Reinforcement Learning

Architectural and memory-based approaches represent a critical frontier in addressing catastrophic forgetting within continual reinforcement learning (CRL), where agents must sequentially master diverse tasks and environments without degrading previously acquired policies. Building directly on the exploration strategies and policy optimization techniques examined in the preceding section, which established how gradient-level constraints and uncertainty-driven exploration can mitigate task interference, this section examines how structural modifications to the agent's representational capacity provide complementary and synergistic solutions to the stability-plasticity challenge.

**Replay-Based and Recurrent Approaches**

Experience replay, originally introduced in deep reinforcement learning to stabilize training by breaking temporal correlations, has been naturally extended to continual RL settings. In replay-based recurrent reinforcement learning, episodic memory buffers store transitions from prior tasks and interleave them with current task data during training, providing the optimizer with a more stationary approximation of the joint data distribution. This mechanism directly parallels experience replay in supervised continual learning, mitigating the interference caused by non-stationary reward signals and shifting environment dynamics. The Progress & Compress framework [183] exemplifies an architectural strategy closely aligned with this philosophy: a knowledge base column, responsible for retaining previously acquired skills, is paired with an active column that efficiently learns new tasks. After each task, the active column is distilled into the knowledge base through a compression phase, ensuring that prior competencies are preserved without requiring access to stored raw data or explicit task-specific parameters. This cycle of progression followed by compression has been demonstrated effective across sequential Atari games and 3D maze navigation tasks, highlighting its applicability to reinforcement learning domains and its natural compatibility with the gradient projection methods discussed in the preceding section.

**Dynamic Architecture Methods**

Dynamic architecture approaches offer a complementary strategy by structurally isolating parameters associated with distinct tasks or environment contexts, thereby preventing destructive weight interference. Rather than relying on a fixed shared parameter space, these methods adaptively expand or restructure network capacity as new tasks are encountered. Techniques such as progressive neural networks, PathNet, and related expansion-based architectures allocate dedicated subnetworks or columns for each task, using lateral connections to enable forward transfer while protecting previously consolidated representations. In the RL context, dynamic architectures must additionally handle the challenge that task boundaries may be implicit or unknown, requiring online mechanisms for detecting environmental shifts and triggering structural adaptations—a concern that connects directly to the change-point detection and task-agnostic online learning challenges raised in the preceding section. Methods combining Bayesian structure adaptation with task-specific subnetwork learning have been explored to automate this process, leveraging posterior estimates over network structures to guide capacity allocation without manual intervention.

**Mixture-of-Experts Models for Multiple Environment Contexts**

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as a promising paradigm for continual RL, particularly when agents encounter multiple distinct environment contexts with potentially disparate state-action dynamics. In MoE-based continual RL, a gating network learns to route inputs to specialized expert subnetworks conditioned on contextual signals, enabling the agent to maintain distinct behavioral policies for different environments while sharing generic representational components. This modular decomposition naturally reduces task interference, as updates to one expert minimally affect the representations of others. The gating mechanism can further serve as a form of implicit task inference, allowing the agent to operate effectively without explicit task identity information at test time—directly addressing the task-agnostic deployment scenarios emphasized in the preceding section. Extensions of this idea integrate episodic memory with MoE routing, enabling context-dependent retrieval of relevant past experiences to further stabilize policy learning under non-stationary conditions.

**Universal Off-Policy Agent Designs**

Universal off-policy agent architectures represent a particularly elegant solution to the multi-task and continual RL problem by jointly representing and learning multiple policies within a single unified model. The Unicorn architecture [184] demonstrates this approach, employing a parallel off-policy learning setup that efficiently maintains a joint representation over multiple tasks with implicit boundaries. By leveraging off-policy learning, Unicorn can reuse experiences collected under any policy for updating all task-relevant value estimates simultaneously, dramatically improving sample efficiency in continual settings. Crucially, this universal agent design avoids the combinatorial explosion of memory and parameters that would result from maintaining entirely separate models per task, instead relying on structured shared representations augmented by task-conditioning mechanisms. Empirical evaluations on a challenging 3D domain with implicit task sequences and sparse rewards demonstrate that Unicorn substantially outperforms standard baseline agents, underscoring the advantages of principled universal architectures in continual RL.

**Broader Considerations and Trade-offs**

Across all architectural and memory-based approaches to continual RL, a common tension exists between expressivity and efficiency: more powerful architectures capable of representing diverse policies tend to require greater memory and computational resources, while lightweight designs risk insufficient capacity to accommodate a growing task repertoire. Furthermore, the non-stationary and delayed feedback characteristic of reinforcement learning complicates the application of methods originally developed for supervised continual learning, as gradient signals are noisier and task difficulty is endogenous to the agent's own evolving behavior. These compounding challenges are especially pronounced in real-world robotic settings, where the architectural and memory-based strategies surveyed here must contend with continuous sensory streams, implicit task boundaries, and strict real-time deployment constraints—considerations that motivate the dedicated treatment of robotic continual learning in the following section. Addressing these challenges requires careful co-design of memory management strategies, architectural inductive biases, and exploration mechanisms, pointing toward fruitful directions for future research in continual reinforcement learning.

### 6.5 Continual Learning for Robotics

## 6.5 Continual Learning for Robotics

Robotics represents one of the most demanding and practically important application domains for continual learning, as autonomous robotic agents must operate in dynamic, open-ended environments where tasks, objects, and interaction contexts evolve continuously over time. Unlike static machine learning settings, robots encounter non-stationary sensory streams from cameras, LiDARs, and proprioceptive sensors, and must adapt their skills incrementally without forgetting prior competencies. This requirement fundamentally aligns with the continual learning paradigm, making robotics both a rich testbed and a compelling motivator for developing robust lifelong learning algorithms. The architectural and memory-based strategies discussed in the preceding section—replay mechanisms, dynamic architectures, and universal off-policy designs—find particularly pressing applications in robotic settings, where the demands of sequential skill acquisition are compounded by the physical realities of real-time deployment.

A central challenge in robotic continual learning is acquiring skills from continuous sensory streams in real-time. Robots operating in physical environments cannot buffer entire datasets for offline retraining; instead, they must update their models online as new experiences arrive, analogous to the online continual learning setting. This imposes strict constraints on memory, computation, and the speed of adaptation. Furthermore, robotic agents frequently must infer which task they are currently performing, particularly in multi-task scenarios where task identity is not explicitly provided. This task inference problem interacts deeply with catastrophic forgetting, since incorrect task identification can cause the application of the wrong skill set or the inadvertent overwriting of relevant prior knowledge.

Sim-to-real transfer further complicates continual learning for robotics. Agents are often pre-trained in simulation environments where data is cheap and safe to collect, but must then adapt to the nuances of the real physical world, including sensor noise, physical contact dynamics, and lighting variability. When continual learning is applied on top of sim-to-real transfer, the agent faces a compound challenge: not only must it bridge the simulation-reality gap, but it must also incrementally learn new real-world tasks without degrading skills acquired during both simulation pre-training and prior real-world deployment.

Self-improving semantic perception is another dimension of robotic continual learning. As a robot navigates its environment, it encounters new object categories, scene configurations, and semantic concepts that were absent during initial training. Continual learning methods must therefore support open-world recognition where the agent incrementally expands its perceptual vocabulary. This connects robotic applications to class-incremental learning challenges observed in computer vision, but with the added complexity that perception in robotics is tightly coupled to action and planning, meaning perceptual errors can have immediate downstream consequences on task execution.

Human-robot interaction (HRI) introduces yet another continual learning challenge, particularly for long-term personalization. A robot assisting a specific user over extended periods must adapt to that individual's preferences, communication styles, and evolving needs without losing generalized capabilities that make it useful to other users or in other contexts. This personalization objective requires the robot to balance individual-specific adaptation with broad behavioral stability, a manifestation of the stability-plasticity dilemma at the level of social and interactive skills [1].

The broader challenge of rethinking continual learning specifically for autonomous agents and robots has been highlighted in the literature, which emphasizes that real-world robotic scenarios demand far more than forgetting prevention on simplified classification benchmarks [10]. Biological learning factors such as developmental and curriculum learning, transfer learning, and intrinsic motivation are identified as critical ingredients for enabling robots to progressively acquire increasingly complex knowledge and skills in a continual fashion [10]. These considerations echo the broader architectural trade-offs identified in continual reinforcement learning, where the co-design of memory management, structural inductive biases, and exploration mechanisms is essential for practical deployment.

To provide a rigorous and ecologically valid evaluation platform for continual reinforcement learning in robotics, the Continual World benchmark was proposed [9]. Built atop the Meta-World suite of robotic manipulation tasks, Continual World comprises a set of meaningfully diverse and realistic tasks designed to assess the three key dimensions of continual learning performance: catastrophic forgetting, forward transfer to new tasks, and backward transfer to previously learned ones. Through an in-depth empirical evaluation of existing continual learning methods on this benchmark, the study reveals important limitations of current approaches and highlights unique algorithmic challenges that arise in the reinforcement learning setting, most notably the tendency of the community to overly focus on forgetting prevention at the expense of promoting positive forward transfer [9]. These findings directly complement the evaluation considerations raised in the following section, which addresses benchmark design and standardized metrics for continual reinforcement learning more broadly.

The Continual World findings underscore that achieving genuine lifelong robotic learning requires balancing opposing desiderata simultaneously: maintaining bounded computational and memory resources, preventing catastrophic forgetting of prior manipulation skills, and enabling positive knowledge transfer to accelerate acquisition of new tasks. Methods that excel at one of these objectives often sacrifice performance on another, pointing to the need for unified frameworks that explicitly optimize for all three simultaneously.

Active forgetting mechanisms also appear relevant in robotic continual learning. When a robot's prior experiences in one task negatively interfere with learning in a new task—for instance, due to conflicting reward structures or opposing motor commands—methods that can selectively attenuate outdated or harmful memories may be beneficial [65]. This biologically inspired principle of regulated forgetting complements the standard anti-forgetting objectives and may prove especially important for robots that operate across highly heterogeneous task domains over their operational lifetimes.

Knowledge transfer between tasks is equally critical for robotic systems. Forward transfer enables a robot to leverage prior skills to more rapidly acquire new manipulation capabilities, while backward transfer—where learning a new task improves performance on previously learned ones—represents an additional benefit that most existing methods fail to exploit [61; 9]. Developing algorithms that actively promote both transfer directions, rather than merely minimizing forgetting, represents a key open challenge and opportunity for the robotics community.

In summary, continual learning for robotics encompasses a rich set of intertwined challenges including real-time sensory stream processing, sim-to-real transfer, multi-task inference, semantic perceptual expansion, and long-term personalization in human-robot interaction. These challenges demand not only advances in algorithmic design—spanning the replay-based, architectural, and universal agent strategies discussed earlier—but also principled evaluation frameworks capable of capturing the full complexity of real-world robotic deployment. Benchmarks such as Continual World [9] provide important steps toward this goal, motivating the broader discussion of evaluation methodologies and standardized metrics for continual reinforcement learning that follows in the next section.

### 6.6 Benchmarks and Evaluation Frameworks for Continual RL

## 6.6 Benchmarks and Evaluation Metrics for Continual Reinforcement Learning

Rigorous evaluation of continual and lifelong reinforcement learning (RL) methods requires carefully designed benchmark environments and standardized metrics that capture the unique challenges of non-stationary sequential decision-making. Unlike supervised continual learning, where benchmarks such as Split-CIFAR and Permuted-MNIST have achieved widespread adoption, the continual RL community has historically lacked consensus evaluation frameworks, making fair algorithmic comparisons difficult and progress harder to assess. This gap is particularly consequential given the empirical findings highlighted in the preceding section on robotic continual learning, where the Continual World benchmark exposed critical limitations of existing methods and underscored the need for evaluation protocols that go beyond measuring forgetting alone.

Among the most prominent evaluation environments developed for continual RL is the Continual World benchmark, which provides a suite of realistic and meaningfully diverse robotic manipulation tasks built on top of the Meta-World framework [9]. Continual World was specifically designed to address a perceived overemphasis on catastrophic forgetting in the community, instead encouraging researchers to jointly measure forward transfer, backward transfer, and forgetting across sequences of robotic tasks. The benchmark offers computationally tractable challenges that allow thorough empirical evaluation, and its open-source codebase promotes reproducibility. Importantly, an in-depth empirical study conducted alongside the benchmark revealed critical limitations of existing continual learning methods when applied to RL settings, highlighting unique algorithmic challenges that do not arise in supervised learning contexts [9]. These findings directly motivate the broader evaluation considerations discussed throughout this section, as well as the demands placed on continual learning systems in the autonomous and smart city applications examined in the following section.

Beyond robotic manipulation, other benchmark environments such as CRLMaze and L2Explorer have been proposed to evaluate continual RL agents in navigation and exploration settings, where agents must adapt their policies to changing environment configurations without forgetting prior navigation skills. These environments stress-test agents under varying degrees of non-stationarity and task diversity, probing their ability to detect distributional changes and respond appropriately. Together with Continual World, they collectively reflect a growing recognition that meaningful progress in continual RL requires evaluation across a diverse range of task structures and environmental dynamics.

Key performance metrics in continual RL evaluation extend beyond the average accuracy and forgetting measures common in supervised continual learning. Transfer efficiency quantifies how knowledge acquired from previous tasks accelerates or hinders learning on new tasks, capturing both forward and backward transfer dynamics. Forgetting measures track degradation in performance on previously mastered tasks as new ones are introduced. Adaptation speed assesses how quickly an agent recovers competent behavior following an environmental shift, which is particularly critical in non-stationary Markov Decision Process settings. Additionally, some evaluation frameworks examine the sensitivity of continual RL methods to hyperparameter choices under limited deployment data, recognizing that in practice agents rarely have access to large validation sets for tuning [9]. This multi-dimensional view of evaluation directly parallels the three-way performance trade-off — forgetting prevention, forward transfer, and backward transfer — identified as central to robotic continual learning in the preceding section.

A recurring challenge in continual RL evaluation is the definition and handling of task boundaries. Some benchmarks provide explicit task identifiers that signal when the environment has changed, while others require agents to infer distributional shifts autonomously, creating substantially different difficulty levels. This distinction parallels debates in supervised continual learning between task-incremental and task-agnostic evaluation protocols, and has significant implications for which algorithmic families are most appropriate. Evaluation frameworks increasingly advocate for task-agnostic or blurred-boundary settings that more faithfully reflect the conditions autonomous agents encounter during real-world deployment — a property that will prove equally central to the autonomous vehicle and smart city applications considered in the following section, where task boundaries are rarely well-defined.

The robotics-focused continual learning literature further emphasizes the importance of evaluating under realistic computational and data constraints [4]. Since robotic platforms typically operate under energy and memory budgets, benchmark evaluations that ignore computational overhead may overestimate the practical viability of certain approaches. This concern mirrors findings from the supervised continual learning community, where analyses under compute-constrained settings reveal that many sophisticated methods fail to outperform simpler baselines when training budgets are restricted [68]. Translating these insights to the continual RL domain suggests an urgent need for benchmark designs that jointly report task performance and computational costs, providing a holistic picture of method suitability for real-world deployment — a requirement that becomes even more pressing in the resource-constrained autonomous systems and edge computing scenarios discussed in the following section.

Overall, the development of standardized, comprehensive, and computationally aware benchmarks remains an open priority for the continual RL community. Existing efforts such as Continual World represent meaningful progress, but broader coverage of diverse task types, non-stationarity regimes, and realistic resource constraints will be essential for driving the field toward deployable lifelong reinforcement learning systems capable of meeting the demands of autonomous vehicles, urban robots, and smart city infrastructure explored in the sections that follow.

### 6.7 Continual Learning in Autonomous Systems and Smart Cities

## 6.7 Continual Learning in Autonomous Systems and Smart Cities

The deployment of continual learning in real-world autonomous systems and smart city infrastructure represents one of the most demanding application frontiers, characterized by persistent non-stationarity, resource constraints, and safety-critical requirements. Unlike the controlled clinical and reinforcement learning environments discussed in preceding sections, real-world autonomous systems must contend with continuously evolving environments where sensor readings drift over time, data distributions shift unpredictably, and adaptation must occur under strict computational budgets without interrupting system operation. This section examines how continual learning principles translate to autonomous vehicles, urban robotic platforms, and smart city infrastructure, highlighting the distinctive challenges that distinguish these deployments from more controlled settings.

### Autonomous Vehicles and Unmanned Aerial Systems

Self-driving vehicles present a compelling and challenging domain for continual learning. These systems must process high-dimensional, multi-modal sensory streams — encompassing LiDAR point clouds, camera imagery, radar signals, and GPS data — while simultaneously maintaining reliable perception, prediction, and planning modules that have been validated on prior driving conditions. The non-stationarity inherent in autonomous driving arises from multiple sources: changes in weather conditions, time of day, geographic regions, road infrastructure, and traffic density all induce distribution shifts that can degrade model performance if not addressed through adaptive learning mechanisms.

A central challenge in autonomous vehicle perception is that newly encountered scenarios, such as construction zones, unusual weather events, or novel object categories, must be incorporated into the vehicle's world model without degrading performance on the vast array of driving situations encountered during initial training. Traditional approaches requiring full retraining from scratch are computationally prohibitive and practically infeasible given that vehicles operate continuously in deployment. Continual learning methods offer a principled framework for incremental adaptation, but must be carefully engineered to avoid catastrophic forgetting of safety-critical behaviors [1].

Unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) face analogous challenges in dynamic operational environments. UAVs deployed for surveillance, delivery, or search-and-rescue operations must adapt their perception and navigation policies to new terrains, lighting conditions, and obstacle configurations encountered during extended missions. The limited onboard computational resources of UAVs make this particularly demanding, as complex replay buffers or large auxiliary networks may exceed available memory and processing budgets. Architectures inspired by neuromodulatory principles that achieve efficient continual learning with minimal parameter overhead are especially relevant in this context [91; 44].

Online continual learning methods that process data in a single pass without requiring explicit task boundaries are particularly well-suited to autonomous system deployments, where the notion of discrete tasks rarely maps cleanly onto the continuous stream of real-world experiences [169]. This property is directly analogous to the task-agnostic evaluation protocols advocated in continual reinforcement learning benchmarks, reinforcing a broader convergence across application domains toward methods capable of handling blurred or absent task boundaries. The ability to adapt to distribution shift without task identity information is essential for vehicles traversing varied geographic regions or UAVs operating across heterogeneous mission profiles.

### Urban Robots and Smart City Applications

Urban robots operating in dynamic city environments — including delivery robots, autonomous cleaning systems, and pedestrian-interaction platforms — face the compounded challenge of adapting to evolving human behavior patterns, seasonal environmental changes, and infrastructural modifications. These systems must maintain stable core navigation and interaction competencies while continuously refining their models based on newly encountered situations. The complementary learning systems perspective, which emphasizes the interaction between rapid episodic adaptation and slower structured knowledge consolidation, provides a biologically motivated blueprint for designing such systems [82; 83].

Smart city infrastructure increasingly relies on machine learning models for traffic flow prediction, air quality monitoring, energy grid management, and communication network optimization. These applications share the characteristic that the statistical properties of the data they process evolve continuously: traffic patterns shift seasonally and in response to urban development, environmental sensor readings drift due to equipment aging and atmospheric variation, and network traffic profiles change in response to evolving user behavior and application demands. Static models trained on historical data progressively lose predictive accuracy as these distributions drift, motivating the integration of continual learning mechanisms that enable models to adapt without full retraining.

Traffic management systems represent a particularly high-value application domain. Deep learning models for vehicle counting, congestion prediction, and incident detection must remain accurate across the full temporal spectrum of urban traffic dynamics, from daily commute cycles to long-term demographic and infrastructure changes. Sensor drift — gradual degradation of camera or loop detector characteristics — introduces an additional source of non-stationarity that is distinct from semantic concept drift and requires dedicated adaptation strategies. Continual learning approaches that disentangle different sources of distribution shift and apply appropriate adaptation mechanisms to each are needed to address this complexity.

Environmental monitoring networks deployed across smart cities generate continuous data streams from heterogeneous sensor arrays measuring air quality, noise levels, temperature, and other urban indicators. These sensors are subject to degradation, replacement, and recalibration over time, inducing distribution shifts that can be mistaken for genuine environmental changes if not accounted for. Continual learning frameworks that incorporate uncertainty estimation and can distinguish between sensor-induced and environment-induced distribution changes are essential for maintaining the reliability of these monitoring systems [93].

Communication networks, including cellular infrastructure and Internet-of-Things mesh networks, present another fertile application domain for smart city continual learning. Traffic classification models, anomaly detection systems, and resource allocation algorithms must adapt to evolving usage patterns and new application types without service interruption. The combination of high data volumes, strict latency constraints, and the impossibility of storing comprehensive historical training datasets makes computationally efficient online continual learning particularly valuable in this context, echoing the compute-constrained evaluation concerns raised for robotic platforms in the preceding section.

### Sensor Drift and Non-Stationarity Challenges

A cross-cutting challenge unifying all autonomous system and smart city applications is the management of sensor drift and data stream non-stationarity. Unlike the clean task boundaries assumed in many academic continual learning benchmarks, real deployments feature gradual, continuous distribution changes superimposed on occasional abrupt shifts. Detecting the onset and magnitude of these changes is prerequisite to applying appropriate adaptation strategies, as aggressive adaptation to transient noise may undermine stability while insufficient adaptation to genuine distribution shifts degrades performance.

Biologically inspired approaches that employ context-selective neurons and local availability mechanisms offer promising solutions to this challenge by enabling models to selectively consolidate representations that prove stable across multiple contexts while remaining plastic to genuine environmental changes [32]. Similarly, sparse coding mechanisms that encode information in non-overlapping neural representations can limit interference between representations of distinct operational contexts, enabling more robust adaptation under non-stationary conditions [85; 94].

### Computational Efficiency in Resource-Constrained Deployments

The deployment of continual learning on resource-constrained autonomous platforms demands algorithms that achieve strong adaptation performance within strict memory, computation, and energy budgets. Many state-of-the-art continual learning methods developed for server-class hardware assume access to large replay buffers, computationally intensive generative models, or expensive second-order gradient computations that are infeasible in embedded deployments. This concern directly parallels findings from both the supervised continual learning and continual reinforcement learning communities, where compute-constrained evaluations consistently reveal that sophisticated methods may fail to outperform simpler baselines when training budgets are restricted. This motivates the development of lightweight continual learning architectures specifically engineered for efficiency, such as neuromodulation-inspired approaches that learn compact task-specific modulation vectors applied to fixed random weights [91], and bio-inspired local learning rule methods that eliminate the need for full backpropagation [44; 45].

Hardware-aware continual learning, which co-designs algorithms with the memory hierarchies and processing capabilities of embedded platforms, represents an important emerging research direction. Neuromorphic hardware implementations, such as devices exploiting quantum tunneling for synaptic consolidation, offer the prospect of ultra-energy-efficient on-device adaptation that closely mirrors the biological mechanisms inspiring many continual learning algorithms [87]. As autonomous systems and smart city deployments increasingly require edge computing capabilities to meet latency and privacy constraints, the intersection of continual learning with neuromorphic and embedded computing is expected to become a major research focus.

### Open Challenges and Future Directions

Despite significant progress, substantial open challenges remain for continual learning in autonomous systems and smart cities. Realistic evaluation protocols that capture the full complexity of real-world deployment — including gradual sensor drift, open-world novelty, heterogeneous task distributions, and safety constraints — remain underdeveloped compared to controlled academic benchmarks [10]. The integration of continual learning with formal safety verification frameworks is essential for safety-critical autonomous applications but remains largely unexplored. Furthermore, the development of interpretable continual learning systems that allow operators to understand, audit, and intervene in the adaptation process is critical for building trust in continually learning autonomous systems deployed in public urban environments.

These challenges also resonate with those facing healthcare continual learning, discussed in the following section, where privacy constraints, long-term distributional drift, and the need for reliable uncertainty estimation similarly demand methods that go beyond standard supervised continual learning formulations. Addressing the open challenges across both domains will require close collaboration between the continual learning research community, autonomous systems engineers, urban planners, clinicians, and regulatory bodies to develop practically deployable solutions that meet the demanding requirements of real-world applications.

### 6.8 Continual Learning for Healthcare and Clinical Data

Healthcare represents one of the most compelling yet challenging application domains for continual learning, where models must adapt to evolving clinical practices, shifting patient populations, and longitudinal data streams while operating under strict privacy constraints. Unlike the autonomous systems and smart city deployments discussed in the preceding section — where non-stationarity arises primarily from environmental dynamics and sensor drift — healthcare introduces a distinct combination of regulatory constraints, long-horizon distributional shift, and the need for reliable, auditable model behavior in life-critical settings. Clinical data is inherently non-stationary: patient demographics, treatment protocols, disease prevalence, and measurement standards all evolve over time, creating persistent covariate shift that can degrade the performance of static models deployed in real-world medical settings.

A particularly important setting involves the adaptation of predictive models to longitudinal records from intensive care units (ICUs). Electronic health records from critical care settings exhibit continuous distributional drift, arising from changes in clinical coding practices, evolving patient populations, and updates to treatment guidelines. Studies evaluating continual learning methods on such data have found that while several regularization-based and architecture-based approaches can mitigate short-term forgetting, domain shift remains a deeply challenging problem over long sequences of tasks [8]. Crucially, only replay-based methods have demonstrated stable long-term performance across extended clinical task sequences, underscoring the importance of memory mechanisms in maintaining model reliability in healthcare deployments [8].

Beyond ICU data, continual learning is increasingly relevant to medical diagnosis, where class-incremental learning scenarios arise naturally as new disease categories, imaging modalities, or diagnostic criteria are introduced over time. Models must incorporate these new classes without degrading their recognition of previously learned conditions, a requirement that maps directly onto the class-incremental continual learning framework. This mirrors challenges encountered in smart city perception systems, where novel object categories or infrastructure changes must similarly be incorporated without disrupting existing recognition capabilities.

Privacy constraints represent a fundamental challenge distinguishing healthcare continual learning from most other domains. Medical data is governed by stringent regulations such as HIPAA and GDPR, which severely restrict the storage and reuse of patient records. This creates a direct tension with replay-based methods, which — despite their demonstrated effectiveness for long-term stability — typically require retaining exemplars from past tasks. Federated continual learning frameworks, which enable distributed training without sharing raw patient data, offer a promising direction for addressing this constraint, though they introduce additional challenges related to heterogeneous local data distributions and communication overhead [104; 105].

Regularization-based methods such as Elastic Weight Consolidation offer privacy-preserving alternatives by consolidating knowledge into parameter importance estimates without retaining raw data, though their scalability to long clinical task sequences remains limited [8]. The development of methods that simultaneously achieve long-term stability, adapt to covariate shift, and respect privacy constraints remains an open and critical challenge for the practical deployment of continual learning in clinical environments. These difficulties foreshadow the challenges encountered in sequential signal-based domains such as activity recognition and automatic speech recognition, where analogous tensions between regularization-based and replay-based approaches emerge, and where the absence of explicit task boundaries similarly demands continual learning frameworks designed for the complexities of real-world, continuously evolving data streams.

### 6.9 Continual Learning for Speech Recognition and Human Activity Recognition

Continual learning in sequential signal-based domains such as automatic speech recognition (ASR) and sensor-based human activity recognition (HAR) presents unique challenges that distinguish these settings from standard image classification benchmarks. Much like the healthcare domain, where models must adapt to non-stationary clinical data streams under real-world operational constraints, sequential signal streams exhibit pronounced non-stationarity arising from speaker variability, environmental acoustic drift, device heterogeneity, and changing user behavior patterns over time. These characteristics demand continual learning frameworks capable of adapting to evolving data distributions without catastrophic forgetting of previously acquired acoustic or activity models.

In the domain of human activity recognition, empirical studies have evaluated how well continual learning methods generalize beyond computer vision to time-series sensor data. Research investigating regularization-based and replay-based approaches on HAR datasets has revealed that most regularization techniques designed for image classification exhibit limited effectiveness when transferred to sequential sensor signals [111]. This work provides a constituent-wise analysis of prominent task-incremental learning techniques, finding that regularization approaches frequently fail to provide substantial benefits in HAR settings, and arguing that continual learning algorithm development should be driven by diverse task domains rather than being exclusively motivated by computer vision scenarios. This finding resonates with the broader observation from healthcare deployments that regularization-based methods struggle to maintain performance over long and complex task sequences.

A critical challenge in real-world HAR deployments is the absence of explicit task boundaries. Streaming sensor data from wearable devices arrives continuously, and it is often impractical to demarcate when one activity distribution ends and another begins. This motivates the development of task-free and data-incremental continual learning frameworks that can process streaming sensor observations without relying on task identity information. Such frameworks must simultaneously detect distributional shifts, consolidate knowledge from past activity patterns, and incorporate new motion signatures, all within tight memory and computational budgets suited to embedded and edge devices [80]. The absence of task boundaries in these settings represents a particularly demanding variant of the continual learning problem, bridging into the privacy, safety, and ethical considerations that become central when such systems are deployed in autonomous or semi-autonomous real-world agents.

The deployment of continual learning on mobile and embedded sensing platforms introduces additional system-level constraints including limited memory, restricted computational throughput, and energy budgets. Empirical evaluations conducted across multiple HAR datasets on edge devices have demonstrated that replay-based methods incorporating stored exemplars, such as iCaRL-style approaches, achieve favorable performance trade-offs even under complex non-stationary scenarios, at the cost of modest storage overhead [80]. These findings establish the practical feasibility of on-device continual learning for HAR applications while highlighting that incremental learning and training latencies remain within acceptable bounds for real-world deployment when devices operate during charging cycles.

For automatic speech recognition, challenges include acoustic environment drift across recording conditions, evolving speaker demographics, and the need to incorporate new vocabulary or acoustic models without degrading recognition accuracy on previously learned speech patterns. The high dimensionality and temporal structure of acoustic features further complicate the application of standard continual learning techniques. Speaker variability introduces a form of natural distribution shift that continual ASR systems must handle gracefully, requiring methods that balance retaining generalizable phonetic representations with adapting to new speaker characteristics or environmental conditions.

Across both domains, the limitations of regularization-based methods observed in HAR [111] suggest that replay-based and architecture-based approaches may offer more reliable foundations for signal-based continual learning. However, the memory constraints of embedded sensing platforms necessitate careful design of memory buffers and rehearsal strategies [80]. Future progress in these domains will require benchmarks and evaluation protocols specifically designed to reflect the continuous, boundary-free nature of real-world sensor and acoustic data streams, moving beyond the discrete task sequences that dominate current continual learning evaluation paradigms. As these systems become increasingly embedded in autonomous agents operating in physical environments, the intersection of signal-based continual learning with the privacy, safety, and ethical considerations discussed in the following section becomes ever more pressing.

### 6.10 Privacy, Safety, and Ethical Considerations in Continual RL and Robotics

## Privacy, Safety, and Ethical Considerations in Continual RL and Robotics

Building upon the challenges of deploying continual learning systems in signal-based domains such as speech recognition and human activity recognition, the stakes are considerably higher when continual learning agents operate in physical environments and interact directly with humans. As continual reinforcement learning systems are increasingly deployed in real-world settings—ranging from autonomous vehicles and medical robots to smart city infrastructure—the intersection of privacy preservation, safety constraints, and ethical accountability becomes critically important. These concerns are not merely auxiliary to system performance; they constitute fundamental requirements for responsible deployment of agents that continuously update their behavior in response to evolving environmental data. The non-stationarity and boundary-free nature of real-world sensor streams, as observed in HAR and ASR deployments, intensifies these concerns when the agent's actions carry physical consequences.

### Privacy Preservation in Continual RL

Privacy challenges in continual reinforcement learning are particularly acute because agents must retain and leverage knowledge from prior interactions, which may include sensitive personal or environmental data. Unlike static supervised learning systems, continual RL agents accumulate experience over time, raising the risk that replay buffers, value functions, or learned policy parameters may inadvertently encode private information about individuals or environments encountered during training. This concern is closely related to the replay-based memory mechanisms that have proven effective in HAR and other signal-based continual learning settings, where stored exemplars may themselves constitute sensitive personal data.

Differential privacy mechanisms represent one of the most principled approaches to mitigating this risk. By injecting calibrated noise into gradient updates or value function estimates during learning, differential privacy provides formal mathematical guarantees that the agent's learned parameters cannot be reverse-engineered to reveal specific training experiences. This approach is particularly relevant in healthcare robotics and personalized assistive systems, where the sequential nature of interactions may expose sensitive behavioral patterns of individual users.

The challenge of applying differential privacy in continual RL is compounded by the stability-plasticity dilemma: the noise introduced to protect privacy also degrades learning signal, potentially accelerating catastrophic forgetting or impairing forward transfer. Balancing privacy budgets across a long sequence of tasks remains an open research problem, as privacy guarantees tend to degrade with the cumulative number of learning episodes.

Federated continual learning offers a complementary privacy-preserving paradigm, wherein distributed agents learn from local data streams without sharing raw experiences with a central server. This architecture naturally limits exposure of individual interaction histories while enabling collective knowledge consolidation. However, heterogeneous task distributions across clients and the risk of inference attacks on shared model updates introduce additional privacy vulnerabilities that require specialized defense mechanisms.

### Safety Constraints and Risk-Sensitive Adaptation

Safety is perhaps the most pressing concern for continually learning agents operating in physical environments. Unlike static deployed systems whose behavior can be certified prior to deployment, continual learners modify their policies during operation, introducing the possibility of unexpected and potentially dangerous behavioral changes. This challenge is especially critical in robotic systems where policy degradation during task transitions can result in physical harm to humans or infrastructure—a risk that is qualitatively distinct from the performance degradation observed in signal classification tasks such as HAR, but shares the same underlying instability arising from catastrophic forgetting.

Risk-sensitive reinforcement learning objectives provide one framework for addressing safety during continual adaptation. Rather than optimizing expected cumulative reward, risk-sensitive formulations incorporate measures such as conditional value-at-risk or worst-case performance bounds into the optimization objective, encouraging agents to maintain conservative behavior during periods of high uncertainty. This is particularly important at task boundaries, where the agent's value function estimates may be temporarily unreliable as the model adapts to new distributional statistics.

Safeguarding system performance during adaptation also motivates the use of constrained policy optimization approaches, wherein the agent is prevented from deviating excessively from its prior policy during any single update step. Trust region methods and proximal policy optimization naturally support this requirement by bounding the magnitude of policy changes, providing a form of soft safety guarantee during continual learning. More stringent safety requirements may necessitate formal verification of policy behavior within specified state-space regions before deployment updates are committed.

The concept of safe exploration is closely related to safety-constrained continual learning. In novel task contexts, agents must balance the need to gather information about new reward structures against the risk of taking dangerous exploratory actions. Safe exploration methods that leverage prior task knowledge to constrain exploration to regions of state space known to be safe from previous experience represent a promising direction for continual RL in safety-critical domains.

### Ethical Deployment and Accountability

Beyond technical privacy and safety mechanisms, the deployment of continually learning agents raises broader ethical questions concerning accountability, transparency, and fairness. A fundamental challenge is that the behavior of a continual learner at deployment time may differ substantially from its behavior at the time of certification or ethical review, as the agent continues to update its policy based on new experiences. This temporal drift in system behavior complicates traditional accountability frameworks that assume static system specifications, and is directly analogous to the distributional shift problems observed across all the application domains surveyed in preceding sections, from healthcare to speech recognition.

Transparency mechanisms that allow human operators to monitor, interpret, and intervene in the continual learning process are therefore essential for responsible deployment. Explainability modules that provide interpretable accounts of why an agent's behavior has changed over time—and what experiences drove that change—enable domain experts to identify problematic adaptations and take corrective action. The design of such monitoring systems must account for the sequential and potentially high-dimensional nature of continual learning trajectories.

Fairness considerations arise in continual RL when the agent's training data stream is non-stationary in ways that reflect societal biases. For instance, a robotic assistant that learns preferentially from interactions with a particular demographic group during one phase of deployment may exhibit degraded or discriminatory performance toward underrepresented groups encountered later. Continual learning systems should therefore incorporate mechanisms for monitoring distributional coverage of training experiences and detecting fairness-relevant distribution shifts.

Human trust and acceptance of continually learning robots represents another important ethical dimension. Users who interact with robotic systems over extended periods form mental models of system behavior, and unexpected behavioral changes arising from continual adaptation can erode trust even when the objective performance of the system improves. Designing continual learning systems that communicate their adaptive behavior to users in accessible and transparent ways is an important open challenge at the intersection of human-robot interaction and continual learning research.

### Regulatory and Governance Considerations

The deployment of continually adapting AI systems in safety-critical domains is beginning to attract regulatory attention, with frameworks such as the European Union AI Act imposing requirements for high-risk AI systems that include provisions for ongoing monitoring, logging of system changes, and human oversight. Continual reinforcement learning systems that modify their own behavior in response to environmental feedback represent a particularly challenging case for regulatory compliance, as traditional conformity assessment procedures designed for static systems may be inadequate.

Taken together, the privacy, safety, and ethical considerations discussed in this section underscore that continual learning is not solely a technical challenge of preventing catastrophic forgetting or maximizing forward transfer—it is equally a sociotechnical challenge demanding careful attention to the deployment context, stakeholder interests, and potential societal impacts. Future research directions in this area include the development of formal certification frameworks for continually learning systems that provide time-varying safety guarantees, the design of privacy-preserving replay mechanisms that satisfy differential privacy requirements without sacrificing learning efficiency, and the integration of ethical constraints directly into the continual learning objective function. Addressing these challenges will be essential for realizing the potential of continually learning agents in domains where the stakes of system failure are highest, and will increasingly shape the theoretical and algorithmic directions that the broader continual learning research community pursues in the years ahead.

## 7 Advanced Topics and Emerging Paradigms in Continual Learning

### 7.1 Unsupervised and Self-Supervised Continual Learning

## 7.1 Unsupervised and Self-Supervised Continual Learning

The vast majority of continual learning research has historically assumed access to labeled data, yet real-world deployment scenarios frequently involve unlabeled or sparsely labeled data streams. This has motivated a growing body of work exploring continual learning in unsupervised and self-supervised settings, where models must acquire and retain useful representations without relying on human-provided annotations. These efforts form a natural foundation for the broader challenge of learning under limited supervision, which extends into the semi-supervised continual learning setting discussed in the following section.

**Unsupervised Continual Representation Learning.** A central question in this area is whether unsupervised representations are inherently more robust to catastrophic forgetting than their supervised counterparts. Empirical investigations suggest a compelling affirmative answer. Studies on unsupervised continual learning (UCL) demonstrate that unsupervised visual representations are surprisingly more resistant to catastrophic forgetting, consistently achieve better generalization performance, and transfer more effectively to out-of-distribution tasks compared to supervised continual learning approaches [177]. This robustness likely stems from the fact that self-supervised objectives encourage learning broadly transferable features rather than task-specific discriminative boundaries, which are more susceptible to interference when the task distribution shifts. Notably, this observation echoes findings in the semi-supervised continual learning literature, where generative representations have similarly been shown to exhibit greater inherent robustness to forgetting than discriminative ones [185], suggesting a consistent pattern across label-scarce continual learning regimes.

**Self-Supervised Pretext Tasks for Continual Learning.** Self-supervised learning methods leverage pretext tasks—such as contrastive learning, masked prediction, or equivariance-based objectives—to learn general-purpose representations from unlabeled data. In the continual learning context, these pretext tasks serve as a form of implicit regularization, encouraging the model to retain feature structure across sequential experiences. Contrastive objectives, in particular, enforce inter-class separation and encourage compact, well-structured embedding spaces that remain stable as new data arrives [177]. Recent prompt-based approaches leveraging pre-trained vision transformers further exploit rich self-supervised representations to dramatically reduce forgetting without explicit replay [135], highlighting the synergy between strong self-supervised pre-training and continual learning.

**Lifelong Unsupervised Mixup.** To further address catastrophic forgetting in label-free settings, the Lifelong Unsupervised Mixup (LUMP) technique has been proposed as a simple yet effective strategy that interpolates between instances from the current task and stored instances from previous tasks [177]. By constructing convex combinations of current and past embeddings, LUMP creates soft transitions between task distributions in the input space, discouraging the sharp representational discontinuities that underlie catastrophic forgetting. This approach requires only minimal memory overhead compared to full replay buffers and is compatible with a wide range of self-supervised objectives.

**Generative Continual Learning with Variational Autoencoders.** Another prominent direction in unsupervised continual learning involves generative models, particularly Variational Autoencoders (VAEs), which can both learn compact latent representations and synthesize pseudo-samples for replay. Generative replay approaches train a generative model alongside a task model, using the generator to reconstruct approximate samples from past task distributions when learning new experiences [6]. In the unsupervised setting, the generative model itself is the primary object of interest, and continual learning of the generative model requires preserving the structure of previously learned latent spaces. Techniques such as latent space realignment aim to ensure that the learned latent codes for past concepts remain consistent and interpretable as the model adapts to new data distributions, preventing the encoder from drifting in ways that degrade reconstructions of previously seen examples.

**Continual Generative Classification.** Extensions of generative replay to classification without strong supervision have been explored through variational approaches that jointly model the input distribution and class structure. By deriving variational bounds on the joint log-likelihood of observations and labels, these methods enable classification models to learn continuously from sequentially observed tasks while preventing catastrophic forgetting via generative memory [132]. Such approaches offer a natural bridge between purely unsupervised representation learning and supervised continual classification, operating in regimes where labels are partially or intermittently available—a characteristic they share with the semi-supervised continual learning methods discussed in the next section.

**Task-Free and Streaming Unsupervised Scenarios.** A particularly challenging variant of unsupervised continual learning is the task-free or data-incremental setting, where neither task boundaries nor labels are provided and data arrives as a continuous, non-stationary stream. Frameworks such as LAPNet-HAR address this challenge in the context of sensor-based data by processing streams in a task-free, data-incremental fashion, combining experience replay with continual prototype adaptation and contrastive objectives to enforce inter-class separation without supervision [81]. These approaches exemplify the direction toward truly label-free, boundary-free continual learning that is essential for real-world deployment, and they share a core concern with semi-supervised continual learning: how to extract reliable, lasting knowledge from data streams in which clean supervisory signal is absent or incomplete.

**Challenges and Open Problems.** Despite encouraging progress, unsupervised and self-supervised continual learning remains significantly less explored than its supervised counterpart [177; 5]. Key open challenges include developing evaluation protocols that measure representational quality independent of downstream task performance, designing replay mechanisms suitable for unlabeled data streams, and scaling generative continual learning to high-dimensional, complex data distributions. Furthermore, the interaction between the choice of self-supervised pretext task and the nature of the distribution shift encountered during continual learning is not yet well understood, representing a rich avenue for future investigation. Many of these challenges carry over directly into the semi-supervised setting, where the additional complexity of mixing labeled and unlabeled data introduces further difficulties around pseudo-label reliability and memory management, as explored in the following section. Addressing these challenges will be critical for deploying continual learning systems in the many real-world settings where labeled data is scarce or unavailable.

### 7.2 Semi-Supervised Continual Learning

## 7.2 Semi-Supervised Continual Learning

Semi-supervised continual learning occupies a challenging intersection between two already demanding paradigms: it requires models to learn from sequential data streams where only a fraction of incoming samples carry explicit labels. Building directly on the unsupervised and self-supervised continual learning challenges discussed in the previous section, this setting introduces the additional complexity of mixing labeled and unlabeled data within the same sequential learning framework. The core difficulty lies not only in preventing catastrophic forgetting of previously acquired knowledge but also in reliably extracting useful information from unlabeled samples whose underlying class distributions may be entirely unknown or may shift unpredictably over time. This setting is considerably more realistic than fully supervised continual learning, as annotation of every sample in a continuous data stream is rarely feasible in real-world deployments.

A fundamental challenge in this setting is that unlabeled data arriving with a new task may contain instances from previously seen classes, future classes, or entirely novel categories, making it non-trivial to determine how such data should influence the model's representations. When the unlabeled data distribution is unreliable or misaligned with the labeled portion, pseudo-labeling strategies can introduce confirmation bias, causing the model to consolidate incorrect knowledge. This instability in knowledge consolidation is exacerbated in long task sequences where errors from early tasks compound over time—a concern that carries forward into the online continual learning setting discussed in the following section, where the single-pass nature of data streams removes the opportunity to revisit and correct such errors.

Several methodological families have been proposed to address these challenges. Gradient-based approaches for semi-supervised continual learning attempt to predict the effect of unlabeled data on the learning trajectory, using gradient information to selectively incorporate unlabeled samples that are beneficial for both current task learning and retention of prior knowledge.
