Dynamic sub-graph distillation methods approach the problem from a structural perspective, constructing task-specific sub-graphs over the data manifold and distilling knowledge through evolving graph representations that can accommodate new unlabeled samples without catastrophically overwriting old structural patterns.

Hypernetwork-based meta-consolidation frameworks represent another promising direction, in which a hypernetwork generates task-specific weight modulations conditioned on a compact task representation, enabling rapid adaptation to new labeled and unlabeled data while preserving a stable meta-level memory of past tasks. These frameworks draw conceptual inspiration from meta-learning, aiming to learn an inductive bias that facilitates fast and stable adaptation across tasks. Label propagation adaptive resonance theory methods extend classical adaptive resonance architectures by propagating soft label estimates from labeled to unlabeled samples within a task, providing a principled mechanism to assign provisional class memberships before committing representations to long-term memory.

The interplay between catastrophic forgetting and semi-supervised learning is further complicated by representation drift. As noted in studies of supervised continual learning, deeper layers of neural networks are disproportionately vulnerable to forgetting [18], and this sensitivity is amplified when unlabeled data introduces additional gradient noise. Probing the representational changes induced by sequential semi-supervised learning reveals that a model's internal geometry may shift substantially even when task performance on labeled evaluations appears stable [156], underscoring the need for representation-level regularization in semi-supervised continual settings. This representation drift phenomenon resurfaces in the online continual learning setting, where it is closely related to the task-recency bias that systematically distorts model geometry in streaming scenarios.

Memory management strategies borrowed from supervised replay-based continual learning also require adaptation for semi-supervised settings. Standard reservoir sampling assumes that stored examples are reliably labeled, but in semi-supervised streams, the episodic buffer may contain examples with uncertain pseudo-labels, necessitating confidence-aware selection criteria that prioritize high-certainty samples for rehearsal. These concerns about memory selection under uncertainty echo the challenges identified in the unsupervised continual learning setting, where the absence of any labels requires entirely different buffer management strategies. Furthermore, the challenge of catastrophic forgetting in semi-supervised continual learning is not confined to discriminative models; generative models trained on mixed labeled and unlabeled streams are also susceptible, although evidence suggests that generative representations may exhibit greater inherent robustness to forgetting than their discriminative counterparts [185]—a finding consistent with observations in the unsupervised continual learning literature and offering a potential pathway for more stable semi-supervised continual learners.

Overall, semi-supervised continual learning remains significantly under-explored relative to its fully supervised counterpart, despite its greater practical relevance. Key open challenges include developing robust pseudo-labeling mechanisms that degrade gracefully under distributional uncertainty, designing memory buffers that efficiently represent both labeled and unlabeled experience, and establishing evaluation protocols that measure not only task accuracy but also the quality of representations learned from unlabeled data across the full task sequence. These challenges are further compounded in the online continual learning setting, where the additional constraints of single-pass data processing and the absence of task boundaries push the demands on label-efficient, representation-preserving learning to their practical limits.

### 7.3 Online Continual Learning

## 7.3 Online Continual Learning

Online continual learning (OCL) represents one of the most demanding settings in the continual learning landscape, requiring models to learn from a single-pass, non-stationary data stream in which each sample is observed only once and explicit task boundaries may be absent. Building on the challenges introduced by semi-supervised continual learning, OCL compounds these difficulties further by removing the assumption of multiple training passes and, in many practical deployments, reliable label availability. Unlike offline continual learning, where multiple passes over task-specific data are permitted, online settings impose severe constraints on both memory utilization and computational resources, making the stability-plasticity dilemma particularly acute [33; 186].

### Core Challenges

The fundamental difficulty of OCL stems from the combination of several compounding factors. First, the limited training signal available from any single data point dramatically reduces the model's capacity to consolidate new knowledge, exacerbating plasticity deficits [33]. Second, the absence of explicit task boundaries means that algorithms cannot rely on task identity for compartmentalizing knowledge or switching between specialized modules, a constraint that is similarly encountered in the federated continual learning setting discussed in the following subsection. Third, the streaming nature of data introduces class imbalance between current and previously observed examples, creating a task-recency bias that systematically favors newly arriving classes at the expense of older ones [186]. This recency bias is closely related to the representation drift phenomenon observed in semi-supervised continual learning, where internal model geometry shifts substantially even when surface-level performance appears stable.

### Representative Methods

A diverse array of methodological strategies has been developed to address these challenges. **Replay-based approaches** form the backbone of many competitive OCL methods, leveraging episodic memory buffers to rehearse stored samples alongside incoming data. However, naive replay is insufficient in the online setting due to severe data imbalance, motivating more sophisticated buffer management and sampling strategies that echo the confidence-aware memory selection concerns raised in the semi-supervised continual learning setting.

To simultaneously address stability and plasticity in streaming settings, several works have proposed multi-scale feature adaptation. The Multi-scale Feature Adaptation Network (MuFAN) [33] utilizes rich contextual encodings extracted at different levels of a pre-trained network, complemented by a structure-wise distillation loss and a novel stability-plasticity normalization module that replaces standard batch normalization. By fusing features at multiple granularities from a frozen encoder, MuFAN maintains high plasticity for novel inputs while preserving structural knowledge of previously encountered distributions, achieving strong performance on benchmarks including SVHN, CIFAR-100, miniImageNet, and CORe50.

**Meta-learning augmented approaches** offer another powerful paradigm for OCL. The MMKDDA framework [34] integrates meta-learning updates with multi-scale knowledge distillation and data augmentation to directly address the data imbalance problem inherent in replay-based online learning. Multiscale distillation captures the evolution of both long-range and short-range spatial relationships across feature levels, while the meta-learning update mechanism is conditioned on the number of previously seen tasks, enabling a principled balance between stability and plasticity that adapts over the course of the task sequence. Mixing episodic memory samples with current task data during training further alleviates distribution shift artifacts.

**Addressing task-recency bias** is another critical component of effective OCL. The Adaptive Focus Shifting (AFS) algorithm [186] tackles the class imbalance problem that arises when the model is continuously exposed to new classes while old class data is confined to a small replay buffer. AFS introduces a revised focal loss that directs attention toward ambiguous, high-value samples to maintain stability, complemented by a virtual knowledge distillation mechanism that assigns enhanced attention to non-target classes, thereby suppressing overconfidence and encouraging broader inter-class discrimination to promote plasticity.

**Semi-supervised and label-free online learning** settings further increase the difficulty, as the model must extract useful representations without consistent annotation. Bio-inspired approaches have sought to leverage sparse, non-overlapping neuronal representations, drawing on neuroscientific evidence that sparsity prevents catastrophic interference [43]. By employing winner-take-all sparsity mechanisms combined with lateral recurrent connections within each layer, such methods can achieve competitive continual learning performance without requiring any explicit task boundary information, aligning more closely with the conditions of genuine online learning.

**Meta-consolidation** approaches such as MERLIN [100] operate in a strictly online setting by modeling the weights of a neural network for solving each task as samples from a learned meta-distribution, which is incrementally consolidated as new tasks arrive. This probabilistic perspective allows the system to generalize across tasks while naturally encoding uncertainty about previous experiences, demonstrating consistent improvements over replay and regularization baselines in online settings.

### Evaluation Protocols

Rigorous evaluation of OCL methods requires specialized protocols that go beyond standard end-of-task accuracy measurements. The stability gap phenomenon [35] highlights a critical limitation of evaluating continual learners only after completing each task: many methods exhibit substantial but temporary forgetting immediately upon beginning a new task, followed by partial recovery. This behavior is revealed only through per-iteration evaluation frameworks that track worst-case performance throughout training. Such fine-grained evaluation exposes transient failures in knowledge retention that coarser protocols systematically obscure, emphasizing the importance of metrics that capture both worst-case stability and long-run plasticity. These concerns complement the evaluation challenges noted in semi-supervised continual learning, where surface-level accuracy metrics can mask significant representational degradation.

Beyond accuracy-based measures, evaluation in OCL increasingly focuses on metrics that jointly quantify information retention (backward transfer and forgetting measures) and online learning efficiency (accuracy on the current task as a function of the number of observed samples). Benchmarks incorporating natural distribution shifts, such as CORe50 with its temporally realistic object recognition sequences, provide more ecologically valid testbeds than artificially constructed task sequences with uniform data quantities [33]. The OCIL setting also requires evaluation under class-incremental conditions where the model must discriminate among all previously encountered classes without task identity information at test time, further increasing the evaluation challenge [186].

### Connections to Broader Themes

Online continual learning intersects deeply with several adjacent research directions. Its connection to **biologically inspired learning** is particularly strong, as the constraint of single-pass learning aligns with the way biological systems process sensory streams in real time [43; 31]. The role of **sparse representations**, neuromodulation, and local learning rules in enabling online adaptation without catastrophic forgetting continues to be an active area of investigation. Furthermore, the relationship between OCL and **meta-learning** remains fertile ground for exploration, as meta-learned initializations and update rules can substantially reduce the amount of data required for effective online adaptation [100; 34]. These themes carry forward into the federated continual learning setting, where the need to handle asynchronous, distributed data streams introduces additional constraints on how knowledge is acquired, retained, and shared across participants.

In summary, online continual learning represents a stringent and practically relevant formulation that distills the core challenges of the field into their most demanding form. Progress in OCL requires innovations across model architecture, optimization strategy, memory management, and evaluation methodology, making it a central focus for researchers seeking to build AI systems capable of genuine lifelong adaptation.

### 7.4 Federated Continual Learning

## 7.4 Federated Continual Learning

Federated continual learning (FCL) sits at the intersection of two important machine learning paradigms: federated learning, which enables collaborative model training across distributed clients without sharing raw data, and continual learning, which enables models to sequentially acquire knowledge from evolving data streams without catastrophic forgetting. Building on the challenges introduced by online continual learning—where single-pass data streams and absent task boundaries already impose severe constraints—FCL compounds these difficulties further by distributing the learning process across multiple clients operating under strict privacy requirements and heterogeneous conditions. The combination of these two paradigms introduces a unique set of challenges that go beyond what either field addresses individually.

In a typical federated continual learning setting, each distributed client independently encounters a stream of tasks or data distributions that evolve over time. Clients must learn from their local non-stationary data while periodically collaborating with a central server or with peer clients to share knowledge. However, unlike standard federated learning where all clients work on the same static task, FCL must contend with the fact that different clients may be experiencing entirely different tasks at different times, leading to heterogeneous task distributions across the federation. This heterogeneity significantly complicates both knowledge aggregation and the prevention of catastrophic forgetting—a difficulty that is closely related to the task-recency bias and class imbalance problems encountered in online continual learning, but now manifested across a distributed system rather than within a single model.

A central challenge in FCL is enabling effective inter-client knowledge transfer without violating data privacy constraints. One representative framework for addressing this is FedWeIT (Federated learning with Weighted Inter-client Transfer), which enables clients to selectively and adaptively borrow and integrate knowledge from other clients through weighted task-specific parameter decomposition. By decomposing model parameters into global shared components and task-specific components, FedWeIT allows clients to benefit from the learning of related tasks performed by remote peers while preventing interference from unrelated tasks. This approach demonstrates that targeted inter-client knowledge sharing can substantially mitigate catastrophic forgetting in federated environments, echoing the broader insight from online continual learning that carefully managing the flow of information—whether across time or across clients—is essential for maintaining stable representations.

Another prominent direction involves leveraging masked autoencoders for efficient federated continual representation learning. Masked autoencoder-based approaches exploit self-supervised reconstruction objectives to build robust and generalizable representations that remain stable across both local task shifts and global federation aggregation steps. By learning representations that are resilient to distribution changes without requiring labeled data, such methods offer a practical and privacy-preserving route to continual learning in federated settings where labeled data may be scarce—a concern that parallels the semi-supervised and label-free challenges discussed in the context of online continual learning.

Lightweight federated continual consensus architectures have also been explored to reduce communication overhead, which is a critical bottleneck when clients must synchronize frequently to combat forgetting. These architectures aim to minimize the volume of information exchanged between clients and the server by distilling consensus knowledge into compact model components, achieving a favorable balance between communication efficiency and continual learning performance.

The FCL problem is further complicated by asynchronous participation patterns among clients. In realistic deployments, different clients may be available for aggregation at different times, and the order in which tasks arrive at each client may vary considerably. This asynchrony means that naive aggregation strategies can introduce additional sources of forgetting, as model updates from clients at different stages of their task sequences may conflict when merged [187]. Addressing asynchronous federated continual learning requires aggregation policies that are sensitive to the task state of each client, as well as local learning strategies robust to the variability in global model states received from the server. The absence of reliable task boundary information in this asynchronous context mirrors the task-free challenges encountered in online continual learning, where models similarly cannot rely on explicit signals to coordinate knowledge consolidation.

Privacy preservation remains a fundamental concern throughout FCL. While federated learning inherently avoids sharing raw data, the model updates or gradients exchanged between clients and servers can still leak sensitive information about local data distributions. Continual learning settings amplify this concern, as the model must retain information about past data distributions, potentially encoding private information in its parameters over extended periods. Future work in FCL must therefore tightly integrate differential privacy mechanisms, secure aggregation protocols, and other privacy-enhancing technologies with continual learning strategies to ensure both effective knowledge retention and strong privacy guarantees. As will be seen in the following discussion of continual learning under distribution shift, the inability to store or replay data representative of prior distributions—whether due to privacy constraints in federated settings or the practical limitations of streaming systems—constitutes a shared challenge that motivates fundamentally similar algorithmic responses.

Beyond privacy, the question of fairness across clients presents another open challenge. If some clients encounter more data-rich or more easily learnable tasks, naive federated aggregation may disproportionately benefit those clients at the expense of others. Equitable FCL systems need to balance the stability-plasticity trade-off not only within each client but also across the federation as a whole, ensuring that knowledge sharing benefits all participants fairly regardless of their local task distributions or data availability. This cross-client equity concern bears a structural resemblance to the broader challenge of managing heterogeneous and potentially conflicting data distributions, a theme that recurs prominently in the context of distribution shift and out-of-distribution robustness.

In summary, federated continual learning represents a rich and practically important research direction where challenges from both federated learning and continual learning compound one another. Key open problems include designing efficient and privacy-preserving inter-client knowledge transfer mechanisms, handling asynchronous and heterogeneous task distributions across clients, reducing communication costs without sacrificing knowledge retention, and ensuring fairness and robustness across diverse deployment environments [187]. These challenges set the stage for the following subsection, which examines how continual learning systems must additionally contend with the more gradual, unpredictable, and often unlabeled distribution shifts that characterize real-world deployment.

### 7.5 Continual Learning under Distribution Shift and Out-of-Distribution Challenges

## 7.5 Continual Learning under Distribution Shift and Out-of-Distribution Challenges

A fundamental challenge in real-world continual learning is that data distributions rarely change in the clean, discrete manner assumed by standard task-incremental benchmarks. In practice, systems must contend with covariate shift—where the input distribution changes while the underlying label mapping remains stable—as well as concept drift, where the relationship between inputs and outputs evolves over time. These phenomena differ critically from standard task boundaries: rather than abrupt, well-defined transitions between distinct tasks, distribution shifts are often gradual, overlapping, and lack explicit signals indicating when a change has occurred. This distinction has motivated a growing body of research specifically targeting the intersection of continual learning and distribution shift. As we will see in the following section on cross-domain continual learning, these challenges become even more pronounced when tasks originate from fundamentally different data domains, making distribution shift robustness a prerequisite for broader continual learning capabilities.

### Distinguishing Distribution Shift from Task Boundaries

In conventional continual learning formulations, the model is typically provided with explicit task identity information or at least clear task boundary signals, enabling controlled evaluation of forgetting and transfer. Distribution shift scenarios, by contrast, involve continuous or irregular changes in data-generating processes without such explicit demarcation. This creates compounded difficulties: the model must simultaneously detect that a shift has occurred, decide how to adapt its representations, and determine what prior knowledge remains relevant. The absence of clear task identifiers also complicates memory management strategies, since the model cannot easily associate stored exemplars with well-defined task contexts.

Furthermore, out-of-distribution (OOD) data—samples drawn from distributions not represented during any previous training phase—poses a related but distinct challenge. When a continual learner encounters OOD inputs, it may either attempt to assimilate them into existing knowledge structures, risking catastrophic interference, or fail to recognize them as novel, leading to overconfident but incorrect predictions. Managing the interplay between in-distribution forgetting and OOD robustness remains an open research problem of considerable practical importance. This concern connects closely to the federated continual learning challenges discussed in the previous section, where heterogeneous client distributions similarly create uncertainty about whether incoming data represents a known distribution variant or a genuinely novel one.

### Covariate Shift and Concept Drift in Continual Settings

Covariate shift arises frequently in deployment scenarios such as autonomous driving, medical imaging, and sensor-based activity recognition, where environmental conditions, acquisition hardware, or population demographics change gradually over time. Continual learning methods designed for clean task sequences often degrade substantially under covariate shift because their forgetting-prevention mechanisms—whether regularization-based, replay-based, or architecture-based—are typically calibrated to task-level granularity rather than to the fine-grained statistical perturbations that characterize real-world distribution drift.

Concept drift introduces an additional layer of complexity: if the ground-truth labeling function itself changes, purely preserving old representations may actively harm performance. This motivates approaches that incorporate controlled or active forgetting mechanisms alongside standard stability mechanisms. Neuro-inspired approaches such as those modeling biological active forgetting have demonstrated that selectively discarding outdated knowledge can benefit adaptation to new distributions, particularly when old and new task distributions conflict [65]. Analogously, the schematic memory framework proposed in [62] introduces both long-term forgetting mechanisms for low-utility memories and short-term forgetting mechanisms inspired by background-information-gated learning, providing a theoretically grounded strategy for managing knowledge under noisy and shifting distributions. This dual approach achieves both robustness to noisy inputs and generalizability across evolving data regimes.

### Domain Adaptation Strategies for Continual Distribution Shift

Domain adaptation techniques offer natural synergies with continual learning under distribution shift, as both fields are concerned with bridging gaps between data-generating processes. A prominent strategy involves leveraging unlabeled or weakly labeled data from the new distribution to guide representation alignment without requiring full retraining. Pseudo-labeling, wherein a model generates provisional labels for incoming unlabeled samples before using them for adaptation, has been widely explored as a low-cost mechanism for continuous domain alignment. However, pseudo-labels generated under distribution shift can be unreliable, propagating errors into the model's feature representations and destabilizing the consolidation of prior knowledge.

Structural distillation approaches address this instability by constraining the evolution of intermediate feature representations rather than merely output predictions. By enforcing that the feature geometry learned on prior distributions is preserved as the model encounters new domains, structural distillation mitigates the representational drift that underlies many forgetting failures under covariate shift. Methods that combine knowledge distillation with contrastive regularization have shown particular promise, as they simultaneously prevent forgetting of prior inter-class relationships and promote alignment of new distributions with existing representational structure. The Knowledge Invariant and Spread-out Properties framework explicitly constrains the evolution of embedding features to preserve inter-instance structural knowledge from previous distributions, directly addressing the learning bias that arises when new data distributions diverge from prior ones [103]. These domain adaptation strategies provide important groundwork for the more extreme distributional gaps encountered in cross-domain continual learning, where tasks may span entirely different modalities or application contexts.

### Task-Free and Online Adaptation to Shifting Distributions

Many real-world distribution shift scenarios are inherently task-free: the model receives a continuous stream of data without explicit task labels or boundary notifications. Online continual learning methods designed for this regime must detect distributional changes implicitly, typically through monitoring prediction confidence, feature drift statistics, or gradient characteristics. Unsupervised approaches such as the continual self-organizing map [188] have explored how neural models can adapt their topology continuously to new data distributions without supervision, offering task-free mechanisms for tracking concept drift. However, scaling such approaches to high-dimensional visual and language domains while maintaining competitive performance remains challenging.

The challenge of handling unknown class distributions in unlabeled data streams is particularly acute when new distributions introduce previously unseen categories. In these open-world scenarios, the model must simultaneously manage forgetting of old classes, adaptation to new distributions, and recognition of genuinely novel inputs—a triply difficult problem that standard continual learning benchmarks rarely capture. This open-world complexity foreshadows the even greater representational demands of cross-domain settings, where novel categories may also come paired with entirely unfamiliar feature distributions.

### Benchmarks Incorporating Natural Distribution Shifts

A persistent limitation of the continual learning literature has been its reliance on artificially constructed task sequences—such as randomly permuted pixels or disjoint class splits—that poorly reflect the natural distribution shifts encountered in real deployments. Recognizing this gap, researchers have increasingly advocated for benchmarks that incorporate natural, temporally realistic distribution shifts. Benchmarks such as CLEAR and similar temporally ordered visual recognition datasets introduce genuine temporal distribution evolution drawn from real-world data collection processes, enabling evaluation under conditions that more faithfully simulate how distributions drift in practice. In the reinforcement learning domain, the Continual World benchmark [9] evaluates agents on sequences of robotic manipulation tasks with meaningfully diverse dynamics, providing a more ecologically valid testbed for assessing adaptation under distribution change compared to grid-world or Atari-based evaluations.

Evaluating continual learners under natural distribution shifts also requires rethinking standard metrics. Average accuracy over a fixed task sequence does not adequately capture how well a model maintains calibrated predictions under continuous drift, nor does it reflect the model's ability to detect and respond to distribution changes adaptively. Newer evaluation frameworks that assess forward transfer, backward transfer, and stability under gradually shifting distributions are increasingly recognized as necessary complements to forgetting-centric metrics [9; 46]. These evaluation considerations extend naturally to cross-domain benchmarks, where multi-dataset evaluation must similarly account for heterogeneous data scales and domain ordering effects.

### Interaction between Negative Transfer and Distribution Shift

An important subtlety in continual learning under distribution shift is the heightened risk of negative transfer: when a new distribution conflicts with previously learned representations, forcing the model to retain old knowledge can actively impede adaptation. This is analogous to the broader challenge of negative forward knowledge transfer identified in standard continual learning [65; 64]. Under distribution shift, the boundary between harmful forgetting of genuinely useful prior knowledge and beneficial discarding of obsolete representations becomes especially blurred. Methods that can quantify task or distribution correlation—and selectively determine which prior knowledge to preserve versus release—are therefore particularly valuable in this regime [65; 64].

Theoretical work on knowledge transfer provides relevant foundations for understanding when distribution-shifted updates are likely to be beneficial or harmful. Gradient projection analyses that identify conditions under which model updates improve performance on old tasks [61] carry direct implications for distribution shift settings: whether updating representations to accommodate a new distribution will constitute backward transfer or catastrophic forgetting depends sensitively on the geometric relationship between old and new data manifolds. Formalizing these relationships in the context of continuous distribution shift—rather than discrete task sequences—represents a promising direction for future theoretical and empirical work, and forms a natural bridge to understanding the even larger representational gaps that arise in cross-domain continual learning.

### Open Directions

Despite recent progress, several important challenges remain underexplored at the intersection of continual learning and distribution shift. First, most existing methods assume that the model can identify task or distribution boundaries at least implicitly, yet many real-world systems must operate entirely without such signals. Developing robust change-point detection mechanisms that can be integrated seamlessly with forgetting-prevention strategies is an important open problem. Second, OOD detection in continual settings—where the definition of "in-distribution" itself evolves—requires uncertainty quantification approaches that remain calibrated across the full history of observed distributions. Third, the interaction between distribution shift and data privacy constraints, particularly in federated or on-device settings, introduces additional complications, as the model may not be permitted to store or replay data representative of prior distributions; this directly echoes the privacy concerns raised in the context of federated continual learning. Addressing these challenges will require tighter integration of distribution shift robustness into the core algorithmic and theoretical frameworks of continual learning, ultimately enabling systems that can operate reliably across the heterogeneous and evolving data distributions encountered in cross-domain deployment scenarios.

### 7.6 Cross-Domain Continual Learning

## 7.6 Cross-Domain Continual Learning

Cross-domain continual learning addresses one of the most challenging and practically relevant variants of the continual learning problem: sequentially acquiring knowledge from tasks that originate from substantially different data distributions, modalities, or application domains. As anticipated in the preceding discussion of distribution shift, the challenges examined there—covariate shift, concept drift, OOD robustness, and the difficulty of detecting distributional boundaries—become dramatically more pronounced when tasks not only shift gradually within a single domain but originate from fundamentally different feature spaces altogether. Unlike standard class-incremental or task-incremental settings where tasks share a common underlying visual or semantic space, cross-domain continual learning must contend with severe inter-domain data drift, feature misalignment across heterogeneous task spaces, and the difficulty of transferring representations learned in one domain to fundamentally different ones.

The core challenge in cross-domain continual learning stems from the fact that feature representations optimized for one domain may be poorly suited or even detrimental to learning in another domain. When a model trained on natural images is subsequently adapted to medical imaging, satellite imagery, or artistic renditions, the distributional gap can be so large that naive sequential fine-tuning causes catastrophic forgetting of prior knowledge while simultaneously impeding forward transfer to new domains. This tension is compounded by the fact that standard regularization approaches, which anchor model parameters near their previous values, may over-constrain adaptation when the new domain requires substantially different feature extraction strategies. In this sense, cross-domain continual learning represents the most extreme manifestation of the negative transfer risks and geometric incompatibilities between data manifolds identified in the distribution shift section—where the question of which prior knowledge to preserve versus release becomes especially consequential.

To address these challenges, researchers have developed methods that combine inter-task and intra-task cross-attention mechanisms with unsupervised domain adaptation principles. These approaches learn domain-invariant feature spaces that can be shared across heterogeneous tasks while preserving task-specific representational components that capture domain-unique structure. By jointly modeling shared and private components of each domain's feature distribution, such methods aim to enable both positive forward transfer to new domains and backward transfer that reinforces rather than degrades previously learned representations. This decomposition into shared and private components parallels the structural distillation and contrastive regularization strategies discussed in the context of distribution shift adaptation, but must operate across far larger representational gaps.

Multi-dataset benchmarks have emerged as an important evaluation vehicle for cross-domain continual learning, reflecting the real-world complexity of deploying models across diverse application contexts. Such benchmarks incorporate datasets from genuinely heterogeneous domains—spanning natural scene recognition, fine-grained categorization, medical imaging, remote sensing, and texture recognition—thereby exposing methods to the full breadth of distribution shift encountered in practical deployments. Evaluations on benchmarks of this type reveal that methods designed purely for within-domain task sequences often fail dramatically when confronted with cross-domain scenarios, motivating the development of more robust and flexible continual learning architectures. As noted in the distribution shift discussion, evaluation metrics must similarly evolve beyond average accuracy to capture forward transfer, backward transfer, and calibration across heterogeneous domains with varying dataset sizes, class granularities, and domain ordering effects.

The integration of large pre-trained vision-language models offers a promising direction for cross-domain continual learning, as these models encode rich, broadly transferable representations spanning diverse semantic and visual concepts. Work on continual learning with pre-trained models such as CLIP has demonstrated that frozen or lightly adapted foundation model representations can exhibit remarkable robustness to cross-domain task sequences [77], as the broad pre-training coverage reduces the effective distributional gap between heterogeneous tasks. Prompt-based adaptation strategies further enable efficient domain-specific tuning while preserving cross-domain knowledge encoded in the frozen backbone [167], making them particularly attractive for cross-domain settings where full model fine-tuning risks overwriting broadly useful representations. These prompt-based mechanisms also anticipate the meta-learning perspective examined in the following section, where the goal shifts from simply preventing forgetting to acquiring higher-order learning strategies that generalize across diverse sequential experiences.

Benchmarks that explicitly incorporate multiple heterogeneous datasets have been proposed to rigorously assess cross-domain continual learning capabilities [57]. These benchmarks reveal that existing continual learning methods exhibit high levels of forgetting and limited forward transfer when tasks span genuinely different domains, highlighting a significant gap between performance on single-domain benchmarks and real-world multi-domain deployment scenarios [76]. The design of such benchmarks must carefully control for factors such as dataset size imbalance, varying class granularities, and domain ordering effects, all of which can dramatically influence measured continual learning performance.

Evaluation on diverse multi-dataset benchmarks also exposes important limitations of current cross-domain continual learning methods with respect to scalability [164]. As the number of domains grows, methods that maintain domain-specific components face increasing memory and computational overhead, while methods that rely on shared representations face growing interference across heterogeneous feature spaces. Addressing these scalability challenges while maintaining the ability to accumulate and transfer knowledge across heterogeneous domains remains an important open problem for the field. As will become apparent in the following section on meta-learning integration, one promising route toward resolving this tension lies in acquiring flexible learning strategies that intrinsically generalize across heterogeneous domains—shifting the burden from hand-designed domain alignment mechanisms to learned inductive biases that enable rapid, interference-free adaptation regardless of the domain of origin.

### 7.7 Continual Learning Integrated with Meta-Learning

## 7.7 Continual Learning Integrated with Meta-Learning

The intersection of meta-learning and continual learning represents one of the most promising avenues for developing systems that can genuinely learn how to learn over sequential task streams. While standard continual learning methods—including those cross-domain approaches discussed in the preceding section—typically focus on preventing forgetting of accumulated knowledge, meta-learning augmented approaches aim to endow models with inductive biases and optimization strategies that intrinsically reduce catastrophic interference while accelerating adaptation to novel tasks. This synergy has given rise to a rich family of meta-continual learning algorithms that address the stability-plasticity dilemma from a higher-order optimization perspective, and whose architectural implications connect naturally to the dynamic architecture methods explored in the following section.

A foundational contribution in this area is the Neuromodulated Meta-Learning Algorithm (ANML), which draws inspiration from neuromodulatory processes in biological neural systems [92]. ANML differentiates through a sequential learning process to meta-learn an activation-gating function that enables context-dependent selective activation within a deep neural network. Specifically, a neuromodulatory network gates the forward pass of a prediction learning network, effectively controlling selective plasticity through the backward pass as well. By meta-learning this gating mechanism across long task sequences, ANML achieves state-of-the-art continual learning performance on benchmarks involving hundreds of sequentially learned classes, demonstrating that meta-learning can directly encode forgetting-resistant inductive biases into network architectures. This notion of learned gating over network substructures anticipates the dynamic and sparse subnetwork methods examined in Section 7.8.

Meta-learning has also been productively combined with experience replay to enhance the efficiency of memory-based continual learning under data-scarce conditions. When each past task is allocated with limited replay memory, standard replay mechanics become less effective; supplementing replay with meta-learning—specifically by employing a meta-learner that learns a learning rate per parameter per past task—enables base learners to produce strong results even when less memory is available [189]. This approach inherits additional meta-learning advantages, including robustness to noisy data and faster convergence to high accuracy with fewer gradient updates, making it practically valuable for on-device and resource-constrained deployments, including cross-domain settings where data from prior domains may be scarce.

The Meta-Consolidation framework (MERLIN) offers another perspective on the meta-learning and continual learning synergy by modeling network weights for solving a given task as samples drawn from a learned meta-distribution [100]. This meta-distribution is incrementally consolidated as new tasks arrive, allowing the model to maintain a compact probabilistic representation of all previously encountered task solutions. Operating in the challenging online continual learning setting where each data point is observed only once, MERLIN demonstrates consistent improvements over baselines on MNIST, CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Mini-ImageNet benchmarks, highlighting the value of meta-distributional modeling for knowledge retention and generalization across diverse task sequences. This probabilistic treatment of task-specific weight distributions parallels the Bayesian structure adaptation approaches discussed in the following section, where probabilistic masks over network weights serve a similar role in mediating inter-task knowledge sharing.

Context-dependent selective activation, a mechanism central to several meta-continual learning approaches, also appears prominently in bio-inspired architectures that use local learning rules and neuromodulation to enable online continual learning without relying on global backpropagation [44; 45]. These architectures incorporate synaptic plasticity mechanisms and neuromodulatory signals to dynamically gate parameter updates based on contextual information, effectively implementing a form of learned selective plasticity that mirrors the meta-learning objective of acquiring task-specific learning strategies. By transferring meta-learned hyperparameters identified through Bayesian optimization across tasks with similarity considerations, such approaches achieve competitive continual learning performance without maintaining large replay buffers [45].

The TAMiL framework further exemplifies how meta-learning principles can be operationalized through attention-based architectures inspired by the Global Workspace Theory of conscious information access in the brain [96]. TAMiL employs task-attention modules to selectively route task-relevant information from a common representation space through an information bottleneck, reducing task interference in a manner conceptually related to meta-learning's goal of learning task-general structure while preserving task-specific adaptations. Experimental results indicate that this method effectively mitigates catastrophic forgetting while remaining well-calibrated with reduced task-recency bias, bridging the gap between fixed-capacity and parameter-isolation approaches. The notion of routing task-relevant information through structured architectural components connects directly to the mixture-of-experts and dynamic architecture strategies discussed in Section 7.8.

Collectively, these meta-continual learning approaches reveal that the learning-to-learn objective provides a powerful organizing principle for continual learning algorithm design. By shifting the optimization target from minimizing forgetting on a fixed task sequence to acquiring flexible learning strategies that generalize across diverse sequential experiences—whether within a single domain or across the heterogeneous domains addressed in Section 7.6—meta-learning offers a principled route toward systems that are simultaneously stable in retaining old knowledge and plastic in acquiring new knowledge. This direct computational instantiation of the stability-plasticity balance is further pursued in the following section through the lens of neural architecture search and dynamic architectures, where structural flexibility itself becomes a mechanism for achieving the same equilibrium.

### 7.8 Continual Learning Integrated with Neural Architecture Search and Dynamic Architectures

## 7.8 Continual Learning Integrated with Neural Architecture Search and Dynamic Architectures

A fundamental challenge in continual learning is determining the appropriate network structure to accommodate an evolving stream of tasks without sacrificing previously acquired knowledge. Static architectures must balance a fixed parameter budget against an unbounded sequence of learning demands, motivating research into dynamic architecture design and automated neural architecture search (NAS) as principled mechanisms for achieving scalable plasticity-stability trade-offs. The approaches surveyed in this section build naturally upon the meta-learned gating and structural modulation strategies introduced in Section 7.7, while anticipating the biological principles of sparse representations, neurogenesis-inspired growth, and neuromodulatory gating explored in Section 7.9.

**Bayesian Structure Adaptation.** One of the most principled approaches to dynamic architecture in continual learning is Bayesian structure adaptation, which learns probabilistic masks over network weights to identify task-specific subnetworks [119]. By treating the selection of active parameters as a Bayesian inference problem, this framework enables different tasks to utilize overlapping yet partially distinct sparse subsets of weights. Crucially, the degree of overlap between subnetworks mediates inter-task knowledge transfer: tasks that share structural components benefit from positive forward and backward transfer, while tasks with largely disjoint subnetworks are protected from catastrophic interference. This probabilistic treatment of task-specific weight distributions parallels the meta-distributional modeling of task solutions introduced in the MERLIN framework discussed in Section 7.7, with both approaches leveraging structured probabilistic representations to mediate inter-task knowledge sharing. Experiments on both supervised and unsupervised benchmarks confirm that this approach performs comparably to or better than state-of-the-art continual learning methods while providing a principled mechanism for capacity allocation [119].

**Parameter Isolation and Sparse Subnetwork Methods.** A closely related direction involves parameter isolation through sparse network architectures. By designating disjoint or minimally overlapping parameter subsets to different tasks, these methods structurally prevent interference at the cost of reduced shared representation. Continual learning with adaptive weights (CLAW) extends this notion through probabilistic modelling and variational inference, allowing the network to adaptively identify which components to share across tasks in a data-driven manner rather than relying on fixed architectural decisions [149]. Similarly, adversarial continual learning decomposes representations into task-invariant shared features and task-specific components, combining architectural growth for task-specific skills with replay for shared skills, demonstrating superiority over purely architecture-based or memory-based approaches alone [127]. The sparse subnetwork paradigm also connects directly to the context-dependent selective activation mechanisms meta-learned by ANML in Section 7.7, where learned gating over network substructures anticipates the more explicit structural parameterization pursued here.

**Mixture-of-Experts and Hierarchical Architectures.** Dynamic architecture principles naturally extend to mixture-of-experts formulations. The Mixture-of-Variational-Experts layer creates multiple information-processing paths governed by a learned gating policy, effectively instantiating a set of specialized sub-networks that can be differentially recruited for different tasks [150; 151]. This design is grounded in a hierarchical information-theoretic optimality principle derived from a Bayesian perspective, facilitating a theoretically motivated trade-off between learning and forgetting. Importantly, the gating mechanism operates in a task-agnostic manner, requiring no explicit task identity at inference time, which substantially broadens applicability to realistic deployment settings. The task-agnostic routing of task-relevant information through structured architectural components connects directly to the TAMiL framework's attention-based routing introduced in Section 7.7, where similar principles were pursued through a meta-learning lens. Empirical results across supervised learning, reinforcement learning, and generative modelling demonstrate the versatility of this architectural principle [151].

**Sparse Distributed Memory and Bio-Inspired Architectures.** Biological neural systems achieve remarkable continual learning capabilities through sparse, distributed representations that minimize interference between stored memories. Translating this principle into artificial systems, sparse distributed memory (SDM) architectures have been shown to function as effective continual learners without requiring any memory replay buffer or explicit task boundary information [42]. Each component of the bio-inspired MLP variant derived from SDM—including sparse connectivity, Hebbian-like update rules, and distributed storage—is found to be individually necessary for robust continual performance, underscoring that architectural choices themselves, rather than only algorithmic strategies, critically determine continual learning capacity. This convergence between architectural sparsity and biological principles of representational economy provides a natural conceptual bridge to the synaptic plasticity and sparse coding mechanisms examined in greater depth in Section 7.9.

**Dynamic Expandable Networks.** Rather than searching for a fixed optimal architecture, dynamic expandable networks grow their capacity in response to new task demands. This approach allocates new neurons or modules when existing capacity is insufficient, while freezing previously learned parameters to preserve acquired knowledge. By coupling expansion with selective parameter sharing, dynamic architectures can scale gracefully to long task sequences where fixed-capacity approaches inevitably saturate. The challenge lies in controlling expansion to avoid unbounded model growth, motivating pruning, quantization, and structured sparsity as complementary mechanisms for maintaining computational efficiency alongside architectural flexibility. This capacity-expansion strategy is directly analogous to neurogenesis-inspired growth in biological brains, a correspondence that the TriRE framework and related multi-mechanism paradigms discussed in Section 7.9 explore more explicitly.

**Neural Architecture Search for Continual Learning.** NAS offers the prospect of automating the discovery of architectural inductive biases particularly suited to sequential task learning. Rather than relying on human-designed architectures, NAS can in principle identify network structures that inherently support knowledge retention while maintaining plasticity for new tasks. Integrating NAS with Bayesian continual learning frameworks—such as those built on variational inference over structural parameters [119; 149]—provides a coherent probabilistic foundation for jointly optimizing task performance and architectural decisions across a task sequence.

**Connections to Parameter-Efficient Tuning and Prompt-Based Methods.** Dynamic architecture principles intersect with parameter-efficient continual learning approaches, particularly prompt tuning and adapter-based methods applied to large pre-trained models. Convolutional prompt creation mechanisms that maintain layer-wise shared embeddings for knowledge transfer across tasks [136] can be viewed as a lightweight form of dynamic architecture, where task-specific structural components are added while the backbone remains frozen. This intersection suggests a promising convergence between NAS-inspired dynamic architectures and parameter-efficient adaptation of foundation models as a scalable paradigm for practical continual learning.

**Challenges and Open Directions.** Despite substantial progress, several open challenges remain. First, the computational cost of NAS itself conflicts with the online or streaming nature of continual learning, where architectural decisions may need to be made rapidly as new tasks arrive. Second, most dynamic architecture methods assume discrete task boundaries and explicit task identifiers, limiting applicability to task-agnostic and online continual learning settings. Third, theoretical understanding of how architectural choices influence the stability-plasticity trade-off under realistic non-stationary data streams remains limited [12; 190]. Addressing these challenges will require tighter integration of architectural flexibility, efficient Bayesian inference, and theoretical analysis of how network structure mediates knowledge accumulation across long and heterogeneous task sequences. These open questions connect naturally to the biological mechanisms surveyed in Section 7.9, where the brain's multi-level organization — spanning molecular plasticity, neuromodulatory gating, and systems-level memory consolidation — offers a rich repertoire of complementary solutions to the same fundamental challenges that dynamic artificial architectures seek to address.

### 7.9 Biologically Inspired and Neuromodulatory Approaches to Continual Learning

## 7.9 Biologically Inspired Continual Learning

The architectural principles explored in the preceding subsection — sparse subnetworks, dynamic capacity expansion, and mixture-of-experts gating — find natural parallels in the organization of biological neural systems. Indeed, biological brains provide a rich source of inspiration for continual learning algorithms, offering mechanisms that have evolved over millions of years to enable flexible yet stable knowledge accumulation in non-stationary environments. Unlike artificial neural networks trained via global backpropagation, the brain employs a diverse array of local, context-sensitive learning mechanisms that collectively address the stability-plasticity dilemma in ways that current deep learning systems struggle to replicate. This subsection surveys the key biological principles that have informed continual learning research, drawing connections between neuroscientific theory and the algorithmic innovations they have motivated.

**Synaptic Plasticity and Local Learning Rules**

One of the most fundamental biological principles relevant to continual learning is synaptic plasticity, which governs how individual synaptic connections are strengthened or weakened based on local activity patterns. Hebbian learning, spike-timing-dependent plasticity, and related rules operate on purely local information — the pre- and post-synaptic activities — without requiring a global error signal propagated through the entire network. Architectures that incorporate such local learning rules can, in principle, perform online continual learning without the interference patterns introduced by gradient descent through shared parameters [44]. These local rules naturally give rise to sparse, distributed representations that reduce interference between distinct memory traces, a property that has been leveraged in computational models drawing from the complementary learning systems theory. The connection to the sparse subnetwork architectures discussed in Section 7.8 is direct: both biological local plasticity and artificial parameter isolation aim to reduce inter-task interference through representational specialization.

**Neuromodulation and Context-Dependent Gating**

Neuromodulatory systems in the brain — including dopaminergic, cholinergic, and noradrenergic pathways — play a critical role in regulating learning rates, gating plasticity, and coordinating memory consolidation. Inspired by these mechanisms, continual learning architectures have incorporated neuromodulatory signals that dynamically adjust which neurons and connections are updated during learning of a new task. Context-dependent gating mechanisms, where a neuromodulatory signal selects task-relevant subnetworks while suppressing changes to task-irrelevant parameters, constitute a powerful strategy for parameter isolation that avoids catastrophic forgetting without explicit task boundary information. Such mechanisms have been shown to yield superior online continual learning performance while operating under strict memory constraints, outperforming many memory-intensive replay-based approaches [44]. This functional parallel to the Bayesian structure adaptation and dynamic gating approaches reviewed in Section 7.8 highlights how architectural and biological motivations converge on similar structural solutions.

**Metaplasticity and Active Forgetting**

Metaplasticity refers to the plasticity of synaptic plasticity itself — the idea that the history of a synapse's activity modulates its future capacity for change. This principle motivates continual learning methods that maintain a form of memory consolidation whereby important synaptic weights become progressively more resistant to modification. Closely related is the concept of active forgetting, wherein the biological brain strategically eliminates outdated or interfering memories rather than passively retaining all past knowledge. Computational analogues of this process have been explored in frameworks that incorporate a "refresh learning" mechanism, inspired by the neuroscientific observation that the brain sheds outdated information to improve retention of crucial knowledge and facilitate new learning [107]. Such approaches first perform an unlearning step on current data before relearning it, effectively preventing the accumulation of stale knowledge patterns that would otherwise interfere with future learning. This active management of memory capacity anticipates the theoretical knowledge transfer considerations developed in Section 7.10, where the geometry of task relationships determines which prior knowledge is beneficial to retain or modify.

**Multi-Mechanism Paradigms: Combining Neurogenesis and Rehearsal**

Biological brains do not rely on a single mechanism to achieve continual learning; rather, they employ multiple complementary systems operating at different temporal and spatial scales. Inspired by this insight, multi-mechanism paradigms have been proposed that integrate several bio-inspired principles simultaneously. The TriRE framework represents one such approach, combining neurogenesis-inspired capacity expansion with experience rehearsal to achieve robust continual learning across diverse task sequences. By allowing the network to grow selectively in response to new tasks (analogous to adult neurogenesis in the hippocampus) while simultaneously rehearsing past experiences, such architectures exploit the complementary strengths of structural plasticity and memory replay. This synergistic combination reflects the interplay between hippocampal rapid encoding and neocortical slow consolidation described in complementary learning systems theory [6]. The dynamic expandable network approaches discussed in Section 7.8 can thus be understood as engineering instantiations of this neurogenesis-inspired growth principle.

**Meta-Learned Feedback Connections**

The brain extensively uses top-down feedback connections that carry predictive and contextual signals to modulate sensory processing and learning in lower areas. Meta-learning approaches that learn these feedback connections — effectively learning how to learn — capture a functional analog of this biological organization. By meta-training a set of feedback weights that guide parameter updates during continual learning, such architectures can adapt rapidly to new tasks while protecting previously acquired knowledge through learned gating signals. These meta-learned modulation mechanisms align with the broader observation that the brain's capacity for continual learning arises not just from individual synaptic rules but from the orchestrated interaction of multiple learned regulatory processes. From a knowledge transfer perspective, meta-learned feedback connections enable a form of structured forward transfer by encoding inductive biases about how to generalize across related tasks — a theme developed more formally in Section 7.10.

**Sparsity and Distributed Representations**

Biological neural circuits exhibit high degrees of sparsity: at any given moment, only a small fraction of neurons are active in response to a stimulus. Sparse representations reduce the overlap between different memory traces, thereby minimizing interference — a principle that directly addresses catastrophic forgetting. Artificial architectures that enforce sparsity through biological mechanisms such as lateral inhibition, winner-take-all competition, or variational Bayesian sparsity priors on neuron activations have demonstrated significant improvements in continual learning performance [109]. By reserving unused model capacity for future tasks and minimizing inter-task parameter interference, sparse neuro-inspired architectures achieve a natural form of parameter isolation that scales gracefully with the number of tasks. This convergence between biological sparsity principles and the sparse distributed memory architectures reviewed in Section 7.8 underscores the cross-cutting importance of representational sparsity as both a biological and engineering solution to continual learning.

**Discussion and Outlook**

While biologically inspired approaches to continual learning offer compelling conceptual advantages, significant challenges remain in translating these principles into practical, scalable algorithms for modern deep learning architectures. The brain's mechanisms operate at multiple levels of organization — molecular, synaptic, cellular, circuit, and systems — and capturing their full complexity in artificial systems is far from trivial. Moreover, many biological learning rules are difficult to apply to deep hierarchical networks where gradient-based optimization remains essential for achieving state-of-the-art performance on complex benchmarks. Nevertheless, the evidence accumulated thus far suggests that incorporating even partial biological principles — such as synaptic consolidation, neuromodulatory gating, local learning rules, and structural plasticity — can substantially improve the robustness of continual learners in non-stationary environments [44; 107]. Future research directions include the development of hybrid architectures that combine backpropagation-based optimization with biologically plausible local plasticity rules, as well as theoretical frameworks that characterize the conditions under which neuro-inspired mechanisms provide provable guarantees against catastrophic forgetting. Such theoretical grounding connects naturally to the broader knowledge transfer analyses presented in the following subsection, where formal tools from learning theory and gradient geometry are brought to bear on the mechanisms by which biological and artificial continual learners accumulate and reuse knowledge across task sequences.

### 7.10 Knowledge Transfer: Theoretical and Empirical Perspectives

## 7.10 Knowledge Transfer: Theoretical and Empirical Perspectives

The biologically inspired mechanisms surveyed in Section 7.9 — synaptic consolidation, neuromodulatory gating, structural plasticity, and sparse distributed representations — collectively serve a deeper functional purpose: enabling the selective transfer and reuse of knowledge across task sequences while guarding against catastrophic interference. This functional objective connects the biological and engineering perspectives examined thus far to a set of theoretical and empirical questions concerning when, why, and how knowledge accumulated from prior tasks can benefit future learning, and vice versa. Knowledge transfer in continual learning encompasses two complementary directions: forward transfer, whereby knowledge acquired from earlier tasks accelerates or improves learning on subsequent tasks, and backward transfer, whereby learning new tasks retroactively refines performance on previously encountered ones. While the foundations section introduced these concepts at a high level, this subsection provides a deeper theoretical and empirical treatment of the mechanisms underlying both transfer directions, the conditions that promote positive transfer, and the methods specifically designed to maximize knowledge reuse across diverse task sequences.

### Theoretical Frameworks for Knowledge Transfer

Theoretical analyses of knowledge transfer in continual learning often draw upon generalization bounds derived from PAC-learning and statistical learning theory to characterize when and how much a learner can benefit from prior task experience. A central insight emerging from these analyses is that the degree of positive transfer is fundamentally governed by the similarity between the distributions and hypothesis classes of the tasks in a sequence. When tasks share substantial feature structure or label semantics, forward transfer can significantly reduce the sample complexity of learning new tasks. Conversely, when tasks are dissimilar or even adversarially related, the same shared parameterization that enables transfer can introduce negative interference, leading to catastrophic forgetting.

Gradient-based analyses provide a complementary lens for understanding transfer. By examining the alignment between gradients computed on current task data and those computed on previously seen task data, researchers can characterize conditions under which a model update simultaneously improves performance across tasks. Gradient projection methods formalize this intuition by constraining updates to lie in subspaces that are approximately orthogonal to the gradient directions associated with past tasks, thereby minimizing interference while preserving plasticity for new learning. These approaches have been shown both theoretically and empirically to reduce forgetting without completely sacrificing the capacity to transfer useful representations. Notably, the gradient geometry underlying these projection methods bears a direct connection to the parameter isolation strategies reviewed in Section 7.8 and the neuromodulatory gating mechanisms discussed in Section 7.9: all three approaches ultimately seek to partition or constrain parameter updates so that task-specific knowledge is preserved while shared structure remains available for transfer.

The role of task ordering in mediating transfer is another theoretically studied phenomenon. Formal analyses suggest that curricula in which tasks are ordered from simpler to more complex, or from structurally related to progressively more distant, can systematically improve both forward and backward transfer. This connects continual learning theory to curriculum learning principles and provides a rationale for empirically observed performance variations across different task orderings in sequential benchmark evaluations.

### Selective Backward Knowledge Transfer

While most continual learning research focuses on preventing the erosion of past performance, the possibility of systematically improving performance on earlier tasks through the process of learning new ones — that is, positive backward transfer — has received comparatively less attention. The CUBER framework represents a notable contribution in this direction, proposing selective backward knowledge transfer via gradient projection. CUBER identifies which prior tasks stand to benefit from knowledge encoded in newly learned tasks and constructs targeted gradient updates that selectively reinforce earlier task representations without requiring explicit storage of large exemplar buffers. This gradient projection strategy operates by identifying subspaces of parameter space that are beneficial for earlier tasks and projecting new learning signals accordingly, enabling a form of constructive interference rather than the destructive interference that characterizes catastrophic forgetting.

The theoretical motivation for selective backward transfer rests on the observation that task relatedness is not symmetric: knowledge from a later task may be highly informative for an earlier task even when the reverse does not hold. By explicitly modeling this asymmetry through gradient projection, CUBER and related methods can achieve backward transfer that is both targeted and controllable, avoiding the risk of inadvertently disrupting well-consolidated knowledge while enabling genuine improvement on selected prior tasks. This asymmetry also resonates with the biological observation from Section 7.9 that neocortical consolidation is not a simple replay of hippocampal encodings, but a selective and transformative process that reorganizes earlier memories in light of newer experiences.

### Weight Sharing in Pruning-Based Continual Learning

Pruning-based continual learning methods offer another mechanism for structured knowledge transfer. By identifying and preserving subnetworks that are particularly important for prior tasks — often through magnitude-based or importance-weighted pruning criteria — these methods create a form of hard parameter isolation that prevents interference. Crucially, the unpruned or free parameters in these architectures serve as shared capacity that can encode task-agnostic representations beneficial for both old and new tasks. This weight sharing strategy enables implicit forward transfer: representations learned for prior tasks that have been encoded in shared weights can be reused and built upon when learning new tasks, reducing the effective sample complexity of subsequent learning problems.

Empirical studies of pruning-based continual learning reveal that the degree of transfer enabled by shared weights depends critically on the sparsity regime. When pruning is too aggressive, little capacity remains for shared representations, and each task is effectively learned in isolation with minimal transfer. When pruning is too conservative, the model retains excessive overlap between task-specific subnetworks, which can reintroduce interference. Optimal pruning rates for maximizing transfer thus represent an important empirical calibration challenge that has motivated the development of adaptive pruning criteria that explicitly account for inter-task similarity. This tension between isolation and sharing directly parallels the sparsity trade-offs identified in the biological and architectural discussions of Sections 7.8 and 7.9, reinforcing the view that managing representational overlap is a unifying challenge across all three perspectives.

### Error Bounds and Conditions for Positive Transfer

Theoretical error bounds for both forward and backward transfer have been derived under varying assumptions about task structure, model capacity, and learning dynamics. Under overparameterized linear models — which serve as tractable theoretical proxies for deep neural networks — analyses have characterized the precise conditions under which sequential learning with shared parameterization yields positive transfer. These results generally show that positive forward transfer is achievable when the angle between the optimal weight vectors of successive tasks is small, and that backward transfer depends on a more nuanced interplay between the geometry of the task sequence and the trajectory of gradient descent updates.

More recent theoretical work has extended these analyses to nonlinear models using Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) approximations, which characterize the evolution of a neural network's predictions during gradient descent through the kernel of the linearized network. The NTK overlap matrix between tasks serves as a quantitative measure of how much learning one task influences predictions on another, thereby providing a principled basis for predicting the sign and magnitude of both forward and backward transfer without running full experiments. These theoretical tools offer promising avenues for task curriculum design and method selection in practical continual learning deployments, and connect naturally to the meta-learned feedback principles introduced in Section 7.9, where inductive biases about task relationships are encoded directly into the learning dynamics.

### Empirical Perspectives and Practical Conditions for Positive Transfer

Empirically, the conditions under which positive transfer can be systematically achieved across diverse task sequences remain an active area of investigation. Studies using large-scale benchmarks spanning multiple domains and modalities have documented wide variability in transfer outcomes depending on task ordering, model architecture, initialization strategy, and learning rate scheduling. Pre-trained model initialization has emerged as one of the most reliable empirical strategies for promoting positive forward transfer: starting from representations pre-trained on large diverse datasets provides a rich feature basis from which new tasks can be learned with reduced interference to the existing knowledge structure.

The interplay between representation quality and transfer has also been studied through the lens of representation similarity analysis, which measures the alignment between internal representations across tasks and layers. Empirical findings consistently show that tasks sharing high representational similarity exhibit stronger positive transfer, validating the theoretical predictions outlined above. Conversely, tasks from substantially different domains can exhibit negative transfer even when architectural separation is employed, motivating the development of cross-domain continual learning methods that explicitly model and accommodate distributional divergence.

Taken together, the theoretical and empirical perspectives reviewed in this subsection underscore that knowledge transfer in continual learning is a multidimensional phenomenon shaped by task geometry, model architecture, optimization dynamics, and data distribution. Achieving systematic positive transfer — in both forward and backward directions — requires moving beyond ad hoc method design toward principled frameworks grounded in learning theory, gradient analysis, and representation geometry. The threads running through Sections 7.8, 7.9, and the present subsection — parameter isolation, biologically motivated plasticity regulation, and formal transfer analysis — collectively point toward a unified view of continual learning in which the management of shared versus task-specific knowledge is the central organizing challenge. The continued development of such frameworks represents one of the most promising and impactful directions for advancing the field of continual learning toward genuinely intelligent systems that accumulate and generalize knowledge over time.

## 8 Open Challenges and Future Directions

### 8.1 Scalability to Long Task Sequences and Realistic Evaluation Settings

## 8.1 Scalability to Long Task Sequences and Realistic Evaluation Settings

One of the most pressing open challenges in continual learning is the ability to scale algorithms to long sequences of numerous, heterogeneous tasks. The majority of existing research evaluates methods on benchmarks comprising only a handful of tasks—often fewer than ten—drawn from datasets of relatively uniform size and complexity. This diverges substantially from the conditions encountered in real-world deployments, where agents must learn from potentially hundreds or thousands of tasks distributed across time in non-stationary, non-uniform streams. As we discuss in the following section, this limitation is closely intertwined with broader issues of benchmark fragmentation and metric insufficiency; however, the scalability problem is itself a distinct and fundamental challenge that deserves dedicated treatment.

A fundamental limitation of current evaluation practices is their reliance on simplified, small-scale benchmarks. As observed in [57], popular continual learning strategies exhibit sub-par performance, high levels of forgetting, and limited ability to leverage curriculum task ordering even under moderately complex multi-dataset benchmarks—raising serious questions about whether existing methods would remain viable under the far greater complexity of real-world task sequences.
