Similarly, theoretical analyses have established that any continual learner requires memory growing linearly with the number of tasks [12], underscoring that the scalability problem carries fundamental computational constraints, not merely engineering challenges.

The challenge is further compounded by the heterogeneity of real-world task sequences. Tasks encountered in practice differ substantially in their data distributions, label spaces, complexities, and frequencies, forming long-tail distributions in which some task types are rare while others recur frequently. Current benchmarks rarely model this richness. Benchmarks such as Split-MNIST or Split-CIFAR, while useful for controlled comparison, do not capture the curriculum structure, domain diversity, or temporal irregularity inherent in realistic scenarios [2]. As emphasized in [57], the research community urgently needs benchmarks that involve multiple heterogeneous tasks of varying complexity, structured according to realistic curriculum orderings.

The online continual learning setting, where data arrives as a single continuous non-stationary stream without explicit task boundaries, represents a closer approximation to real-world conditions and exposes limitations of methods that rely on clear task demarcations [7]. In this setting, each incoming batch of data must serve simultaneously for evaluation and training, demanding both plasticity and stability without the luxury of multiple learning epochs per task. Evaluating continual learning algorithms under such conditions reveals failure modes that are invisible in offline, bounded-task benchmarks—failure modes that standard evaluation protocols, as discussed in the next section, are ill-equipped to capture.

The robotics and reinforcement learning communities have made some progress in this direction. Environments like Continual World [9] expose algorithms to sequences of meaningfully diverse robotic manipulation tasks and emphasize forward transfer alongside forgetting prevention. However, even such benchmarks remain limited in the number and heterogeneity of tasks relative to the lifespan of a real autonomous agent [10; 4]. More broadly, the gap between laboratory evaluation and deployment settings remains wide: real-world agents such as robots, autonomous vehicles, or healthcare monitoring systems must process continuous streams of sensory data over extended periods, with task distributions that shift gradually and unpredictably [11].

Several additional dimensions of scalability warrant attention. First, class-incremental scenarios with class repetition—where previously seen classes reappear in later stages of the sequence—are rarely evaluated despite being common in practice [5]. Second, the assumption of fixed, known task boundaries, on which many methods depend, is unrealistic in streaming settings [81]. Third, computational and memory budgets impose practical ceilings that most current evaluations ignore: methods that perform well in unconstrained settings often fail when subjected to realistic resource constraints [3].

Addressing these scalability challenges requires a concerted community effort toward the design of richer, more realistic evaluation protocols. This includes the development of benchmarks with long-tail task distributions, natural distribution shifts, curriculum structures, and repetition, as well as standardized metrics that capture performance across all stages of a long task sequence rather than at a single snapshot. Such advances are not only essential for moving continual learning research from controlled laboratory settings toward robust, real-world deployment, but they also motivate the broader standardization agenda—encompassing unified benchmarks, metrics, and software frameworks—that we examine in the following section.

### 8.2 Standardized Benchmarks, Metrics, and Software Frameworks

## 8.2 Standardized Benchmarks, Metrics, and Software Frameworks

Building on the scalability challenges discussed in the previous subsection, a closely related and equally pressing open problem concerns the pervasive fragmentation of evaluation protocols across the continual learning literature. Different research groups routinely employ different benchmarks, metrics, experimental setups, and even conflicting definitions of core scenarios such as task-incremental, class-incremental, and domain-incremental learning, making direct comparison across methods extremely difficult. This fragmentation has significantly impeded scientific progress, as improvements claimed by newly proposed methods may not generalize beyond the specific experimental configurations under which they were evaluated. Moreover, as we argue below, the insufficiency of current metrics extends beyond accuracy to encompass reliability and interpretability concerns—a theme that the following subsection develops further in the context of uncertainty estimation and explainability.

### The Problem of Fragmented Evaluation

The current landscape of continual learning benchmarks spans a wide range of datasets and protocols, from simple Split-MNIST and Permuted-MNIST experiments to more complex benchmarks involving Split-CIFAR, Split-ImageNet, and CORe50. However, the lack of consensus on how these benchmarks should be used—including choices of task ordering, memory buffer size, number of epochs per task, and evaluation timing—means that results reported in different papers are often not directly comparable. Studies such as [19] and [174] have highlighted that the experimental protocol itself critically shapes the conclusions drawn about catastrophic forgetting, and that naive comparisons across papers can lead to misleading assessments of algorithmic progress.

A further complication arises from the dominant focus on classification benchmarks with small, artificially constructed task sequences. As noted by work studying knowledge accumulation in long task sequences [174], standard benchmarks often involve only a handful of tasks with uniform sizes and clear boundaries—conditions that, as emphasized in Section 8.1, diverge substantially from the non-stationary, long-horizon data streams encountered in real-world deployments. This narrow focus may cause algorithms optimized for short academic benchmarks to fail when deployed in more realistic settings where tasks are heterogeneous, boundaries are ambiguous, and data reoccurrence patterns are complex.

### Insufficiency of Existing Metrics

Standard evaluation metrics in continual learning—including average accuracy, backward transfer, forward transfer, and forgetting measure—while informative, do not fully capture the multidimensional cognitive capacities required of a practical continual learning system. These metrics generally fail to disentangle stability from plasticity contributions to overall performance, neglect the increasing difficulty that arises with longer task sequences, and provide limited insight into memory efficiency, computational overhead, and the system's sensitivity to dynamic task variations. Works proposing more nuanced evaluation of forgetting dynamics, such as those examining representation-level forgetting [156] or analyzing the internal mechanisms underlying accuracy drops [21; 18], suggest that surface-level accuracy metrics may obscure important information about how and why models forget.

There is a growing recognition that a comprehensive evaluation framework should assess multiple cognitive capacities simultaneously, including adaptability to new tasks, robustness to dynamic task variations, efficiency of memory utilization, calibration of predictive uncertainty, and resistance to catastrophic forgetting under diverse distribution shifts. Notably, the absence of calibration and uncertainty metrics from standard evaluation suites represents a significant gap—one that becomes especially consequential in safety-critical applications, as the following subsection on uncertainty estimation and explainability elaborates. The development of such unified metrics thus remains an important open research direction that directly motivates the concerns raised in Section 8.3.

### Role of Software Frameworks in Unifying Research

Several software frameworks have been developed with the explicit goal of standardizing continual learning research and enabling fair comparison across methods. Frameworks such as Avalanche, Sequoia, and Continuum provide unified implementations of standard benchmarks, scenarios, and algorithms, along with consistent evaluation pipelines that reduce the risk of inadvertent experimental inconsistencies. These tools have made meaningful contributions toward reproducibility by providing shared codebases that researchers can build upon rather than reimplementing algorithms from scratch. Efforts like BeGin extend this standardization to graph-based continual learning settings, recognizing that different data modalities introduce unique challenges that require tailored evaluation infrastructure.

Despite these contributions, adoption of shared frameworks across the broader research community remains incomplete. Many published works continue to rely on custom experimental setups, and even papers using the same nominal benchmarks may differ in subtle but impactful implementation details. This ongoing inconsistency underscores the need for stronger community-wide standardization efforts, and highlights that software infrastructure alone is insufficient without accompanying consensus on evaluation protocols and metric definitions.

### Future Directions for Standardization

Moving forward, the continual learning community would benefit substantially from coordinated efforts to establish standardized benchmark suites that cover diverse scenarios, task lengths, data modalities, and distribution shift types. Such benchmarks should be accompanied by clearly specified evaluation protocols detailing memory constraints, computational budgets, task boundary information availability, and permissible data access patterns. The introduction of benchmarks that explicitly assess long-horizon learning [174], incorporate realistic distribution shifts, and evaluate under computationally constrained deployment conditions would help close the gap between academic research and practical application—directly addressing the scalability concerns raised in Section 8.1.

Equally important is the development of richer metric suites that go beyond accuracy to assess stability-plasticity balance, sample efficiency, knowledge transfer efficiency, robustness to task ordering variations, and—as argued more fully in the following subsection—predictive calibration and uncertainty quantification. Community challenges and competitions—analogous to those held in computer vision and NLP—could play a valuable role in driving adoption of standardized protocols and surfacing methods that genuinely advance the state of the art across diverse and realistic evaluation conditions. Ultimately, a coordinated push toward standardization, spanning benchmarks, metrics, evaluation protocols, and software infrastructure, is essential for ensuring that progress in continual learning research translates reliably into improved real-world systems, and provides the foundation upon which principled uncertainty estimation and explainability frameworks, discussed next, can be meaningfully evaluated.

### 8.3 Model Calibration, Uncertainty Estimation, and Explainability

## 8.3 Uncertainty Estimation, Calibration, and Explainability in Continual Learning

Building on the standardization challenges identified in the previous subsection, a closely related yet distinct set of open problems concerns the reliability and interpretability of continual learning systems beyond raw accuracy metrics. Model calibration and uncertainty estimation represent critical yet underexplored challenges in this context. As models are sequentially trained on evolving task distributions, their confidence estimates can become poorly calibrated, particularly for previously learned tasks that may be revisited or tested under distribution shift. This miscalibration problem is compounded by the stability-plasticity dilemma [29; 1], where the tension between retaining old knowledge and acquiring new knowledge makes it inherently difficult to maintain well-calibrated predictive distributions across all tasks encountered during a model's lifetime.

A fundamental issue is that standard evaluation protocols in continual learning primarily measure accuracy-based metrics, largely ignoring whether the model's confidence scores remain meaningful after sequential task exposure [36]. When a continual learner forgets previous tasks or undergoes catastrophic interference, not only does accuracy degrade, but the model's uncertainty estimates for forgotten knowledge become unreliable. This renders deployed systems potentially dangerous in safety-critical applications such as healthcare, autonomous driving, or robotics, where knowing when a model is uncertain is as important as its predictive accuracy. This concern connects directly to the broader metric insufficiency problem raised in Section 8.2, reinforcing the argument that richer evaluation frameworks must account for calibration and uncertainty alongside accuracy.

Several directions have emerged to address uncertainty in continual learning. Bayesian and variational approaches provide natural frameworks for uncertainty quantification during sequential learning. Variational methods that treat network weights as probability distributions can, in principle, maintain uncertainty estimates over previously learned tasks while incorporating new information. These approaches connect naturally to regularization-based continual learning methods that leverage Fisher information and second-order approximations to protect important parameters [1]. However, scaling these Bayesian frameworks to large modern architectures remains computationally challenging, and approximations inevitably degrade uncertainty quality over long task sequences.

The problem of setup-induced forgetting and its interaction with calibration deserves particular attention. As noted in evaluations of continual learning metrics [36], the increasing difficulty of classification as more classes or tasks are added inherently causes performance degradation that is independent of catastrophic forgetting per se. This increasing task difficulty also affects calibration, as models trained on early simpler configurations may produce overconfident predictions when the problem space expands. Future metrics and evaluation protocols must therefore disentangle genuine miscalibration due to forgetting from apparent miscalibration arising from increased problem complexity.

Explainability and interpretability present another important dimension of this challenge. As continual learning systems are deployed in practice, domain experts and end users need mechanisms to understand what the model has learned, what it has forgotten, and why particular predictions are made. Visualization tools that allow practitioners to inspect the evolution of learned representations across task sequences would greatly enhance trustworthiness. Methods that track how individual neurons or network pathways contribute to specific tasks over time provide a promising direction. For example, architectures that maintain task-specific subnetworks or sparse pathways [37; 41] offer a natural basis for post-hoc interpretation, as the functional specialization of network components can be analyzed and visualized directly.

The stability gap phenomenon [35] further underscores the need for uncertainty-aware evaluation. The temporary but substantial performance drops observed when learning new tasks suggest that models pass through phases of high uncertainty and unreliability that are currently invisible under standard end-of-task evaluation protocols. A continual evaluation framework that monitors predictive uncertainty in real time, not just accuracy, would provide a more complete picture of system reliability throughout the learning process — a concern that also bears directly on the on-device and computationally budgeted deployment settings discussed in the following subsection.

Neuromodulatory and bio-inspired continual learning architectures offer intriguing connections to explainability [31; 41]. Systems that modulate network activity based on task context, analogous to neuromodulatory processes in the brain, create interpretable mechanisms whereby the system's behavior in different contexts can be traced to specific architectural components. Similarly, sparse distributed memory approaches [42] naturally support interpretability by limiting the number of active parameters for any given input, making it easier to identify which stored patterns are retrieved and combined during inference.

Future research directions in this area should pursue several complementary goals. First, developing standardized calibration metrics specifically designed for continual learning, accounting for both within-task and cross-task calibration over time, is essential. Second, integrating uncertainty estimation directly into the learning objective of continual learners, rather than treating it as a post-hoc analysis step, would enable models to maintain well-calibrated uncertainty throughout sequential training. Third, building explainability modules that allow dynamic adjustment of the continual learning process based on expert feedback represents a promising direction for human-in-the-loop continual learning systems. Finally, connecting uncertainty estimation to memory management strategies, for instance by prioritizing the storage and replay of samples for which the model is most uncertain [29], could create a principled bridge between uncertainty quantification and practical continual learning performance improvements.

Ultimately, the integration of model calibration, uncertainty estimation, and explainability into continual learning is not merely an academic concern but a prerequisite for responsible deployment of continually learning systems in high-stakes real-world environments. Without reliable uncertainty estimates and interpretable behavior, the adoption of continual learning in domains such as clinical decision support, autonomous systems, and personalized services will remain severely limited, regardless of how well these systems perform on standard accuracy-based benchmarks. These concerns become even more acute when continual learning systems must operate under strict computational constraints, as explored in the following subsection.

### 8.4 On-Device and Computationally Budgeted Continual Learning

## 8.4 On-Device and Computationally Budgeted Continual Learning

Deploying continual learning systems on resource-constrained embedded and edge devices represents one of the most pressing yet underexplored challenges in the field, and it connects directly to the reliability and interpretability concerns raised in the previous subsection. In safety-critical applications such as autonomous vehicles, mobile healthcare monitors, industrial sensors, and robotic platforms, systems must not only maintain well-calibrated uncertainty and interpretable behavior, but must do so under strict limitations on memory footprint, computational throughput, and energy consumption. Unlike controlled academic benchmarks that assume unlimited computational budgets and multiple training epochs over each task, practical on-device learning must process incoming data streams with minimal latency and memory overhead — constraints that render many state-of-the-art continual learning approaches fundamentally inapplicable in their current form.

A fundamental tension exists between the algorithmic demands of continual learning methods and the constraints of edge hardware. Replay-based methods, for instance, require maintaining memory buffers of past examples and performing iterative optimization over both new and stored data. While these approaches have demonstrated strong empirical performance in standard evaluations [56], their computational overhead during replay and multi-epoch training renders them impractical when inference queries must be served in real time or when energy budgets are strictly limited. Similarly, regularization-based approaches, despite their conceptual elegance, require computing and storing parameter importance matrices that can be prohibitively expensive for large models deployed on edge devices [178].

Architectural expansion methods present a complementary set of challenges. Dynamic architectures that grow network capacity with each new task [161] may offer strong forgetting prevention, but the increasing parameter count and associated computational cost conflict directly with the fixed hardware resources available on embedded platforms. Parameter isolation strategies, while avoiding interference between tasks, similarly accumulate resource demands over long task sequences.

The online continual learning setting, where models learn from a single-pass non-stationary data stream [53; 191], is particularly relevant for on-device scenarios since it eliminates the need for storing and repeatedly iterating over data. However, the challenge of achieving strong performance with a single pass through data while maintaining a minimal memory footprint remains largely unsolved. Empirical surveys of online continual learning methods reveal that most suffer from significant underfitting and stability problems under tight computational budgets [56], reinforcing the observation that current methods are not well-calibrated for resource-constrained deployment. This practical inadequacy also has direct implications for the uncertainty and calibration concerns discussed in the previous subsection: under tight computational budgets, the Bayesian and variational methods that are most naturally suited to uncertainty quantification become even harder to scale.

The issue is further compounded in online settings with natural distribution shifts [7], where gradient-based optimization must continuously adapt representations without the luxury of large batch sizes or extended training periods. In such settings, the computational cost of even lightweight operations such as gradient episodic memory updates or meta-learning inner loops may exceed device capabilities.

Addressing these challenges requires several complementary research directions. First, hardware-aware continual learning algorithm design must explicitly incorporate device constraints — such as memory bandwidth, floating-point operation limits, and battery life — as first-class optimization objectives rather than treating them as post-hoc deployment concerns. This hardware-aware perspective also demands that evaluation methodology evolve accordingly, a theme consistent with the broader call for richer benchmarks raised in Section 8.2.

Second, model compression techniques including quantization, pruning, and knowledge distillation offer promising avenues for reducing the memory and compute footprints of continual learners without sacrificing accuracy. Pruning-based continual learning methods that maintain sparse task-specific subnetworks [192] naturally align with on-device constraints by minimizing active parameter counts during inference. Integrating quantization-aware training into continual learning pipelines remains an open and important research direction.

Third, neuromorphic hardware platforms — designed for event-driven, sparse, and low-power computation — offer a potentially transformative substrate for continual learning. Biological neural systems, which serve as inspiration for continual learning algorithms, operate on principles of local plasticity, sparse activation, and energy efficiency that are architecturally well-matched to neuromorphic chips such as Intel's Loihi or IBM's TrueNorth. Translating the bio-inspired continual learning mechanisms discussed in the literature into neuromorphic hardware implementations represents a high-impact frontier for the field, and one that connects naturally to the personalized, on-device adaptation scenarios explored in the following subsection.

Fourth, hardware-aware meta-learning approaches that learn to initialize models or adapt learning rates in ways that are explicitly optimized for fast on-device adaptation under compute constraints merit further investigation. Such approaches could enable continual learners that require minimal gradient steps to consolidate new knowledge, reducing both time and energy costs per task update — a property that is equally desirable in the personalized continual adaptation settings discussed next.

Finally, the evaluation methodology for on-device continual learning must evolve. Current benchmarks rarely measure wall-clock time, energy consumption, or memory bandwidth utilization, focusing instead on accuracy metrics alone. Introducing standardized hardware-constrained evaluation protocols — measuring accuracy under fixed floating-point operation budgets or fixed memory limits — would enable fairer and more practically relevant comparisons across methods, encouraging the development of algorithms genuinely suited to real-world edge deployment. Such protocols would also need to account for calibration and uncertainty quality under resource constraints, bridging the evaluation gaps identified in the previous subsection with the practical deployment realities examined here.

In summary, the gap between the computational demands of state-of-the-art continual learning algorithms and the constraints of real-world deployable hardware remains one of the most critical open challenges in the field. Closing this gap requires coordinated progress across algorithm design, model compression, hardware co-design, and evaluation methodology — progress that must proceed in tandem with advances in uncertainty estimation, interpretability, and personalization if continual learning systems are to be responsibly deployed at scale.

### 8.5 Personalization, Model Editing, and Specialization

## 8.5 Personalization and Domain-Specific Continual Adaptation

Personalization represents a critical yet underexplored frontier in continual learning research. While most existing continual learning frameworks optimize for aggregate performance across a generic population of tasks or users, real-world deployments increasingly demand systems that can adapt to the preferences, habits, and evolving needs of individual users or specialized domains. This tension between general knowledge retention and personalized specialization introduces a novel dimension to the stability-plasticity dilemma that standard continual learning formulations do not adequately address. Importantly, this challenge extends naturally from the resource-constrained deployment concerns discussed in the previous section: personalized adaptation must often occur on-device, under strict computational budgets, and with sensitivity to user privacy.

The connection between continual learning and model editing is particularly salient in this context. Model editing refers to the targeted updating of specific knowledge within a trained model without disrupting unrelated capabilities — a goal that aligns closely with the objectives of continual learning but operates at a finer granularity. While continual learning typically manages task-level sequential updates, model editing addresses fact-level or concept-level modifications. Integrating these two paradigms could enable systems that not only accumulate knowledge over time but also surgically correct, refine, or personalize that knowledge for specific users or deployment contexts. This integration is especially relevant for large language models and multimodal systems, where factual knowledge must be kept current and contextually appropriate for diverse user populations.

Personalized continual learning is particularly compelling in dialogue and language-learning applications. Systems designed for task-oriented dialogue, for instance, must continuously accommodate new user intents, domain vocabularies, and interaction styles without forgetting previously acquired conversational competencies [193]. Parameter-efficient approaches that store lightweight, user-specific adaptation modules — such as prompt tokens or low-rank adapters — offer a promising avenue for achieving personalization without incurring the computational cost of full model retraining. Such approaches allow a shared backbone model to be rapidly specialized to individual users by learning and storing minimal per-user parameters, while the core model remains frozen and protected from catastrophic forgetting. This parameter efficiency also directly addresses the on-device memory constraints highlighted in the preceding section, suggesting a natural convergence between hardware-aware and personalization-aware continual learning design.

In the context of specialized domain adaptation, continual learning methods must balance the acquisition of domain-specific knowledge against the preservation of general capabilities. Methods such as sub-network discovery and soft-masking [145] and selective knowledge transfer mechanisms [194] provide foundational tools for this challenge, enabling models to allocate dedicated capacity for specialized tasks while leveraging shared representations for knowledge transfer. However, these methods have largely been evaluated in controlled academic settings and have yet to be systematically extended to personalization scenarios involving heterogeneous user-specific data streams.

A particularly underexplored challenge is the handling of negative transfer in personalization settings. When a model is specialized for one user or domain, the acquired knowledge may interfere with the needs of other users or broader deployment contexts [65]. Mechanisms for active forgetting — selectively discarding knowledge that is harmful for current specialization objectives — thus become essential components of personalized continual learning systems. This requires principled methods for measuring and managing knowledge relevance at the individual user level, a problem that intersects with areas such as federated learning and privacy-preserving machine learning. The biological analogy is instructive here as well: as discussed in the following section, adaptive forgetting of irrelevant or outdated information is increasingly recognized as a functional feature of biological memory systems rather than a failure mode, suggesting that artificial systems should similarly embrace principled forgetting as a tool for personalization.

Looking forward, several promising research directions emerge at the intersection of continual learning and personalization. First, developing lightweight personalization modules that can be efficiently stored and retrieved per user, analogous to prompt pools in vision-language continual learning, represents an immediate practical need. Second, integrating model editing techniques with continual learning pipelines could enable fine-grained, on-demand knowledge updates that serve individual user requirements without disrupting global model capabilities. Third, the design of evaluation benchmarks that explicitly measure personalization effectiveness — such as adaptation speed to individual user preferences, stability of personalized knowledge over time, and interference between different users' adaptations — is urgently needed to drive progress in this area. Finally, continual learning systems deployed in personalization contexts must grapple with user privacy constraints, necessitating the development of on-device, privacy-preserving continual adaptation mechanisms that do not require sharing sensitive user data with centralized servers. Addressing these open challenges will be essential for realizing the vision of truly adaptive, user-centric artificial intelligence systems — systems that must ultimately draw on the computational efficiency principles, biologically inspired mechanisms, and robust evaluation methodologies discussed throughout this chapter.

### 8.6 Bridging Artificial and Biological Continual Learning Systems

## 8.6 Bridging Artificial and Biological Continual Learning Systems

Despite decades of progress in continual learning research, a profound gap remains between how biological organisms learn across a lifetime and how artificial neural networks handle sequential knowledge acquisition. Biological systems — from rodents navigating changing environments to humans accumulating decades of expertise — exhibit remarkable resilience to catastrophic forgetting, a challenge that continues to plague artificial systems. Bridging this gap represents one of the most intellectually rich and practically consequential open problems in the field, and one that carries direct implications for the personalization, efficiency, and ethical deployment challenges discussed in the surrounding sections of this survey. In particular, the biologically inspired mechanisms examined here — including adaptive forgetting, neuromodulatory gating, and multi-timescale consolidation — offer foundational principles for addressing the stability-plasticity dilemma that motivates the ethical concerns around fairness, transparency, and trust taken up in the following section.

### The Biological Blueprint

Neuroscience has long offered inspiration for continual learning researchers. Biological brains employ a rich arsenal of mechanisms that collectively enable stable long-term memory alongside rapid adaptation to new experiences. Among the most influential of these is the **Complementary Learning Systems (CLS) theory**, which proposes that the hippocampus enables fast, episodic encoding of new experiences while the neocortex supports slow, distributed consolidation of generalized knowledge. This dual-memory architecture has directly inspired replay-based continual learning methods [195], which simulate hippocampal replay by interleaving old experiences with new learning signals. However, current artificial implementations remain crude approximations of the biological system: they typically rely on stored exemplars or generative models rather than genuine memory consolidation processes, and they lack the rich contextual gating that characterizes hippocampal function. Notably, the privacy concerns associated with replay-based methods — discussed in the following section — further motivate the development of more biologically faithful consolidation mechanisms that do not require raw data retention.

**Synaptic plasticity** offers another critical biological principle. The brain dynamically regulates synaptic strength according to activity-dependent rules, with individual synapses varying in their susceptibility to modification — a property sometimes referred to as metaplasticity. Prototype-based continual learning approaches have begun incorporating metaplasticity-inspired mechanisms, adapting learning rates individually per prototype to mitigate forgetting in open-world scenarios [78]. Yet these implementations only scratch the surface of biological synaptic sophistication, which involves cascades of molecular events operating across multiple timescales, from milliseconds to years.

**Schematic memory** represents a higher-level cognitive abstraction that biological systems leverage to efficiently encode new information by relating it to existing knowledge structures or schemas. Inspired by this principle, the SMART framework [62] introduces mechanisms for long-term and short-term forgetting, distinguishing between information that should persist (schematic knowledge) and information that should be actively discarded (transient details). This biologically grounded perspective directly challenges the dominant assumption in continual learning that all forgetting is undesirable — in reality, adaptive forgetting of irrelevant or outdated information is a feature rather than a bug of biological memory systems. As noted in the preceding section on personalization, such principled forgetting is also functionally valuable for user-specific adaptation, where the selective discarding of irrelevant knowledge enables more efficient specialization.

### Local Learning Rules and the Backpropagation Gap

One of the deepest disconnects between biological and artificial learning systems concerns the learning algorithm itself. Backpropagation through time, the workhorse of modern deep learning, requires global error signals to propagate backward through network layers, a process widely regarded as biologically implausible. Biological neurons learn through local synaptic rules, most famously captured by Hebbian learning: "neurons that fire together, wire together." Hebbian continual representation learning approaches [196] combine sparse neural networks with Hebbian update rules to construct models whose weights remain interpretable and whose learning dynamics do not require global error signals. Such approaches have demonstrated promising results in unsupervised continual settings, though scaling to complex tasks remains an open challenge.

The biological implausibility of backpropagation is not merely an aesthetic concern: it has direct consequences for continual learning. Global weight updates create interference across tasks because modifying weights for a new task unavoidably perturbs representations used for old tasks. Local learning rules, by contrast, can in principle update only the synaptic connections most relevant to the current experience, naturally limiting cross-task interference. Future research should invest in developing local learning algorithms that approach the performance of backpropagation on complex tasks while inheriting the interference-limiting properties of biological plasticity rules.

### Neuromodulation and Context-Dependent Gating

Biological brains do not learn uniformly across all contexts. Neuromodulatory systems — including dopaminergic, cholinergic, and noradrenergic pathways — dynamically regulate which neural circuits are plastic at any given moment, depending on behavioral context, novelty, and reward signals. This context-dependent gating of plasticity allows biological systems to selectively update relevant representations without disturbing consolidated knowledge. Artificial continual learning systems have only begun to incorporate neuromodulation-inspired mechanisms. Dynamic sparse training approaches [121] allocate distinct sparse subnetworks to different tasks, which can be viewed as a rudimentary form of context-dependent gating. However, biological neuromodulation is far more sophisticated, operating across multiple levels of neural hierarchy simultaneously and adapting dynamically based on ongoing task performance rather than relying on explicit task labels. This dynamic, context-sensitive regulation also resonates with the need for personalized adaptation discussed in the previous section, where per-user gating of model plasticity could enable efficient specialization without cross-user interference.

### Structural Plasticity and Neurogenesis

Beyond synaptic weight modification, biological brains undergo structural changes including neurogenesis (the formation of new neurons), synaptogenesis (formation of new synaptic connections), and synaptic pruning. These processes allow the brain to physically expand its representational capacity in response to new learning demands while pruning redundant or rarely used connections. Dynamic expandable network architectures in artificial continual learning loosely mirror structural plasticity by adding new neurons or modules when existing capacity is insufficient. However, biological neurogenesis is tightly regulated by activity patterns, experience, and molecular signals in ways that have not yet been translated into principled artificial algorithms. Developing principled analogues of structural plasticity — where network growth and pruning are guided by biologically inspired rules rather than heuristic capacity thresholds — represents a promising frontier with natural connections to the resource-aware deployment concerns discussed earlier in this chapter.

### Multi-Timescale Learning and Consolidation

Biological memory consolidation operates across multiple timescales: rapid encoding in the hippocampus is followed by slower cortical consolidation during sleep, during which hippocampal replay of recent experiences drives gradual strengthening of neocortical representations. This multi-timescale organization allows biological systems to integrate new information with existing knowledge structures without catastrophic interference. Most artificial continual learning systems, by contrast, operate on a single timescale determined by gradient descent updates. Incorporating explicit multi-timescale mechanisms — such as fast weights for rapid encoding and slow weights for consolidated long-term memory — could substantially improve the stability-plasticity balance. Generative replay during an offline consolidation phase [195] represents one step in this direction, but a more principled integration of multi-timescale dynamics remains an open research challenge. The ethical implications of such mechanisms are also worth noting: offline consolidation phases that operate on stored or generated data must be designed with the same privacy protections in mind that govern online learning, a concern developed further in the following section.

### Open-World Novelty Detection

Biological organisms continuously encounter novel stimuli and must rapidly determine whether incoming information represents a genuinely new category requiring dedicated learning resources or merely a variation of something already known. This open-world competence — the ability to detect, characterize, and learn from genuine novelty without explicit supervision — is largely absent from current artificial continual learning systems, which typically assume that the set of possible categories or tasks is known in advance. The prototype-based approach of [78] makes progress on this front by enabling unsupervised novelty detection in robotic vision settings, but scaling such capabilities to complex real-world environments with high-dimensional sensory inputs remains a major open challenge. From an ethical standpoint, systems that autonomously identify and learn from novel patterns in open-world settings must also be equipped with robust transparency and oversight mechanisms to ensure that unanticipated knowledge acquisition does not lead to unintended behavioral changes — a concern directly relevant to the ethical framework developed in the following section.

### Future Research Directions

Translating neuroscientific insights into practical and scalable continual learning algorithms requires progress on several fronts:

**Biologically plausible learning algorithms at scale**: Current Hebbian and local learning approaches [196] demonstrate proof-of-concept results on simple datasets. Future work should systematically investigate how to scale local plasticity rules to deep networks trained on complex tasks, potentially by combining local learning with hierarchical predictive coding frameworks.

**Principled implementation of multi-timescale dynamics**: Future architectures should explicitly model fast and slow memory systems, with the fast system enabling rapid encoding and the slow system supporting gradual consolidation. Replay mechanisms that operate during dedicated offline phases — analogous to sleep-dependent memory consolidation — deserve further investigation, with careful attention to the privacy implications of retained or generated replay data.

**Adaptive forgetting as a feature**: Drawing inspiration from schematic memory principles [62], future continual learning systems should incorporate mechanisms for principled active forgetting that selectively discard outdated or low-utility information, improving memory efficiency while maintaining generalization performance. As highlighted in the preceding personalization section, such mechanisms are equally valuable for user-specific adaptation, where irrelevant knowledge should be discarded to make room for individualized specialization.

**Neuromodulation-inspired dynamic plasticity control**: Rather than applying uniform learning rates across all parameters and tasks, future systems should develop context-sensitive plasticity control mechanisms inspired by biological neuromodulation, dynamically regulating which parameters can be modified based on task context and ongoing performance signals.

**Richer complementary learning system models**: Current replay-based methods capture only a fraction of the sophistication of biological CLS theory. Future implementations should model the asymmetric communication between fast and slow memory systems, including the role of reactivation patterns, contextual tags, and attention signals in determining which experiences are consolidated.

**Integration with neuromorphic hardware**: Biologically inspired continual learning algorithms may be most naturally implemented on neuromorphic hardware platforms that support spiking neural networks and local on-chip plasticity rules. Prototype-based neuromorphic implementations [78] have demonstrated the feasibility of this direction, pointing toward a future where continual learning algorithms and hardware co-evolve in mutual inspiration — a trajectory that also holds promise for the energy-efficient, on-device deployment scenarios discussed in earlier sections.

In summary, the gap between biological and artificial continual learning systems remains wide, but the distance is narrowing. Progress will require genuine interdisciplinary collaboration between neuroscientists, cognitive scientists, and machine learning researchers, moving beyond superficial analogies toward mechanistic implementations of biological principles that are both computationally tractable and empirically effective on real-world tasks. Crucially, as the following section argues, this technical progress must be pursued in concert with the ethical principles of privacy, fairness, and transparency that will ultimately determine whether biologically inspired continual learning systems can be responsibly deployed in the high-stakes domains where they are most needed.

### 8.7 Ethical Considerations, Privacy, and Trustworthiness in Continual Learning

## 8.7 Ethical Considerations in Continual Learning

Just as bridging biological and artificial learning systems demands careful attention to the mechanisms underlying adaptation and memory, deploying continual learning systems in the real world demands equally careful attention to the ethical dimensions of systems that continuously update their behavior in response to evolving data streams. As continual learning moves from controlled benchmarks toward real-world applications, a constellation of ethical challenges emerges that the research community must address with urgency and rigor. These challenges span data privacy, algorithmic fairness, transparency, and the broader trustworthiness of systems whose knowledge states are inherently dynamic and shifting.

### Data Privacy in On-Device and Federated Settings

One of the most pressing ethical concerns in continual learning is the protection of sensitive data. In on-device continual learning, models must adapt to personal user data — medical records, behavioral patterns, financial transactions — without transmitting raw data to central servers. This requirement is particularly acute in healthcare applications, where longitudinal patient data must be processed under stringent regulatory frameworks such as HIPAA and GDPR. Replay-based methods, while effective at mitigating catastrophic forgetting, inherently require storing or regenerating past data samples [6], which raises significant privacy risks when those samples contain personally identifiable information. The tension between maintaining an episodic memory buffer for effective knowledge retention and protecting individual privacy represents a fundamental challenge that purely accuracy-focused research has largely overlooked.

Federated continual learning settings, where distributed clients independently learn on evolving local data streams, compound these privacy concerns. Although federated architectures avoid raw data sharing, they remain vulnerable to membership inference attacks, gradient inversion attacks, and model inversion techniques that can reconstruct sensitive training samples from shared model updates. The development of differentially private mechanisms for continual learning — where noise is carefully calibrated to protect individual data contributions without severely degrading model performance across sequential tasks — represents an underexplored but critical research frontier. Furthermore, the heterogeneous and non-stationary nature of local data distributions in federated continual learning makes it especially difficult to provide strong privacy guarantees while maintaining competitive learning performance.

### Fairness Under Shifting Data Distributions

Algorithmic fairness in continual learning presents unique challenges that are absent in static batch learning settings. As data distributions shift over time, a continual learning system may inadvertently develop or amplify biases toward demographic groups that are underrepresented in recent data streams while forgetting patterns learned from historically well-represented groups. This dynamic form of bias drift is particularly insidious because it can occur silently, without any explicit change in model architecture or objective function. For instance, a medical diagnosis system that continually learns from clinical data may progressively become less accurate for patient populations whose data becomes sparse in later training phases, even if it initially demonstrated equitable performance.

The stability-plasticity dilemma [1; 46] has direct implications for fairness: methods that prioritize stability by anchoring model parameters to their initial configurations may perpetuate historical biases encoded in earlier training phases, while highly plastic methods may rapidly forget equitable representations learned from balanced early datasets. Neither extreme is satisfactory from a fairness perspective. Future research must develop fairness-aware continual learning objectives that explicitly monitor and constrain disparate impact across demographic groups throughout the learning trajectory, not merely at deployment time.

### Transparency and Explainability in Continuously Updating Systems

Transparency poses distinctive challenges in continual learning contexts because the model's behavior evolves over time, making it difficult for users, auditors, and regulatory bodies to maintain a consistent understanding of system capabilities and limitations. In safety-critical domains such as autonomous driving, clinical decision support, and financial services, stakeholders must be able to understand not only what a model currently knows but also what it has forgotten, what new patterns it has recently acquired, and how confident it is in its predictions across the full spectrum of previously encountered task distributions. Static explainability techniques developed for fixed models are insufficient for this purpose, as they cannot capture the dynamic knowledge state of a continually learning system.

The development of temporal explainability frameworks — tools that can visualize the evolution of model representations, decision boundaries, and feature attributions across task sequences — represents a significant open problem. Such frameworks would need to account for the fact that internal representations may shift dramatically as new tasks are learned [96], potentially rendering earlier explanations obsolete or misleading. Furthermore, methods that use architectural isolation strategies [41] or neuromodulatory gating [92; 31] to protect task-specific knowledge may create explanations that are fragmented across subnetworks, complicating holistic interpretability.

### Trust and Human Perceptions of Continually Learning Systems

The question of how humans perceive and trust systems that continuously update their behavior is a largely unexplored dimension of continual learning research. In human-robot interaction settings, users develop mental models of robot capabilities over time, and unexpected behavioral changes — whether due to the acquisition of new skills or the forgetting of previously demonstrated competencies — can significantly erode trust. A robot that reliably performs a task during initial deployment but subsequently fails on that same task after learning new skills may be perceived as untrustworthy or incompetent, even if its overall performance across all tasks has improved. This asymmetry in how humans evaluate forgetting versus learning gains has important implications for the design of continual learning systems intended for human-facing applications.

Building trustworthy continual learning systems requires mechanisms for communicating knowledge boundaries to users — clearly indicating which tasks fall within the current competence envelope of the system and which may have degraded due to catastrophic forgetting [1]. Confidence calibration is particularly important in this context: a well-calibrated continual learner should express higher uncertainty on tasks where forgetting has occurred, rather than confidently producing incorrect outputs. This connects to the broader challenge of uncertainty quantification in continual learning, where methods such as Bayesian continual learning frameworks [109] offer principled approaches to maintaining calibrated uncertainty estimates across sequential task distributions.

### Responsible AI Principles and Future Research Directions

Moving toward responsible deployment of continual learning systems requires the integration of privacy-preserving mechanisms, fairness constraints, and transparency tools as first-class objectives in algorithm design, rather than post-hoc additions. This imperative connects directly to the forward-looking integration directions discussed in the following section: as continual learning is increasingly combined with foundation models, meta-learning frameworks, and federated architectures, the ethical stakes of these integrations only grow larger.

Several promising directions merit concerted research attention. First, the development of privacy-preserving replay mechanisms — such as differentially private generative replay or cryptographically protected episodic memory buffers — could enable effective knowledge retention without compromising data subject privacy. The use of generative models for pseudo-rehearsal [197; 195] offers a potential pathway, as synthetic data generation can decouple replay from raw data storage, though the fidelity and privacy properties of generated samples must be rigorously evaluated.

Second, fairness-regularized continual learning objectives should be developed that explicitly penalize disparate forgetting rates across demographic groups, ensuring that the benefits of knowledge retention are equitably distributed. Third, auditing frameworks for continual learning systems — analogous to model cards and datasheets in static machine learning — should be developed to document the sequential training history, data distribution shifts, and performance trajectories that characterize a deployed continual learning system. Such documentation would support regulatory compliance and enable meaningful oversight by affected communities.

Finally, the broader question of accountability in systems that evolve autonomously over time — potentially in ways not anticipated by their designers — requires careful consideration. When a continually learning system causes harm due to knowledge acquired after deployment, the attribution of responsibility between system designers, data providers, and deploying organizations remains legally and ethically ambiguous. Establishing clear governance frameworks for continual learning deployments, particularly in high-stakes domains such as healthcare [62] and autonomous systems [10], represents one of the most important long-term challenges at the intersection of continual learning research and responsible AI development. Addressing these ethical dimensions in concert with the technical advances described throughout this survey will be essential for ensuring that continual learning systems are not only capable but also trustworthy, equitable, and accountable as they are integrated into the most consequential domains of human activity.

### 8.8 Integration with Foundation Models, Meta-Learning, and Emerging Paradigms

## 8.8 Integration with Emerging Paradigms and Future Directions

The ethical imperatives outlined in the preceding section — privacy preservation, fairness under distribution shift, transparency, and accountability — do not exist in isolation from technical development; rather, they must be addressed in concert with the forward-looking integrations that define the next frontier of continual learning research. The integration of continual learning with pre-trained foundation models, meta-learning frameworks, and emerging paradigms such as self-supervised learning and neural architecture search represents one of the most promising such frontiers in the field. As the research community continues to grapple with catastrophic forgetting and the stability-plasticity dilemma, leveraging the rich prior knowledge encoded in large pre-trained models offers a compelling pathway toward more robust and scalable continual learning systems — while simultaneously raising the ethical stakes that demand careful governance.

**Foundation Models and Large Language Models.** Pre-trained foundation models, including large language models and vision-language models, provide powerful initialization points that inherently reduce the severity of catastrophic forgetting. Because these models have been trained on vast and diverse corpora, their representations tend to be general and transferable, meaning that fine-tuning on sequential tasks causes comparatively less destructive interference than training from scratch. The future challenge, however, lies in developing principled methods for continually updating these foundation models as new knowledge, domains, and tasks emerge over time. Key open questions include how to perform selective knowledge integration without overwriting prior capabilities, how to manage the enormous computational cost of updating billion-parameter models in a continual fashion, and how to evaluate the fidelity of retained knowledge across diverse downstream benchmarks. The use of large language models for data synthesis is also an emerging direction: generative models can synthesize pseudo-examples of prior tasks, reducing the reliance on stored real data and addressing privacy constraints inherent in sensitive deployment scenarios [3].

**Meta-Learning Integration.** The connection between meta-learning and continual learning has long been recognized as a productive area of inquiry. Meta-learning approaches, which train models to acquire learning-to-learn capabilities across a distribution of tasks, are naturally aligned with the continual learning objective of rapid adaptation without forgetting [102]. Methods such as Meta-Experience Replay combine experience replay with gradient-based meta-learning to explicitly minimize interference between tasks while maximizing forward transfer [98]. Similarly, self-attention meta-learners learn to selectively leverage prior knowledge representations for each new task, reducing inter-task interference while building on accumulated experience [99]. The formulation of continual learning as a sequence modeling problem further opens the door to leveraging powerful sequence models, including transformers and their efficient variants, as meta-continual learning engines [101]. Future research directions include scaling these meta-continual learning frameworks to longer task sequences and more heterogeneous task distributions, as well as exploring how meta-learned initialization strategies can be combined with foundation model fine-tuning to achieve both rapid adaptation and robust retention.

**Self-Supervised and Unsupervised Continual Learning.** A compelling observation from recent empirical work is that unsupervised and self-supervised representations are surprisingly more robust to catastrophic forgetting than supervised counterparts, achieve better overall performance, and generalize more effectively to out-of-distribution tasks [177]. This finding suggests that the continual learning community should more aggressively explore the synergies between self-supervised pre-training and continual adaptation. Techniques such as Lifelong Unsupervised Mixup, which interpolates between current and past task instances to alleviate forgetting in the absence of labels, point toward a broader class of label-free continual learning strategies that are more applicable to real-world settings where annotations are scarce or unavailable [177]. Open research questions include how to design self-supervised pretext tasks that promote the formation of representations stable under sequential distribution shifts, and how to combine self-supervised continual pre-training with downstream task-specific fine-tuning in a way that preserves both generality and specialization. Notably, the greater distributional robustness of self-supervised representations connects directly to the practical deployment challenges discussed in the following section, where continual learning systems must contend with open-world novelty and unpredictable distribution shift.

**Neural Architecture Search and Dynamic Architectures.** Neural architecture search offers the tantalizing prospect of automatically discovering network structures that strike an optimal balance between plasticity and stability for a given continual learning setting. Bayesian approaches to structure adaptation can learn task-specific subnetworks while enabling knowledge sharing across tasks through overlapping sparse parameter subsets [119]. Sparse distributed memory architectures, drawing from biological principles, provide another avenue for scaling continual learning through parameter isolation and selective activation [42]. Future work should investigate how to make architecture search computationally tractable for the continual learning setting, where the search itself must be performed online and without access to all tasks simultaneously. The dynamic architectures that emerge from such search procedures also bear directly on the long-horizon deployment scenarios examined in the following section, where unbounded growth in model size must be avoided without sacrificing capacity for new knowledge.

**Federated and Distributed Paradigms.** The integration of continual learning with federated learning introduces additional complexity, as distributed clients independently encounter evolving local data streams while collaborating without sharing raw data. This paradigm sits at the intersection of the privacy concerns raised in the preceding section and the system-level engineering challenges addressed in the following one. Frameworks such as FedWeIT decompose network weights into global and task-specific parameters, enabling weighted inter-client knowledge transfer while minimizing interference from incompatible tasks [104]. Masked autoencoders offer an efficient mechanism for federated continual representation learning, using masking strategies integrated with task attention mechanisms to enable selective knowledge transfer across clients [105]. Scaling these federated continual learning frameworks to larger numbers of clients, more heterogeneous task distributions, and stricter privacy constraints remains an important open problem that bridges the ethical imperatives and practical deployment demands discussed in the surrounding sections.

**Open Research Questions.** Across all these integration directions, several overarching challenges remain unresolved. First, evaluation protocols must evolve to assess continual learning performance at the scale and diversity demanded by real-world deployment, moving beyond simplified benchmarks toward settings that reflect the complexity of heterogeneous, long-tail task sequences [3] — a theme that is taken up in depth in the following section on practical deployment. Second, the computational and memory costs of integrating continual learning with large foundation models must be addressed through techniques such as parameter-efficient fine-tuning, model compression, and hardware-aware optimization. Third, the theoretical foundations for these integrations remain underdeveloped; rigorous analyses of how pre-trained representations affect forgetting dynamics, what forms of meta-learned initialization guarantee bounded interference, and under what conditions self-supervised objectives promote stable continual representations are all needed. Addressing these questions will be essential for realizing the full potential of integrating continual learning with the most powerful tools in the modern machine learning arsenal, and for ensuring that such systems can be deployed responsibly, efficiently, and at scale in the real-world settings that motivate the entire endeavor.

### 8.9 Toward Practical Real-World Deployment of Continual Learning Systems

## 8.9 Toward Practical Real-World Deployment of Continual Learning Systems

Building upon the integrations explored in the preceding discussion—foundation models, meta-learning, self-supervised learning, neural architecture search, and federated paradigms—a natural and urgent question arises: how do these advances translate into systems that function reliably in genuine real-world settings? Bridging the gap between controlled academic benchmarks and practical deployment represents one of the most pressing imperatives for the continual learning community. While impressive progress has been made on standardized benchmarks such as Split-MNIST, Split-CIFAR, and CORe50, the conditions under which these evaluations are conducted differ substantially from the messy, unpredictable conditions encountered in autonomous robots, smart city infrastructures, healthcare monitoring systems, and cloud-based machine learning services. Realizing the promise of continual learning in such settings demands a coordinated effort to address several intertwined challenges spanning algorithm design, evaluation methodology, hardware constraints, and system architecture.

**From Benchmark Assumptions to Real-World Complexity**

Most existing continual learning research operates under simplifying assumptions that rarely hold in practice. Task boundaries are typically assumed to be known and crisp, the number of tasks is small and uniformly distributed, and the evaluation window is short [110]. In real-world deployments, however, data arrives as continuous, potentially non-stationary streams with no clear delineation between tasks, class distributions are highly imbalanced, and systems must operate over arbitrarily long horizons spanning months or years [122]. The benchmarks commonly used today are therefore insufficient to capture the full difficulty of real-world non-stationarity, and there is a pressing need for evaluation protocols that reflect long-tail task distributions, repetition of earlier concepts, and open-world novelty [57]. This gap between benchmark assumptions and deployment realities is further compounded when continual learning systems must be integrated with the large-scale foundation models and federated frameworks discussed previously, where the complexity of real-world data distributions is even more pronounced.

**Handling Open-World Novelty and Distribution Shift**

In real deployments, continual learning systems inevitably encounter data from classes, domains, or distributions that were never seen during training. Existing methods largely assume a closed-world setting where the universe of possible inputs is fixed and known in advance. Moving toward open-world continual learning requires integrating reliable out-of-distribution detection with the ability to autonomously decide whether novel inputs constitute a new task, a new class within an existing task, or an outlier to be ignored. Methods that rely on explicit task identity signals at test time [153] are ill-suited for this regime. Instead, future systems must perform simultaneous task inference and prediction, gracefully managing uncertainty as new concepts emerge. The integration of domain-incremental learning strategies [170] with open-set recognition is a promising but largely unexplored direction. Notably, the self-supervised and unsupervised representations discussed in the preceding section—which exhibit greater robustness to distribution shift—may offer a natural foundation for such open-world continual learning systems.

**Dynamic Hyperparameter Adaptation and Self-Regulation**

Practical deployment exposes another fundamental limitation of current continual learning research: hyperparameters such as learning rates, regularization strengths, memory buffer sizes, and replay frequencies are typically tuned once on a validation set and held fixed throughout evaluation. In real systems, these settings may need to vary dynamically depending on task difficulty, data volume, and available computational resources. The self-paced mechanisms explored in approaches like self-paced weight consolidation [198] hint at the potential for adaptive regularization, but a general framework for online hyperparameter adaptation across diverse real-world conditions remains absent. Incorporating meta-learning principles to automate these adaptations—potentially through learning-to-learn frameworks that acquire tuning strategies from past task sequences, as discussed in the context of meta-continual learning—represents a critical future direction that directly connects algorithmic advances to deployment readiness.

**Computational Efficiency and On-Device Deployment**

A recurring obstacle to real-world deployment is the computational overhead imposed by many continual learning methods. Replay-based approaches incur storage and retraining costs that scale with buffer size [116; 114], while architectural expansion methods [119] and generative replay approaches [118] introduce additional model complexity. For applications deployed on edge devices, embedded sensors, or mobile platforms—such as wearable health monitors, autonomous vehicles, or industrial robots—memory footprints and inference latencies must remain tightly bounded. Empirical studies on mobile and embedded systems [80] demonstrate that many state-of-the-art methods are feasible at small scale, but their generalizability to diverse sensing modalities and longer task sequences remains to be established. Future work should prioritize compression-aware continual learning, jointly optimizing for accuracy and resource utilization through quantization and pruning techniques [120], and explore neuromorphic hardware platforms that intrinsically support sparse, event-driven learning. These efficiency considerations are especially acute in the context of continually updating large foundation models, where the parameter-efficient fine-tuning strategies highlighted earlier become not merely desirable but essential.

**Long-Horizon Task Sequences and Knowledge Accumulation**

One of the most underexplored challenges in real-world continual learning is the management of knowledge over extremely long task sequences—potentially comprising hundreds or thousands of incremental learning experiences. Current methods have been validated primarily on sequences of five to twenty tasks, leaving open fundamental questions about how regularization penalties, memory buffers, and architectural capacity scale as sequences grow. Computational complexity analyses establish that optimal continual learning is NP-hard and requires memory growing with the number of tasks [115], imposing fundamental theoretical limits on what can be achieved with bounded resources. Practical deployments must therefore incorporate principled forgetting mechanisms—selectively discarding outdated or redundant knowledge to free capacity for future experiences, drawing inspiration from neuroscience theories of active forgetting and memory consolidation [107]. The development of dynamic sparse training techniques [121] and parameter isolation strategies [108] offers a promising direction for managing long-horizon sequences without unbounded growth in model size, complementing the neural architecture search and dynamic architecture approaches discussed in the preceding section.

**Continual Learning as a Service and System-Level Integration**

Beyond algorithmic advances, transitioning continual learning to production systems requires addressing system-level engineering challenges. Cloud-based Continual Learning-as-a-Service platforms must handle concurrent model updates from multiple data streams, coordinate knowledge sharing across distributed deployments while preserving data privacy, and provide rollback mechanisms when model updates degrade performance. These requirements intersect directly with the federated learning paradigms discussed previously, where privacy-preserving distributed training is essential, and with the need for standardized evaluation frameworks that enable fair comparison of deployed systems over time [199; 200]. Healthcare applications illustrate these demands vividly: models processing longitudinal clinical records must adapt to patient population shifts and emerging disease patterns while strictly protecting sensitive data [201]. The scalability challenges inherent in such service-level deployments underscore why the theoretical, algorithmic, and systems perspectives developed throughout this survey must ultimately be synthesized into a coherent engineering discipline.

**Toward a Deployment Roadmap**

A viable roadmap for practical real-world deployment of continual learning systems should progress along several parallel tracks. First, the research community must develop richer, more realistic benchmarks that incorporate long task sequences, open-world novelty, imbalanced distributions, and resource constraints [110; 57]. Second, algorithms must be co-designed with hardware and system constraints in mind, ensuring that methods demonstrated effective in laboratory settings translate to deployable implementations. Third, evaluation protocols must be standardized across domains and shared through community-maintained software frameworks, enabling reproducible comparison and meaningful measurement of progress [199]. Fourth, interdisciplinary collaboration with neuroscientists, ethicists, and domain experts is essential to ensure that deployed systems are not only effective but also safe, transparent, and aligned with human values. By pursuing these directions in concert—and by drawing together the full range of advances surveyed throughout this work, from regularization and replay to architecture design, meta-learning, and foundation model integration—the continual learning community can move decisively from the promise demonstrated on academic benchmarks toward the transformative impact envisioned in autonomous systems, personalized healthcare, smart cities, and beyond.
